Global sections of equivariant line bundles on the p p -adic upper half plane
Konstantin Ardakov, Simon Wadsley

Abstract
. Let F F be a finite extension of ℚ p \mathbb{Q}_{p} , let Ω F \Omega_{F} be Drinfeld’s upper half-plane over F F and let G 0 G^{0} the subgroup of G ​ L 2 ​ ( F ) GL_{2}(F) consisting of elements whose determinant has norm 1 1 . Let ℒ \mathscr{L} be a torsion G 0 G^{0} -equivariant line bundle with connection on Ω F \Omega_{F} . We show that the strong dual of ℒ ⁡ ( Ω F ) \mathscr{L}(\Omega_{F}) is an admissible locally F F -analytic representation of G 0 G^{0} of topological length at most 2 2 . It is topologically irreducible if and only if the underlying connection on ℒ \mathscr{L} is non-trivial. We give an explicit formula for the length of the strong dual of the space of globally-defined rigid analytic functions on a G 0 G^{0} -equivariant finite étale rigid analytic covering of Ω F \Omega_{F} with abelian Galois group as an admissible locally F F -analytic representation of G 0 G^{0} .



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Main results

Let
p
be a prime number, let
F
be a finite field extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with residue field
k_{F}
of size
q
and let
K
be a complete discretely valued field extension of
F
. For any rigid
F
-analytic variety
X
, we denote by
X_{K}:=K\times_{F}X
its base-change to a rigid
K
-analytic variety. Let
\Omega_{F}
be the rigid
F
-analytic
p
-adic upper half plane and let
G^{0}
denote the group of
2\times 2
matrices with entries in
F
whose determinant has absolute value
1
. In
[
8
]
the authors classified torsion
G^{0}
-equivariant line bundles with connection on
\Omega:=\Omega_{F}\times_{F}K
. As explained there, this was motivated by wanting to understand the first covering in Drinfeld’s tower. In this paper we seek to understand the global sections of these line bundles as modules over the locally
F
-analytic distribution algebra
D(G^{0},K)
of
G^{0}
, or equivalently by
[
44
, p.176, Definition]
and
[
43
, Corollary 3.3]
, the strong duals of these global sections as locally
F
-analytic representations of
G^{0}
.

Theorem A
.

Let
\mathscr{L}
be a torsion
G^{0}
-equivariant line bundle with connection on
\Omega
. Then
\mathscr{L}(\Omega)
is a coadmissible
D(G^{0},K)
-module of length at most
2
.
Moreover
\mathscr{L}(\Omega)
is topologically irreducible as a
D(G^{0},K)
-module if and only if the underlying connection on
\mathscr{L}
is non-trivial.

Let
\mathbf{C}
denote a complete and algebraically closed field extension of
K
. Theorem
A
has the following consequence.

Corollary B
.

Let
f:X\to\Omega
be a
G^{0}
-equivariant finite étale rigid
K
-analytic covering of
\Omega
, with abelian Galois group
\Gamma
of exponent
e
, where we assume that
K
contains a primitive
e
-th root of unity. Let
H
be a closed subgroup of
G^{0}
that contains an open subgroup of
\mathbb{SL}_{2}(F)
. Then
\mathcal{O}(X)
is a coadmissible
D(H,K)
-module, whose length is given by

\ell_{D(H,K)}\left(\mathcal{O}(X)\right)=|\Gamma|+|H\backslash\mathbb{P}^{1}(F)|\cdot|\pi_{0}(X_{\mathbf{C}})|.

Following
[
8
, Introduction]
we write
\Sigma_{1}:=\mathcal{M}_{1}\times_{\mathcal{M}_{0}}\Omega
, defined with respect to some inclusion
\Omega\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{0}
into the level-zero Rapoport-Zink space
\mathcal{M}_{0}
, so that for a suitable choice of the ground field
K
,
\Sigma_{1}
is a
G^{0}
-equivariant
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}^{\times}
-Galois covering of
\Omega
arising in Drinfeld’s tower. It follows from Corollary B that
\mathcal{O}(\Sigma_{1})
is a coadmissible
D(G^{0},K)
-module of length
q^{2}+q-2
. We note in passing that the coadmissibility part of this statement was already addressed by Patel, Schmidt and Strauch in
[
38
]
.

1.2.
Summary of this paper

1.2.1.
Chapter 2

We consider the rigid analytic affine line
\mathbb{A}
equipped with a fixed local coordinate
x
. We view the
K
-algebra
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{A})
of infinite-order differential operators on
\mathbb{A}
that was introduced in
[
7
]
as a ‘rigid analytic quantisation’ of the cotangent bundle
T^{\ast}\mathbb{A}
. The choice of coordinate
x
on
\mathbb{A}
gives us a coordinate
(x,y)
on
T^{\ast}\mathbb{A}\cong\mathbb{A}^{2}
, and we study various completions of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{A})
. For every affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{A}
, we consider the ‘affinoid box’
X\times\{|y|\leqslant r\}\subseteq T^{\ast}\mathbb{A}
and we show that provided the positive real number
r
is big enough (depending on
X
) we can define a completion
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
of
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{A})
which we regard as a ‘rigid analytic quantisation‘ of this box:
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}(X\times\{|y|\leqslant r\})
as a Banach
\mathcal{O}(X)
-module. We show that this construction, as well as its overconvergent version
\mathcal{D}_{r}^{\dagger}
, satisfies an analogue of Tate’s Acyclicity Theorem - see Theorem
2.3.10
. With an eye on the applications in later chapters, we also consider certain ‘twisting-automorphisms’
\theta_{u,d}
of the algebra of finite-order differential operators
\mathcal{D}(X)
that are morally given by the conjugation by a
d
-th root of a unit
u\in\mathcal{O}(X)
, and we show in Theorem
2.4.9
that these automorphisms extend to
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
whenever
d
is coprime to
p
.

1.2.2.
Chapter 3

In this more technical section, we establish Noetherianity of the rings
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
at Corollary
3.1.11
. We show in Theorem
3.3.1
that
\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)
is (abstractly) flat as a
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
-module on both sides whenever
Y
is an affinoid subdomain
X
such that both
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
and
\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)
are well-defined. We also show in Theorem
3.4.1
that
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
is (abstractly) flat as a
\mathcal{D}_{s}(X)
-module whenever
s>r
. These results further support the ‘rigid-analytic quantisation’ intuition: more general precise conjectures in the direction of this philosophy can be found at
[
20
, Chapter 5]
. The proofs in Chapter 3 borrow heavily from the earlier work of Berthelot
[
13
]
.

1.2.3.
Chapter 4

Here we begin to study in earnest the torsion line bundles with connection
\mathscr{L}
on the upper half plane
\Omega
. Write
\mathbb{D}
and
w\mathbb{D}
for the two closed subdiscs of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of radius
1
, centred at
0
and
\infty
respectively. With an eye on the rigid-analytic Beilinson-Bernstein theorem
[
9
]
, we view the two-element affinoid covering
\{\mathbb{D},w\mathbb{D}\}
of the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
as fundamental. Our approach is therefore to first restrict these line bundles to what we call the ‘local Drinfeld space’
\Upsilon:=\mathbb{D}\cap\Omega
, and then to study their further restrictions to the affinoid subdomains
\Upsilon_{n}
of
\mathbb{D}
obtained by removing from
\mathbb{D}
all open discs of radius
|\pi_{F}^{n}|
centred at the
F
-rational points, simultaneously for all
n\geqslant 0
. Since
\{\Upsilon_{n}:n\geqslant 0\}
forms an admissible affinoid open covering of
\Upsilon
, no information about
\mathscr{L}
is lost in this way. Actually, we study the sections of
\mathscr{L}
on
\Upsilon_{n}
that
overconverge
into those holes of
\Upsilon_{n}
that are wholly contained within
\mathbb{D}
. These sections form a sheaf
\mathscr{L}_{n}
on
\Upsilon
that could reasonably be viewed as a ‘truncation’ of
\mathscr{L}
.

Logically, our first major result of Chapter 4 is Theorem
4.3.7
which ensures that the torsion overconvergent line bundle with connection
\mathscr{L}_{n}
is
algebraic
: assuming that the order
d
of
\mathscr{L}
in
\PicCon(\Upsilon)
is coprime to
p
, we show that for any fixed (finite) set
\mathcal{S}_{n}
of coset representatives for
\pi_{F}^{n+1}\mathcal{O}_{F}
in
\mathcal{O}_{F}
, there is a
rational function

u_{n}
with zeroes and poles contained in
\mathcal{S}_{n}
such that
\mathscr{L}_{n}
can be extended as a
\mathcal{D}
-module to a
d
-torsion line bundle with connection
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{S}_{n},u_{n},d)
on
\mathbb{D}
that is generated as an
\mathcal{O}
-module by a
d
-th root of
u_{n}
and has singularities in
\mathcal{S}_{n}
only.

In
\lx@sectionsign\ref{KummerSect}
, we perform a more general study of the line bundles with connection
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{S},u,d)
on the affine line that are generated as an
\mathcal{O}
-module by
u^{\frac{1}{d}}
and have singularities constrained to a finite set
\mathcal{S}
. Again, assuming that
d
is coprime to
p
, we show in Theorem
4.2.21
that the corresponding subsheaf
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{S},u,d)_{\overline{U_{t}}}
of sections of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{S},u,d)
overconverging into the open discs of radius
t
centred at the finite set of (possible) singularities
\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{S},u,d)
is in fact
finitely presented
as a module over the appropriate sheaf of infinite order differential operators
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/t}
that was introduced earlier on in Chapter 2. More precisely, we recall a certain well-known order-one differential operator
R(u,d)
at Definition
4.2.10
(b), and show that the well-known classical presentation
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}\cdot R(u,d)
for the algebraic-
\mathcal{D}
-module version of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{S},u,d)
in fact induces ‘the same’ presentation of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{S},u,d)_{\overline{U_{t}}}
as a
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/t}
-module:
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{S},u,d)_{\overline{U_{t}}}\cong\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/t}/\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/t}\cdot R(u,d)
.

We conclude Chapter 4 by applying methods of Berthelot in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{LocIrred}
— notably, his theory of
Frobenius descent
— to show that the simplest possible examples of the cyclic one-relator
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}
-modules of this form, namely those where the relator
R(u,d)
equals
x\partial_{x}-\lambda
for some scalar
\lambda
, are in fact
irreducible
for generic values of
\lambda
.

1.2.4.
Chapter 5

Returning to the truncation
\mathscr{L}_{n}
to
\Upsilon_{n}
of our line bundle with connection
\mathscr{L}
on
\Upsilon
, Theorem
4.3.7
and Theorem
4.2.21
give us a presentation

(1)

\mathscr{L}_{n}\cong\mathscr{D}_{n}/\mathscr{D}_{n}\cdot R(u_{n},d)

for a certain rational function
u_{n}
: here
\mathscr{D}_{n}
is a convenient abbreviation for the sheaf of infinite-order differential operators
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}}
that is of relevance when studying
\Upsilon_{n}
and
\mathscr{L}_{n}
. Chapter 5 forms the core of our paper: we investigate what happens with these presentations as we begin to vary the parameter
n
. Since for any affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{D}
, the inverse limit of the rings
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)
over all sufficiently large
n
can be shown to be isomorphic to the algebra
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X)
from
[
7
]
— see Corollary
7.2.9
— one may wonder, writing
j:\Upsilon\hookrightarrow\mathbb{D}
to denote the open inclusion, whether
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}
is in fact a coadmissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}
-module on
\mathbb{D}
in the sense of
[
7
]
. Although we cannot rule this out, we believe this not to be the case, the main reason being that the divisors
\Div(u_{n})
of the rational functions
u_{n}
grow without bound in complexity as
n
increases: consequently, the characteristic cycles of the algebraic approximations
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}R(u_{n},d)
to
\mathscr{L}
grow without bound with
n
as well. In fact, one can use Berthelot–Abe’s
characteristic cycles
from
[
14
]
and
[
2
]
to show that the natural comparison map

(2)

\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}\to\mathscr{L}_{n}

is in fact
not
an isomorphism.

In this situation, the underlying
equivariance
of the line bundle
\mathscr{L}
comes to the rescue. Let
I
denote the
Iwahori subgroup
: it is of interest to us as it maps onto the stabiliser of
\mathbb{D}
in
\GL_{2}(F)
under the natural Möbius action of
\GL_{2}(F)
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Under the assumption that
\mathscr{L}
is
I
-equivariant, the action of
\mathscr{D}_{n}
on the truncated line bundles
\mathscr{L}_{n}
naturally extends to the (rather large) skew-group ring
\mathscr{D}_{n}\rtimes I
. We show in Theorem
5.3.6
that that sufficiently small open subgroups
I_{n+1}
of
I
act on
\mathscr{L}_{n}
through certain infinite-order differential operators contained in the unit group of
\mathscr{D}_{n}
: this is done as a consequence of the contents of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{IwahoriAction}
, where we show that the natural action of elements
g\in\GL_{2}(K)
on rigid analytic functions by Möbius transformations can in fact be expressed using certain infinite-order differential operators
\beta(g)\in\mathscr{D}_{n}^{\times}
, provided
g
is sufficiently close to
1
: see Proposition
5.2.10
for a more precise statement. Consequently, the action of
\mathscr{D}_{n}\rtimes I
on
\mathscr{L}_{n}
factors through a particular crossed product
\mathscr{D}_{n}\rtimes_{I_{n+1}}I
of
\mathscr{D}_{n}
with
I/I_{n+1}
. These crossed products are of relevance to us for two reasons: firstly, they are more manageable than the skew-group ring
\mathscr{D}_{n}\rtimes I
as the group
I/I_{n+1}
is finite, and secondly, for any affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{D}
, the inverse limit of the crossed products
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\rtimes_{I_{n+1}}I
over all sufficiently large
n
is naturally isomorphic to the completed skew-group ring
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,I)
that underpins the definition of coadmissible equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-modules given in
[
9
]
— see Corollary
7.2.9
.

With these crossed products acting on
\mathscr{L}_{n}
, we have the natural comparison map

(3)

\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{I_{n+1}}{\rtimes}I\quad\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}\underset{I_{n+2}}{\rtimes}I}{\otimes}{}\quad\mathscr{L}_{n+1}\quad\longrightarrow\quad\mathscr{L}_{n}.

In
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CPandSA}
we explain how to work with the complicated-looking tensor product appearing on the left hand side: we show in Theorem
5.1.8
that it is isomorphic to a particular quotient of the
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-module
\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}
appearing in (
2
) above. In fact, we explain how the operators
\beta(g)
from Definition
5.2.8
give rise to a certain left
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-linear action of the finite group
I_{n+1}/I_{n+2}
on
\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}
that we call the ‘secret action’: the left hand side of
(\ref{CompMapIntro})
can then be identified with the
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-module of coinvariants of
\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}
under this secret action.

The main result of Chapter 5 is then Theorem
5.3.16
, which (essentially) says that the comparison map (
3
) is always an isomorphism.

1.2.5.
Chapter 6

Here we give a proof of Theorem
5.3.16
. Working locally on
\mathbb{D}
, at the end of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CompSect}
we reduce the problem to showing that the comparison map (
3
) is an isomorphism upon restriction to a particular affinoid subdomain
W_{a,n}
contained in a closed disc of radius
|\pi_{F}|^{n}
centred at some
a\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
— see Theorem
5.3.12
. Some further work reduces us further to the case where
n=0
and where the algebraic approximations to
\mathscr{L}_{1}
and
\mathscr{L}_{0}
given by (
1
) take a particularly simple explicit form — see Theorem
6.5.8
. Sections
\lx@sectionsign\ref{MicRings}
and
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CharCycleSect}
are devoted to the calculation of the characteristic cycles of
\mathscr{D}_{0}\otimes_{\mathscr{D}_{1}}\mathscr{L}_{1}
and
\mathscr{L}_{0}
, building on the work of Abe
[
2
]
, where it becomes apparent that the cycle of
\mathscr{D}_{0}\otimes_{\mathscr{D}_{1}}\mathscr{L}_{1}
is larger than that of
\mathscr{L}_{0}
. An abstract criterion given by Theorem
5.1.11
then provides three conditions that we must verify in order to deduce Theorem
5.3.12
. The last two of these are relatively straightforward, however the first one, namely the non-triviality of the secret action, turns out to be surprisingly difficult to check. Arguing by contradiction and working with some microlocalisation of
\mathscr{D}_{n}
, we show in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{TheIntegral}
that the triviality of this secret action would imply that a certain
p
-adic differential equation would have a rigid analytic solution
\zeta_{w,d}
everywhere on
W_{a,n}
. We find an explicit local formal solution of this differential equation at Proposition
6.3.16
. Looking at the growth rate of the
p
-adic valuations of the Taylor coefficients of this solution then provides the required contradiction — see Theorem
6.3.18
. This relies on detailed estimates of
p
-adic valuations of certain binomial coefficients, which we perform in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{BinEsts}
.

1.2.6.
Chapter 7

Here we draw everything together and invoke the results of
[
9
]
by the first author to establish Theorem A and Corollary B. In
\lx@sectionsign\ref{QcohTower}
and
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CompareSandT}
we carry out the necessary preliminary work to establish Corollary
7.2.9
that was mentioned above. In
\lx@sectionsign\ref{LocalCoad}
we crucially use Theorem
5.3.16
together with the elementary fact that
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}\cong\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}\mathscr{L}_{n}
as an
I
-equivariant locally Fréchet
\mathcal{D}
-module on
\mathbb{D}
to establish that
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{D}/I}
in Theorem
7.3.7
. It is interesting to point out that for this local coadmissibility statement, we do not need to use the action of the entire Iwahori subgroup
I
on
\mathscr{L}
: we are able to show that
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{D}/J}
for
any
closed subgroup
J
of
I
that contains an open subgroup of the ‘translation subgroup’
\begin{pmatrix}1&F\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
of
\GL_{2}(F)
. In
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CoadIrredSect}
we globalise the material in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{LocalCoad}
from
\mathbb{D}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and apply the equivariant Beilinson-Bernstein Localisation Theorem — Theorem
7.4.8
— to prove the coadmissibility assertion on
\mathscr{L}(\Omega)
in Theorem A. Along the way we apply the local irreducibility results from
\lx@sectionsign
4.4
to establish Theorem
7.4.7
which deals with those
\mathscr{L}
in Theorem A that are non-trivial as line bundles with connection. Finally, in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{JstarO}
, we treat the case of the trivial line bundle with connection
\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
. We use the Induction Equivalence and the equivariant Kashiwara equivalence from
[
5
]
to show that the pushforward of
\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a coadmissible
G^{0}
-equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-module on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
of length
2
, and use this to deduce Theorem A together with Corollary B.

1.3.
Conventions and Notation

F
will denote a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{F}
, uniformiser
\pi_{F}
and residue field
k_{F}
of order
q
.
K
will denote a field containing
F
that is complete with respect to a non-archimedean norm
|\cdot|
such that
|p|=1/p
. We will write:

•

\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
to denote the divisible subgroup of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
generated by
|K^{\times}|
,

•

K^{\circ}:=\{a\in K:|a|\leqslant 1\}
for the valuation ring of
K
,

•

K^{\circ\circ}:=\{a\in K:|a|<1\}
for the maximal ideal of
K^{\circ}
,

•

\overline{K}
for a fixed algebraic closure of
K
, and

•

\mathbf{C}
for the completion of
\overline{K}
.

Note that
\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}=\left|\overline{K}^{\times}\right|
.

A
K
-Banach space
will be a
K
-vector space
V
equipped with a norm
|\cdot|
compatible with the norm on
K
. A morphism of Banach spaces
T\colon V\to W
will be a bounded linear map from
V
to
W
and
||T||
will denote the operator norm of
T
:
||T||=\sup_{v\in V\backslash 0}\frac{|Tv|}{|v|}
. Thus a
K
-Banach algebra

A
will be a
K
-Banach space equipped with an associative unital multiplication map
A\times A\to A
that is bounded: there is a constant
C
such that
|ab|\leqslant C|a||b|
for all
a,b\in A
.

Given a
K
-Banach space
V
we will write
\mathcal{B}(V)
to denote the Banach algebra of endomorphisms of
V
equipped with the operator norm. Following
[
30
, Definition 6.1.3]
the
spectral radius
of
T\in\mathcal{B}(V)
is defined to be

|T|_{\sp,V}:=\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}||T^{k}||^{1/k}.

When
V
and
W
are
K
-Banach spaces we recall
[
41
, §17]
that the
tensor product norm
on
V\otimes_{K}W
is given by

|u|=\inf\left\{\max\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}|v_{i}||w_{i}|:u=\sum_{i=1}^{r}v_{i}\otimes w_{i},v_{i}\in V,w_{i}\in W\right\}

and that
V\widehat{\otimes}_{K}V
is the completion of
V\otimes_{K}W
with respect to this norm. When we write
V\otimes W
or
V\widehat{\otimes}W
unadorned we will always mean tensor product or completed tensor product over
K
.

Let
X
be a rigid
K
-analytic variety. When
Y
is a subset
X
, we will say that
Y
is an
affinoid subdomain
of
X
to mean that
Y
is an admissible open subspace of
X
, itself isomorphic to an affinoid
K
-variety. When
X
itself happens to be a
K
-affinoid variety, this agrees by
[
17
, Corollary 8.2.1/4]
with the standard definition found at
[
17
, Definition 7.2.2/2]
. We will write

•

|\cdot|_{X}
to denote the (power-multiplicative) supremum seminorm on
X
,

•

\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}:=\{f\in\mathcal{O}(X):|f|_{X}\leqslant 1\}
,

•

\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ\circ}:=\{f\in\mathcal{O}(X):|f|_{X}<1\}
, and

•

\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times\times}:=1+\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ\circ}
, the subgroup of
small units
in
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
.

When
X
is a
K
-affinoid variety,
g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}\in\mathcal{O}(X)
generate the unit ideal, and
r\in\sqrt{|K|^{\times}}
we will write
X(rg_{1}/g_{0},\ldots,rg_{n}/g_{0})
to denote the rational subdomain of
X
given by

X(rg_{1}/g_{0},\ldots,rg_{n}/g_{0}):=\{x\in X\mid r|g_{i}(x)|\leqslant|g_{0}(x)|\mbox{ for }i=1,\ldots,n\}

c.f.
[
21
, Example 2.1.9]
. We note that when
a\in K^{\times}
,

X(|a|g_{1}/g_{0},\ldots,|a|g_{n}/g_{0})=X((ag_{1})/g_{0},\ldots,(ag_{n})/g_{0}).

When
X
is a smooth rigid analytic variety over
K
we may form the tangent sheaf
\mathcal{T}_{X}
which together with its identity map forms a Lie algebroid on
X
. The sheaf of enveloping algebras
\mathcal{D}_{X}
of (finite order) differential operators is given on affinoid subdomains
Y
of
X
by the enveloping algebra
U(\mathcal{T}_{X}(Y))
. See
[
7
]
for more details. As in
[
8
, Definition 3.1]
,
\PicCon(X)
will denote the group of the isomorphism classes of line bundles with flat connection on
X
with the group operation given by tensor product and
\Con(X)
will denote the subgroup of
\PicCon(X)
consisting of isomorphism classes of line bundles with flat connection on
X
that are trivial after forgetting the connection.

We will write
\mathbb{A}:=\mathbb{A}_{K}:=\mathbb{A}_{K}^{1,\an}
to denote the rigid
K
-analytic affine line, equipped with a fixed choice of local coordinate
x\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{A})
and
\mathbb{D}
to denote the unit disc
\Sp K\langle x\rangle
in
\mathbb{A}
with respect to this coordinate. We write
\mathbb{P}^{1}
to denote the rigid
K
-analytic projective line.

We recall
[
8
, §4.1]
that a
K
-cheese
is an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
of the form

\Sp K\left\langle\frac{x-\alpha_{0}}{s_{0}},\frac{s_{1}}{x-\alpha_{1}},\cdots,\frac{s_{g}}{x-\alpha_{g}}\right\rangle

for some
\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{g}\in K
and
s_{0},\ldots,s_{g}\in K^{\times}
satisfying certain conditions. An affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}

splits
over a finite field extension
K^{\prime}/K
if
X_{K^{\prime}}
is a finite union of pairwise disjoint
K^{\prime}
-cheeses. Every affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
splits for some finite extension
K^{\prime}/K
by
[
8
, Theorem 4.1.8]
.

Let
A
be an abelian group and let
d
be a non-zero integer. We will write

•

A[d]=\{a\in A\mid da=0\}
to denote the
d
-torsion subgroup of
A
,

•

A[p^{\prime}]:=\bigcup_{(d,p)=1}A[d]
to denote the prime-to-
p
torsion subgroup of
A
,

•

A[p^{\infty}]:=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}A[p^{n}]
to denote the
p
-power torsion subgroup of
A
.

Let a group
G
act on a set
X
. We will write

•

G_{x}:=\{g\in G:gx=x\}
for the stabilizer of a point
x\in X
, and

•

X^{G}:=\{x\in X:gx=x
for all
g\in G\}
for the set of elements fixed by
G
.

It will be convenient to define
\varpi=p^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
\varepsilon:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{if }p>2,\\
2&\mbox{if }p=2.\end{array}\right.

2.
Sheaves of completed differential operators

2.1.
Skew-Ore and skew-Laurent extensions

We recall
[
25
, p27]
that if
A
is a ring and
\delta\colon A\to A
is a derivation, then one can form the
skew-Ore extension

A[T;\delta]
which is an over-ring of
A
that as a left
A
-module is free on the symbols
\{T^{i}\mid i\geqslant 0\}
and satisfies
Ta-aT=\delta(a)
for all
a\in A
and
T^{i}T^{j}=T^{i+j}
for
i,j\geqslant 0
.

We recall that
A[T;\delta]
satisfies a natural looking universal property.

Proposition 2.1.1
.

(
[
25
, Exercise 2F]
) For every ring homomorphism
\phi\colon A\to B
and element
b\in B
such that
b\phi(a)-\phi(a)b=\phi(\delta(a))
for all
a\in A
, there is a unique ring homomorphism
\psi\colon A[T;\delta]\to B
such that
\psi|_{A}=\phi
and
\psi(T)=b
.

We wish to form a kind of skew-Laurent-polynomial ring
A[T,T^{-1};\delta]
by formally inverting
T
in
A[T;\delta]
and also understand its subring generated by
A
and
T^{-1}
. This is not possible in general but we will see that it
is
possible if
\delta
is locally nilpotent; i.e. for each
a\in A
there is
n\geqslant 0
such that
\delta^{n}(a)=0
. The first step is to prove that the set
\{T^{n}\mid n\geqslant 0\}
satisfies the Ore condition (both left and the right versions) under this locally nilpotent hypothesis.

We recall a criterion for a multiplicatively closed subset of a ring to satisfy Ore’s condition.

Proposition 2.1.2
.

(
[
23
, p457]
) If
S
is a multiplicatively closed subset of a ring
A
such that
\ad(s)
is locally nilpotent for all
s\in S
then
S
satisfies both the left and right Ore conditions.

Lemma 2.1.3
.

If
s,t\in A
such that
\ad(s)
and
\ad(t)
are both locally nilpotent and
st=ts
then
\ad(st)
is locally nilpotent.

Proof.

If we write
l_{s}\colon A\to A
to denote left multiplication by
a
and
r_{t}\colon A\to A
to denote right multiplication by
t
then for
a\in A

\ad(st)(a)=sta-ast=(sta-sat)+(sat-ast)=l_{s}\circ\ad(t)(a)+r_{t}\circ\ad(s)(a).

Thus since
\ad(s),l_{s},r_{t}
and
\ad(t)
all commute

(\ad(st))^{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\binom{n}{i}l_{s}^{i}r_{t}^{n-i}\ad(t)^{i}\ad(s)^{n-i}

and it is follows easily from the local nilpotence of
\ad(s)
and
\ad(t)
that
\ad(st)
is locally nilpotent.
∎

Corollary 2.1.4
.

If
\delta\colon A\to A
is a locally nilpotent derivation then
\{T^{n}\mid n\geqslant 0\}
is a left and right Ore set in
A[T;\delta]
.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.1.2
it suffices to show that
\ad(T^{n})
acts locally nilpotently on
A[T;\delta]
and by Lemma
2.1.3
this can be reduced to the case
n=1
.

But

\ad(T)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}a_{i}T^{i}\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\delta(a_{i})T^{i}

so local nilpotence of
\ad(T)
on
A[T;\delta]
is a straightforward consequence of the local nilpotence of
\delta
on
A
.
∎

Notation 2.1.5
.

For
\delta
a locally nilpotent derivation of
A
we write
A[T,T^{-1};\delta]
to denote the localisation of
A[T;\delta]
at the Ore set
\{T^{n}\mid n\geqslant 0\}
.

We can perform the following calculation in
A[T,T^{-1};\delta]
:

Lemma 2.1.6
.

For each
a\in A
,
T^{-1}a=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\delta^{n}(a)T^{-n-1}
.

Proof.

First note that the right-hand side always makes sense because
\delta
is locally nilpotent. Since
T
is a unit in
A[T,T^{-1};\delta]
it thus suffices to show that

T\left(\sum_{n\geqslant 0}(-1)^{n}\delta^{n}(a)T^{-n-1}\right)=a.

But
T\delta^{n}(a)=\delta^{n}(a)T+\delta^{n+1}(a)
so

\displaystyle T\left(\sum_{n\geqslant 0}(-1)^{n}\delta^{n}(a)T^{-n-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geqslant 0}(-1)^{n}\left(\delta^{n}(a)T^{-n}+\delta^{n+1}(a)T^{-n-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a+\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\left((-1)^{n}+(-1)^{n+1}\right)\delta^{n+1}(a)T^{-n-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a.\qed

It follows immediately that the subring of
A[T,T^{-1};\delta]
generated by
A
and
T^{-1}
is the (free) left
A
-submodule of
A[T,T^{-1};\delta]
on the set
\{T^{-n}\mid n\geqslant 0\}
.

Definition 2.1.7
.

For
\delta
a locally nilpotent derivation of
A
we write
A[T^{-1};\delta]
to denote the subring of
A[T,T^{-1};\delta]
generated by
A
and
T^{-1}
.

This algebra satisfies the following universal property.

Proposition 2.1.8
.

Let
f\colon A\to B
be a ring homomorphism and let
\delta
be a locally nilpotent derivation of
A
. Suppose that
b\in B
is such that

bf(a)=\sum\limits_{n\geqslant 0}(-1)^{n}f(\delta^{n}(a))b^{n+1}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad a\in A.

Then there is a unique ring homomorphism

g\colon A[T^{-1};\delta]\to B

extending
f
such that
g(T^{-1})=b
.

Proof.

Given a pair
(f,b)
as in the statement, we define
g:A[T^{-1};\delta]\to B
by

g\left(\sum\limits_{n\geqslant 0}a_{n}T^{-n}\right):=\sum f(a_{n})b^{n}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad a_{n}\in A.

It suffices to prove that this is a ring homomorphism. Since for
a\in A
and
m\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle g(T^{-1}aT^{-m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g\left(\sum_{n\geqslant 0}(-1)^{n}\delta^{n}(a)T^{-n-1-m}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geqslant 0}(-1)^{n}f(\delta^{n}(a))b^{n+1+m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle bf(a)b^{m},

we see that
g(T^{-1}aT^{-m})=g(T^{-1})g(aT^{-m})
. An easy induction argument together with left
A
-linearity of
g
shows that
g
respects multiplication.
∎

2.2.
Skew-Laurent and skew-Tate algebras

We recall from §
2.1
that for a ring
A
and a derivation
\delta\colon A\to A
one can form the
skew-Ore extension

A[\partial;\delta]
. As noted previously, the set
\{\partial^{n}\mid n\geqslant 0\}
is not an Ore set in
A[\partial;\delta]
in general so we cannot form a Laurent polynomial-style ring extension
A[\partial,\partial^{-1};\delta]
of
A[\partial;\delta]
; though by Corollary
2.1.4
this is possible when
\delta
is locally nilpotent. In this section we will show that when
A
is a
K
-Banach algebra and
\delta
is topologically nilpotent in a suitable sense we can form certain completed versions of
A[\partial;\delta]
and
A[\partial,\partial^{-1};\delta]
that are
K
-Banach algebras. These will satisfy suitable analogues of Proposition
2.1.1
. In the case where
A=\mathcal{O}(X)
is the
K
-affinoid algebra of rigid analytic functions on a
K
-affinoid variety
X
, we might think of
\mathcal{O}(X)\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
as being the algebra of ‘rigid analytic functions on a non-commutative annulus over
X
’, and we refer the reader to Schneider’s Appendix in
[
49
]
for some related constructions.

Let
r\geqslant s
denote arbitrary positive real numbers; frequently it will be convenient to restrict our attention to only those
r
and
s
lying in
\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
.

Definition 2.2.1
.

Let
A
be a
K
-Banach space and let
\partial
be a formal variable. We define

A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle:=\left\{\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{j}\partial^{j}\in\prod_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}A\partial^{j}:\lim_{|j|\to\infty}|a_{j}|t^{j}=0\mbox{ if }s\leqslant t\leqslant r\right\}

and give it the norm

\left|\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{j}\partial^{j}\right|:=\sup\limits_{s\leqslant t\leqslant r}\sup\limits_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}|a_{j}|t^{j}.

Of course this norm can be written in the somewhat less symmetric form

(4)

\left|\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{j}\partial^{j}\right|=\max\left\{\sup\limits_{j\geqslant 0}|a_{j}|r^{j},\sup\limits_{j<0}|a_{j}|s^{j}\right\}.

Note that
A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
becomes
K
-Banach space when equipped with this norm. We will now explain how to give it the structure of an associative non-commutative
K
-Banach algebra.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

Let
A
be a
K
-Banach algebra and let
\delta\in\mathcal{B}(A)
be such that

|\delta|_{\sp,A}<s.

Let
u,v\in A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
.

(1)

For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following limit exists in
A
:

(5)

u\ast_{k}v:=\lim\limits_{I\to\infty}\sum\limits_{i=-I}^{I}u_{i}\lim\limits_{M\to\infty}\sum\limits_{m=0}^{M}\binom{i}{m}\delta^{m}(v_{k-i+m}).

(2)

There is a constant
C>0
depending only on
A
and
\delta
such that

\sup\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|u\ast_{k}v|t^{k}\leqslant C\cdot|u|\cdot|v|\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t\in[s,r].

(3)

For all
t\in[s,r]
,
|u\ast_{k}v|t^{k}\to 0
as
|k|\to\infty
.

Proof.

(a) and (b). We can find
C_{1}
such that
|ab|\leqslant C_{1}|a||b|
for all
a,b\in A
; let
C_{2}=\sup\limits_{\ell\geqslant 0}||\delta^{\ell}||/s^{\ell}<\infty
. Fix
k,i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
t\in[s,r]
. Then for all
m\geqslant 0
,

(6)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\left|u_{i}\binom{i}{m}\delta^{m}(v_{k-i+m})\right|\cdot t^{k}&\leqslant&C_{1}\cdot|u_{i}|\cdot||\delta^{m}||\cdot|v_{k-i+m}|\cdot t^{k}\\
&\leqslant&C_{1}\cdot|u_{i}|t^{i}\cdot C_{2}\cdot|v_{k-i+m}|t^{k-i+m}\end{array}

which tends to
0
as
m\to\infty
because
v\in A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
. Therefore the inner series in the definition of
u\ast_{k}v
converges to an element
(u\ast_{k}v)_{i}\in A
, such that

\left|(u\ast_{k}v)_{i}\right|\cdot t^{k}\leqslant C_{1}\cdot|u_{i}|t^{i}\cdot C_{2}\cdot|v|.

Since
|u_{i}|t^{i}\to 0
as
|i|\to\infty
because
u\in A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
, we see that the partial sums
\sum\limits_{i=-I}^{I}(u\ast_{k}v)_{i}
converge to an element
u\ast_{k}v\in A
as
|i|\to\infty
, which satisfies

|u\ast_{k}v|t^{k}\leqslant C_{1}\cdot|u|\cdot|v|\cdot C_{2}.

We may therefore take
C:=C_{1}C_{2}
.

(c) Let
\epsilon>0
be given, and choose positive integers
I,M
and
J
such that

•

|u_{i}|t^{i}<\frac{\epsilon}{C_{1}C_{2}|v|}
whenever
|i|>I
,

•

||\delta^{m}||/s^{m}<\frac{\epsilon}{C_{1}|u|\hskip 2.84544pt|v|}
whenever
m>M
, and

•

|v_{j}|r^{j}<\frac{\epsilon}{C_{1}C_{2}|u|}
whenever
|j|>J
.

Now suppose that
|k|>I+M+J
.

Firstly, if
|i|>I
, then using (
6
) we see that

\left|u_{i}\delta^{m}(v_{k-i+m})\right|\cdot t^{k}<C_{1}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{C_{1}C_{2}|v|}\cdot C_{2}\cdot|v|=\epsilon.

Next, if
m>M
then again using (
6
) we have

\left|u_{i}\delta^{m}(v_{k-i+m})\right|\cdot t^{k}<C_{1}\cdot|u|\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{C_{1}|u||v|}\cdot|v|=\epsilon.

Finally if
|i|\leqslant I
and
m\leqslant M
, then since
|i|+m+|k-i+m|\geqslant|k|>I+M+J
, we must have
|k-i+m|>J
and so (
\ref{StarProdEst})
gives

\left|u_{i}\delta^{m}(v_{k-i+m})\right|\cdot t^{k}<C_{1}\cdot|u|\cdot C_{2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{C_{1}C_{2}|u|}=\epsilon.

We conclude that in all cases, we have the inequality

|u\ast_{k}v|t^{k}<\epsilon\quad\mbox{whenever}\quad|k|>I+J+M

and part (c) follows.
∎

Definition 2.2.3
.

Let
A
be a
K
-Banach algebra and let
\delta:A\to A
be a bounded
K
-linear derivation such that
|\delta|_{\sp,A}<s
. We define the
star product
of two elements
u,v\in A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
to be

u\ast v:=\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(u\ast_{k}v)\partial^{k}.

It is not hard to see that this star product gives a
K
-bilinear map
\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}
where
\mathcal{E}:=A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
. Our next task will be to show that this star product on
\mathcal{E}
is associative. Because
\delta:A\to A
is a derivation we have at our disposal the skew-Ore extension
A[\partial;\delta]
. Its associated graded ring with respect to the filtration
F_{\bullet}A[\partial;\delta]
by degree in
\partial
is the polynomial ring
A[y]
where
y=\gr\partial
is the principal symbol of
\partial
; we now consider the classical algebraic microlocalisation
Q:=Q_{S}A[\partial,\delta]
, where
S:=\cup_{n=0}^{\infty}(\partial^{n}+F_{n-1}A[\partial,\delta])
; see, for example,
[
11
, §3]
. By construction, this is an associative ring
(Q,\cdot)
equipped with a complete and exhaustive
\mathbb{Z}
-filtration, whose associated graded ring is
A[y,y^{-1}]
. In this way, we can identify
Q
with the following set of formal Laurent power series:

Q=A[[\partial^{-1}]][\partial]:=\left\{\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{k}\partial^{k}:a_{k}=0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad k>>0\right\}.

The ring
Q
, as well as the
A
-module
\mathcal{E}
, both contain the
A
-submodule
A[\partial,\partial^{-1}]
whose elements are
finite

A
-linear combinations of integer powers of the formal symbol
\partial
. Also, note that
Q
and
\mathcal{E}
are both contained in the large
A
-module of all bi-directional power series
P:=\prod\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}A\partial^{k}
.

Lemma 2.2.4
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a\in A
,
\partial\cdot(\partial^{n}\ast a)=\partial^{n+1}\ast a
.

Proof.

A direct computation using Definition
2.2.3
gives that

(7)

\partial^{n}\ast a=\sum_{k=-\infty}^{n}\binom{n}{n-k}\delta^{n-k}(a)\partial^{k}.

Thus, since
(Q,\cdot)
is associative and
\partial\cdot a=\delta(a)+a\partial
is the defining property of the skew-Ore extension
A[\partial;\delta]
, we can further compute

\displaystyle\partial\cdot(\partial^{n}\ast a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial\cdot\left(\sum_{k=-\infty}^{n}\binom{n}{n-k}\delta^{n-k}(a)\partial^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=-\infty}^{n}\binom{n}{n-k}\delta^{n-k+1}(a)\partial^{k}+\sum_{k=-\infty}^{n}\binom{n}{n-k}\delta^{n-k}(a)\partial^{k+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=-\infty}^{n+1}\left(\binom{n}{n-k}+\binom{n}{n-k+1}\right)\delta^{n-k+1}(a)\partial^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=-\infty}^{n+1}\binom{n+1}{n+1-k}\delta^{n+1-k}(a)\partial^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta^{n+1}\ast a

as required.
∎

Lemma 2.2.5
.

We have
u\cdot v=u\ast v
in
P
for all
u,v\in A[\partial,\partial^{-1}]
.

Proof.

First we note that for
a\in A
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
an easy computation gives that

(au)\ast(v\partial^{n})=a\cdot(u\ast v)\cdot\partial^{n}.

Next, using the
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinearity of both products we may reduce to the case
u=a\partial^{n}
and
v=b\partial^{n^{\prime}}
with
a,b\in A
and
n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
u\ast v=(a\partial^{n})\ast(b\partial^{n^{\prime}})=a\cdot(\partial^{n}\ast b)\cdot\partial^{n^{\prime}}
so as
\cdot
is associative we may further reduce to the case
a=1
and
n^{\prime}=0
. So, it remains to prove that

\partial^{n}\cdot b=\partial^{n}\ast b\quad\mbox{for all}\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}\quad\mbox{and}\quad b\in A.

When
n\geqslant 0
we can verify this directly by induction on
n
using Lemma
2.2.4
and associativity of
(Q,\cdot)
:

\partial^{n+1}\ast b=\partial\cdot(\partial^{n}\ast b)=\partial\cdot\partial^{n}\cdot b=\partial^{n+1}\cdot b.

We also prove the case
n<0
by induction, this time on
-n
. Suppose inductively that
\partial^{n}\cdot b=\partial^{n}\ast b
. Then using Lemma
2.2.4
we have

\partial\cdot(\partial^{n-1}\ast b)=\partial^{n}\ast b=\partial\cdot(\partial^{-1}\cdot(\partial^{n}\ast b))=\partial\cdot(\partial^{n-1}\cdot b).

Now
\partial^{n-1}\cdot b\in Q
and
\partial^{n-1}\ast b\in Q
by equation (
7
). Since
\partial
is a unit in the associative ring
(Q,\cdot)
, we conclude that
\partial^{n-1}\cdot b=\partial^{n-1}\ast b
.∎

Theorem 2.2.6
.

Suppose that
A
is a
K
-Banach algebra and that
\delta\in\mathcal{B}(A)
is a derivation such that
|\delta|_{\sp,A}<s
. Then
A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
becomes an associative
K
-Banach algebra when equipped with the star product
\ast
.

Proof.

We have to show that the star product
\ast
on
\mathcal{E}:=A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
is associative. Using Lemma
2.2.5
, we see that the two possible
P=\prod\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}A\partial^{k}
-valued multiplications on
A[\partial,\partial^{-1}]\subseteq Q\cap\mathcal{E}
coincide in the sense that the diagram

\textstyle{Q^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{Q}}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A[\partial,\partial^{-1}]^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m_{\mathcal{E}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

is commutative. Because
(Q,\cdot)
is an associative ring, the associator
\Phi:\mathcal{E}^{3}\to\mathcal{E}
must vanish on
A[\partial,\partial^{-1}]^{3}
. Since
A[\partial,\partial^{-1}]
is dense in
\mathcal{E}
and
\Phi
is continuous, we conclude that
\Phi
must be identically zero.∎

Because of Lemma
2.2.5
and Theorem
2.2.6
, we will now drop the symbol
\ast
and simply write
ab
to denote the product in the associative skew-Laurent algebra
A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
. Next, we establish a universal property for this algebra.

Proposition 2.2.7
.

Suppose that
A
,
\delta
and
0<s\leqslant r
are as in Theorem
2.2.6
. Let
B
be a
K
-Banach algebra, let
\theta\colon A\to B
is a
K
-Banach algebra homomorphism and
b\in B
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

the map
\theta:A\to B
extends to a
K
-Banach algebra homomorphism
\phi\colon A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle\to B
which sends
\partial
to
b
;

(2)

(a)

b\in B^{\times}
,

(b)

b\theta(a)-\theta(a)b=\theta(\delta(a))
for all
a\in A
,

(c)

\sup\limits_{n\geqslant 0}|b^{n}|/r^{n}<\infty
and
\sup\limits_{n\leqslant 0}|b^{n}|/s^{n}<\infty
.

Proof.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b). Let
C:=A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
and suppose that
\phi:C\to B
is a bounded
K
-algebra homomorphism that extends
\theta
and that sends
\partial
to
b
. The first two conditions hold because
\partial\in C^{\times}
and
\partial a-a\partial=\delta(a)
holds for all
a\in A
.

For the last condition: since
\theta:C\to B
is bounded, there is a constant
L
such that
|\theta(c)|\leqslant L|c|
for all
c\in C
. Hence
|b^{n}|=|\theta(\partial^{n})|\leqslant L|\partial^{n}|
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. By Definition
2.2.1
, we get
|b^{n}|\leqslant Lr^{n}
for all
n\geqslant 0
and
|b^{n}|\leqslant Ls^{n}
for all
n\leqslant 0
.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a). Note first that
b^{n}
makes sense for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
by condition (i). Choose constants
M,D>0
such that
|\theta(a)|\leqslant M|a|
for all
a\in A
and
|b_{1}b_{2}|\leqslant D|b_{1}||b_{2}|
for all
b_{1},b_{2}\in B
; then for all
a\in A
and all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we have
|\theta(a)b^{n}|\leqslant MD|a||b^{n}|
. Using (iii), choose
L>0
such that
|b^{n}|\leqslant Lr^{n}
for all
n\geqslant 0
and
|b^{n}|\leqslant Ls^{n}
for all
n\leqslant 0
. Then for any
\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{n}\partial^{n}\in C
we have

(8)

|\theta(a_{n})b^{n}|\leqslant\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}LMD|a_{n}|r^{n}&\quad\mbox{for all}&n\geqslant 0,\\
LMD|a_{n}|s^{n}&\quad\mbox{for all}&n\leqslant 0.\end{array}\right.

Since
|a_{n}|r^{n}\to 0
as
n\to+\infty
and
|a_{n}|s^{n}\to 0
as
n\to-\infty
by Definition
2.2.1
, we obtain
|\theta(a_{n})b^{n}|\to 0
as
|n|\to\infty
. So, we can define a map
\phi:C\to B
by setting

\phi\left(\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{n}\partial^{n}\right):=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\theta(a_{n})b^{n}\quad\mbox{for any}\quad\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{n}\partial^{n}\in C.

It is easy to check that
\phi
is
K
-linear. Using (
8
) and (
4
), we see that for all
\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{n}\partial^{n}\in C
we have

\left|\phi\left(\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{n}\partial^{n}\right)\right|\leqslant LMD\max\{\sup\limits_{n\geqslant 0}|a_{n}|r^{n},\sup\limits_{n\leqslant 0}|a_{n}|s^{n}\}=LMD\left|\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{n}\partial^{n}\right|.

Thus
\phi
is a bounded
K
-linear map which satisfies
||\phi||\leqslant LMD
.

To see that
\phi
is a ring homomorphism, by its continuity and
K
-linearity it suffices to verify that
\phi(\partial^{n}a)=\phi(\partial^{n})\phi(a)
for all
n
in
\mathbb{Z}
. By induction on
n
we can reduce to the cases
n=\pm 1
. The case
n=1
is immediate from condition (iii). For the case
n=-1
, we know from Lemma
2.2.5
together with equation (
7
) that

\partial^{-1}a=\sum_{m\geqslant 0}\binom{-1}{m}\delta^{m}(a)\partial^{-m-1}.

Since
\binom{-1}{m}=(-1)^{m}
we must prove that
b^{-1}\theta(a)=\sum\limits_{m\geqslant 0}(-1)^{m}\theta(\delta^{m}(a))b^{-m-1}
. Since
b
is a unit in
B
it suffices to prove that
\theta(a)=\sum\limits_{m\geqslant 0}(-1)^{m}b\theta(\delta^{m}(a))b^{-m-1}
. But, by condition (iii), the right-hand side of this formula is

\sum\limits_{m\geqslant 0}(-1)^{m}\left(\theta(\delta^{m}(a))b^{-m}+\theta(\delta^{m+1}(a))b^{-m-1}\right).

We can now see that this sum telescopes down to
\theta(a)
. ∎

We will now discuss the
skew-Tate
algebra
A\langle\partial/r\rangle
.

Definition 2.2.8
.

Let
A
be a
K
-Banach space and let
\partial
be a formal variable. We define the
Skew-Tate algebra

A\langle\partial/r\rangle:=\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}\partial^{j}\in A[[\partial]]:\lim\limits_{j\to\infty}|a_{j}|r^{j}=0\right\}

and give it the norm

\left|\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}\partial^{j}\right|:=\sup\limits_{j\geqslant 0}|a_{j}|r^{j}.

Again,
A\langle\partial/r\rangle
is a
K
-Banach space when equipped with this norm.

Lemma 2.2.9
.

Suppose that
A
and
\delta
are as in Theorem
2.2.6
.

(1)

The natural map
A\langle\partial/r\rangle\to A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
is an isometric embedding of
K
-Banach spaces with closed image.

(2)

A\langle\partial/r\rangle
is closed under the star product in
A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
.

Proof.

(a) This is evident.

(b) We have to check that
u\ast_{k}v=0
for any
u,v\in A\langle\partial/r\rangle
and any
k<0
. Fix
m\geqslant 0
and consider the term
\binom{i}{m}\delta^{m}(v_{k-i+m})
appearing in the definition of
u\ast_{k}v
in Lemma
2.2.2
(a). If
m>i
then the binomial coefficient is zero, so suppose that
m\leqslant i
. But then
k-i+m\leqslant k<0
so
v_{k-i+m}=0
as
v\in A\langle\partial/r\rangle
.
∎

When
||\delta||\leqslant r
and the norm on
A
satisfies
|ab|=|a|\cdot|b|
for all
a,b\in A
, it also follows from the work of Pangalos — see
[
37
, Proposition 2.1.2]
— that
A\langle\partial/r\rangle
is an associative
K
-Banach algebra.

Lemma 2.2.10
.

Let
B
be another
K
-Banach algebra. Given a bounded
K
-Banach algebra homomorphism
f\colon A\to B
and an element
b\in B
, the following are equivalent.

(1)

There is a bounded
K
-Banach algebra homomorphism
g\colon A\langle\partial/r\rangle\to B
extending
f
such that
g(\partial)=b
;

(2)

(a)

bf(a)-f(a)b=f(\delta(a))
for all
a\in A
and

(b)

\sup\limits_{\ell\geqslant 0}|b^{\ell}|/r^{\ell}<\infty
.

Moreover when such a
g
exists it is unique.

Proof.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b). Since for all
a\in A
,
\partial a-a\partial=\delta(a)
, we have

bf(a)-f(a)b=g(\partial)g(a)-g(a)g(\partial)=g(\delta(a))=f(\delta(a)),

so (i) holds. Moreover, because
|\partial^{\ell}|=r^{\ell}
in
A\langle\partial/r\rangle
for all
\ell\geqslant 0
,

||g||\geqslant|g(\partial^{\ell})|/r^{\ell}=|b^{\ell}|/r^{\ell}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\ell\geqslant 0,

so (ii) holds.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a) Let
L:=\sup\limits_{\ell\geqslant 0}|b^{\ell}|/r^{\ell}
and choose constants
M,D>0
such that
|f(a)|\leqslant M|a|
for all
a\in A
and
|b_{1}b_{2}|\leqslant D|b_{1}||b_{2}|
for all
b_{1},b_{2}\in B
.

By condition (i), together with the universal property of the skew-Ore extension
A[T;\delta]
— see Proposition
2.1.1
—
f
extends uniquely to a
K
-algebra map
h\colon A[T;\delta]\to B
such that
h(T)=b
. We compute that for
a_{0},\ldots,a_{n}\in A

\left|\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}h(a_{i}T^{i})\right|=\left|\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}f(a_{i})b^{i}\right|\leqslant D\sup\limits_{i\geqslant 0}(|f(a_{i})||b^{i}|)\leqslant DML\sup\limits_{i\geqslant 0}|a_{i}|r^{i}.

Thus, identifying
A[T;\delta]
with its image in
A\langle\partial/r\rangle
under the map
\varphi
in the proof of Lemma
2.2.9
we see that
h
is bounded with respect to the subspace norm on
A[T;\delta]
and so
h
extends uniquely to a bounded
K
-linear map
g\colon A\langle\partial/r\rangle\to B
. Finally, consider the continuous map
\Psi\colon A\langle\partial/r\rangle\times A\langle\partial/r\rangle\to B
given by

\Psi(u,v)=g(uv)-g(u)g(v).

Since
\Psi
vanishes on the dense subset
A[T,\delta]\times A[T,\delta]
of its domain, it is identically zero and so
g
is a
K
-algebra homomorphism.
∎

It turns out that the skew-Tate algebra
A\langle\partial/r\rangle
admits a natural involution.

Lemma 2.2.11
.

Let
A
be a
K
-Banach algebra and let
\delta\in\mathcal{B}(A)
be a derivation such that
|\delta|_{\sp,A}<r
. Then there is a bounded
K
-Banach algebra isomorphism

(-)^{T}:A\langle\partial/r\rangle\to A\langle\partial/r\rangle^{\op}

such that
a^{T}=a
for all
a\in A
,
\partial^{T}=-\partial
and
(Q^{T})^{T}=Q
for all
Q\in A\langle\partial/r\rangle
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
2.2.10
with
A=\mathcal{O}(X)
,
\delta=\partial_{x}\in\mathcal{B}(A)
,
B:=A\langle\partial/r\rangle^{\op}
,
b=-\partial
and
f:A\to B
the natural inclusion. Writing
\cdot
for the product in the
K
-Banach algebra
B
, we compute that for all
a\in A
we have

b\cdot f(a)-f(a)\cdot b=a(-\partial)-(-\partial)a=[\partial,f(a)]=f(\delta(a)),

so the condition (b)(i) in Lemma
2.2.10
holds. The condition (b)(ii) is clear since
|b^{\ell}|=|(-\partial)^{\ell}|=r^{\ell}
holds in
B
for all
\ell\geqslant 0
by the definition of the norm on
B
found at Definition
2.2.8
. So by Lemma
2.2.10
we obtain a bounded
K
-Banach algebra homomorphism
(-)^{T}:A\langle\partial/r\rangle\to B
such that
a^{T}=a
for all
a\in A
and
\partial^{T}=-\partial
.

It remains to check that this map is self-inverse. To this end, note that the map
(-)^{TT}:A\langle\partial/r\rangle\to A\langle\partial/r\rangle
fixes
A
and
\partial
pointwise. Since it is also a bounded
K
-Banach algebra homomorphism and since
A
and
\partial
generate a dense
K
-subalgebra of
A\langle\partial/r\rangle
, we see that
(-)^{TT}
is the identity map on all of
A\langle\partial/r\rangle
.
∎

2.3.
The sheaf on
\mathcal{D}_{r}
on
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible subdomains of
\mathbb{A}

In this section, on we will be interested in affinoid subdomains of the rigid-analytic affine line
\mathbb{A}
. Let
\partial_{x}
denote the derivation
\frac{d}{dx}
of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}}
.

Definition 2.3.1
.

Let
X
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
.

(1)

We define
the spectral radius of
X
to be
r(X):=|\partial_{x}|_{\sp,\mathcal{O}(X)}
.

(2)

Let
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. We say that
X
is
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible
if and only if

r>r(X).

We emphasise that this notion of spectral radius
r(X)
depends on the choice of global vector field
\partial_{x}
on
\mathbb{A}
, i.e. on the choice of coordinate
x
on
\mathbb{A}
.

Proposition 2.3.2
.

Let
X,Y
be two affinoid subdomains of
\mathbb{A}
. Then

r(X\cap Y)\leqslant\max\{r(X),r(Y)\}.

For any
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, if
X
and
Y
are
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible, then so is
X\cap Y
.

To prove this result, we will need the following elementary statements from
p
-adic functional analysis that we were unable to find in the literature.

Proposition 2.3.3
.

Let
V,W
be two
K
-Banach spaces, let
S:V\to V
and
T:W\to W
be bounded
K
-linear maps.

(1)

Let
U:V\widehat{\otimes}W\to V\widehat{\otimes}W
be the bounded
K
-linear map
U:=S\widehat{\otimes}1+1\widehat{\otimes}T
.

|U|_{\sp,V\widehat{\otimes}W}\quad\leqslant\quad\max\{|S|_{\sp,V},|T|_{\sp,W}\}.

(2)

Suppose that
W\leqslant V
is closed and that
T
is the restriction of
S
to
W
. Let
\overline{S}:V/W\to V/W
be the induced bounded
K
-linear map. Then

|\overline{S}|_{\sp,V/W}\leqslant|S|_{\sp,V}.

(3)

Suppose that
K^{\prime}
is a complete field extension of
K
and that
V
is of countable type. Let
V^{\prime}:=V\widehat{\otimes}K^{\prime}
and let
S^{\prime}:=S\widehat{\otimes}1:V^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
. Then

|S|_{\sp,V}=|S^{\prime}|_{\sp,V^{\prime}}.

Proof.

(a) Write
s:=|S|_{\sp,V}
and
t:=|T|_{\sp,W}
. It follows from the definition of spectral radius that there are real constants
A,B>1
such that

|S^{n}(v)|\leqslant As^{n}\quad\mbox{and}\quad|T^{n}(w)|\leqslant Bt^{n}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad n\geqslant 0,v\in V,w\in W.

Given
v\in V
and
w\in W
, we compute that

U^{n}(v_{i}\otimes w_{i})=\left(\sum_{m=0}^{n}\binom{n}{m}S^{m}\otimes T^{n-m}\right)(v\otimes w)=\sum_{m=0}^{n}S^{m}(v)\binom{n}{m}T^{n-m}(w).

Hence, given an element
u\in V\otimes W
, whenever we can write
u=\sum_{i=1}^{r}v_{i}\otimes w_{i}
with
v_{i}\in V
and
w_{i}\in W
, we can calculate that

\begin{array}[]{lll}|U^{n}(u)|&\leqslant&\max\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}\max\limits_{0\leqslant m\leqslant n}|S^{m}(v_{i})|\cdot|\binom{n}{m}|\cdot|T^{n-m}(w_{i})|\leqslant\\
&\leqslant&As^{m}\cdot Bt^{n-m}\cdot\max\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}|v_{i}||w_{i}|=\\
&=&AB\max\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}|v_{i}||w_{i}|\cdot\max\{s,t\}^{n}.\end{array}

Taking the infimum over all ways of writing
u
in this form, and using the density of
V\otimes W
in
V\widehat{\otimes}W
, we conclude that

|U^{n}|_{V\widehat{\otimes}W}\leqslant AB\max\{s,t\}^{n}

for all
n\geqslant 0
. Extracting
n^{\rm{th}}
-roots and letting
n\to\infty
gives the result.

(b) Write
s:=|S|_{\sp,V}
and choose a real number
A>1
such that
||S^{n}||\leqslant As^{n}
for all
n\geqslant 0
. Then

|\overline{S}^{n}(v+W)|_{V/W}=\inf\limits_{w\in W}|S^{n}(v+w)|\leqslant\inf\limits_{w\in W}As^{n}|v+w|=As^{n}|v+W|.

We conclude that
||\overline{S}^{n}||\leqslant As^{n}
for all
n\geqslant 0
. Again, extracting
n^{\rm{th}}
-roots and letting
n\to\infty
gives the result.

(c) Because
V
is of countable type, by
[
22
, Proposition 1.2.1(3)]
we can find a
K
-Banach space isomorphism
\varphi:c_{0}(K)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}V
. We identify
c_{0}(K^{\prime})
with
c_{0}(K)\widehat{\otimes}K^{\prime}
; this gives us an induced isomorphism
\varphi^{\prime}:c_{0}(K^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}V^{\prime}
given by
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi\widehat{\otimes}1
. Let
U:c_{0}(K)\to c_{0}(K)
be the bounded
K
-linear endomorphism defined by
U:=\varphi^{-1}\circ S\circ\varphi
, and let
U^{\prime}:c_{0}(K^{\prime})\to c_{0}(K^{\prime})
be defined by
U^{\prime}=(\varphi^{\prime})^{-1}\circ S^{\prime}\circ\varphi^{\prime}
. Then we can find positive real constants
a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}>0
such that for all
n\geqslant 0
we have

a||U^{n}||\leqslant||S^{n}||\leqslant b||U^{n}||\quad\mbox{and}\quad a^{\prime}||U^{\prime n}||\leqslant||S^{\prime n}||\leqslant b^{\prime}||U^{\prime n}||.

This quickly implies that
|S|_{\sp,V}=|U|_{\sp,c_{0}(K)}
and
|S^{\prime}|_{\sp,V^{\prime}}=|U^{\prime}|_{\sp,c_{0}(K^{\prime})}
.

Let
\{e_{1},e_{2},\cdots\}
be the standard orthonormal basis for the
K
-Banach space
c_{0}(K)
; then it also forms an orthonormal basis for
c_{0}(K^{\prime})
as a
K^{\prime}
-Banach space. Since
S^{\prime}=S\widehat{\otimes}1
and
\varphi^{\prime}=\varphi\widehat{\otimes}1
, we see that
U^{\prime}=U\widehat{\otimes}1
, and we can calculate

||U^{n}||=\sup\limits_{m\geqslant 0}|U^{n}(e_{m})|=\sup\limits_{m\geqslant 0}|U^{\prime n}(e_{m})|=||U^{\prime n}||\quad\mbox{for all}\quad n\geqslant 0.

Hence
|U|_{\sp,c_{0}(K)}=|U^{\prime}|_{\sp,c_{0}(K^{\prime})}
and the result follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.3.2
.

We can give
\mathcal{O}(X)\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{O}(Y)
the structure of a
K[\partial_{x}]
-module by making
\partial_{x}
act by
\partial_{x}\widehat{\otimes}1+1\widehat{\otimes}\partial_{x}
as in Proposition
2.3.3
(a). Then there is a bounded surjective
K[\partial_{x}]
-linear map

\mathcal{O}(X)\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{O}(Y)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}(X\cap Y)

induced by the map
\mathcal{O}(X)\times\mathcal{O}(Y)\to\mathcal{O}(X\cap Y)
that sends
(f,g)
to
f|_{X\cap Y}g|_{X\cap Y}
.
Since the kernel of this map is closed and
\partial_{x}
-stable, by Proposition
2.3.3
we have

\begin{array}[]{lllll}r(X\cap Y)&=&|\partial_{x}|_{\sp,\mathcal{O}(X\cap Y)}\\
&\leqslant&|\partial_{x}\widehat{\otimes}1+1\widehat{\otimes}\partial_{x}|_{\sp,\mathcal{O}(X)\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{O}(Y)}\\
&\leqslant&\max\{|\partial_{x}|_{\sp,\mathcal{O}(X)},|\partial_{x}|_{\sp,\mathcal{O}(Y)}\}\\
&=&\max\{r(X),r(Y)\}.\end{array}

The second statement is an easy consequence of the first.
∎

Definition 2.3.4
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. The
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible
G
-topology
on
\mathbb{A}
has the
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoids as its admissible sets, and finite coverings as its admissible coverings. We denote this
G
-topology by
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
.

It follows easily from Proposition
2.3.2
that
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
is indeed a
G
-topology in the sense of
[
17
, Definition 9.1.1/1]
.

Definition 2.3.5
.

For any affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{A}
, let
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
denote
K
-Banach space
\mathcal{O}(X)\langle\partial/r\rangle
from Definition
2.2.8
.

Proposition 2.3.6
.

Let
Y\subseteq X
be
(\partial_{x}/r)
-admissible affinoid subsets of
\mathbb{A}
.

(1)

\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
carries an associative, unital
K
-Banach algebra structure.

(2)

The function
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)
that sends
\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}\partial^{n}
to
\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}(a_{n})_{|Y}\partial^{n}
is a bounded homomorphism of
K
-Banach algebras.

Proof.

Since
r(X)<r
, it follows from Definition
2.3.1
(a) that

\lim\limits_{\ell\to\infty}||\partial_{x}^{\ell}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}/r^{\ell}=0.

Now Lemma
2.2.9
gives the required associative, unital,
K
-Banach algebra structure on
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)=\mathcal{O}(X)\langle\partial/r\rangle
, proving part (a).

For part (b), we apply Lemma
2.2.10
with
A=\mathcal{O}(X)
,
B=\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)
and
b=\partial\in B
to obtain a bounded
K
-algebra homomorphism
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)
which extends the restriction map
\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(Y)
and sends
\partial\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
to
\partial\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)
.
∎

In this way,
\mathcal{D}_{r}
becomes a presheaf of
K
-Banach algebras on
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
. Evidently, we are thinking of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
as a particular Banach completion of the usual ring of finite-order differential operators
\mathcal{D}(X)
on
X
. The following Lemma makes this more precise.

Lemma 2.3.7
.

There is an injective homomorphism
j:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}_{r}
from the restriction of
\mathcal{D}
to
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
to
\mathcal{D}_{r}
. The image of
j(X)
is dense for all
X\in\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
.

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
. Then
\mathcal{D}(X)
is the skew-polynomial ring
\mathcal{O}(X)[\partial;\partial_{x}]
; this gives us an injective left
\mathcal{O}(X)
-linear map
j(X):\mathcal{D}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
which sends
\partial^{n}\in\mathcal{D}(X)
to
\partial^{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
for all
n\geqslant 0
. Lemma
2.2.4
implies that
j(X)
is a ring homomorphism. The image of
j(X)
is dense by the definition of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
, and it is clear that the maps
j(X)
commute with the restriction maps in the (pre)sheaves
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}_{r}
.
∎

Proposition 2.3.8
.

Suppose that
r\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{r}
is a sheaf of
K
-Banach algebras on
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
, with vanishing higher Čech cohomology.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.3.6
,
\mathcal{D}_{r}
is a presheaf of
K
-Banach algebras on the
G
-topology
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
; here we regard it only as a presheaf of
K
-Banach spaces. Let
\mathbb{D}_{r}
denote the closed disc of radius
r
: the condition we imposed on
r
ensures that
\mathbb{D}_{r}
is a
K
-affinoid variety. Then for every
X\in\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
, there is an isomorphism of
K
-Banach spaces

\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)=\mathcal{O}(X)\langle\partial/r\rangle\cong\mathcal{O}(X\times\mathbb{D}_{r})

which is functorial in
X
. Let
\mathcal{U}:=\{X_{1},\cdots,X_{m}\}
be a finite affinoid covering in
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
. Then because
\mathbb{D}_{r}
is itself an affinoid variety,
\mathcal{V}:=\{X_{1}\times\mathbb{D}_{r},\cdots,X_{m}\times\mathbb{D}_{r}\}
is a finite affinoid covering of
X\times\mathbb{D}_{r}
, so by Tate’s Acyclicity Theorem
[
22
, Theorem 4.2.2]
, the natural map
\mathcal{O}(X\times\mathbb{D}_{r})\to\check{H}^{0}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{O})
is an isomorphism, and
\check{H}^{j}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{O})=0
for
j>0
. The result follows.
∎

We will also need an overconvergent version of these definitions.

Definition 2.3.9
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

(1)

An affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{A}
is
(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
-admissible
if and only if

r\geqslant r(X).

(2)

The
(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
-admissible
G
-topology
on
\mathbb{A}
has the
(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
-admissible affinoids as its admissible sets, and finite coverings as the admissible coverings.

(3)

We denote this
G
-topology by
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
.

(4)

For each
X\in\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
, we define

\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}(X):=\underset{c>r}{\colim}{}\hskip 2.84544pt\mathcal{D}_{c}(X).

Note that this colimit is computed in the category of associative unital
K
-algebras. The connecting maps
\mathcal{D}_{c}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{c^{\prime}}(X)
appearing in this colimit are all injective, so one should think of
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}(X)
as the union of the
\mathcal{D}_{c}(X)
as
c
runs over all real numbers strictly bigger than
r
.

Theorem 2.3.10
.

For any
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}
is a sheaf of
K
-algebras on
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
with vanishing higher Čech cohomology.

Proof.

It is clear from the definitions that
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}\subset\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/c)
whenever
c>r
. Hence
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}(X)
is a
K
-algebra for every
X\in\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
by Proposition
2.3.6
. The restriction maps in
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}
respect the
K
-algebra structure, so
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}
is a presheaf of
K
-algebras on
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
. We can find a decreasing sequence
c_{0}>c_{1}>\cdots
in
\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
converging to
r
from above, so that as presheaves on
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
we have

\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}=\underset{n\to\infty}{\colim}{}\hskip 2.84544pt\mathcal{D}_{c_{n}}.

The sheaf property and the vanishing of higher Čech cohomology of
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}
now follow from Corollary
2.3.8
and the exactness of direct limits.
∎

For future use, we record here how the functors
\mathcal{D}_{r}
and
\mathcal{D}_{r}^{\dagger}
behave with respect to finite base change of the ground field. We leave the easy proof to the reader.

Lemma 2.3.11
.

Let
X
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
and let
K^{\prime}
be a finite field extension of
K
.

(1)

For all
r>r(X)
,
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X_{K^{\prime}})\cong\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\otimes K^{\prime}
as
K^{\prime}
-Banach algebras.

(2)

For all
r\geqslant r(X)
,
\mathcal{D}_{r}^{\dagger}(X_{K^{\prime}})\cong\mathcal{D}_{r}^{\dagger}(X)\otimes K^{\prime}
as
K^{\prime}
-algebras.

The sheaf of rings
\mathcal{D}_{r}
acts naturally on
\mathcal{O}
; more precisely, we have the following Lemma which will be frequently useful in performing explicit calculations.

Lemma 2.3.12
.

Let
r>0
and
X\in\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
.

(1)

The natural action of
\mathcal{D}(X)
on
\mathcal{O}(X)
induces a bounded
K
-algebra map

\sigma_{r}:\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))

such that
\sigma_{r}(a)(b)=ab
for all
a,b\in\mathcal{O}(X)
, and
\sigma_{r}(\partial)=\partial_{x}
.

(2)

The map
\sigma_{r}
is injective.

Proof.

(a) The map
\sigma_{r}:\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))
which sends
a\in\mathcal{O}(X)
to the operator of multiplication by
a
is a bounded
K
-algebra homomorphism. Definition
2.3.1
directly implies that
||\partial_{x}^{k}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}/r^{k}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
. Hence
\sup\limits_{\ell\geqslant 0}||\partial_{x}^{\ell}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}/r^{\ell}<\infty
, so by Lemma
2.2.10
,
\sigma_{r}
extends uniquely to a bounded
K
-algebra homomorphism
\sigma_{r}:\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)=\mathcal{O}(X)\langle\partial/r\rangle\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))
with the required properties.

(b) Suppose that
Q=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}a_{n}\partial^{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
with
a_{n}\in\mathcal{O}(X)
not all zero. Let
m\geqslant 0
be least such that
a_{m}\neq 0
. Then
x^{m}\in\mathcal{O}(X)
and

\sigma_{r}\left(\sum_{n\geqslant 0}a_{n}\partial^{n}\right)(x^{m})=m!a_{m}.

Therefore
Q\notin\ker\sigma_{r}
and
\sigma_{r}
is injective.
∎

We now turn to the question of calculating, at least in theory, the spectral radius
r(X)
of an affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{A}
. See
[
8
, §4.1]
for the terminology and notation. The spectral radius behaves well with respect to base change:

Lemma 2.3.13
.

Let
K^{\prime}
be a complete field extension of
K
and let
X
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
. Then

r(X)=r(X_{K^{\prime}}).

Proof.

The
K
-affinoid algebra
\mathcal{O}(X)
is a quotient of a Tate algebra, and is therefore of countable type as a
K
-Banach space. Now apply Proposition
2.3.3
(c) to the bounded
K
-linear operator
\partial_{x}:\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(X)
, noting that
\mathcal{O}(X_{K^{\prime}})=\mathcal{O}(X)\widehat{\otimes}K^{\prime}
by definition of
X_{K^{\prime}}
.∎

In view of
[
8
, Theorem 4.1.8]
, we finish
\lx@sectionsign\ref{DrSect}
by explicitly calculating the spectral radius of affinoid subdomains of the affine line which are finite unions of cheeses.

Definition 2.3.14
.

(1)

We define
\rho(C(\mathbf{\alpha},\mathbf{s})):=\min\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant g}|s_{i}|
.

(2)

If
X=X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{m}
is a disjoint union of cheeses
X_{i}
, we define

\rho(X):=\min\limits_{i}\rho(X_{i}).

Recall that
\partial_{x}^{[n]}:=\partial_{x}^{n}/n!:\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(X)
denotes the
n
th divided power
of
\partial_{x}
.

Lemma 2.3.15
.

Let
X
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
that is split over
K
. Then

||\partial_{x}^{[n]}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}=\frac{1}{\rho(X)^{n}}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad n\geqslant 0.

Proof.

If
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}
are the connected components of
X
, then
\mathcal{O}(X)
is the orthogonal direct sum of
K
-Banach spaces
\mathcal{O}(X_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{O}(X_{m})
, and the differential operators
\partial_{x}^{[n]}
respects this decomposition. Therefore
||\partial_{x}^{[n]}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}=\max\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m}||\partial_{x}^{[n]}||_{\mathcal{O}(X_{i})}
. On the other hand, by definition we have that
\rho(X)^{-n}=\max\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant m}\rho(X_{i})^{-n}
, and this reduces us to the case where
X=C(\mathbf{\alpha},\mathbf{s})
is a cheese.

For all
n,\ell\geqslant 0
and all
i=1,\ldots,g
we have the estimate

\left|\partial_{x}^{[n]}\left(\frac{s_{i}}{x-\alpha_{i}}\right)^{\ell}\right|_{X}=\left|(-1)^{n}\binom{\ell-n+1}{n}\left(\frac{-1}{s_{i}}\right)^{n}\left(\frac{s_{i}}{x-\alpha_{i}}\right)^{\ell+n}\right|_{X}\leqslant|s_{i}|^{-n},

as well as

\left|\partial_{x}^{[n]}\left(\frac{x-\alpha_{0}}{s_{0}}\right)^{\ell}\right|_{X}=\left|\binom{\ell}{n}\left(\frac{1}{s_{0}}\right)^{n}\left(\frac{x-\alpha_{0}}{s_{0}}\right)^{\ell-n}\right|_{X}\leqslant|s_{0}|^{-n}.

Since
\rho(X)=\min\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant g}|s_{i}|
by definition and since
\min\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant g}|s_{i}|\leqslant|s_{0}|
by
[
8
, Definition 4.1.1]
, we can apply the explicit description of
\mathcal{O}(C(\alpha,\mathbf{s}))
given in
[
35
, Proposition 2.4.8(a)]
to obtain
||\partial_{x}^{[n]}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}\leqslant\rho(X)^{-n}
. For the reverse inequality, say
\rho(X)=|s_{i}|
for some
1\leqslant i\leqslant g
. Then taking
\ell=1
in the first estimate above shows that

||\partial_{x}^{[n]}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}\geqslant\left|(-1)^{n}\left(\frac{-1}{s_{i}}\right)^{n}\left(\frac{s_{i}}{x-\alpha_{i}}\right)\right|_{X}\left/\right.\left|\left(\frac{s_{i}}{x-\alpha_{i}}\right)\right|_{X}=|s_{i}|^{-n}=\rho(X)^{-n}.\qed

Recall from
\lx@sectionsign\ref{ConvNotn}
that
|p|:=1/p
and that
\varpi:=p^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Lemma 2.3.16
.

For all
n\geqslant 0
, we have
1\leqslant\frac{|n!|}{\varpi^{n}}\leqslant pn
.

Proof.

v_{p}(n!)=\frac{n-s_{p}(n)}{p-1}
where
s_{p}(n)
is the sum of the
p
-adic digits of
n
, so

\frac{|n!|}{\varpi^{n}}=|p|^{v_{p}(n!)-\frac{n}{p-1}}=|p|^{-\frac{s_{p}(n)}{p-1}}=p^{\frac{s_{p}(n)}{p-1}}.

Now
0\leqslant s_{p}(n)\leqslant(p-1)(\log_{p}(n)+1)
, so
1\leqslant\frac{|n!|}{\varpi^{n}}\leqslant p^{\log_{p}(n)+1}=pn
.
∎

Corollary 2.3.17
.

For
X
as in Lemma
2.3.15
, we have

r(X)=\varpi/\rho(X).

For
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
X
is
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible if and only if
r>\varpi/\rho(X)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.3.15
we have
||\partial_{x}^{n}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}=|n!|\cdot||\partial_{x}^{[n]}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}=\frac{|n!|}{\rho(X)^{n}}
. Take
n^{\rm{th}}
-roots, let
n\to\infty
and apply Lemma
2.3.16
to obtain the first statement. The second one follows directly from Definition
2.3.1
(b).
∎

It will be useful to know that for a
general
affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{A}
, the spectral radius
r(X)
is in fact completely determined by
||\partial_{x}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}
; for this we need the following elementary

Lemma 2.3.18
.

Let
V
be a
K
-Banach space, let
K^{\prime}
be a finite field extension of
K
and let
V^{\prime}:=V\widehat{\otimes}K^{\prime}
. Let
T:V\to V
be a bounded
K
-linear map and let
T^{\prime}:=T\widehat{\otimes}1:V^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
. Then

||T^{\prime}||=||T||.

Proof.

Because
[K^{\prime}:K]<\infty
we can find a basis
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
for
(K^{\prime})^{\circ}
as a
K^{\circ}
-module, where
a_{1}=1
. Then
V^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V\widehat{\otimes}a_{i}
, and by
[
41
, Lemma 17.2]
the tensor product norm on
V^{\prime}
is given by

\left|\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}\widehat{\otimes}a_{i}\right|=\max\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}|v_{i}||a_{i}|.

Therefore

\left|T^{\prime}\left(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}\widehat{\otimes}a_{i}\right)\right|=\max\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}|T(v_{i})||a_{i}|\leqslant||T||\cdot\max\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}|v_{i}||a_{i}|=||T||\hskip 2.84544pt\left|\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}\widehat{\otimes}a_{i}\right|

which implies that
||T^{\prime}||\leqslant||T||
. For the reverse inequality, note that for any
v\in V
we have
|T(v)|=|T^{\prime}(v\otimes a_{1})|\leqslant||T^{\prime}|||v\otimes a_{1}|=||T^{\prime}||\hskip 2.84544pt|v|
; hence
||T||\leqslant||T^{\prime}||
.
∎

Corollary 2.3.19
.

Let
X
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
. Then

r(X)=\varpi\hskip 2.84544pt||\partial_{x}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}.

Proof.

Both sides of the equation are invariant under passing to a finite field extension, by Lemma
2.3.13
and Lemma
2.3.18
. By
[
8
, Theorem 4.1.8]
, we may then assume that
X
splits over
K
. But now
r(X)=\varpi/\rho(X)
by Corollary
2.3.17
, whereas
1/\rho(X)=||\partial_{x}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}
by Lemma
2.3.15
.
∎

2.4.
Twisting-automorphisms of
\mathcal{D}_{r}

In this section we study the line bundles with connectionn that arise from certain Kummer-étale coverings of
X
, and we investigate when the action of
\mathcal{D}
on these line bundles extends to an action of
\mathcal{D}_{r}
.

Lemma 2.4.1
.

Let
X
be a smooth rigid
K
-analytic variety, let
u\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
and let
d
be a non-zero integer. There is an
\mathcal{O}(X)
-linear ring automorphism

\theta_{u,d}:\mathcal{D}(X)\to\mathcal{D}(X)

such that

(9)

\theta_{u,d}(\delta)=\delta-\frac{1}{d}\frac{\delta(u)}{u}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\delta\in\mathcal{T}(X).

Proof.

By considering an affinoid covering of
X
, we quickly reduce to the case where
X
is itself affinoid. Let
Z\to X
be any étale map such that
Z
is affinoid and
\mathcal{O}(Z)
contains a unit
z
such that
z^{d}=u
; for example we could take
Z:=\Sp\mathcal{O}(X)[T]/(T^{d}-u)
. There is a canonical
K
-algebra homomorphism
\mathcal{D}(X)\to\mathcal{D}(Z)
; it is injective, and we will identify
\mathcal{D}(X)
with its image in
\mathcal{D}(Z)
.

Since
z
is a unit in
\mathcal{D}(Z)
, conjugation by
z
is defines an
\mathcal{O}(Z)
-linear ring automorphism
\theta_{u,d}:\mathcal{D}(Z)\to\mathcal{D}(Z)
with inverse
\theta_{u^{-1},d}
. Let
\delta\in\mathcal{T}(X)
. Then
\delta(u)=\delta(z^{d})=dz^{d-1}\delta(z)=duz^{-1}\delta(z)
, and
0=\delta(zz^{-1})=z\delta(z^{-1})+\delta(z)z^{-1}
together show that

(10)

z\delta(z^{-1})=-\delta(z)z^{-1}=-\frac{1}{d}\frac{\delta(u)}{u}.

Therefore for all
f\in\mathcal{O}(X)
we have

\theta_{u,d}(\delta)(f)=(z\delta z^{-1})(f)=z\delta(z^{-1}f)=z\delta(z^{-1})f+\delta(f)=\delta(f)-\frac{1}{d}\frac{\delta(u)}{u},

which shows that
\theta_{u,d}
preserves the image of
\mathcal{O}(X)+\mathcal{T}(X)
inside
\mathcal{D}(Z)
. Since
X
is smooth,
\mathcal{D}(X)
is generated by
\mathcal{O}(X)
and
\mathcal{T}(X)
, so
\theta_{u,d}
also preserves the entire image of
\mathcal{D}(X)
in
\mathcal{D}(Z)
, and therefore defines an endomorphism
\theta_{u,d}
of
\mathcal{D}(X)
. Since the same is true for
\theta_{u^{-1},d}
, this endomorphism must be bijective. ∎

As our notation suggests,
\theta_{u,d}
only depends on
u
and
d
and not on any particular choice of the étale map
Z\to X
. It could have been also defined without introducing the covering space
Z
of
X
. For future use, we record the following useful observation, obtained by taking
Z\to X
to be the identity map.

Lemma 2.4.2
.

Let
z\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
. Then
\theta_{z^{d},d}(Q)=zQz^{-1}
for all
Q\in\mathcal{D}(X)
.

Our main aim of the remainder of this section is to show that when
X
is an
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
then
\theta_{u,d}
extends to a bounded
K
-algebra automorphism of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
and thus that
\theta_{u,d}
extends to a
K
-algebra automorphism of
\mathcal{D}_{r}^{\dagger}(X)
whenever
X
is
(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
-admissible. To do this we will use Lemma
2.2.10
and so we must estimate the norms of the images of the elements
\theta_{u,d}(\delta^{\ell})
under the natural inclusion
\mathcal{D}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
.

Lemma 2.4.3
.

For all
u,v\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
we have

\theta_{uv,d}=\theta_{u,d}\circ\theta_{v,d}.

Proof.

This is a straightforward calculation on the covering space of
X
obtained by adjoining a
d^{th}
-root of both
u
and
v
, thinking of
\theta_{uv,d}
as being conjugation by the
d^{th}
root of
uv
. It can also be checked directly using the formula (
9
).∎

Our next result tells us how to rewrite
\theta_{u,d}(\partial^{[n]})
in standard form as a differential operator of finite order.

Proposition 2.4.4
.

For every
n\geqslant 0
and every
\delta\in\mathcal{T}(X)
we have

\theta_{u,d}(\delta^{[n]})=\sum\limits_{\alpha=0}^{n}\theta_{u,d}(\delta^{[n-\alpha]})(1)\hskip 2.84544pt\delta^{[\alpha]}.

Proof.

Choose any étale map
Z\to X
as in the proof of Lemma
2.4.1
. For each
a\in\mathcal{D}(Z)
, let
\ell_{a}
and
r_{a}
denote the operations of left, respectively, right, multiplication by the element
a
, and let
\ad_{a}:=\ell_{a}-r_{a}
. Then

\ad_{\delta}^{[k]}(f)=\delta^{[k]}(f)

for each
k\geqslant 0
and each
f\in\mathcal{O}(Z)
. Using the binomial theorem, we now have

\begin{array}[]{lll}\delta^{[n]}z^{-1}&=&\ell_{\delta}^{[n]}(z^{-1})=\left(r_{\delta}+\ad(\delta)\right)^{[n]}(z^{-1})\\
&=&\sum\limits_{\alpha=0}^{n}r_{\delta}^{[\alpha]}\ad_{\delta}^{[n-\alpha]}(z^{-1})=\sum\limits_{\alpha=0}^{n}\delta^{[n-\alpha]}(z^{-1})\delta^{[\alpha]}.\\
\end{array}

Hence
\theta_{u,d}(\delta^{[n]})=z\hskip 2.84544pt\delta^{[n]}z^{-1}=\sum\limits_{\alpha=0}^{n}z\hskip 2.84544pt\delta^{[n-\alpha]}(z^{-1})\delta^{[\alpha]}
. Evaluating both sides of this equation at
1\in\mathcal{O}(X)
shows that
z\delta^{[n]}(z^{-1})=\theta_{u,d}(\delta^{[n]})(1)
.
∎

After these generalities, we return to the affine line
\mathbb{A}
with its local coordinate
x\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}}
.
We fix an affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{A}
until the end of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{DivTwDer}
.

Definition 2.4.5
.

For every
n\geqslant 0
, we define

h_{u,d}^{[n]}:=\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x}^{[n]})(1)\in\mathcal{O}(X).

Note that for any étale map
Z\to X
as in the proof of Lemma
2.4.1
, we have

h_{u,d}^{[n]}=z\hskip 2.84544pt\partial_{x}^{[n]}(z^{-1}).

Our next task will be to obtain an upper bound for
|h_{u,d}^{[n]}|_{X}
. Using Proposition
2.4.4
this will enable us to estimate the norms
|\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x}^{n})|_{\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)}
.

Corollary 2.4.6
.

Suppose that
\alpha\in K
such that
x-\alpha\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
and
Y\to X
is an étale cover such that there is
y\in\mathcal{O}(Y)
with
y^{d}=x-\alpha
. Then

\partial_{x}^{[m]}(y^{k})=\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m}y^{k}(x-\alpha)^{-m}\quad\mbox{ for all}\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}\quad\mbox{and}\quad m\geqslant 0.

Proof.

The case
m=0
is trivial. Since
1=\partial_{x}(y^{d})=d\partial_{x}(y)y^{d-1}
we see that
\partial_{x}(y)=\frac{1}{dy^{d}}y=\frac{1}{d(x-\alpha)}y

which implies by the Leibniz rule that
\partial_{x}(y^{k})=\frac{k}{d}\frac{y^{k}}{x-\alpha}
as required for the case
m=1
. Now we proceed by induction on
m
:

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{m+1}(y^{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\left(m!\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m}\frac{y^{k}}{(x-\alpha)^{m}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m!\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m}\left(\frac{k}{d}\frac{y^{k}}{x-\alpha}\cdot(x-\alpha)^{-m}-my^{k}(x-\alpha)^{-m-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m!\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m}\frac{y^{k}}{(x-\alpha)^{m+1}}\left(\frac{k}{d}-m\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(m+1)!\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m+1}\frac{y^{k}}{(x-\alpha)^{m+1}}.

This completes the induction.∎

Here is our upper bound.

Proposition 2.4.7
.

Let
X
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
, let
u\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
and suppose that
p\nmid d
. Then

|h_{u,d}^{[n]}|_{X}\leqslant\left(\frac{r(X)}{\varpi}\right)^{n}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad n\geqslant 0.

Proof.

Suppose first that
X=C(\mathbf{\alpha},\mathbf{s})
is a cheese. Using
[
35
, Proposition 2.4.8(b)]
, write
u=\lambda\cdot\nu\cdot(x-\alpha_{1})^{k_{1}}\cdots(x-\alpha_{g})^{k_{g}}
for some
\lambda\in K^{\times},\nu\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times\times}
and
k_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. We form the étale cover
Y\to X
given by

\mathcal{O}(Y)=\mathcal{O}(X)[T_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,T_{g}^{\pm 1}]/\left\langle T_{1}^{d}-(x-\alpha_{1}),\cdots,T_{1}^{d}-(x-\alpha_{d})\right\rangle.

For each
i=1,\ldots,g
, let
z_{i}
denote the image of
T_{i}
in
\mathcal{O}(Y)
so that
z_{i}^{d}=x-\alpha_{i}
. Since
p\nmid d
, by
[
8
, Lemma 4.3.2(a)]
we can find
\epsilon\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times\times}
such that
\nu=\epsilon^{d}
, so that
u=\lambda\cdot(z_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots z_{g}^{k_{g}}\epsilon)^{d}
. Note that
\theta_{\lambda,d}
is the identity map because
\partial(\lambda)=0
; using Lemma
2.4.3
we see that we can assume
\lambda=1
so that
z:=z_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots z_{g}^{k_{g}}\epsilon
satisfies
z^{d}=u
. Using the Leibniz rule, and Corollary
2.4.6
, we obtain

(11)

\begin{array}[]{lll}z\hskip 2.84544pt\partial_{x}^{[n]}(z^{-1})&=&\epsilon\hskip 2.84544ptz_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots z_{g}^{k_{g}}\hskip 2.84544pt\partial_{x}^{[n]}(z_{1}^{-k_{1}}\cdots z_{g}^{-k_{g}}\epsilon^{-1})\\
&=&\sum\limits_{|m|=n}\left(\prod\limits_{i=1}^{g}\binom{-\frac{k_{i}}{d}}{m_{i}}(x-\alpha_{i})^{-m_{i}}\right)\cdot\epsilon\hskip 2.84544pt\partial_{x}^{[m_{g+1}]}(\epsilon^{-1})\end{array}

where the sum runs over all
m\in\mathbb{N}^{g+1}
such that
|m|:=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{g+1}m_{i}
equals
n
. Now

|\epsilon\hskip 2.84544pt\partial_{x}^{[m_{g+1}]}(\epsilon^{-1})|_{X}\leqslant|\epsilon|_{X}\cdot||\partial_{x}^{[m_{g+1}]}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}\cdot|\epsilon^{-1}|_{X}=\rho(X)^{-m_{g+1}}

by Lemma
2.3.15
because
|\epsilon|_{X}=1
. Using
(\ref{MuCalc})
together with Corollary
2.3.17
, we now obtain the required estimate

|h_{u,d}^{[n]}|_{X}=|z\hskip 2.84544pt\partial_{x}^{[n]}(z^{-1})|_{X}\leqslant\max\limits_{|m|=n}\prod_{i=1}^{g}|s_{i}|^{-m_{i}}\cdot\rho(X)^{-m_{g+1}}\leqslant\rho(X)^{-n}=\left(\frac{r(X)}{\varpi}\right)^{n}.

Next we consider the case where
X
splits over
K
; that is every connected component
X_{i}
of
X
is a cheese. Once again, choose any étale map
Z\to X
as in the proof of Lemma
2.4.1
. Let
u_{i}
denote the restriction of
u
to
X_{i}
and let
z_{i}
denote the restriction of
z
to
Z_{i}:=Z\times_{X}X_{i}
. Then using Definition
2.3.14
(b) together with Corollary
2.3.17
, we have

\begin{array}[]{lllllll}|h_{u,d}^{[n]}|_{X}&=&|z\hskip 2.84544pt\partial_{x}^{[n]}(z^{-1})|_{X}&=&\max\limits_{i}|z_{i}\hskip 2.84544pt\partial_{x}^{[n]}(z_{i}^{-1})|_{X_{i}}&&\\
&\leqslant&\max\limits_{i}\rho(X_{i})^{-n}&=&\rho(X)^{-n}&=&\left(\frac{r(X)}{\varpi}\right)^{n}.\end{array}

Finally, suppose that
X
is arbitrary. By
[
8
, Theorem 4.1.8]
, we can find a finite extension
K^{\prime}
of
K
