such that
X
splits over
K^{\prime}
. Since
r(X)=r(X_{K^{\prime}})
by Lemma
2.3.13
, we obtain

|h_{u,d}^{[n]}|_{X}=|h_{u,d}^{[n]}|_{X_{K^{\prime}}}=\left(\frac{r(X_{K^{\prime}})}{\varpi}\right)^{n}=\left(\frac{r(X)}{\varpi}\right)^{n}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad n\geqslant 0.\qed

Our next main result, Theorem
2.4.9
, requires the following elementary estimate.

Lemma 2.4.8
.

Let
0<\rho<1
. Then
\max\limits_{t\geqslant 0}\hskip 2.84544ptt\hskip 2.84544pt\rho^{t}=(e\log(\rho^{-1}))^{-1}
.

Proof.

Write
\lambda:=\log(\rho^{-1})>0
, and consider the function
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
f(t):=t\hskip 2.84544pt\rho^{t}=te^{-\lambda t}
. Then
f^{\prime}(t)=e^{-\lambda t}-\lambda te^{-\lambda t}
vanishes precisely when
t=\lambda^{-1}
, and
f^{\prime\prime}(t)=-\lambda e^{-\lambda t}-\lambda(1-\lambda t)e^{-\lambda t}
is strictly negative at
t=\lambda^{-1}
. So
t=\lambda^{-1}
a global maximum of
f(t)
with value
f(\lambda^{-1})=\lambda^{-1}e^{-1}=(e\log(\rho^{-1}))^{-1}
.∎

Theorem 2.4.9
.

Let
r>0
, let
X
be an
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
, let
u\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
and suppose that
p\nmid d
. Then
\theta_{u,d}\in\Aut_{K}\mathcal{D}(X)
extends to a bounded
K
-algebra automorphism
\theta_{u,d}
of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.3.7
, there is an injective
\mathcal{O}(X)
-linear
K
-algebra map
j:\mathcal{D}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
such that
j(\partial_{x})=\partial
. We will show that the
K
-algebra homomorphism

f:=j\circ\theta_{u,d}:\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)

extends to a bounded
K
-algebra homomorphism

\theta_{u,d}:\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)=\mathcal{O}(X)\langle\partial/r\rangle\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)

which sends
\partial\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
to
b:=j(\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x}))=\partial-\frac{1}{d}\frac{\partial_{x}(u)}{u}\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
. This follows from Lemma
2.2.10
, provided we can verify conditions (i) and (ii) in this Lemma.

(i) Let
a\in\mathcal{O}(X)
. Then because
\theta_{u,d}
and
j
are
K
-algebra homomorphisms,

[b,f(a)]=[j(\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x})),j(\theta_{u,d}(a))]=j(\theta_{u,d}([\partial_{x},a]))=f(\partial_{x}(a)).

(ii) Let
\ell\geqslant 0
. Then by Proposition
2.4.4
we have

b^{\ell}=f(\partial_{x}^{\ell})=\ell!\sum\limits_{\alpha=0}^{\ell}h_{u,d}^{[\ell-\alpha]}\partial^{[\alpha]}=\sum\limits_{\alpha=0}^{\ell}h_{u,d}^{[\ell-\alpha]}(\ell-\alpha)!\binom{\ell}{\alpha}\partial^{\alpha}

inside
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
. Using Proposition
2.4.7
we can now estimate

\begin{array}[]{lll}|b^{\ell}|/r^{\ell}&\leqslant&\sup\limits_{0\leqslant\alpha\leqslant\ell}\left|h_{u,d}^{[\ell-\alpha]}\right|_{X}\cdot|(\ell-\alpha)!|\cdot r^{\alpha-\ell}\leqslant\\
&\leqslant&\sup\limits_{0\leqslant\alpha\leqslant\ell}\left(\frac{r(X)}{\varpi}\right)^{\ell-\alpha}\cdot\frac{|(\ell-\alpha)!|}{r^{\ell-\alpha}}=\\
&=&\sup\limits_{0\leqslant n\leqslant\ell}\left(\frac{r(X)}{r}\right)^{n}\cdot\frac{|n!|}{\varpi^{n}}.\end{array}

Since
X
is
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible, the ratio
\rho:=r(X)/r
is strictly less than
1
. Lemma
2.3.16
together with Lemma
2.4.8
now show that

\sup\limits_{\ell\geqslant 0}|b^{\ell}|/r^{\ell}\leqslant\sup\limits_{\ell\geqslant 0}\left(p\hskip 2.84544pt\sup\limits_{n\geqslant 0}n\rho^{n}\right)<\infty.

Lemma
2.2.10
now gives the required
K
-algebra endomorphism
\theta_{u,d}
of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
, which is in fact bijective because
\theta_{u,d}\circ\theta_{u^{-1},d}=\theta_{u^{-1},d}\circ\theta_{u,d}=1_{\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)}
.
∎

Remark 2.4.10
.

It can be shown that the operator norm of the automorphism
\theta_{u,d}
of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
satisfies

||\theta_{u,d}||\leqslant p\left[e\hskip 2.84544pt\log\left(\frac{r}{r(X)}\right)\right]^{-1}.

We omit the proof as we do not need this estimate.

Inspecting the definition of
\theta_{u,d}:\mathcal{D}(X)\to\mathcal{D}(X)
given in the statement of Lemma
2.4.1
shows that it commutes with the restriction maps
\mathcal{D}(X)\to\mathcal{D}(Y)
for any affinoid subdomain
Y
of
X
. Therefore these automorphisms assemble to give an automorphism
\theta_{u,d}:\mathcal{D}_{X}\to\mathcal{D}_{X}
of the sheaf of
K
-algebras
\mathcal{D}_{X}
for any admissible open subspace
X
of
\mathbb{A}
and any
u\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
. If
X
is an admissible open subset of
\mathbb{A}
we define
X(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
to be the
G
-topology obtained by restricting the
G
-topology
\mathbb{A}(d_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
from Definition
2.3.9
to those affinoid open sets contained in
X
.

Corollary 2.4.11
.

Let
X
be an admissible open subspace of
\mathbb{A}
, let
u\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
and suppose that
p\nmid d
. Then for every
r>0
, the restriction of
\theta_{u,d}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{D}
to the
G
-topology
X(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
extends to an automorphism
\theta_{u,d}:\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}\to\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}
of sheaves of
K
-algebras on
X(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Theorem
2.4.9
.
∎

For future use, we record the fact that the automorphisms
\theta_{u,d}
are
inner
whenever
u
happens to be a power of
d

in
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
. More precisely, we have the following

Lemma 2.4.12
.

Let
r>0
, let
X
be an
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
, let
v\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
and suppose that
p\nmid d
. Then

\theta_{z^{d},d}(Q)=z\hskip 2.84544ptQ\hskip 2.84544ptz^{-1}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad Q\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X).

Proof.

Conjugation by
z
is a bounded
K
-linear automorphism of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
, which agrees with
\theta_{z^{d},d}
on
\mathcal{D}(X)
by Lemma
2.4.2
. Also,
\theta_{z^{d},d}
is bounded and
K
-linear on
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
by Theorem
2.4.9
. The result now follows since
\mathcal{D}(X)
is dense in
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
.∎

2.5.
Affine transformations of
\mathbb{A}

In this section we consider how the action of the group of affine transformations of
\mathbb{A}
relates to various constructions we have made. Let
\mathbb{B}:=\{g\in\mathbb{GL}_{2}:g_{21}=0\}
be the subgroup scheme of upper-triangular matrices in
\mathbb{GL}_{2}
.

Definition 2.5.1
.

Let
\varrho\colon\mathbb{B}(K)\to K^{\times}
be the character given by

\varrho\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&d\end{pmatrix}\right)\mapsto\frac{a}{d}.

For the necessary background on equivariant structures and equivariant sheaves, we refer the reader to
[
9
, §2.3]
.

Lemma 2.5.2
.

(1)

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
has a natural
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
-equivariant structure as a sheaf of
K
-algebras given by
(g\cdot f)(z)=f(g^{-1}z)
.

(2)

The action of
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
preserves affinoid subdomains.

(3)

If
X
is an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
G\leqslant\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
stabilises
X
then there is a natural group homomorphism
\rho\colon G\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))^{\times}
.

(4)

The
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
-equivariant structure on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
extends to an
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
-equivariant structure as a sheaf of
K
-algebras on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
via

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\cdot\partial_{x}=\frac{(-cx+a)^{2}}{ad-bc}\partial_{x}.

(5)

The
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
-equivariant structure on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
restricts to a
\mathbb{B}(K)
-equivariant structure on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{A}}
which satisfies

g\cdot\partial_{x}=\varrho(g)\partial_{x}\quad\mbox{and}\quad g\cdot(x-z)=\varrho(g)^{-1}(x-g\cdot z)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad z\in\overline{K}.

Proof.

(a) The affine algebraic group
\mathbb{GL}_{2}
acts on the scheme
\mathbb{P}^{1}
by Möbius transformations. By
[
9
, Theorem 6.3.4]
, the action of
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
on the rigid analytic variety
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is continuous. In the proof of
[
9
, Lemma 3.4.3]
it is explained how the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}
and the sheaf of finite-order differential operators
\mathcal{D}_{X}
on a smooth rigid analytic variety
X
equipped with a continuous action of a
p
-adic Lie group
G
can be endowed in a standard way with natural
G
-equivariant structures. These constructions do not require
G
to be a
p
-adic Lie group, so they can also be applied in our setting to the continuous
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
-action on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

(b) For any affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, each
g\in\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
induces an isomorphism of rigid analytic
K
-varieties
(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\to(g(X),\mathcal{O}_{g(X)})
. Thus
g(X)
is an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

(c) is an immediate consequence of (a) together with
[
17
, Theorem 6.1.3/1]
.

(d) Using
[
9
, Example 2.1.4 and Corollary 2.1.9]
we see that for all
g\in\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
stabilising
X
, the
g
-action on
\mathcal{T}(X)
is given by

(12)

\displaystyle(g\cdot\partial)(f)=g\cdot\partial(g^{-1}\cdot f)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\partial\in\mathcal{T}(X),f\in\mathcal{O}(X).

This implies that
\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\cdot\partial_{x}\right)(x)=\frac{(-cx+a)^{2}}{ad-bc}
, and (d) follows.

(e) The first statement is now immediate given the formula in part (d). For the second one, we write
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&d\end{pmatrix}
and compute

g\cdot(x-z)=\frac{dx-b}{a}-z=\frac{d}{a}\left(x-\left(\frac{az+b}{d}\right)\right)=\varrho(g)^{-1}(x-g\cdot z).\qed

Proposition 2.5.3
.

Let
X
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
and let
g\in\mathbb{B}(K)
. Then

r(g(X))=\frac{r(X)}{|\varrho(g)|}.

Proof.

We note that the isomorphism of Banach algebras
g^{\mathcal{O}}(X)\colon\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(gX)
is even an isometry since for every
f\in\mathcal{O}(X)
we have

|g\cdot f|_{gX}=\sup_{x\in X}f(g^{-1}gx)=|f|_{X}.

Using Lemma
2.5.2
(e), we see that for any
f\in\mathcal{O}(gX)
and
n\geqslant 0
, we have

|\partial_{x}^{n}(f)|_{g(X)}=|g^{-1}\cdot\left(\partial_{x}^{n}(f)\right)|_{X}=|\varrho(g)|^{-n}|\partial_{x}^{n}(g^{-1}\cdot f)|_{X}.

Hence
r(g(X))=|\partial_{x}|_{\sp,\mathcal{O}(g(X))}=\frac{1}{|\varrho(g)|}|\partial_{x}|_{\sp,\mathcal{O}(X)}=\frac{r(X)}{|\varrho(g)|}
as required.
∎

In view of Definitions
2.3.1
(b) and
2.3.9
(a), Proposition
2.5.3
and Lemma
2.5.2
(b) together imply the following

Corollary 2.5.4
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Then every
g\in\mathbb{B}(K)
induces homeomorphisms

g:\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{A}(|\varrho(g)|\partial_{x}/r)\quad\mbox{and}\quad g:\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{A}(|\varrho(g)|\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}.

Lemma 2.5.5
.

Let
g\in\mathbb{B}(K)
and let
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

(1)

There is an equivalence of categories

g_{\ast}\colon\PreSh(\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\PreSh(\mathbb{A}(|\varrho(g)|\partial_{x}/r))

given by
(g_{\ast}\mathcal{F})(X)=\mathcal{F}(g^{-1}X)
for all
\mathcal{F}\in\PreSh(\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r))
.

(2)

g^{\mathcal{D}}
induces an isomorphism of presheaves of
K
-Banach algebras on
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)

g_{r}\colon\mathcal{D}_{r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}g^{\ast}\mathcal{D}_{\frac{r}{\varrho(g)}}.

Proof.

(a) This follows immediately from Corollary
2.5.4
.

(b) Let
X\in\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
. We will apply Lemma
2.2.10
with the following parameters:
A=\mathcal{O}(X)
,
B=\mathcal{D}_{\frac{r}{\varrho(g)}}(gX)
,
\delta=\partial_{x}\in\mathcal{B}(A)
,
f:A\to B
is the composition of
g^{\mathcal{O}}(X):\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(gX)
and the inclusion
\mathcal{O}(gX)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\frac{r}{\varrho(g)}}(gX)
, and
b:=\varrho(g)\partial\in B
. First, we must verify conditions (b)(i) and (b)(ii) of this Lemma.

(i) Let
a\in\mathcal{O}(X)
. Using Lemma
2.5.2
(e) and (
12
), we compute in
B
as follows:

[b,f(a)]=[\varrho(g)\partial,g\cdot a]=\varrho(g)\partial_{x}(g\cdot a)=(g\cdot\partial_{x})(g\cdot a)=g\cdot(\partial_{x}\cdot a)=f(\delta(a)).

(ii) Let
\ell\geqslant 0
; then
|\partial^{\ell}|=(r/|\varrho(g)|)^{\ell}
in
B=\mathcal{D}_{\frac{r}{\varrho(g)}}(gX)
by Definition
2.2.8
. Hence

\sup\limits_{\ell\geqslant 0}|b^{\ell}|/r^{\ell}=\sup\limits_{\ell\geqslant 0}|\varrho(g)|^{\ell}|\partial^{\ell}|/r^{\ell}=1.

Hence by Lemma
2.2.10
(b)
\Rightarrow
(a), the map
f:A\to B
extends to a bounded
K
-Banach algebra homomorphism
g_{r}(X):A\langle\partial/r\rangle=\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{\frac{r}{\varrho(g)}}(gX)
. By construction, this map makes the following diagram commutative:

(13)

\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\mathcal{D}}(X)}
\scriptstyle{j_{r}(X)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(gX)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\frac{r}{|\varrho(g)|}}(gX)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{r}(X)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{\frac{r}{|\varrho(g)|}}(gX)}

where the vertical maps come from Lemma
2.3.7
. Since the images of these maps are dense by Lemma
2.3.7
, the fact that
(g^{-1})^{\mathcal{D}}(gX)
is a two-sided inverse to
g^{\mathcal{D}}(X)
implies that
(g^{-1})_{\frac{r}{\varrho(g)}}(gX)
is a two-sided inverse for
g_{r}(X)
. By construction, the maps
g_{r}
commute with restriction maps in
\mathcal{D}_{r}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\frac{r}{|\varrho(g)|}}
. Hence
g_{r}
is an isomorphism of sheaves of
K
-Banach algebras as claimed.
∎

Corollary 2.5.6
.

Let
g\in\mathbb{B}(K)
and let
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

(1)

There is an equivalence of categories

g_{\ast}\colon\mathrm{PreSh}(\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger})\to\mathrm{PreSh}(\mathbb{A}(|\varrho(g)|\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger})

given by
(g_{\ast}\mathcal{F})(X)=\mathcal{F}(g^{-1}X)
for all
\mathcal{F}\in\PreSh(\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r))
.

(2)

g^{\mathcal{D}}
induces an isomorphism of sheaves of
K
-algebras on
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}

g^{\dagger}_{r}\colon\mathcal{D}_{r}^{\dagger}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}g^{\ast}\mathcal{D}_{\frac{r}{\varrho(g)}}^{\dagger}.

Proof.

(a) follows from Proposition
2.5.3
. (b) follows from (a), Definition
2.3.9
and Lemma
2.5.5
(b), noting that
\mathcal{D}_{r}^{\dagger}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\frac{r}{\varrho(g)}}^{\dagger}
are in fact sheaves on
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
and
\mathbb{A}(\varrho(g)\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
, respectively, by Theorem
2.3.10
.
∎

Finally, we record a purely algebraic calculation which tells us how the maps
g^{\mathcal{D}}:\mathcal{D}\to g^{\ast}\mathcal{D}
defining the
G
-equivariant structure on
\mathcal{D}
interact with the twisting automorphisms
\theta_{u,d}
from
\lx@sectionsign\ref{DivTwDer}
.

Lemma 2.5.7
.

Let
g\in\mathbb{B}(K)
and let
W
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
. For each
u\in\mathcal{O}(W)^{\times}
and
d\geqslant 1
, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{u,d}}
\scriptstyle{g^{\mathcal{D}}(W)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\mathcal{D}}(W)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(gW)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{g\cdot u,d}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}(gW).}

Proof.

Because
\mathcal{D}(W)
is generated as a ring by
\mathcal{O}(W)
and
\partial_{x}
, it is enough to check that
g\cdot\theta_{u,d}(f)=\theta_{g\cdot u,d}(g\cdot f)
when
f=\partial_{x}
and when
f\in\mathcal{O}(W)
. Now

\begin{array}[]{lllll}g\cdot\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x}))&=&g\cdot(\partial_{x}-\frac{1}{d}\frac{\partial_{x}(u)}{u})&=&g\cdot\partial_{x}-\frac{1}{d}\frac{g\cdot\partial_{x}(u)}{g\cdot u}=\\
&=&g\cdot\partial_{x}-\frac{1}{d}\frac{(g\cdot\partial_{x})(g\cdot u)}{g\cdot u}&=&\theta_{g\cdot u,d}(g\cdot\partial_{x}),\end{array}

which gives the first equality, and the second one holds because the twisting automorphisms
\theta_{u,d}
and
\theta_{g\cdot u,d}
are
\mathcal{O}
-linear.
∎

3.
Noetherianity of
\mathcal{D}_{r}
and flatness of connecting maps

In this section we will establish a different interpretation of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
, based on work of Berthelot
[
13
]
, when
X
is a
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoid subdomain of the rigid-analytic affine line
\mathbb{A}
and use it to prove some basic structural facts about these Banach algebras and the relationships between them. In particular we will prove that they are Noetherian whenever
r\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
and
X\in\mathbb{D}(\partial_{x}/r)
, and that
\mathcal{D}_{s}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)
is flat on both sides whenever
s\geqslant r
with
r,s\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
and
Y\subseteq X\in\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
.

3.1.
Sections of
\mathcal{D}_{r}
as divided power algebras

We will discuss a construction essentially due to Berthelot involving level
m
divided powers of
\partial_{x}
.

Throughout
\lx@sectionsign\ref{FlatnessChapter}
, we fix the non-negative integer
m
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

We recall some notation from
[
13
, §1.1.2]
.

For
k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}\geqslant 0
with
k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k
:

(1)

q_{k}:=\lfloor k/p^{m}\rfloor
so that
k=q_{k}p^{m}+r_{k}
with
0\leqslant r_{k}<p^{m}
;

(2)

\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{k'}:=\frac{q_{k}!}{q_{k^{\prime}}!q_{k^{\prime\prime}}!}\in\mathbb{N}
;

(3)

\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{k'}:=\binom{k}{k^{\prime}}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{k'}^{-1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
; and given a formal variable
\partial
,

(4)

\partial^{\langle k\rangle}:=q_{k}!\partial^{[k]}=\frac{q_{k}!}{k!}\partial^{k}
.

It is understood that all of these quantities depend on the parameter
m
, so it would be more correct to write
\langle k\rangle_{m}
instead of
\langle k\rangle
everywhere. However, following Berthelot, we suppress the parameter
m
from this notation.

Now we suppose that
X
is an affinoid variety and that
\partial
is a derivation of
\mathcal{O}(X)
.

Lemma 3.1.2
.

The following relations hold in
\mathcal{D}(X)
:

(1)

for all
k,k^{\prime}\geqslant 0
, we have

\partial^{\langle k\rangle}\partial^{\langle k^{\prime}\rangle}=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k+k'}{k}\partial^{\langle k+k^{\prime}\rangle};

(2)

for all
k\geqslant 0
and
f\in\mathcal{O}(X)
, we have

\partial^{\langle k\rangle}f=\sum_{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{k'}\partial^{\langle k^{\prime}\rangle}(f)\partial^{\langle k^{\prime\prime}\rangle}.

Proof.

This follows from
[
25
, p27]
together with an easy computation using Notation
3.1.1
. ∎

Definition 3.1.3
.

We define
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial]^{(m)}
to be the
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{D}(X)
generated by the set
\left\{\partial^{\langle k\rangle}|k\geqslant 0\right\}
.

We note that in particular
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial]^{(0)}=\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial]
.

Proposition 3.1.4
.

(1)

If
\sup_{k\geqslant 1}||\partial^{\langle k\rangle}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}\leqslant 1
, then
\{\partial^{\langle k\rangle}\mid k\geqslant 0\}
is a free generating set for
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial]^{(m)}
as a left
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-module; in particular
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial]^{(m)}
is
p
-adically separated flat
K^{\circ}
-algebra.

(2)

If there is
\pi\in K^{\circ\circ}
with
|\pi|\geqslant\max(|p|,\sup\limits_{k\geqslant 1}||\partial^{\langle k\rangle}||)
, then
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial]^{(m)}/(\pi)
is a commutative
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi)
-algebra of finite presentation.

Proof.

(a) That the set
S=\{\partial^{\langle k\rangle}\mid k\geqslant 0\}
is
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-linearly independent in the left module
\mathcal{D}(X)
follows from its
\mathcal{O}(X)
-linear independence. It thus suffices to show that the (free left)
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-submodule of
\mathcal{D}(X)
generated by
S
is a subring of
\mathcal{D}(X)
. This follows from Lemma
3.1.2
and the assumption
\partial^{\langle k\rangle}(\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ})\subset\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
for all
k\geqslant 0
.

(b) Now suppose that
\pi
is as in the statement. As in
[
13
, Corollaire 2.2.5]
we see that if
k=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}c_{j}p^{j}+cp^{m}
with
0\leqslant c_{j}<p
for all
j
then

(14)

\partial^{\langle k\rangle}=u\left(\prod\limits_{j=0}^{m-1}(\partial^{\langle p^{j}\rangle})^{c_{j}}\right)(\partial^{\langle p^{m}\rangle})^{c}

for some
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. Thus
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial]^{(m)}/(\pi)
is generated over
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi)
by the images of
\partial^{\langle p^{j}\rangle}
for
0\leqslant j\leqslant m
. Moreover these generators commute with
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi)
by Lemma
3.1.2
(b) together with the assumption on
\pi
and they obviously commute with each other.

Now for any
0\leqslant j<m
we have

(\partial^{\langle p^{j}\rangle})^{p}=\frac{p^{j+1}!}{(p^{j}!)^{p}}\partial^{\langle p^{j+1}\rangle}\in p\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial]^{(m)}\subseteq\pi\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial]^{(m)}.

Using this together with part (a) and equation (
14
), we conclude that

\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial]^{(m)}/(\pi)\cong\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi)[t_{0},\ldots,t_{m}]/(t_{0}^{p},\ldots,t_{m-1}^{p}).

Hence
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial]^{(m)}/(\pi)
is finitely presented over
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi)
, as required.
∎

Definition 3.1.5
.

Suppose that
X
is an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
. We write

\mathcal{D}_{s}^{(m)}(X):=\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial_{x}/s]^{(m)}

for any non-zero
s\in K
.

This algebra is only well-behaved under specific restrictions on the parameter
s
.

Corollary 3.1.6
.

Suppose that
X
is an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
that is split over
K
. Let
s\in K^{\times}
satisfy
|s|>1/\rho(X)
.

(1)

\mathcal{D}_{s}^{(m)}(X)
is a free left
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-module on
\{(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle k\rangle}\mid k\geqslant 0\}
; in particular
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{(m)}(X)
is
p
-adically separated flat
K^{\circ}
-algebra.

(2)

Suppose further that there exists
\pi\in K^{\times}
such that

\max\left\{|p|,\frac{1}{s\rho(X)}\right\}\leqslant|\pi|<1.

Then
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{(m)}(X)/(\pi)
is a finitely presented commutative
K^{\circ}/(\pi)
-algebra.

Proof.

(a) By Lemma
2.3.15
, for all
k\geqslant 1
we have

||(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle k\rangle}||_{X}\leqslant\frac{||\partial_{x}^{[k]}||_{X}}{|s|^{k}}\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{|s|\rho(X)}\right)^{k}\leqslant\frac{1}{|s|\rho(X)}<1.

Now we can apply Proposition
3.1.4
(a).

(b) The above inequality shows that
|\pi|\geqslant\frac{1}{|s|\rho(X)}\geqslant\sup\limits_{k\geqslant 1}||(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle k\rangle}||_{X}
. Hence by Proposition
3.1.4
(b),
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial]^{(m)}/(\pi)
is a commutative
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi)
-algebra of finite presentation. It remains to prove that
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi)
is a finitely presented
K^{\circ}/(\pi)
-algebra.

Now,
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
is a topologically finitely generated
K^{\circ}
-algebra by
[
34
, 2.4.8(a)]
. Thus
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
is a topologically finitely presented
K^{\circ}
-algebra by
[
18
, Proposition 1.1(c)]
and so
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi)
is a finitely presented
K^{\circ}/(\pi)
-algebra as required.
∎

Definition 3.1.7
.

When
X
and
s
satisfy the hypotheses of Corollary
3.1.6
we let
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{s}^{(m)}(X)
denote the
p
-adic completion of
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{(m)}(X)
and

\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{s}^{(m)}(X)_{K}=K\otimes_{K^{\circ}}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{s}^{(m)}(X).

It follows from the presentation of
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{(m)}(X)
as a free
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-module in Proposition
3.1.4
that elements of
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{s}^{(m)}(X)_{K}
can be written uniquely as convergent sums

\sum_{k\geqslant 0}f_{k}(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle k\rangle}\mbox{ with }f_{k}\in\mathcal{O}(X)\mbox{ and }|f_{k}|_{X}\to 0\mbox{ as }k\to\infty.

We will view
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{s}^{(m)}(X)_{K}
as a
K
-Banach algebra with unit ball
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{s}^{(m)}(X)
, so that the defining Banach norm on
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{s}^{(m)}(X)_{K}
is given by

\left|\sum_{k\geqslant 0}f_{k}(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle k\rangle}\right|=\sup_{k\geqslant 0}|f_{k}|_{X}.

Notation 3.1.8
.

Let
\varpi_{m}:=(p^{m})!^{\frac{1}{p^{m}}}\in\overline{K}
so that
|\varpi_{m}|=p^{-\frac{p^{m}-1}{p^{m}(p-1)}}>\varpi
.

Theorem 3.1.9
.

Let
X
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
that is split over
K
. Let
s\in K^{\times}
be such that
|s|>1/\rho(X)
. There is an isomorphism of
K
-Banach algebras

\mathcal{D}_{|\varpi_{m}s|}(X)\cong\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{s}^{(m)}(X)_{K}.

Before we prove Theorem
3.1.9
we need another
p
-adic binomial estimate.

Lemma 3.1.10
.

For all
k\geqslant 0
,
1\leqslant\frac{|k!|}{|q_{k}!||\varpi_{m}|^{k}}\leqslant p^{m}
.

Proof.

Recalling from Notation
3.1.1
(a) that
k=q_{k}p^{m}+r_{k}
with
0\leqslant r_{k}<p^{m}
, and that
s_{p}(n)
denotes the sum of the
p
-adic digits of
n
, we compute directly

\displaystyle(p-1)\log_{p}\left(\frac{|k!|}{|q_{k}!||\varpi_{m}|^{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(q_{k}-s_{p}(q_{k}))-(k-s_{p}(k))+\frac{k(p^{m}-1)}{p^{m}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s_{p}(r_{k})-\frac{r_{k}}{p^{m}}\leqslant(p-1)m

since
0\leqslant r_{k}<p^{m}
. Since also
s_{p}(r_{k})-\frac{r_{k}}{p^{m}}\geqslant 0
the result follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1.9
.

Let
r=|\varpi_{m}s|
so that
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)=\mathcal{O}(X)\langle\partial/r\rangle
. Since
B:=\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{s}^{(m)}(X)_{K}
is a
K
-Banach algebra we may use Lemma
2.2.10
to construct a
K
-Banach algebra homomorphism

\phi\colon\mathcal{O}(X)\langle\partial/r\rangle\to B:

the inclusion
\iota\colon\mathcal{O}(X)\to B
is a
K
-Banach algebra homomorphism and
\partial_{x}f-f\partial_{x}=\partial_{x}(f)
, so there is a unique way to define
\phi
that extends
\iota
and sends
\partial
to
\partial_{x}
provided
\sup_{\ell\geqslant 0}\left(|\partial_{x}^{\ell}|_{B}/r^{\ell}\right)<\infty
. But

\left|\partial_{x}^{\ell}\right|_{B}=\left|\frac{\ell!}{q_{\ell}!}s^{\ell}\partial^{\langle\ell\rangle}\right|_{B}=\left|\frac{\ell!}{q_{\ell}!\varpi_{m}^{\ell}}\right|r^{\ell}\leqslant p^{m}r^{\ell}

by Lemma
3.1.10
. Now if
f_{0},f_{1},\ldots\in\mathcal{O}(X)
with
|f_{k}|r^{k}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, then

\phi\left(\sum\limits_{n\geqslant 0}f_{k}\partial^{k}\right)=\sum\limits_{k\geqslant 0}f_{k}\frac{k!}{q_{k}!}s^{k}\partial^{\langle k\rangle}.

Therefore, because

\left|\sum\limits_{k\geqslant 0}f_{k}\frac{k!}{q_{k}!}s^{k}\partial^{\langle k\rangle}\right|_{B}=\sup\limits_{k\geqslant 0}\left(|f_{k}|r^{k}\left|\frac{k!}{q_{k}\varpi_{m}^{k}}\right|\right),

we see, using Lemma
3.1.10
again, that
\phi
is a bijection with continuous inverse. Therefore it is an isomorphism of
K
-Banach algebras.
∎

Corollary 3.1.11
.

Suppose that
X
is an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
and
r\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
with
r>r(X)
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
is Noetherian.

Proof.

Using
[
8
, Theorem 4.1.8]
, choose a finite extension
K^{\prime}
of
K
such that
X
is split over
K^{\prime}
. Then Lemma
2.3.13
and Corollary
2.3.17
tell us that

r>r(X)=r(X_{K^{\prime}})=\varpi/\rho(X_{K^{\prime}}).

Since
(|\varpi_{m}|)_{m=0}^{\infty}
is a decreasing sequence converging to
\varpi
from above, there is some
m\geqslant 0
such that
r>|\varpi_{m}|/\rho(X_{K^{\prime}})
. By enlarging
K^{\prime}
if necessary we may also assume that
r/|\varpi_{m}|=|s|
for some
s\in K^{\prime\times}
.

Now by Theorem
3.1.9
,
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X_{K^{\prime}})\cong\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{s}^{(m)}(X_{K^{\prime}})_{K^{\prime}}
and the latter is Noetherian by Corollary
3.1.6
and
[
9
, Theorem 4.1.5]
, so the former is too.

Lemma
2.3.11
(a) now gives an isomorphism of
K^{\prime}
-Banach algebras

\mathcal{D}_{r}(X_{K^{\prime}})\cong K^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{r}(X).

Since
K^{\prime}
is faithfully flat over
K
, we deduce that
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
is also Noetherian.∎

3.2.
Discussion of flatness

Our main goal for the remainder of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{FlatnessChapter}
is to prove the following theorem about the rings introduced at Definition
2.3.9
(d).

Theorem 3.2.1
.

If
s\geqslant r>0
and
Y\subseteq X
both lie in
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
, then
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}(Y)
is a flat
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{s}(X)
-module on both sides.

In this section will will perform some reductions and record some technical results from
[
9
]
that we will use to prove Theorem
3.2.1
. First, we recall some very general results about flatness and direct limits.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Let
U
be a ring and suppose
M_{i}
is a directed system of flat
U
-modules. Then
\colim M_{i}
is a flat
U
-module.

Proof.

This is
[
28
, Proposition 5.4.6]
.
∎

Lemma 3.2.3
.

Let
U
be a
K
-algebra,
M
a
U
-module and
K^{\prime}
a field extension of
K
. If
M_{K^{\prime}}:=K^{\prime}\otimes M
is flat over
U_{K^{\prime}}:=K^{\prime}\otimes U
then
M
is flat over
U
.

Proof.

We assume that
M
is a left module but the same argument works for right modules. Let
N
be any right
U
-module and write
N_{K^{\prime}}=K^{\prime}\otimes N
. For
i>0
,

K^{\prime}\otimes\Tor_{i}^{U}(N,M)\cong\Tor_{i}^{U_{K^{\prime}}}(N_{K^{\prime}},M_{K^{\prime}})=0

since
M_{K^{\prime}}
is flat over
U_{K^{\prime}}
. As
K^{\prime}
is faithfully flat over
K
it follows that
\Tor_{i}^{U}(N,M)=0
for all
i>0
and so
M
is flat as claimed. ∎

The following lemma can be found for commutative rings at
[
45
, 05UU]
with essentially the same proof.

Lemma 3.2.4
.

Let
R_{i}
be a directed system of rings and
R=\colim R_{i}
. Suppose that
M
is an
R
-module that is flat over
R_{i}
for each
i
. Then
M
is flat as an
R
-module.

Proof.

Again we assume that
M
is a left module but an identical argument works for right modules.

Let
I
be a finitely generated right ideal of
R
. By
[
28
, Proposition 5.4.11]
it suffices to show that
I\otimes_{R}M\to M
is an injection. Since
I
is finitely generated we can find an index
i
such that a set of its generators lies in the image of
R_{i}\to R
. That is we may choose a finitely generated right ideal
I_{i}
of
R_{i}
such that
I=I_{i}R
(suitably interpreted). Then
I=\colim_{j\geqslant i}I_{i}R_{j}
and
I\otimes_{R}M\to M
is the colimit of the maps
I_{i}R_{j}\otimes_{R_{j}}M\to M
over all
j\geqslant i
. Since
M
is flat over each
R_{j}
the maps in this directed family are all injective by
[
28
, Proposition 5.4.11]
again. Since colimits commute with tensor products and colimits over directed sets are exact we’re done. ∎

We now reduce Theorem
3.2.1
to proving the flatness of the restriction maps in the sheaves
\mathcal{D}_{r}
constructed in Proposition
2.3.6
(a).

Proposition 3.2.5
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)
is a flat
\mathcal{D}_{s}(X)
-module on both sides whenever
s,r\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
with
s\geqslant r
and
Y\subseteq X
both lie in
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
. Then Theorem
3.2.1
holds.

Proof.

Suppose that whenever
s^{\prime},r^{\prime}\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
with
s^{\prime}\geqslant r^{\prime}
and
Y\subseteq X
both lie
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r^{\prime})
then
\mathcal{D}_{s^{\prime}}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r^{\prime}}(Y)
is flat on both sides.

To prove Theorem
3.2.1
, pick
s\geqslant r>0
,
Y\subseteq X
in
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\dagger}
. Then for every
r^{\prime}>r
,
X,Y\in\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r^{\prime})
. Now Lemma
3.2.2
gives that
\mathcal{D}_{s^{\prime}}(X)\to\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}(Y)
is flat on both sides whenever
s^{\prime}\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
and
s^{\prime}>r
, since
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}(Y)=\colim_{r^{\prime}>r}\mathcal{D}_{r^{\prime}}(Y)
and we may view the colimit as only running over
r^{\prime}\in(r,s^{\prime})\cap\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
. Then Lemma
3.2.4
gives that
\mathcal{D}_{s}^{\dagger}(X)\to\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r}(Y)
is flat on both sides, as required, since
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{s}(X)=\colim_{s^{\prime}>s}\mathcal{D}_{s^{\prime}}(X)
and again we may view the colimit as only running over
s^{\prime}\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
. ∎

Finally, we recall a couple of results from
[
9
, §4.1]
that will help us prove the flatness of the maps between the Banach algebras that appear in Proposition
3.2.5
. For both, we suppose that
\pi\in K^{\circ\circ}
such that:

•

\mathcal{U}
is a
\pi
-adically complete and separated and flat
K^{\circ}
-algebra,

•

\mathcal{U}/\pi\mathcal{U}
is a commutative
K^{\circ}/\pi K^{\circ}
-algebra of finite presentation,

•

U:=K\otimes_{K^{\circ}}\mathcal{U}
.

Proposition 3.2.6

(Proposition 4.1.7 of
[
9
]
)
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
is another
\pi
-adically complete, separated and flat
K^{\circ}
-algebra which contains
\mathcal{U}
. Let
y\in\mathcal{V}
, and suppose that the map
\mathcal{U}/\pi\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{V}/\pi\mathcal{V}
extends to an
K^{\circ}/\pi K^{\circ}
-algebra isomorphism
(\mathcal{U}/\pi\mathcal{U})[Y]\cong\mathcal{V}/\pi\mathcal{V}
which sends
Y
to
y+\pi\mathcal{V}
. Let
C
be the centraliser of
y
in
U
. Then

(1)

V:=K\otimes_{K^{\circ}}\mathcal{V}
is a flat
U
-module on both sides, and

(2)

for every finitely generated
U
-module
M
there is a natural isomorphism of
C\langle Y\rangle
-modules
\eta_{M}\colon M\langle Y\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}V\otimes_{U}M
.

Theorem 3.2.7

(Theorem 4.1.8 of
[
9
]
)
.

Suppose that
y\in U
is such that
[y,\mathcal{U}]\subseteq\pi\mathcal{U}
, that
\mathcal{V}
is the
\pi
-adic completion of
\mathcal{U}[Y;\ad(y)]
and
V=K\otimes_{K^{\circ}}\mathcal{V}
. Then
V/(Y-y)V
is a flat
U
-module on both sides.

3.3.
Flatness of divided power algebras under change of base

Our goal for this section is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 3.3.1
.

If
r\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
and
Y\subseteq X
both lie in
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
, then
\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)
is a flat
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
-module on both sides.

Let
s\in K^{\times}
and consider the following two affinoid subdomains of
X
:

X_{1}:=X\left(\frac{x}{s}\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad X_{2}:=X\left(\frac{s}{x}\right).

Thus, we might have something like the following picture.
X
X_{1}
X_{2}

Define
\rho:=\min(\rho(X),|s|)
and note that
\rho\leqslant\min\rho(X_{1}),\rho(X_{2})
. Recall from Notation
3.1.8
that
\varpi_{m}=(p^{m})!^{\frac{1}{p^{m}}}
. We will first deal with the special case where the following Hypothesis holds; we will see in the proof of Theorem
3.3.1
that the general case can be reduced to this one.

Hypothesis 3.3.2
.

•

r\in|K^{\times}|
;

•

X\in\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
is split over
K
;

•

Y
is non-empty, and
Y=X_{1}=X(\frac{x}{s})
or
Y=X_{2}=X(\frac{s}{x})
for some
s\in K^{\times}
,

•

there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\varpi_{m}\in K^{\times}
and
|\varpi_{m}|/r<\rho
.

We will assume that Hypothesis
3.3.2
holds throughout
\lx@sectionsign\ref{flatbasesec}
. The above conditions imply that there exists an element
t\in K^{\times}
such that
|t|=r/|\varpi_{m}|
, which we fix from now on. Then since
|t|>1/\rho(X)
and
|t|>1/\rho(X_{i})
for
i=1,2
, Theorem
3.1.9
gives us isomorphisms

\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\cong\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{(m)}(X)_{K}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{D}_{r}(X_{i})\cong\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{(m)}(X_{i})_{K}\quad\mbox{for}\quad i=1,2.

Thus proving the flatness of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(X_{i})
for
i=1,2
amounts to proving the flatness of
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{(m)}(X)_{K}\to\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{t}^{(m)}(X_{i})_{K}
.

Let
Z:=X\times\mathbb{D}
with a coordinate
y
on
\mathbb{D}
. Then
\mathcal{O}(Z)=\mathcal{O}(X)\langle y\rangle
, and the projection map
Z\to X
induces
K
-Banach algebra isomorphisms
\mathcal{O}(X_{1})\cong\mathcal{O}(Z)/(sy-x)
and
\mathcal{O}(X_{2})\cong\mathcal{O}(Z)/(xy-s)
via maps
p_{i}\colon\mathcal{O}(Z)\to\mathcal{O}(X_{i})
for
i=1,2
.

Let
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
be the bounded
K
-linear derivations of
\mathcal{O}(Z)
that extend
\partial_{x}
on
\mathcal{O}(X)
and which satisfy
\delta_{1}(y)=1/s
and
\delta_{2}(y)=-y^{2}/s
. Thus
\delta_{1}(sy-x)=0
and
\delta_{2}(xy-s)=-(xy-s)y/s
, so

\mathcal{D}(X_{1})\cong\mathcal{O}(Z)[\delta_{1}]/(sy-x)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathcal{D}(X_{2})\cong\mathcal{O}(Z)[\delta_{2}]/(xy-s).

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Let
i=1
or
i=2
. Then for all
n\geqslant 0
we have

(15)

||\delta_{i}^{\langle n\rangle}||_{Z}\leqslant\rho^{-n}.

Proof.

Since
\delta_{i}^{\langle n\rangle}=q_{n}!\delta_{i}^{[n]}
with
q_{n}!\in\mathbb{N}
, it suffices to show that
||\delta_{i}^{[n]}||_{Z}\leqslant\rho^{-n}
. We compute that for all
k,m\geqslant 0
, we have

\delta_{1}^{[k]}(y^{m})=\binom{m}{k}y^{m-k}/s^{k}

and

\delta_{2}^{[k]}(y^{m})=\binom{m+k-1}{k}y^{m+k}/(-s)^{k}.

This means that
|\delta_{i}^{[k]}(y^{m})|_{Z}\leqslant 1/|s|^{k}
for all
k,m\geqslant 0
. Let
f\in\mathcal{O}(X)
and
m,n\geqslant 0
. Using Lemma
2.3.15
and the fact that
\rho=\min(\rho(X),|s|)
, we have

\left|\delta_{i}^{[n]}(fy^{m})\right|_{Z}=\left|\sum_{j+k=n}\partial_{x}^{[j]}(f)\delta_{i}^{[k]}(y^{m})\right|_{Z}\leqslant\sup_{j+k=n}\frac{|f|_{X}}{\rho(X)^{j}}\cdot\frac{1}{|s|^{k}}\leqslant\frac{|f|_{X}}{\rho^{n}}.

Hence
||\delta_{i}^{[n]}||_{Z}\leqslant\rho^{-n}
for all
n\geqslant 0
as required. ∎

The following Lemma will enable us to perform the construction of Definition
2.1.7
with
A=\mathcal{D}_{t}^{(m)}(X)
and
\delta=\ad(x/s)
.

Lemma 3.3.4
.

\ad(\frac{x}{s})\colon\mathcal{D}_{t}^{(m)}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{t}^{(m)}(X)_{K}
is locally nilpotent and has image contained in
\frac{1}{ts}\mathcal{D}_{t}^{[m]}(X)
.

Proof.

Suppose
k\geqslant 1
. Then by Lemma
3.1.2
2
, we have

\displaystyle\left[(\partial_{x}/t)^{\langle k\rangle},\frac{x}{s}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{i}(\partial_{x}/t)^{\langle i\rangle}\left(\frac{x}{s}\right)(\partial/t)^{\langle k-i\rangle}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{ts}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{1}(\partial_{x}/t)^{\langle k-1\rangle}\in\frac{1}{ts}\mathcal{D}_{t}^{(m)}(X).

By Proposition
3.1.4
(a) applied with
\partial=\partial_{x}/t
, we know that
\{(\partial_{x}/t)^{\langle k\rangle}\mid k\geqslant 0\}
is a free generating set for
\mathcal{D}_{t}^{(m)}(X)=\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}[\partial_{x}/t]^{(m)}
as a left
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-module. Now use the fact that
\ad(\frac{x}{s})
is an
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-linear derivation.∎

Notation 3.3.5
.

The following notation will be used in the remainder of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{flatbasesec}
:

•

\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{t}^{(m)}(X)
and
D=\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{K}
;

•

\mathcal{D}_{i}=\mathcal{D}_{t}^{(m)}(X_{i})
and
D_{i}=(\widehat{\mathcal{D}_{i}})_{K}
for
i=1,2
;

•

\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{O}(Z)^{\circ}[\delta_{i}/t]^{(m)}
and
U_{i}=(\widehat{\mathcal{U}_{i}})_{K}
for
i=1,2
;

•

\mathcal{V}_{1}=\mathcal{D}[T;\ad(x/s)]
and
V_{1}=(\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{1}})_{K}
;

•

\mathcal{V}_{2}=\mathcal{D}[T^{-1};\ad(x/s)]
and
V_{2}=(\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{2})}_{K}
.

We will establish that, for
i=1,2
, there is an isomorphism of
K
-Banach algebras
V_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{i}
, and also that
D_{i}\cong V_{i}/(v_{i})
for explicit central elements
v_{i}
of
V_{i}
. Together with Propositions
3.2.6
and
3.2.7
, this will allow us to prove that
D_{i}
is flat over
D
.

Proposition 3.3.6
.

The
K^{\circ}
-algebras
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
,
\mathcal{D}_{2}
,
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
are all
p
-adically separated and flat
K^{\circ}
-algebras. There exists
\pi\in K
with
0<|\pi|<1
, such that their reductions mod
\pi
are all finitely presented commutative algebras over
K^{\circ}/(\pi)
.

Proof.

Since
X
is split over
K
, we know that
\rho(X)\in|K^{\times}|
. Since also
s,t\in K^{\times}
, we can find an element
\pi\in K
such that

(16)

|\pi|=\max\left\{|p|,\frac{1}{\rho|t|}\right\}.

Since
|p|<1
and
\frac{1}{\rho|t|}<1
by Hypothesis
3.3.2
, we see that
0<|\pi|<1
. Now, because
X
,
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are split over
K
and

|t|>\rho^{-1}>\max\{\rho(X)^{-1},\rho(X_{1})^{-1},\rho(X_{2})^{-1}\},

the cases
\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
follow from Corollary
3.1.6
. To deal with
\mathcal{U}_{i}
for
i=1
or
i=2
, we can apply Lemma
3.3.3
to see that

\sup\limits_{n\geqslant 1}||(\delta_{i}/t)^{\langle n\rangle}||\leqslant\sup\limits_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{(\rho|t|)^{n}}\leqslant\frac{1}{|\rho|t}<1.

Now we can apply Proposition
3.1.4
with
X=Z
and
\partial=\delta_{i}/t
.∎

Lemma 3.3.7
.

For
n>0
,
i=1,2
and
\pi
given by Proposition
3.3.6
:

(1)

\mathcal{O}(Z)^{\circ}/(\pi^{n})
is a free
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi^{n})
-module on
\{y^{\ell}+(\pi^{n})\mid\ell\geqslant 0\}
;

(2)

\mathcal{U}_{i}/(\pi^{n})
is a free
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi^{n})
-module on
\{\partial_{i}^{\langle k\rangle}y^{\ell}+(\pi^{n})\mid k,\ell\geqslant 0\}
;

(3)

\mathcal{V}_{1}/(\pi^{n})
is a free
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi^{n})
-module on
\{\partial_{x}^{\langle k\rangle}T^{\ell}+(\pi^{n})\mid k,\ell\geqslant 0\}
;

(4)

\mathcal{V}_{2}/(\pi^{n})
is a free
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi^{n})
-module on
\{\partial_{x}^{\langle k\rangle}T^{\ell}+(\pi^{n})\mid k\geqslant 0,\ell\leqslant 0\}
.

Proof.

(a)
\mathcal{O}(Z)=\mathcal{O}(X)\langle y\rangle
, so
\mathcal{O}(Z)^{\circ}=\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}\langle y\rangle
and the result follows easily.

(b) Since
\partial_{i}^{\langle k\rangle}y^{\ell}\equiv y^{\ell}\partial_{i}^{\langle k\rangle}\mod\pi
this follows from (a) together with Proposition
3.1.4
and
[
32
, I.2.3.17]
.

(c) and (d) follow from Proposition
3.1.4
together with the definitions of
A[T;\delta]
and
A[T^{-1};\delta]
from §
2.1
.
∎

Lemma 3.3.8
.

For each
k\geqslant 0
we have the following equality in
\mathcal{U}_{2}
:

y(\delta_{2}/t)^{\langle k\rangle}=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}(-\ad(x/s))^{n}\left((\delta_{2}/t)^{\langle k\rangle}\right)y^{n+1}.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.3.4
,
\ad(x/s)
acts locally nilpotently on
\mathcal{D}
. Hence the sum on the right hand side of the formula is finite. Since
\mathcal{U}_{2}
is flat over
K^{\circ}
we may invert
p
and then after multiplication by a scalar in
K
reduce to proving that

y\delta_{2}^{k}=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}(-\ad(x/s))^{n}(\delta_{2}^{k})y^{n+1}

holds inside
\mathcal{O}(Z)[\delta_{2}]
. Now we recall that
\ell_{\delta_{2}}=\ad(\delta_{2})+r_{\delta_{2}}
so, using a computation from the proof of Lemma
3.3.3
, we have

\displaystyle y\delta_{2}^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\ell_{\delta_{2}}-\ad(\delta_{2}))^{k}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{k}\binom{k}{n}\delta_{2}^{k-n}(-\delta_{2})^{n}(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{k}\binom{k}{n}\delta_{2}^{k-n}\binom{n}{n}n!y^{n+1}/s^{n}.

On the other hand, we saw in the proof of Lemma
3.3.4
that

(-\ad(x/s))^{n}(\delta_{2}^{k})=1/s^{n}\binom{k}{n}n!\delta_{2}^{k-n}.

The result now follows easily.
∎

Proposition 3.3.9
.

There are
\mathcal{D}(X)
-linear isomorphisms of
K
-Banach algebras
\phi_{i}\colon V_{i}\to U_{i}
with
\phi_{1}(T)=y
and
\phi_{2}(T^{-1})=y
.

Proof.

Since
\delta_{i}|_{\mathcal{O}(X)}=\partial_{x}
, the natural map
\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(Z)
arising from the projection
Z\to X
extends to maps
\mathcal{D}(X)\to\mathcal{D}(Z)
sending
\partial_{x}
to
\delta_{i}
. It is easy to verify that these in turn restrict to ring homomorphisms
g_{i}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{U}_{i}
.

Since
y-x/s
is central in
\mathcal{U}_{1}
it is easy to verify that for each
P\in\mathcal{D}
,

yg_{1}(P)-g_{1}(P)y=(x/s)g_{1}(P)-g_{1}(P)(x/s)=g_{1}(\ad(x/s)(P)).

The universal property of
\mathcal{V}_{1}
given by Proposition
2.1.1
thus provides that
g_{1}
extends to a unique ring homomorphism
h_{1}\colon\mathcal{V}_{1}\to\mathcal{U}_{1}
such that
h_{1}(T)=y
. This in turn induces ring homomorphisms
h_{1,n}\colon\mathcal{V}_{1}/(\pi^{n})\to\mathcal{U}_{1}/(\pi^{n})
that we claim are isomorphisms. To see this is suffices to observe that
h_{1,n}
is
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi^{n})
-linear and sends
\partial_{x}^{\langle k\rangle}T^{\ell}+(\pi^{n})
to
\delta_{1}^{\langle k\rangle}y^{\ell}+(\pi^{n})
; now apply Lemma
3.3.7
(b,c). It follows that
\widehat{h_{1}}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{1}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{U}_{1}}
is an isomorphism and we may take
\phi_{1}=(\widehat{h_{1}})_{K}
.

Similary, since every element of
\mathcal{D}
is an
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-linear combination of the elements
(\partial_{x}/t)^{\langle k\rangle}
by Proposition
3.1.4
(a), and since
\mathcal{O}(Z)
is commutative, it follows from Lemma
3.3.8
that inside
\mathcal{U}_{2}
, for all
P\in\mathcal{D}
we have

yg_{2}(P)=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}g_{2}((-\ad(x/s))^{n}(P))y^{n+1}.

Then the universal property of
\mathcal{V}_{2}
given by Proposition
2.1.8
shows that
g_{2}
extends to a unique ring homomorphism
h_{2}\colon\mathcal{V}_{2}\to\mathcal{U}_{2}
sending
T^{-1}
to
y
. Again this induces ring homomorphisms
h_{2,n}\colon\mathcal{V}_{2}/(\pi^{n})\to\mathcal{U}_{2}/(\pi^{n})
that we claim are isomorphisms. To see this is suffices to observe that each map
h_{2,n}
is
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi^{n})
-linear and sends
\partial^{\langle k\rangle}T^{-\ell}+(\pi^{n})
to
\partial^{\langle k\rangle}y^{\ell}+(\pi^{n})
so we can appeal to Lemma
3.3.7
(b,d) again and then see that
\phi_{2}=(\widehat{h_{2}})_{K}
is an isomorphism as before.∎

Proposition 3.3.10
.

Let
i=1
or
i=2
. There is an exact sequence of
V_{i}
-modules

0\to V_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot v_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}V_{i}\to D_{i}\to 0,

where the
v_{i}
is the central element of
V_{i}
given by

v_{1}=sT-x\quad\mbox{and}\quad v_{2}=xT^{-1}-s.

Proof.

Let
u_{1}=sy-x
and
u_{2}=xy-s
. By Proposition
3.3.9
it suffices to show that the sequences

0\to U_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot u_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{1}\to D_{1}\to 0\quad\mbox{and}\quad 0\to U_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot u_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{2}\to D_{2}\to 0

are exact. We may pick
n\geqslant 0
such that
\pi^{n}u_{1},\pi^{n}u_{2}\in\mathcal{O}(Z)^{\circ}
. Since

0\to\mathcal{O}(Z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot u_{i}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{O}(Z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{i}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{O}(X_{i})\to 0

is exact, it follows that

0\to\mathcal{O}(Z)^{\circ}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot\pi^{n}u_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}(Z)^{\circ}\to\mathcal{O}(X_{i})^{\circ}\to 0

has
\pi
-torsion cohomology. Moreover the Banach Open Mapping Theorem,
[
41
, Proposition 8.6]
tells us that the maps
\cdot u_{i}
and
p_{i}
are open onto their image and so there is some
N>0
such that
\pi^{N}
annnihilates the cohomology groups. We can then use Proposition
3.1.4
to deduce that

0\to\mathcal{U}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot\pi^{n}u_{i}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{U}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}_{i}\to 0

also has cohomology groups annihilated by
\pi^{N}
. Now apply
[
7
, Lemma 3.6]
.
∎

Proposition 3.3.11
.

Suppose that Hypothesis
3.3.2
holds. Then
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are flat as
D
-modules on both sides.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be given by Proposition
3.3.6
. Since
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
is a
\pi
-adically complete and separated
K^{\circ}
-algebra, flat over
K^{\circ}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}/(\pi)
is a commutative
K^{\circ}/(\pi)
-algebra of finite presentation we will apply the results from §
3.2
with
\mathcal{U}:=\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
.

By Proposition
3.3.10
,
D_{1}\cong V_{1}/(sT-x)V_{1}
. By Lemma
3.3.4
,
[\frac{x}{s},\mathcal{D}]\subseteq\frac{1}{ts}\mathcal{D}
. By (
16
) we have
|\pi ts|\geqslant|s|/\rho
. Since
\rho=\min(\rho(X),|s|)\leqslant|s|
, we see that
|\frac{1}{ts}|\leqslant|\pi|
which implies that
\frac{1}{ts}\mathcal{D}\subseteq\pi\mathcal{D}
. We can now apply Theorem
3.2.7
with
y=x/s
to deduce that
D_{1}\cong V_{1}/(T-\frac{x}{s})V_{1}
is a flat
D
-module.

Next, by Proposition
3.3.10
, we have a presentation
D_{2}\cong V_{2}/v_{2}V_{2}
where
v_{2}=xT^{-1}-s\in V_{2}
. By
[
7
, Proposition 4.4]
applied to the ring map
U\to V_{2}
and the element
v_{2}\in V_{2}
, to prove the flatness of
D_{2}
as a
D
-module it suffices to prove that
v_{2}
is a regular element in
V_{2}
,
V_{2}
is flat as a right
U
-module and that
V_{2}\otimes_{D}M
is
v_{2}
-torsion-free for all finitely generated left
D
-modules
M
.

Note that in
\mathcal{V}_{2}
we can use Lemma
2.1.6
to see that for all
P\in\mathcal{D}
we have

\ad(T^{-1})(P)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\ad(x/s)^{n}(P)T^{-n-1}.

We saw above that Lemma
3.3.4
implies that
\ad(x/s)^{n}(\mathcal{D})\subseteq\frac{1}{ts}\mathcal{D}\subseteq\pi\mathcal{D}
for all
n\geqslant 1
. Hence
\ad(T^{-1})(\mathcal{D})\subseteq\pi\mathcal{V}_{2}
, so the hypotheses of Proposition
3.2.6
are satisfied with
\mathcal{U}=\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
,
\mathcal{V}=\widehat{\mathcal{V}_{2}}
and
y=T^{-1}
. The centraliser of
T^{-1}
in
D
certainly contains
\mathcal{O}(X)
. Proposition
3.2.6
now tells us that
V_{2}
is a flat
U
-module on both sides, and that for every finitely generated
D
-module
M
there is a natural isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}(X)\langle T^{-1}\rangle
-modules
M\langle T^{-1}\rangle\cong V_{2}\otimes_{D}M
. We can now apply
[
7
, Lemma 4.1(a)]
with
t=T^{-1}
and
f=x/s
to see that
V_{2}\otimes_{D}M
is
v_{2}
-torsion-free for every finitely generated
D
-module
M
. In particular, setting
M=D
shows that
v_{2}
is a regular element in
V_{2}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.3.1
.

We may certainly assume that
Y\neq\emptyset
as the result is trivial otherwise. By Lemma
3.2.3
and Lemma
2.3.11
(a) we may replace
K
by any finite extension. In particular, by
[
8
, Theorem 4.1.8]
we may assume that
X
and
Y
are both split over
K
and
r\in|K^{\times}|
.

If
Y=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}
is a disjoint union of cheeses then
\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y_{i})
and so by
[
28
, Proposition 5.4.2]
we may further assume that
Y
is a single cheese.

Next we observe that as
r(Y)<r
,
\rho(Y)>\varpi/r
by Corollary
2.3.17
. That is
Y=C_{K}(\alpha,\bf{s})
for some
\alpha,\bf{s}
satisfying the conditions of
[
8
, Definition 4.1.1]
and moreover
|s_{i}|>\varpi/r
for all
i=0,\ldots,g
. Since
Y\subset X
if we define
X_{0}=X(\frac{x-\alpha_{0}}{s_{0}})
and
X_{i}=X_{i-1}(\frac{s_{i}}{x-\alpha_{i}})
for
i=1,\ldots,g
then we see that
X_{g}=Y
. Then, since a composition of flat morphisms is flat, an induction argument reduces us to the cases
Y=X(\frac{x-\alpha}{s})
and
Y=X(\frac{s}{x-\alpha})
where
\alpha\in K
and
s\in K^{\times}
. By a change of coordinate
x\mapsto x-\alpha
, we may assume that
\alpha=0
.

Since
Y
lies in
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
by assumption, applying Corollary
2.3.17
shows that

|s|\geqslant\rho(Y)=\varpi/r(Y)>\varpi/r.

Since
\varpi_{m}/r
is a decreasing sequence converging to
\varpi/r
from above, we can find a sufficiently large
m
such that
\rho(Y)>|\varpi_{m}|/r
. Since
Y=X(x/s)
or
X(s/x)
, it is clear from Definition
2.3.14
that
\rho(X)\geqslant\rho(Y)
. Hence

\rho=\min(\rho(X),|s|)\geqslant\rho(Y)>|\varpi_{m}|/r.

By further enlarging
K
if necessary, we may assume that
\varpi_{m}\in K^{\times}
, and now all conditions of Hypothesis
3.3.2
are satisfied.

Choose any
t\in K^{\times}
with
|t|=r/|\varpi_{m}|
. Theorem
3.1.9
tells us that

\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\cong\widehat{\mathcal{D}_{t}^{(m)}(X)}=D\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{D}_{r}(X_{i})\cong D_{i}\quad\mbox{for}\quad i=1,2.

Hence
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X_{1})
and
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X_{2})
are flat
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
-modules on both sides by Proposition
3.3.11
. Since either
Y=X_{1}=X(x/s)
or
Y=X_{2}=X(s/x)
, we conclude that
\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)
is a flat
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
-module on both sides. ∎

3.4.
Flatness of divided power algebras in cotangent direction

Our goal in this section is to prove the following Theorem and then use it to complete the proof of Theorem
3.2.1
.

Theorem 3.4.1
.

Let
s,r\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
be given with
s\geqslant r
, and suppose that
X
lies in
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
. Then
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
is a flat
\mathcal{D}_{s}(X)
-module on both sides.

As in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{flatbasesec}
, we can enlarge
K
to reduce to the case where
X
is split over
K
. We can also assume that
|K|^{\times}
contains any particular finite set of elements in
\sqrt{|K|^{\times}}
. We will first deal with the special case where the following Hypothesis holds; we will see in the proof of Theorem
3.4.1
given at the end of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{flatcotdirsec}
below that the general case can be reduced to this one.

Hypothesis 3.4.2
.

•

X
is an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{A}
split over
K
;

•

m\in\mathbb{N}
is such that
p^{-1/p^{m}}\in K^{\times}
;

•

s,r\in K^{\times}
are such that
|s|>|r|>1/\rho(X)
and
|s/r|<|p|^{-1/p^{m}}
;

•

\pi\in K^{\times}
is such that
\max\left\{|p|,\frac{1}{|r|\rho(X)}\right\}\leqslant|\pi|<1
.

We will assume that Hypothesis
3.4.2
holds until the end of the proof of Corollary
3.4.9
below.

Definition 3.4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{U}^{0}=\mathcal{D}_{s}^{(m)}(X)
. For each
i=0,\ldots,m
, define inductively

\mathcal{U}^{i+1}:=\mathcal{U}^{i}[(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}]\quad\subset\quad\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{r}(X)

so that
\mathcal{U}^{0}\subset\mathcal{U}^{1}=\mathcal{D}_{s}^{(m)}(X)[\partial_{x}/r]\subset\mathcal{U}^{2}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{U}^{m+1}={\mathcal{D}}_{r}^{(m)}(X)
.

We will show that for each
i=0,\cdots,m
,
\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{i+1}_{K}}
is flat as a
\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{i}_{K}}
-module on both sides. Then we will appeal to transitivity of flatness to deduce that
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{r}^{(m)}(X)_{K}
is flat as a
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{s}^{(m)}(X)_{K}
-module on both sides. Then Theorem
3.1.9
will imply that
\mathcal{D}_{|r\varpi_{m}|}(X)
is flat over
\mathcal{D}_{|s\varpi_{m}|}(X)
.

First we prove an important generalisation of Proposition
3.1.4
.

Proposition 3.4.4
.

Let
i=1,\ldots,m
.

(1)

\mathcal{U}^{i}
is a free
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-module with basis

T_{i}:=\left\{(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha\rangle}(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{i}\beta\rangle}:0\leqslant\alpha<p^{i},\beta\geqslant 0\right\}.

(2)

\mathcal{U}^{i}/(\pi)
is a finitely presented commutative
K^{\circ}/\pi K^{\circ}
-algebra.

Proof.

(a) Let
k\geqslant 0
. Recall from
(\ref{d<k>formula})
that when we write
k=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}c_{j}p^{j}+cp^{m}
for some
c_{0},\ldots,c_{m-1}\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}
and
c\geqslant 0
, there is a unit
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
such that

(17)

\partial_{x}^{\langle k\rangle}=u\left(\prod\limits_{j=0}^{m-1}(\partial_{x}^{\langle p^{j}\rangle})^{c_{j}}\right)(\partial_{x}^{\langle p^{m}\rangle})^{c}.

Using this equation together with Proposition
3.1.4
, we see that
T_{i}
is a generating set for
\mathcal{U}^{i}
as a
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-algebra and
T_{i}
is linearly independent over
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
. To show that
\mathcal{U}^{i}=\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}\cdot T_{i}
it will suffice to prove that
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}\cdot T_{i}
is closed under multiplication. Since
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
is closed under multiplication and
K^{\circ}\subset\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
, it will suffice to show that

(1)

T_{i}\cdot\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}\cdot T_{i}
, and

(2)

T_{i}\cdot T_{i}\subseteq K^{\circ}\cdot T_{i}
.

(1) Let
0\leqslant\alpha<p^{i}
and
\beta\geqslant 0
be given, so that
t_{\alpha,\beta}:=(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha\rangle}(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{i}\beta\rangle}
is a typical element of
T_{i}
. We can of course rewrite
t_{\alpha,\beta}=(r/s)^{p^{i}\beta}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha\rangle}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle p^{i}\beta\rangle}
. By applying (
17
) to
\partial_{x}^{\langle\alpha\rangle}
,
\partial_{x}^{\langle p^{i}\beta\rangle}
and
\partial_{x}^{\langle\alpha+p^{i}\beta\rangle}
in turn, and using the fact that
0\leqslant\alpha<p^{i}
, we find that

(18)

t_{\alpha,\beta}=u_{\alpha,\beta}(r/s)^{p^{i}\beta}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha+p^{i}\beta\rangle}\quad\mbox{for some}\quad u_{\alpha,\beta}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}.

Let
f\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
. Then Lemma
3.1.2
2
tells us that

(19)

ft_{\alpha,\beta}=u_{\alpha,\beta}(r/s)^{p^{i}\beta}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\alpha+p^{i}\beta}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\alpha+p^i \beta}{k}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha+p^{i}\beta-k\rangle}(f)(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle k\rangle}.

For each
0\leqslant k\leqslant\alpha+p^{i}\beta
, write
k=\alpha_{k}+p^{i}\beta_{k}
with
0\leqslant\alpha_{k}<p^{i}
and
\beta_{k}\geqslant 0
, so that necessarily we have
\beta_{k}\leqslant\beta
. Then using (
18
) we have

(r/s)^{p^{i}\beta}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle k\rangle}=\frac{(r/s)^{p^{i}(\beta-\beta_{k})}}{u_{\alpha_{k},\beta_{k}}}t_{\alpha_{k},\beta_{k}}\in K^{\circ}\cdot T_{i}.

This allows us to rewrite
(\ref{ftab})
as follows:

(20)

ft_{\alpha,\beta}=u_{\alpha,\beta}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\alpha+p^{i}\beta}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\alpha+p^i \beta}{k}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha+p^{i}\beta-k\rangle}(f)\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{(r/s)^{p^{i}(\beta-\beta_{k})}}{u_{\alpha_{k},\beta_{k}}}t_{\alpha_{k},\beta_{k}}.

We have chosen
\pi\in K^{\times}
to satisfy
\max\left\{|p|,\frac{1}{|r|\rho(X)}\right\}\leqslant|\pi|<1
, so
\mathcal{D}^{(m)}_{r}(X)/(\pi)
is a commutative algebra by Corollary
3.1.6
(b). This implies that

(21)

(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle j\rangle}(f)\in\pi\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad j\geqslant 1.

Using
(\ref{ftab2})
, we can now see that
ft_{\alpha,\beta}\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}T_{i}
, and hence
T_{i}\cdot\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}\cdot T_{i}
.

(2) Let
0\leqslant\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}<p^{i}
be given. Using Lemma
3.1.2
1
together with (
17
) we see that if
\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}<p^{i}
then

(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha\rangle}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha^{\prime}\rangle}=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\alpha+\alpha'}{\alpha}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}\rangle},

whereas if
p^{i}\leqslant\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}<2p^{i}
then

(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha\rangle}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha^{\prime}\rangle}=u(s/r)^{p^{i}}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\alpha+\alpha'}{\alpha}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}-p^{i}\rangle}(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}

for some
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. Similarly for
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\geqslant 0
, we have

(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{i}\beta\rangle}(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{i}\beta^{\prime}\rangle}=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p^i(\beta+\beta')}{p^i\beta}(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{i}(\beta+\beta^{\prime})\rangle}.

Thus to see that
K^{\circ}T_{i}
is closed under multiplication it suffices to show that
(s/r)^{p^{i}}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\alpha+\alpha'}{\alpha}\in K^{\circ}
whenever
0\leqslant\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}<p^{i}
but
\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}\geqslant p^{i}
.

Now
0\leqslant\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}<p^{i}
implies that
\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\alpha+\alpha'}{\alpha}=1
. If in addition we have
\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}\geqslant p^{i}
, there must be at least one carry when adding
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}

p
-adically. Therefore by Kummer’s Theorem (Theorem
6.4.3
) we have

v_{p}\left(\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\alpha+\alpha'}{\alpha}\right)=v_{p}\left(\binom{\alpha+\alpha^{\prime}}{\alpha}\right)\geqslant 1.

However we have assumed that
|s|
and
|r|
are sufficiently close, in the sense that
|s/r|\leqslant|p|^{-1/p^{m}}
. This implies that
|(s/r)^{p^{i}}|\leqslant|p|^{-p^{i}/p^{m}}\leqslant|p|^{-1}
as
i\leqslant m
, so
p(s/r)^{p^{i}}\in K^{\circ}
. Hence
(s/r)^{p^{i}}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\alpha+\alpha'}{\alpha}\in K^{\circ}
as required.

(b) The commutativity of
\mathcal{U}^{i}/(\pi)
is clear given equations
(\ref{ftab2})
and
(\ref{dxrjf})
.

It follows from (a) that
\mathcal{U}^{i}/(\pi)
is a free
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-module on the image of
T_{i}
. Moreover writing
\tau_{\alpha,\beta}
for the image of
t_{\alpha,\beta}
in
\mathcal{U}^{i}/(\pi)
we see, by (
14
), that the
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi)
-submodule
U
generated by
\{\tau_{0,p^{m-i}\gamma}\mid\gamma\geqslant 0\}
is a polynomial algebra
U=\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi)[\tau_{0,p^{m-i}}]
. Moreover
\mathcal{U}^{i}
is a free
U
-module on the finite set
\{\tau_{\alpha,\beta}\mid 0\leqslant\alpha<p^{i},0\leqslant\beta<p^{m-i}\}
.

By the proof of Corollary
3.1.6
(b),
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}/(\pi)
is a finitely presented
K^{\circ}/(\pi)
-algebra. Thus, by
[
45
, 00F4]
,
U
is also a finitely presented
K^{\circ}/(\pi)
-algebra. Finally
\mathcal{U}^{i}/(\pi)
is a finitely presented
K^{\circ}/(\pi)
-algebra by
[
45
, 0D46]
.
∎

Recall that for each
u\in\mathcal{D}(X)
,
\ad(u)\colon\mathcal{D}(X)\to\mathcal{D}(X)
is a
K
-linear derivation.

Lemma 3.4.5
.

For any
i=0,\ldots,m
, we have
\ad\left((\partial_{x}/{r})^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}\right)(\mathcal{U}^{i})\subseteq\pi\mathcal{U}^{i}.

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
. By Lemma
3.1.2
(b), we have

\ad\left((\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}\right)(f)=\sum_{k=0}^{p^{i}-1}\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{p^i}{k}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle{p^{i}-k}\rangle}(f)\cdot(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle k\rangle}.

Now
(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle{p^{i}-k}\rangle}(f)\in\pi\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
by
(\ref{dxrjf})
and
(\partial_{x}/{r})^{\langle k\rangle}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}
for all
k<p^{i}
by (
17
), so the sum on the right hand side lies in
\pi\mathcal{U}^{i}
. By Proposition
3.4.4
(a), we know that
\mathcal{U}^{i}
is a free left
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-module with basis
T_{i}
. The result follows because
[(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle p^{i}\rangle},ft]=[(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle p^{i}\rangle},f]t\in\pi\mathcal{U}^{i}
for all
t\in T_{i}
. ∎

Lemma 3.4.6
.

For each
i=0,\ldots,m-1
,
\left((\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}\right)^{p}\in\mathcal{U}^{0}
.

Proof.

The assumption
i+1\leqslant m
implies that
q_{p^{i}}=q_{p^{i+1}}=0
. Hence
\partial^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}=\partial^{[p^{i}]}
and
\partial^{\langle p^{i+1}\rangle}=\partial^{[p^{i+1}]}
by Definition
3.1.1
(d), so

\left((\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}\right)^{p}=\frac{p^{i+1}!}{(p^{i}!)^{p}}(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle p^{i+1}\rangle}=\frac{p^{i+1}!}{(p^{i}!)^{p}}(s/r)^{p^{i+1}}(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{i+1}\rangle}.

Now as
v_{p}(p^{i}!)=\frac{p^{i}-1}{p-1}
, we can see that
v_{p}\left(\frac{p^{i+1}!}{(p^{i}!)^{p}}\right)=1
. Since
i\leqslant m-1
and
|s/r|\leqslant|p|^{-1/p^{m}}
, we see that
|(s/r)^{p^{i+1}}|\leqslant|p|^{-1}
. Hence
\frac{p^{i+1}!}{(p^{i}!)^{p}}(s/r)^{p^{i+1}}\in K^{\circ}
, so
\left((\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}\right)^{p}\in K^{\circ}\cdot(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{i+1}\rangle}\subset\mathcal{D}_{s}^{(m)}(X)=\mathcal{U}^{0}
as required.∎

Notation 3.4.7
.

Let
i=0,\ldots,m
.

•

Let
y_{i}=(\partial_{x}/{r})^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}
.

•

Let
\delta_{i}
be
\ad(y_{i})
viewed as a derivation of
\mathcal{U}^{i}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{V}^{i}
be the skew-Ore extension
\mathcal{U}^{i}[Y_{i};\delta_{i}]
.

•

Let
s_{i}=(r/s)^{p^{i}}\in K^{\circ}
.

Note that
\delta_{i}:\mathcal{U}^{i}\to\mathcal{U}^{i}
is well-defined by Lemma
3.4.5
.

Proposition 3.4.8
.

For each
0\leqslant i\leqslant m
there is a surjective ring homomorphism
\phi_{i}\colon\mathcal{V}^{i}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}^{i+1}
extending the inclusion map
\mathcal{U}^{i}\to\mathcal{U}^{i+1}
and sending
Y_{i}
to
y_{i}
. The kernel contains the central element
s_{i}Y_{i}-(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}
and

s_{i}^{2p-1}\cdot\left(\ker\phi_{i}/\mathcal{V}^{i}(s_{i}Y_{i}-(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{i}\rangle})\right)=0.

Proof.

We write
z_{i}=(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}=s_{i}y_{i}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}
. Note that
z_{i}
and
Y_{i}
commute in
\mathcal{V}^{i}
.
The existence of
\phi_{i}
follows from the universal property of the skew-Ore extension, Proposition
2.1.1
. Note that
\phi_{i}
is surjective because
\mathcal{U}^{i+1}
is generated by
\mathcal{U}^{i}
together with
y_{i}
, by Definition
3.4.3
. The element
s_{i}Y_{i}-z_{i}
is central in the skew-Ore extension
\mathcal{V}^{i}
because
s_{i}\delta_{i}
,
\ad(s_{i}y_{i})
and
\ad(z_{i})
define the same derivation
\mathcal{U}^{i}\to\mathcal{U}^{i}
. Moreover,
\phi_{i}\left(s_{i}Y_{i}-z_{i}\right)=s_{i}y_{i}-z_{i}=0
, so
\mathcal{V}^{i}(s_{i}Y_{i}-z_{i})\subseteq\ker\phi_{i}
.

We suppose first that
i\leqslant m-1
. Lemma
3.4.6
gives that
y_{i}^{p}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}
, so that
(z_{i}/s_{i})^{p}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}
. Then, writing
n=qp+r
with
0\leqslant r<p
and
q\geqslant 0
we see that

(22)

s_{i}^{p-1}(z_{i}/s_{i})^{n}=s_{i}^{p-1-r}z_{i}^{r}(z_{i}/s_{i})^{qp}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad n\geqslant 0.

It follows that for any
n\geqslant 0
we have

(23)

s_{i}^{p-1}(Y_{i}-z_{i}/s_{i})^{n}=s_{i}^{p-1}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}(-z_{i}/s_{i})^{k}Y_{i}^{n-k}\in\mathcal{V}^{i}.

Take an element
u=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{l}u_{n}Y_{i}^{n}\in\ker\phi_{i}
with
u_{0},\cdots,u_{l}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}
. Then

\displaystyle s_{i}^{2p-1}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n=0}^{l}s_{i}^{2p-1}u_{n}(Y_{i}-z_{i}/s_{i}+z_{i}/s_{i})^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n=0}^{l}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}u_{n}s_{i}^{p-1}(z_{i}/s_{i})^{n-k}s_{i}^{p}(Y_{i}-z_{i}/s_{i})^{k}.

When
k\geqslant 1
, we can rewrite the summand on the right hand side as follows:

\left(\binom{n}{k}u_{n}\right)\cdot\left(s_{i}^{p-1}(z_{i}/s_{i})^{n-k}\right)\cdot\left(s_{i}^{p-1}(Y_{i}-z_{i}/s_{i})^{k-1}\right)\cdot(s_{i}Y_{i}-z_{i}).

The first factor lies in
\mathcal{U}^{i}
, the second factor lies in
\mathcal{U}^{i}
by
(\ref{sip1})
and the third factor lies in
\mathcal{V}^{i}
by
(\ref{sip2})
. Hence

s_{i}^{2p-1}u\equiv\sum\limits_{n=0}^{l}s_{i}^{2p-1}u_{n}(z_{i}/s_{i})^{n}\quad\mod\mathcal{V}^{i}(s_{i}Y_{i}-z_{i}).

But since
u\in\ker\phi_{i}
, we have

(24)

0=\phi_{i}(s_{i}^{2p-1}u)=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{l}s_{i}^{2p-1}u_{n}y_{i}^{n}=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{l}u_{n}s_{i}^{2p-1}(z_{i}/s_{i})^{n}

and we deduce that
s_{i}^{2p-1}u\in\mathcal{V}^{i}(s_{i}Y_{i}-z_{i})
as claimed.

Now we consider the case
i=m
. Again we suppose that
u=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{l}u_{n}Y_{m}^{n}\in\ker\phi_{m}
with
u_{0},\cdots,u_{l}\in\mathcal{U}^{m}
. This time

0=\phi_{m}(s_{m}^{l}u)=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{l}s_{m}^{l}u_{n}y_{m}^{n}=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{l}s_{m}^{l-n}u_{n}z_{m}^{n}.

Thus

\displaystyle s_{m}^{l}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n=0}^{l}s_{m}^{l-n}u_{n}(s_{m}Y_{m}-z_{m}+z_{m})^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{n=0}^{l}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{n}s_{m}^{l-n}\binom{n}{k}u_{n}(s_{m}Y_{m}-z_{m})^{k}z_{m}^{n-k}

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{m}(s_{m}Y_{m}-z_{m})+\sum_{n=0}^{l}s_{m}^{l-n}u_{n}z_{m}^{n}=\mathcal{V}^{m}(s_{m}Y_{m}-z_{m}).

So for all
u\in\ker\phi_{m}
, we can find
t\in K^{\circ}
and
v=\sum\limits_{n\geqslant 0}v_{n}Y_{m}^{n}\in\mathcal{V}^{m}
such that
tu=v(s_{m}Y_{m}-z_{m})
. Expanding out this equation gives

\left(-tu_{0}+\sum_{n\geqslant 1}(s_{m}v_{n-1}-tu_{n})Y_{m}^{n}\right)=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}v_{n}Y_{m}^{n}z_{m}

and equating coefficients of
Y_{m}^{n}
we obtain

(25)

\displaystyle v_{0}z_{m}=-tu_{0}\mbox{ and }v_{n}z_{m}=(s_{m}v_{n-1}-tu_{n})\mbox{ for all }n\geqslant 1.

Now we recall from Proposition
3.4.4
(a) that
\mathcal{U}^{m}
is a free
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-module on

T_{m}=\{(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha\rangle}(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{m}\beta\rangle}:0\leqslant\alpha<p^{m},\beta\geqslant 0\}.

Using
(\ref{d<k>formula})
, we see that
(\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{m}\beta\rangle}
only differs from
z_{m}^{\beta}=\left((\partial_{x}/s)^{\langle p^{m}\rangle}\right)^{\beta}
by a
p
-adic unit, for any
\beta\geqslant 0
. Therefore

T^{\prime}_{m}:=\{(\partial_{x}/r)^{\langle\alpha\rangle}z_{m}^{\beta}:0\leqslant\alpha<p^{m},\beta\geqslant 0\}

is also a basis for
\mathcal{U}^{m}
as a
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
-module. Now right multiplication by
z_{m}
in
\mathcal{U}^{m}
maps
T^{\prime}_{m}
to
T^{\prime}_{m}
and so if
wz_{m}\in t\mathcal{U}^{m}
for some
w\in\mathcal{U}^{m}
then
w\in t\mathcal{U}_{m}
. Using (
25
) we thus see inductively that
v_{n}\in t\mathcal{U}_{m}
for all
n\geqslant 0
. It follows that
v/t\in\mathcal{V}^{m}
and so
u=(v/t)(s_{m}Y_{m}-z_{m})\in\mathcal{V}^{m}(s_{m}Y_{m}-z_{m})
. We have shown that
\ker\phi_{m}=\mathcal{V}^{m}(s_{m}Y_{m}-z_{m})
, which is even stronger than what we need.
∎

Corollary 3.4.9
.

The homomorphism
\widehat{\phi_{i,K}}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{i}_{K}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{i+1}_{K}}
induces an isomorphism
\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{i}_{K}}/(Y_{i}-y_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{i+1}_{K}}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.4.8
, the complex
0\to\mathcal{V}^{i}(s_{i}Y_{i}-z_{i})\to\mathcal{V}^{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{U}^{i+1}\to 0
has cohomology killed by
s_{i}^{2p-1}
. In this situation,
[
7
, Lemma 3.6]
tells us that the functor
\widehat{()}_{K}
turns this complex into a short exact sequence. Hence the result. ∎

Proof of Theorem
3.4.1
.

We note that by Lemma
3.2.3
and Lemma
2.3.11
(a) it will suffice to prove this result after replacing
K
by a finite extension. In particular by
[
8
, Theorem 4.1.8]
we may assume that
X
splits over
K
and
s,r\in|K^{\times}|
. Then
r>r(X)=\varpi/\rho(X)
by Corollary
2.3.17
. We may also assume
s>r
.

Since the sequence
|\varpi_{m}|
converges to
\varpi
from above, we may choose
m
sufficiently large, so that
r>|\varpi_{m}|/\rho(X)
. We fix this
m
for the rest of this proof. Now, we enlarge
K
if necessary, to ensure that
\varpi_{m}\in K^{\times}
and
p^{-1/p^{m}}\in K^{\times}
. We can then choose
s^{\prime},r^{\prime}\in K
such that
|\varpi_{m}s^{\prime}|=s
and
|\varpi_{m}r^{\prime}|=r
. By enlarging
K
further, we may assume that
K^{\times}
contains an element
\pi
such that
\max\left\{|p|,\frac{1}{|r^{\prime}|\rho(X)}\right\}\leqslant|\pi|<1
.

Now since
r<s
we see that
1/\rho(X)<|r^{\prime}|<|s^{\prime}|
. Then by Theorem
3.1.9
,

\mathcal{D}_{s}(X)\cong\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{s^{\prime}}^{(m)}(X)_{K}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\cong\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{r^{\prime}}^{(m)}(X)_{K}.

We first consider the case when
s
and
r
are sufficiently close, in the sense that

1<s/r<|p|^{-1/p^{m}}.

After this assumption, since
|s^{\prime}/r^{\prime}|=s/r
, we note that all conditions of Hypothesis
3.4.2
are satisfied for the elements
s^{\prime}
and
r^{\prime}
of
K^{\times}
in place of
s
and
r
, respectively.

We form
\mathcal{U}^{0}:=\mathcal{D}_{s^{\prime}}^{(m)}(X)
, and inductively,
\mathcal{U}^{i+1}:=\mathcal{U}^{i}[(\partial_{x}/{r^{\prime}})^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}]\subset\mathcal{D}_{r^{\prime}}^{(m)}(X)
as in Definition
3.4.3
, for each
i=0,\cdots,m
. We let
\mathcal{V}^{i}
be the skew-Ore extension
\mathcal{U}^{i}[Y_{i};\delta_{i}]
where
\delta_{i}:\mathcal{U}^{i}\to\mathcal{U}^{i}
is the derivation
\ad(y_{i})
with
y_{i}:=(\partial_{x}/r^{\prime})^{\langle p^{i}\rangle}
, as in Notation
3.4.7
. Lemma
3.4.5
ensures that
[y_{i},\mathcal{U}^{i}]\subseteq\pi\mathcal{U}^{i}
. Now Theorem
3.2.7
together with Corollary
3.4.9
imply that
\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{i+1}_{K}}\cong\widehat{\mathcal{V}^{i}_{K}}/(Y_{i}-y_{i})
is a flat
\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{i}_{K}}
-module on both sides. By the transitivity of flatness, we see that
\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{m+1}_{K}}=\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{(m)}_{r^{\prime}}(X)_{K}=\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
is a flat
\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{0}_{K}}=\widehat{\mathcal{D}}^{(m)}_{s^{\prime}}(X)_{K}=\mathcal{D}_{s}(X)
-module on both sides, as claimed.

Returning to full generality, we can find a sequence
r=r_{0}<r_{1}<\ldots<r_{N}=s
, all in
|K^{\times}|
, such that
1<r_{i}/r_{i-1}\leqslant|p|^{-1/p^{m}}
for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
. The transitivity of flatness now reduces us to the special case considered above.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.2.1
.

By Proposition
3.2.5
it suffices to show that if
s,r\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
with
s\geqslant r
and
Y\subseteq X
both lie in
\mathbb{A}(\partial_{x}/r)
, the map
\mathcal{D}_{s}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)
is flat on both sides. By Theorem
3.4.1

\mathcal{D}_{s}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
is flat on both sides, and by Theorem
3.3.1
,
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(Y)
is flat on both sides. We’re now done by the transitivity of flatness.
∎

4.
Overconvergent
\mathcal{D}
-modules

4.1.
The Huber space and overconvergent sheaves

Recall from
[
48
, §5]
that to every rigid analytic space
X
we can associate a topological space
\tilde{X}
which we call the
Huber space
of
X
. The elements of
\tilde{X}
are the prime filters on the admissible open subsets of
X
. The sets of the form
\tilde{U}:=\{p\in\tilde{X}:U\in p\}
as
U
ranges over the admissible open subsets of
X
form a basis for the topology on
\tilde{X}
. If
X
is quasi-compact and quasi-separated (qcqs), then
\tilde{X}
is quasi-compact but in general not Hausdorff. There is a set-theoretic embedding
X\hookrightarrow\tilde{X}
given by
x\mapsto\mathfrak{{m}}_{x}
, where
\mathfrak{{m}}_{x}
is the principal maximal filter consisting of the admissible open subsets of
X
containing
x
. We will identify
X
with its image in
\tilde{X}
via this embedding. By
[
48
, §5]
there is an equivalence of categories between the abelian sheaves on
X
and the abelian sheaves on
\tilde{X}
. Given an abelian sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
X
, we will denote the corresponding sheaf on
\tilde{X}
by
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
; it follows from the proof of
[
48
, Theorem 1]
that
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(\tilde{W})=\mathcal{F}(W)
for any admissible open subset
W
of
X
.

Definition 4.1.1
.

Let
X
be a rigid analytic space, let
U
be an admissible open subset of
X
and let
\mathcal{F}
be an abelian sheaf on
X
.

(1)

Let
i:\overline{\tilde{U}}\to\tilde{X}
denote the inclusion of the closure of
\tilde{U}
into
\tilde{X}
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}}
be the abelian sheaf on
X
defined by

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}}}=i_{\ast}i^{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{F}}.

In the setting of Definition
4.1.1
,
i^{-1}
is left adjoint to
i_{\ast}
, and the counit of the adjunction gives a natural morphism
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}}
called restriction.

Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}}}
is denoted
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\overline{\tilde{U}}}
in
[
29
, §2.3]
. This construction is local, in the following sense.

Lemma 4.1.2
.

Let
X
be a rigid analytic space, let
U
be an admissible open subset of
X
and let
\mathcal{F}
be an abelian sheaf on
X
. Let
Y
be another admissible open subset of
X
. Then there is a natural isomorphism of abelian sheaves on
Y

(\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}})_{|Y}\cong(\mathcal{F}_{|Y})_{\overline{U\cap Y}}.

Proof.

Because
\tilde{Y}
is open in
\tilde{X}
, we deduce from
[
19
, Chapter I, §1.6, Proposition 5]
that
\tilde{Y}\cap\overline{\tilde{U}}
is the closure in
\tilde{Y}
of
\tilde{Y}\cap\tilde{U}=\widetilde{Y\cap U}
. Let
f:\tilde{Y}\hookrightarrow\tilde{X}
be the open inclusion. Applying the formula
[
29
, (2.3.19)]
with
Z:=\overline{\tilde{U}}
gives

\widetilde{(\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}})_{|Y}}=f^{-1}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\overline{\tilde{U}}})=(f^{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{F}})_{f^{-1}(\overline{\tilde{U}})}=(f^{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{F}})_{\overline{\widetilde{U\cap Y}}^{\tilde{Y}}}=\widetilde{(\mathcal{F}_{|Y})_{\overline{U\cap Y}}}.

Now use the equivalence of categories
\Ab(Y)\cong\Ab(\tilde{Y})
mentioned above. ∎

The following result will prove useful later.

Proposition 4.1.3
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{U}
is an admissible cover of
X
that is totally ordered by inclusion and
\mathcal{F}
is an abelian sheaf on
X
. Then the restriction maps
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}}
for
U\in\mathcal{U}
induce an isomorphism

\mathcal{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\lim\limits_{\longleftarrow}\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}}.

Proof.

If
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
with
U\subset V
then, by
[
29
, Proposition 2.3.6(iii)]

\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}}=(\mathcal{F}_{\overline{V}})_{\overline{U}}

and so it is easy to verify that there is a natural morphism
\mathcal{F}\to\lim\limits_{\longleftarrow}\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}}
with the connecting maps in the limit also given by restriction.

Moreover, by Lemma
4.1.2
, for
U\subset V
in
\mathcal{U}
we can compute

\mathcal{F}_{\overline{V}}|_{U}=(\mathcal{F}|_{U})_{\overline{U\cap V}}=\mathcal{F}|_{U}.

Since for all
W\in\mathcal{U}
there is
V\in\mathcal{U}
containing both
U
and
W
,
\{V\in\mathcal{U}\mid U\subset V\}
is cofinal in
\mathcal{U}
, and it follows that
\mathcal{F}|_{U}\cong\left(\lim\limits_{\longleftarrow}\mathcal{F}_{\overline{V}}\right)|_{U}
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Since
\mathcal{U}
is a cover of
X
the result follows.
∎

We note that the proof above works whenever
\mathcal{U}
is a directed set under inclusion; that is whenever any two elements of
\mathcal{U}
have a common upper bound in
\mathcal{U}
under inclusion.

The operation
\mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}}
on
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules behaves well with respect to tensor products.

Lemma 4.1.4
.

If
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
are two
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules and
U
is an admissible open subset of
X
then there is a natural isomorphism

(\mathcal{F}\underset{\mathcal{O}_{X}}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{G})_{\overline{U}}\cong\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}}\underset{(\mathcal{O}_{X})_{\overline{U}}}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{G}_{\overline{U}}.

Proof.

By
[
29
, Proposition 2.3.10]
we have

\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}}\cong(\mathcal{O}_{X})_{\overline{U}}\underset{\mathcal{O}_{X}}{\otimes}\mathcal{F}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{G}_{\overline{U}}\cong(\mathcal{O}_{X})_{\overline{U}}\underset{\mathcal{O}_{X}}{\otimes}\mathcal{G}.

Now tensor these together over
(\mathcal{O}_{X})_{\overline{U}}
and contract tensors.
∎

We will now restrict to the case where
X
is an affinoid variety, and explain how
\mathcal{F}_{\overline{U}}
is related to the more well-known operations in rigid analytic geometry.

Definition 4.1.5
.

Suppose that
X
is affinoid, and
U=X\left(\frac{g_{1}}{g_{0}},\frac{g_{2}}{g_{0}},\cdots,\frac{g_{n}}{g_{0}}\right)
is a rational subdomain of
X
, where
g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}\in\mathcal{O}(X)
generate the unit ideal in
\mathcal{O}(X)
. Following
[
22
, Exercise 7.1.12(5)]
, for each
s\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
such that
s>1
, define

U(s):=\{a\in X:|g_{i}(a)|\leqslant s|g_{0}(a)|\quad\mbox{for all}\quad i=1,\ldots,n\}.

These slightly larger rational subdomains of
X
form a cofinal system of
wide open neighbourhoods
of
U
in
X
, by
[
22
, Exercise 7.1.12(5)(a)]
. Note that this notation is slightly misleading because the sets
U(s)
depend in general on the choice of presentation
U=X\left(\frac{g_{1}}{g_{0}},\frac{g_{2}}{g_{0}},\cdots,\frac{g_{n}}{g_{0}}\right)
of
U
as a rational subdomain of
X
.

Lemma 4.1.6
.

Let
V
be an admissible open subset of
X
. Then
\tilde{V}
contains the closure
\overline{\tilde{U}}
in
\tilde{X}
if and only if
V
contains
U(s)
for some
s\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
with
s>1
.

Proof.

Let
r:\tilde{X}\to\mathscr{M}(X)
be the retraction map onto the Berkovich space
\mathscr{M}(X)
associated to
X
. We claim that
\overline{\tilde{U}}=r^{-1}(\mathscr{M}(U))
. To see this, note that
\mathscr{M}(U)=r(\tilde{U})
is closed in
\mathscr{M}(X)
because
\mathscr{M}(U)
is compact and
\mathscr{M}(X)
is Hausdorff; since
r
is continuous this gives
\overline{\tilde{U}}\subseteq r^{-1}(\mathscr{M}(U))
. For the other inclusion, let
x\in r^{-1}(\mathscr{M}(U))
so that
r(x)=r(y)
for some
y\in\tilde{U}
. Therefore
x\in r^{-1}(r(y))=\overline{\{y\}}
, by
[
48
, Lemma 3.2ii]
, and thus
x\in\overline{\{y\}}\subseteq\overline{\tilde{U}}
as claimed.

It follows that
\tilde{V}\supseteq\overline{\tilde{U}}
if and only if
r^{-1}(a)\subset\tilde{V}
for all
a\in\mathscr{M}(U)
. Now,
\tilde{V}\supseteq\overline{\tilde{U}}
implies that
V
is a wide open neighbourhood of
U
in the sense of
[
22
, Exercise 7.1.12(4)]
. Hence
V
contains some
U(s)
by
[
22
, Exercise 7.1.12(5)(a)]
. Conversely, if
V
contains some
U(s)
then
V
is a wide open neighbourhood of
U
so for all
a\in\mathscr{M}(U)
we can find an open neighbourhood
W_{a}
of
r^{-1}(a)
contained in
V
. But then
\overline{\tilde{U}}=r^{-1}(\mathscr{M}(U))=\bigcup\limits_{a\in\mathscr{M}(U)}r^{-1}(a)\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{a\in\mathscr{M}(U)}\tilde{W_{a}}\subseteq\tilde{V}
as required.∎

Lemma 4.1.7
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be an abelian presheaf on an affinoid variety
X
, let
U
be a rational subdomain of
X
, and let
\mathcal{F}^{\dagger}_{U}
be the presheaf

\mathcal{F}^{\dagger}_{U}:Y\mapsto\underset{s\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|},s>1}{\colim}{}\hskip 2.84544pt\mathcal{F}(Y\cap U(s))

on the weak
G
-topology of
X
. Then
\mathcal{F}^{\dagger}_{U}
is a sheaf whenever
\mathcal{F}
is a sheaf, and
\mathcal{F}^{\dagger}_{U}
is acyclic whenever
\mathcal{F}
is acyclic.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{Y}:=\{Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\}
is a finite affinoid covering of an affinoid subdomain
Y
of
X
, and if
s\in\sqrt{|K^{\times}|}
and
s>1
, then
\mathcal{Y}_{s}:=\{Y_{1}\cap U(s),\ldots,Y_{n}\cap U(s)\}
is a finite affinoid covering of
Y\cap U(s)
. Hence there is an isomorphism of augmented Čech complexes
