\begin{array}[]{lll}\partial\cdot x^{m}v_{\lambda}&=&\partial x\cdot x^{m-1}v_{\lambda}=(x^{m-1}\partial x+[\partial x,x^{m-1}])\cdot v_{\lambda}=\\
&=&x^{m-1}(x\partial+1+(m-1))v_{\lambda}=(\lambda+m)x^{m-1}v_{\lambda}.\end{array}

It follows that for any
a\in K^{\circ}\langle\partial\rangle
and any
m\geqslant 1
, we have

\partial\cdot ax^{m}\equiv a(\lambda+m)x^{m-1}\mod Dt.

Suppose now that
a_{n}x^{n}+a_{n-1}x^{n-1}+\cdots+a_{1}x+a_{0}\equiv 0\mod I
, for some
a_{i}\in K^{\circ}\langle\partial\rangle
with
a_{n}\neq 0
and
n\geqslant 0

least possible
. Suppose for a contradiction that
n\geqslant 1
. Then

0=\partial\cdot 0\equiv\partial\cdot\sum\limits_{m=0}^{n}a_{m}x^{m}\equiv\sum\limits_{m=1}^{n}a_{m}(\lambda+m)x^{m-1}+\partial a_{0}\quad\mod I.

Since
\lambda\notin\mathbb{Z}
and since
K^{\circ}\langle\partial\rangle
is a domain, we see that
a_{m}(\lambda+m)\neq 0
, so this congruence contradicts the minimality of
n
. Therefore
n=0
and
I\cap K^{\circ}\langle\partial\rangle\neq\{0\}
as claimed. Otherwise, by Corollary
4.4.10
, we have the congruence
a_{n}\partial^{n}+a_{n-1}\partial^{n-1}+\cdots+a_{1}\partial+a_{0}\equiv 0\mod I
for some
a_{i}\in\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{X})
with
a_{n}\neq 0
. A similar calculation shows that

(36)

x\cdot a\partial^{m}\equiv a(\lambda-m+1)\partial^{m-1}\quad\mod Dt\quad\mbox{for all}\quad m\geqslant 1,a\in\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{X})

and using this congruence we conclude using a similar argument that in fact we can choose
n=0
and therefore
I\cap\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{X})\neq\{0\}
.
∎

Corollary 4.4.12
.

If
\lambda\in K^{\circ}\backslash\mathbb{Z}
, then the only
\pi_{K}
-closed left ideal
I
of
D
properly containing
D(x\partial-\lambda)
is
D
itself.

Proof.

Every non-zero ideal in the Tate algebra
K\langle\partial\rangle
is generated by a non-zero element of
K[\partial]
. So, if
I\cap K^{\circ}\langle\partial\rangle
is non-zero, then
I_{K}\cap K[\partial]
is non-zero. Clearing denominators, we may even assume that
I\cap K^{\circ}[\partial]
is non-zero. By applying an appropriate linear combination of the Euler operators
E_{n}
as in the proof of Proposition
4.4.9
to a non-zero element of
I\cap K^{\circ}[\partial]
, and using the fact that
I
is
\pi_{K}
-closed, we see that
\partial^{n}\in I
for some
n\geqslant 0
. Choosing
n
minimal possible and using the relation
(\ref{xdm})
together with the fact that
\lambda\notin\mathbb{Z}
, we see that
n=0
and hence
1\in I
.

Otherwise, by Proposition
4.4.11
, we know that
I\cap\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{X})
is non-zero. Again, every ideal in the affinoid algebra
\mathcal{O}(X)=\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{X})_{K}
is generated by a non-zero element of
K[x]
, and we similarly conclude that
1\in I
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.4.3
.

Corollary
4.4.6
(a) tells us that
\mathscr{M}^{(m)}(\lambda)(\mathscr{X})
is zero if
\lambda\notin\bigcup\limits_{i=0}^{p^{m}-1}i+p^{m}K^{\circ}
, so we may assume that
\lambda

does
lie in this union. Then we can apply Corollary
4.4.6
(b) to see that
F^{\natural}(\mathscr{M}^{(m)}(\lambda))
is isomorphic to
\frac{\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{(0)}_{\mathscr{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{Q}}}{\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{(0)}_{\mathscr{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{Q}}(x^{\prime}\partial^{\prime}-\frac{\lambda-i}{p^{m}})}
for some uniquely determined
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,p^{m}-1\}
.

Since
F^{\natural}
is an equivalence of categories by
[
15
, Théorème 2.3.6]
, it is enough to prove that
F^{\natural}(\mathscr{M}^{(m)}(\lambda))(\mathscr{X}^{\prime})
is a simple
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{(0)}_{\mathscr{X}^{\prime},\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X}^{\prime})
-module. Note that
\frac{\lambda-i}{p^{m}}\notin\mathbb{Z}
because
\lambda\notin\mathbb{Z}
by assumption, so we have reduced the problem to the case where
m=0
. But now
\mathscr{M}^{(0)}(\lambda)(\mathscr{X})=D_{K}/D_{K}(x\partial-\lambda)
, which is a simple
D_{K}
-module by Corollary
4.4.12
.
∎

5.
Group actions and equivariance

5.1.
The crossed product and the secret action

We recall some constructions from
[
9
, §2.1]
. Let
L
be a commutative ring, let
S
be an
L
-algebra and let
G
be a group acting on
S
by
L
-algebra automorphisms from the left. We denote the result of the group action of
g\in G
on
s\in S
by
g\cdot s
. The
skew-group ring

S\rtimes G
is a free left
S
-module with basis
G
, and its multiplication is determined by

(37)

(sg)\cdot(s^{\prime}g^{\prime})=(s(g\cdot s^{\prime}))(gg^{\prime})\quad\mbox{for all}\quad s,s^{\prime}\in S,\quad g,g^{\prime}\in G.

The skew-group ring satisfies the following universal property.

Lemma 5.1.1
.

Suppose that
U
is an
L
-algebra,
\sigma\colon S\to U
is an
L
-algebra homomorphisms and
\rho\colon G\to U^{\times}
is a group homomorphism such that

\rho(g)\sigma(s)\rho(g)^{-1}=\sigma(g\cdot s)\mbox{ for all }g\in G\mbox{ and }s\in S.

There is a unique
L
-algebra homomorphism
\sigma\rtimes\rho\colon S\rtimes G\to U
such that

(\sigma\rtimes\rho)(se)=\sigma(s)\mbox{ for all }s\in S\mbox{ and }(\sigma\rtimes\rho)(1g)=\rho(g)\mbox{ for all }g\in G.

Proof.

It is straightforward to verify that
\sigma\rtimes\rho
must be the
L
-linear extension of the map sending
sg
to
\sigma(s)\rho(g)
and that by (
37
) this is indeed an
L
-algebra homomorphism.
∎

Definition 5.1.2
.

Let
S\rtimes G
be a skew-group ring. A
trivialisation
is a group homomorphism
\beta:G\to S^{\times}
such that for all
g\in G
, the conjugation action of
\beta(g)\in S^{\times}
on
S
coincides with the action of
g\in G
on
S
.

Definition 5.1.3
.

Let
N
be a normal subgroup of
G
, and suppose that
\beta:N\to S^{\times}
is a trivialisation of the sub-skew-group ring
S\rtimes N
.

(1)

We define

S\rtimes_{N}^{\beta}G:=\frac{S\rtimes G}{(S\rtimes G)\cdot\{\beta(n)^{-1}n-1:n\in N\}}.

(2)

We say that
\beta
is
G
-equivariant
if

\beta({}^{g}n)=g\cdot\beta(n)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad g\in G\quad\mbox{and}\quad n\in N.

Here
{}^{g}n:=gng^{-1}
denotes the conjugation action of
G
on
N
.

Note that
S\rtimes_{N}^{\beta}G
is
a priori
only a left
S\rtimes G
-module.

Lemma 5.1.4
.

Suppose that
N
is a normal subgroup of
G
, and that
\beta:N\to S^{\times}
is a
G
-equivariant trivialisation. Then

(1)

S\rtimes_{N}^{\beta}G
is an associative ring.

(2)

S\rtimes_{N}^{\beta}G
is isomorphic to a crossed product
S\ast(G/N)
.

Proof.

This is
[
9
, Lemma 2.2.4]
.∎

We will now place ourselves in the following situation.

Hypothesis 5.1.5
.

(1)

G
is a group,
S
and
S^{\prime}
are
L
-algebras,

(2)

G
acts on
S
and
S^{\prime}
by
L
-algebra automorphisms,

(3)

f:S^{\prime}\to S
is a
G
-equivariant
L
-algebra homomorphism,

(4)

H^{\prime}\leqslant H
are two normal subgroups of
G
,

(5)

\beta:H\to S^{\times}
is a
G
-equivariant trivialisation of the
G
-action on
S
,

(6)

\beta^{\prime}:H^{\prime}\to S^{\prime\times}
is a
G
-equivariant trivialisation of the
G
-action on
S^{\prime}
,

(7)

f(\beta^{\prime}(h^{\prime}))=\beta(h^{\prime})
for all
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
.

It follows from
[
9
, Lemma 2.2.7]
that the ring homomorphism
f:S^{\prime}\to S
extends to a ring homomorphism
f\rtimes 1:S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}^{\beta^{\prime}}G\to S\rtimes_{H}^{\beta}G
. To aid legibility, we will now drop the trivialisations
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
from the notation.

Lemma 5.1.6
.

Let
M^{\prime}
be an
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-module. Then there is an action of
H
on
M:=S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
by left
S
-linear automorphisms, given by

h\star(s\otimes m^{\prime})=s\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(h)^{-1}\otimes h\cdot m^{\prime}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad h\in H,s\in S,m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}.

The restriction of this action to
H^{\prime}
is trivial.

Proof.

We will first check that the formula makes sense, that is
h\star(ss^{\prime}\otimes m^{\prime})=h\star(s\otimes s^{\prime}m^{\prime})
holds for all
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
,
s\in S
,
h\in H
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
. We have

\begin{array}[]{lll}(ss^{\prime})\beta(h)^{-1}\otimes h\cdot m^{\prime}&=&s\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(h)^{-1}\hskip 2.84544pt\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(h)s^{\prime}\beta(h)^{-1}\otimes h\cdot m^{\prime}=\\
&=&s\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(h)^{-1}(h\cdot s^{\prime})\otimes h\cdot m^{\prime}=\\
&=&s\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(h)^{-1}\otimes(h\cdot s^{\prime})(h\cdot m^{\prime})=\\
&=&s\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(h)^{-1}\otimes h\cdot(s^{\prime}m^{\prime})\end{array}

as required. It is straightforward to verify that the given formula defines a left
H
-action on
M
by left
S
-linear automorphisms. Because
M^{\prime}
is a
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-module, by Definition
5.1.3
(a) we may view it as an
S^{\prime}\rtimes G
-module where
h^{\prime}\cdot m^{\prime}=\beta^{\prime}(h^{\prime})\cdot m^{\prime}
for all
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
. So if
h^{\prime}
lies in
H^{\prime}
then by Hypothesis
5.1.5
(g),

s\beta(h^{\prime})^{-1}\otimes h^{\prime}\cdot m^{\prime}=sf(\beta^{\prime}(h^{\prime})^{-1})\otimes h^{\prime}\cdot m^{\prime}=s\otimes\beta^{\prime}(h^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot(h^{\prime}\cdot m^{\prime})=s\otimes m^{\prime}

and hence
h^{\prime}\star(s\otimes m^{\prime})=s\otimes m^{\prime}
for all
h^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
,
s\in S
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
as claimed.∎

Definition 5.1.7
.

Let
M^{\prime}
be an
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-module. We call the
H
-action on the
S
-module
S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M
defined in Lemma
5.1.6
the
secret
H
-action
.

It follows from Lemma
5.1.6
that via the secret
H
-action,
S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
is naturally a left
S[H/H^{\prime}]
-module. We will use the notation

(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})_{H}:=\frac{S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}}{\{h\star v-v:v\in S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime},h\in H\}}.

to denote the module of left
H
-coinvariants of
S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
under the secret
H
-action.

Theorem 5.1.8
.

For every
S^{\prime}\otimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-module
M^{\prime}
, there is a natural isomorphism

(S\rtimes_{H}G)\underset{(S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G)}{\otimes}{}M^{\prime}\quad\cong\quad(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})_{H}

of left
S
-modules.

Proof.

Let
[v]
denote the image of
v\in S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
in
(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})_{H}
. Consider the map
\alpha:(S\rtimes G)\times M^{\prime}\longrightarrow(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})_{H}
given by
(sg,m^{\prime})\mapsto[s\otimes g\cdot m^{\prime}]
for
s\in S,g\in G
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
. Let
s\in S,g\in G,h\in H
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
. Since

\begin{array}[]{lll}\alpha(sg\beta(h),m^{\prime})&=&\alpha(s\beta({}^{g}h)g,m^{\prime})=[s\beta({}^{g}h)\otimes g\cdot m^{\prime}]=\\
&=&[s\otimes{}^{g}h\cdot(g\cdot m^{\prime})]=[s\otimes(gh)\cdot m^{\prime}]=\alpha(sgh,m^{\prime})\end{array}

we see that
\alpha
is zero on
(S\rtimes G)\{\beta(h)-h:h\in H\}\hskip 2.84544pt\times\hskip 2.84544ptM^{\prime}
and therefore descends to a map
\overline{\alpha}:(S\rtimes_{H}G)\times M^{\prime}\longrightarrow(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})_{H}.
Recalling from
[
9
, §2.2(3)]
that
s\gamma(g)
denotes the image of
sg\in S\rtimes G
in the crossed product
S\rtimes_{H}G
, we see that
\overline{\alpha}
is given by
\overline{\alpha}(s\gamma(g),m^{\prime})=[s\otimes g\cdot m^{\prime}]
for all
s\in S,g\in G
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
.

Let
s\gamma(g)\in S\rtimes_{H}G
,
s^{\prime}\gamma(g^{\prime})\in S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
. Then we compute

\begin{array}[]{lll}\overline{\alpha}(s\gamma(g)\cdot s^{\prime}\gamma(g^{\prime}),m^{\prime})&=&\overline{\alpha}(sf(g\cdot s^{\prime})\gamma(gg^{\prime}),m^{\prime})=[sf(g\cdot s^{\prime})\otimes(gg^{\prime}\cdot m^{\prime})],\\
\overline{\alpha}(s\gamma(g),s^{\prime}\gamma(g^{\prime})\cdot m^{\prime})&=&[s\otimes g\cdot(s^{\prime}\cdot(g^{\prime}\cdot m^{\prime}))]=[s\otimes((g\cdot s^{\prime})g)\cdot(g^{\prime}\cdot m^{\prime})]=\\
&=&[s\otimes(g\cdot s^{\prime})\cdot(gg^{\prime}\cdot m^{\prime})]=[sf(g\cdot s^{\prime})\otimes(gg^{\prime}\cdot m^{\prime})].\end{array}

Hence
\overline{\alpha}
is
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-balanced, and therefore descends to a well-defined map

(38)

\theta:(S\rtimes_{H}G)\underset{(S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G)}{\otimes}{}M^{\prime}\longrightarrow(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})_{H}

given by
\theta(s\gamma(g)\otimes m^{\prime})=[s\otimes g\cdot m^{\prime}]
for all
s\in S,g\in G
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
. It is clear that
\theta
is
S
-linear. On the other hand, there is evidently a well-defined
S
-linear map

\varphi:S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}\longrightarrow(S\rtimes_{H}G)\underset{(S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G)}{\otimes}{}M^{\prime}

given by
\varphi(s\otimes m^{\prime})=s\otimes m^{\prime}
for all
s\in S
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
. Since

\begin{array}[]{lll}\varphi(h\star(s\otimes m^{\prime}))&=&\varphi(s\beta(h)^{-1}\otimes h\cdot m^{\prime})=s\beta(h)^{-1}\otimes h\cdot m^{\prime}=\\
&=&s\gamma(h)^{-1}\otimes h\cdot m^{\prime}=s\otimes h^{-1}\cdot(h\cdot m^{\prime})=s\otimes m^{\prime},\end{array}

we see that
\varphi
factors through
(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})_{H}
and induces an
S
-linear map

\psi:(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})_{H}\longrightarrow(S\rtimes_{H}G)\underset{(S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G)}{\otimes}{}M^{\prime}

given by
\psi([s\otimes m^{\prime}])=s\otimes m^{\prime}
for all
s\in S
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
. It is now straightforward to check that
\theta
and
\psi
are mutually inverse bijections.
∎

Now let
M^{\prime}
be an
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-module, let
M
be an
S\rtimes_{H}G
-module and suppose we’re given an
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-linear map

\tau:M^{\prime}\to M,

where we regard
M
as an
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-module via restriction along the ring homomorphism
f\rtimes 1:S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}^{\beta^{\prime}}G\to S\rtimes_{H}^{\beta}G
coming from
[
9
, Lemma 2.2.7]
. Our next goal is to establish a sufficient criterion for the induced
S\rtimes_{H}G
-linear map

(39)

\widetilde{\tau}:(S\rtimes_{H}G)\underset{(S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G)}{\otimes}{}M^{\prime}\longrightarrow M

given by
\widetilde{\tau}(v\otimes m^{\prime})=v\cdot\tau(m^{\prime})
, where
v\in S\rtimes_{H}G
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
, to be injective.

Lemma 5.1.9
.

Let
M^{\prime}
be an
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-module.

(1)

There is an
S
-
semi
linear action of
G
on
S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
given by

g\cdot(s\otimes m^{\prime})=(g\cdot s)\otimes(g\cdot m^{\prime})\quad\mbox{for all}\quad g\in G,s\in S,m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}.

(2)

For every
g\in G,h\in H
and
v\in S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
we have

g\cdot(h\star(g^{-1}\cdot v))=({}^{g}h)\star v.

Proof.

(a) We check that the formula is well-defined. Let
g\in G
,
s\in S
,
s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
. Because
f:S^{\prime}\to S
is
G
-equivariant, we have

\begin{array}[]{lll}g\cdot(sf(s^{\prime})\otimes m^{\prime})&=&(g\cdot sf(s^{\prime}))\otimes(g\cdot m^{\prime})=(g\cdot s)(g\cdot f(s^{\prime}))\otimes(g\cdot m^{\prime})=\\
&=&(g\cdot s)f(g\cdot s^{\prime})\otimes(g\cdot m^{\prime})=(g\cdot s)\otimes(g\cdot s^{\prime})(g\cdot m^{\prime})\\
&=&(g\cdot s)\otimes g\cdot(s^{\prime}\cdot m^{\prime})=g\cdot(s\otimes s^{\prime}\cdot m^{\prime})\end{array}

as required. It is straightforward to see that this defines a
G
-action on
S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
which is
S
-semilinear in the sense that

g\cdot(s\cdot v)=(g\cdot s)\cdot(g\cdot v)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad g\in G,s\in S,v\in S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}.

(b) We may assume that
v=s\otimes m^{\prime}
with
s\in S
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
. Let
g\in G
and
h\in H
; using Definition
5.1.3
(b), we compute

\begin{array}[]{lll}g\cdot(h\star(g^{-1}\cdot(s\otimes m^{\prime})))&=&g\cdot(h\star(g^{-1}\cdot s\otimes g^{-1}\cdot m^{\prime}))=\\
&=&g\cdot((g^{-1}\cdot s)\beta(h)^{-1}\otimes h\cdot(g^{-1}\cdot m^{\prime}))=\\
&=&g\cdot((g^{-1}\cdot s)\beta(h^{-1}))\otimes g\cdot(h\cdot(g^{-1}\cdot m^{\prime}))=\\
&=&s\hskip 2.84544ptg\cdot\beta(h^{-1})\otimes ghg^{-1}\cdot m^{\prime}=\\
&=&s\beta(({}^{g}h)^{-1})\otimes({}^{g}h)\cdot m^{\prime}=({}^{g}h)\star(s\otimes m^{\prime}).\qed\end{array}

We will now restrict our scope slightly, and assume until the end of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CPandSA}
that

•

H^{\prime}
has finite index in
H
,

•

L
is a field of characteristic zero.

Let
M^{\prime}
be an
S^{\prime}\otimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-module. By Lemma
5.1.6
and the above assumptions, we see that
S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
is a module over the group ring
L[H/H^{\prime}]
which is semisimple because we’re assuming that our ground field
L
has characteristic zero. Because the action of
L[H/H^{\prime}]
on
S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
commutes with the action of
S
, we obtain a canonical
S
-module decomposition

(40)

S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}\cong\bigoplus_{[V]\in\Irr_{L}(H/H^{\prime})}e_{V}\star(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})

where
\Irr_{L}(H/H^{\prime})
is the set of isomorphism classes of simple
L[H/H^{\prime}]
-modules, and for each
[V]\in\Irr_{L}(H/H^{\prime})
,
e_{V}
is the primitive central idempotent of
L[H/H^{\prime}]
such that
1-e_{V}
generates the annihilator of
V
in
L[H/H^{\prime}]
as a two-sided ideal.

Let
V
be an
L[H/H^{\prime}]
-module and let
g\in G
. Let
gV:=\{[g]v:v\in V\}
where
[g]
is a formal symbol, and define an
H
-action on
gV
by the rule
h\cdot[g]v=[g]h^{g}v
for all
h\in H
and
v\in V
. This is well defined because
H
is normal in
G
, and since
H^{\prime}
acts trivially on
V
and is also normal in
G
, we see that
[g]V
is again an
L[H/H^{\prime}]
-module. In this way we obtain a permutation action of
G
on
\Irr_{L}(H/H^{\prime})
given by
g\cdot[V]=[gV]
.

Corollary 5.1.10
.

Let
M^{\prime}
be an
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-module. The
S
-semilinear
G
-action on
S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
permutes the direct summands appearing on the right hand side of (
40
):

g\cdot\left(e_{V}\star(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})\right)=e_{gV}\star(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})\quad\mbox{for all}\quad g\in G\quad\mbox{and}\quad[V]\in\Irr_{L}(H/H^{\prime}).

Proof.

The conjugation action of
g\in G
on
L[H/H^{\prime}]
sends
e_{V}
to
e_{gV}
. Now

g\cdot(e_{V}\star v)={}^{g}e_{V}\star(g\cdot v)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad g\in G,v\in S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}

by Lemma
5.1.9
(b) and the result follows.
∎

Given an
S
-module
M
, let
\ell(M)
denote its length. We can now state and prove our criterion.

Theorem 5.1.11
.

Assume that Hypothesis
5.1.5
holds, that
H^{\prime}
has finite index in
H
and that
L
is a field of characteristic zero. Let
M^{\prime}
be an
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-module, let
M
be a
S\rtimes_{H}G
-module, let
\tau:M^{\prime}\to M
be an
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-linear map and consider the
S
-linear map

\Phi=1\otimes\tau:S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}\longrightarrow M

induced by
\tau
. Suppose further that the following conditions hold.

(1)

the secret
H
-action on
S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
is non-trivial,

(2)

the
G
-action on
\Irr_{L}(H/H^{\prime})
has exactly two orbits, and

(3)

\ell(\ker\Phi)\leqslant|\Irr_{L}(H/H^{\prime})|-1
.

Then
\Phi
factors through the coinvariants
(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})_{H}
under the secret
H
-action on
S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
, and the induced map
\overline{\Phi}:(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})_{H}\longrightarrow M
is injective.

Proof.

Consider the
S
-module decomposition of
N:=S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
given by (
40
):

N=e_{\mathbbm{1}}\star N\quad\oplus\quad(H-1)\star N,\quad\mbox{where}\quad(H-1)\star N=\bigoplus\limits_{[V]\neq[\mathbbm{1}]}e_{V}\star N

and where
\mathbbm{1}
denotes the trivial
L[H/H^{\prime}]
-module. Note that

\begin{array}[]{lll}\Phi(h\star(s\otimes m^{\prime}))&=&\Phi(s\beta(h)^{-1}\otimes h\cdot m^{\prime})=s\beta(h)^{-1}\cdot\tau(h\cdot m^{\prime})=\\
&=&s\beta(h)^{-1}\cdot(h\cdot\tau(m^{\prime}))=s\cdot\tau(m^{\prime})=\Phi(s\otimes m^{\prime})\end{array}

for all
h\in H,s\in S
and
m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}
, because the
G
-action on
M
satisfies
h\cdot m=\beta(h)\cdot m
as
M
is an
S\rtimes_{H}G
-module. In other words:
(H-1)\star N\subseteq\ker\Phi
. Hence

\ell((H-1)\star N)\leqslant\ell(\ker\Phi).

We know that
e_{V}\star N\neq 0
for at least one non-trivial
[V]\in\Irr_{L}(H/H^{\prime})
by (a). Since
g[\mathbbm{1}]=[\mathbbm{1}]
for all
g\in G
, Corollary
5.1.10
together with (b) then implies that
e_{V}\star N\neq 0

for all
non-trivial
[V]\in\Irr_{L}(H/H^{\prime})
. This gives us the inequality

\left|\Irr_{L}(H/H^{\prime})\right|-1\leqslant\ell\left(\bigoplus\limits_{[V]\neq[\mathbbm{1}]}e_{V}\star N\right)=\ell((H-1)\ast N).

On the other hand, (c) gives that

\ell(\ker\Phi)\leqslant\left|\Irr_{K}(H/H^{\prime})\right|-1.

It follows that the inclusion
(H-1)\star N\subseteq\ker\Phi
is in fact an equality.

Hence
\Phi
factors through the module of coinvariants
N_{H}
of
N
under the secret
H
-action, and induces an
S
-linear injection
\overline{\Phi}:N_{H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}M
.
∎

5.2.
The Iwahori action on differential operators on the closed unit disc

In this section, we explore in detail the action of the
p
-adic Lie group
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
on the rigid
K
-analytic projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}:=\mathbb{P}^{1,\an}
. Fix a coordinate
x\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
. Because of the local nature of our constructions, we will focus specifically on the affinoid subdomains of the closed unit disc
\mathbb{D}=\Sp K\langle x\rangle=\{z\in\mathbb{P}^{1}:|z|\leqslant 1\}
.

Definition 5.2.1
.

The
generalised Iwahori subgroup of
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
is

\mathcal{G}:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K^{\circ}):|c|<1\right\}.

We note that if
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{G}
then
|a|,|d|=1
since
|ad-bc|=1
,
|a|,|b|,|d|\leqslant 1
and
|c|<1
. We also see that
\mathcal{G}
stabilises
\mathbb{D}
under the standard Möbius action of
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. In fact, the stabiliser of
\mathbb{D}
in
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
is
\mathcal{G}Z
, where
Z
is the centre of
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
. By Lemma
2.5.2
the
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathbb{D}
induces
\mathcal{G}
-actions on
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{D})
. The
\mathcal{G}
-action on the divided powers
\partial_{x}^{[n]}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{D})
of
\partial_{x}
is given as follows.

Lemma 5.2.2
.

Let
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{G}
. Then

(1)

g\cdot x=\frac{dx-b}{-cx+a}
, and

(2)

g\cdot\partial_{x}^{[n]}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\binom{n-1}{i-1}(-cx+a)^{n+i}(-c)^{n-i}}{{(ad-bc)^{n}}}\partial_{x}^{[i]}
for all
n\geqslant 0
.

Proof.

(a) This is straightforward. (b) The case
n=1
follows from Lemma
2.5.2
(c). In general, let
y:=-cx+a
so that
\partial_{x}(y)=-c
and
\partial_{x}=-c\partial_{y}
. Then the case
n=1
can be rewritten as
g\cdot\partial_{x}=-\frac{cy^{2}}{\det(g)}\partial_{y}
, and it follows that
g\cdot\partial_{x}^{[n]}=\left(\frac{-c}{\det(g)}\right)^{n}(y^{2}\partial_{y})^{[n]}
. Now, by Vandermonde’s identity, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle(y^{2}\partial_{y})^{[n]}(y^{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\binom{n+k-1}{n}y^{k+n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}\binom{n-1}{n-i}\binom{k}{i}y^{k+n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\binom{n-1}{i-1}y^{n+i}\partial_{y}^{[i]}(y^{k}).

Thus as
K[y]
is dense in
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})
we see that

(y^{2}\partial_{y})^{[n]}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\binom{n-1}{i-1}y^{n+i}\partial_{y}^{[i]}

from which the result quickly follows.
∎

Recall the natural inclusion
j:\mathcal{D}(X)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
from Lemma
2.3.7
.

Corollary 5.2.3
.

Let
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{G}
. Let
r>0
and let
X
be an
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible and
g
-stable affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
. Then
|a-cx|_{X}=1
, and for all
n\geqslant 1
,

|j(g\cdot\partial_{x}^{n})|=\max\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\left|\binom{n-1}{i-1}\frac{n!}{i!}\right|\hskip 2.84544pt|c|^{n-i}\hskip 2.84544ptr^{i}.

Proof.

Note that
|x|_{X}\leqslant 1
since
X\subset\mathbb{D}
by assumption. Since
|a|=1
and
|c|<1
by Definition
5.2.1
, we see that
|a-cx|_{X}=1
, as well as
|ad-bc|=1
. We can now use Lemma
5.2.2
(b) together with Definition
2.2.8
to obtain the result. ∎

Recall that if
X\subseteq\mathbb{D}
is an affinoid subdomain,
\mathcal{G}_{X}
denotes its stabiliser in
\mathcal{G}
.

Proposition 5.2.4
.

Let
X
be a
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
and let
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
. Then the
g
-action on
\mathcal{D}(X)
extends to a bounded
K
-algebra automorphism
\rho_{r}(g)
of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
.

Proof.

Fix
n\geqslant 0
. Using Corollary
5.2.3
we have

\frac{|j(g\cdot\partial_{x}^{n})|}{|r^{n}|}=\max\limits_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}\left|\binom{n-1}{i-1}\frac{n!}{i!}\right||c|^{n-i}r^{i-n}.

Now
\left|\binom{n-1}{i-1}\frac{n!}{i!}\right|=\left|(n-i)!\binom{n-1}{i-1}\binom{n}{i}\right|\leqslant|(n-i)!|
, so by Lemma
2.3.16
,

\frac{|j(g\cdot\partial_{x}^{n})|}{|r^{n}|}\leqslant\max\limits_{0\leqslant i\leqslant n-1}|i!|\left(\frac{|c|}{r}\right)^{i}\leqslant p\max\limits_{0\leqslant i\leqslant n-1}i\left(\frac{|c|\varpi}{r}\right)^{i}.

By
[
8
, Theorem 4.1.8]
, there is a finite extension
K^{\prime}
of
K
such that
X
splits over
K^{\prime}
. Lemma
2.3.13
and Corollary
2.3.17
now imply that

r(X)=r(X_{K^{\prime}})=\varpi/\rho(X_{K^{\prime}})\geqslant\varpi,

because
\rho(X_{K^{\prime}})\leqslant 1
as
X_{K^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathbb{D}_{K^{\prime}}
. Since
X
is
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible, we conclude that
r>r(X)\geqslant\varpi
, so that
|c|\varpi/r<|c|<1
. Lemma
2.4.8
now tells us that

\sup\limits_{n\geqslant 0}\frac{|j(g\cdot\partial_{x}^{n})|}{|r^{n}|}<\infty.

Recall the group homomorphism
\rho\colon\mathcal{G}_{X}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))^{\times}
given by Lemma
2.5.2
(c). Using the above inequality, we can now apply Lemma
2.2.10
with
A=\mathcal{O}(X)
,
\delta=\partial_{x}
,
B=\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
,
f=j\circ\rho(g)
and
b=j(g\cdot\partial_{x})
to deduce that
\rho(g)
extends to a bounded
K
-algebra endomorphism
\rho_{r}(g)
of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
. It is easy to see that
\rho_{r}(gh)=\rho_{r}(g)\rho_{r}(h)
for
g,h\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
, so in fact each
\rho_{r}(g)
is an automorphism.
∎

Note that the automorphism
\rho_{r}(g)
in Proposition
5.2.4
is only
\mathcal{O}(X)
-semilinear. We now have at our disposal the skew-group ring

\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\rtimes\mathcal{G}_{X}

whenever
r>r(X)
.

Corollary 5.2.5
.

Let
X
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
.

(1)

For every
r>r(X)
, the
\mathcal{O}(X)
-semilinear action of
\mathcal{G}_{X}
on
\mathcal{D}(X)
extends to an
\mathcal{O}(X)
-semilinear action on
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
by bounded
K
-linear automorphisms.

(2)

The
\mathcal{O}(X)
-semilinear action of
\mathcal{G}_{X}
on
\mathcal{D}(X)
extends to an
\mathcal{O}(X)
-semilinear action on
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{r(X)}(X)
by
K
-algebra automorphisms.

Proof.

(a) This follows immediately from Proposition
5.2.4
.

(b) This follows from part (a) in view of Definition
2.3.9
(d).∎

We now explain how
\rho(\mathcal{G}_{X})
and
\sigma_{r}(\mathcal{D}_{r}(X))
interact inside
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))
.

Lemma 5.2.6
.

Let
X
be a
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
. The maps
\rho\colon\mathcal{G}_{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))^{\times}
from Lemma
2.5.2
(c) and
\sigma_{r}\colon\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))
from Lemma
2.3.12
satisfy

\rho(g)\circ\sigma_{r}(Q)\circ\rho(g)^{-1}=\sigma_{r}(g\cdot Q)

for all
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
and all
g\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
, where
g\cdot Q=\rho_{r}(g)(Q)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
g\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
and
f,h\in\mathcal{O}(X)
. Then

(\rho(g)\circ\sigma_{r}(f)\circ\rho(g)^{-1})(h)=g\cdot(f\hskip 2.84544ptg^{-1}\cdot h)=(g\cdot f)\hskip 2.84544pth=\sigma_{r}(g\cdot f)(h),

and using (
12
) we see that

(\rho(g)\circ\partial_{x}\circ\rho(g)^{-1})(h)=g\cdot(\partial_{x}(g^{-1}\cdot h))=(g\cdot\partial_{x})(h).

Since
\sigma_{r}
is a
K
-algebra homomorphism and
\rho_{r}(g)
acts on
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
by
K
-algebra homomorphisms it follows that
\rho(g)\circ\sigma_{r}(Q)\circ\rho(g)^{-1}=\sigma_{r}(g\cdot Q)
for all
Q\in\mathcal{D}(X)
and then for all
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
by continuity.
∎

The universal property of skew group rings, Lemma
5.1.1
, gives the following

Corollary 5.2.7
.

Let
X
be a
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
. The natural
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\rtimes\mathcal{G}_{X}
-action on
\mathcal{O}(X)
induces a
K
-algebra homomorphism
\sigma_{r}\rtimes\rho:\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\rtimes\mathcal{G}_{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))
.

We will now explain how to construct a trivialisation of a certain sufficiently large sub-skew-group ring of this skew-group ring.

Definition 5.2.8
.

For each real number
r>\varpi
, we define

(1)

a subgroup
\mathcal{G}_{r}:=\{g\in\mathcal{G}:\quad|g\cdot x-x|_{\mathbb{D}}<\varpi/r\}
of
\mathcal{G}
, and

(2)

a function
\beta:\mathcal{G}_{r}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathbb{D})
defined by

\beta(g):=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}(g\cdot x-x)^{n}\partial^{[n]}.

Lemma
2.3.16
shows that for any
g\in\mathcal{G}_{r}
we have

\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\left|\frac{(g\cdot x-x)^{n}}{n!}\right|_{\mathbb{D}}\cdot r^{n}\leqslant\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\left(\frac{|g\cdot x-x|_{\mathbb{D}}}{\varpi/r}\right)^{n}=0.

Hence
\beta(g)

does
define an element of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathbb{D})
whenever
g\in\mathcal{G}_{r}
by Definition
2.2.8
. The following explicit description of the group
\mathcal{G}_{r}
will be useful later.

Lemma 5.2.9
.

\mathcal{G}_{r}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{G}:\hskip 2.84544pt|b|<\frac{\varpi}{r},\hskip 2.84544pt|c|<\frac{\varpi}{r},\hskip 2.84544pt|a-d|<\frac{\varpi}{r}\right\}
.

Proof.

Let
g=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{G}
. Since
|c|<1
, the invertibility of
g
in
M_{2}(K^{\circ})
forces
|a|=|d|=1
. By Corollary
5.2.3
, we have
|a-cx|_{\mathbb{D}}=1
. Next, using Lemma
5.2.2
(a) we have

g\cdot x-x=\frac{cx^{2}+(d-a)x-b}{-cx+a}\quad.

Since
|\cdot|_{\mathbb{D}}
is multiplicative, we conclude that
|g\cdot x-x|_{\mathbb{D}}=|cx^{2}+(d-a)x-b|_{\mathbb{D}}=\max\{|c|,|d-a|,|b|\}
. Hence
g\in\mathcal{G}_{r}
if and only if
|b|,|c|,|d-a|
are all strictly less than
\varpi/r
as claimed.
∎

The main reason for Definition
5.2.8
comes from the following

Proposition 5.2.10
.

Let
X
be a
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
. Then

\sigma_{r}(\beta(g))=\rho(g)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad g\in\mathcal{G}_{r}\cap\mathcal{G}_{X}.

Proof.

Fix
g\in\mathcal{G}_{r}\cap\mathcal{G}_{X}
and write
w:=g\cdot x-x
. Then for every
a,b\in\mathcal{O}(X)
,

\left(\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}w^{n}\partial_{x}^{[n]}\right)(ab)=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}w^{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n}\partial^{[i]}_{x}(a)\partial_{x}^{[n-i]}(b)=\left(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{\infty}w^{i}\partial_{x}^{[i]}(a)\right)\left(\sum\limits_{j=0}^{\infty}w^{j}\partial_{x}^{[j]}(b)\right).

It follows that
\sigma_{r}(\beta(g)):\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(X)
is a
K
-algebra homomorphism
, so for every integer
m\geqslant 0
we have

\sigma_{r}(\beta(g))(x^{m})=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}w^{n}\binom{m}{n}x^{m-n}=(w+x)^{m}=(g\cdot x)^{m}=\rho(g)(x^{m}).

Because
\sigma_{r}(\beta(g))
and
\rho(g)
are both continuous
K
-linear operators on
\mathcal{O}(X)
, we conclude that
\sigma_{r}(\beta(g))\rho(g)^{-1}:\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(X)
is a
K
-algebra homomorphism which fixes
K\langle x\rangle=\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})\subseteq\mathcal{O}(X)
pointwise. Since
\mathcal{O}(X)
contains a dense
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})
-subalgebra generated by elements of the form
a/b
with
a,b\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})
, the continuity of
\sigma_{r}(\beta(g))
and
\rho(g)
implies that
\sigma_{r}(\beta(g))\rho(g)^{-1}
in fact fixes all of
\mathcal{O}(X)
.
∎

Theorem 5.2.11
.

Let
X
be a
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
. Then

\beta:\mathcal{G}_{r}\cap\mathcal{G}_{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)^{\times}

is a
\mathcal{G}_{X}
-equivariant trivialisation.

Proof.

According to Definition
5.1.2
and
5.1.3
(b), we have to verify that

(1)

\beta
is a group homomorphism into
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)^{\times}
,

(2)

\beta(h)\hskip 2.84544pta\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(h)^{-1}=h\cdot a
for all
h\in H:=\mathcal{G}_{r}\cap\mathcal{G}_{X}
and
a\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
,

(3)

H
is normal in
\mathcal{G}_{X}
, and

(4)

\beta(ghg^{-1})=g\cdot\beta(h)
for all
g\in\mathcal{G}_{X}
and
h\in H
.

(a) Using Proposition
5.2.10
, we note that

\sigma_{r}(\beta(g)\beta(h))=\sigma_{r}(\beta(g))\sigma_{r}(\beta(h))=\rho(g)\rho(h)=\rho(gh)=\sigma_{r}(\beta(gh))

for all
g,h\in H
. Since
\sigma_{r}
is injective by Lemma
2.3.12
(b), we conclude that the map
\beta:\mathcal{G}_{r}\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
is multiplicative. Since
\beta(1)=1
it follows that the image of
\beta
is contained in
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)^{\times}
.

(b) Using Proposition
5.2.10
and Lemma
5.2.6
we see that

\sigma_{r}(\beta(g)\hskip 2.84544pta\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(g)^{-1})=\rho(g)\hskip 2.84544pt\sigma_{r}(a)\hskip 2.84544pt\rho(g)^{-1}=\sigma_{r}(g\cdot a)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad a\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X).

The result follows from the injectivity of
\sigma_{r}
again.

(c) It suffices to prove that
\mathcal{G}_{r}
is normal in
\mathcal{G}
. For each
0\neq s\in\overline{K}^{\circ}
, let
\mathcal{G}(s):=\{g\in\mathcal{G}:|g\cdot x-x|\leqslant|s|\}
; since
|\overline{K}^{\times}|
is dense in
\mathbb{R}
, we see that

\mathcal{G}_{r}=\underset{|s|<\varpi/r}{\bigcup}{}\mathcal{G}(s).

Hence it suffices to see that each
\mathcal{G}(s)
is normal in
\mathcal{G}
. However, the action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\overline{K}^{\circ}\langle x\rangle
is
\overline{K}^{\circ}
-linear and therefore induces an action of
\mathcal{G}
on the polynomial ring
(\overline{K}^{\circ}/s\overline{K}^{\circ})[x]
. Then
\mathcal{G}(s)
is the kernel of this action and is therefore normal in
\mathcal{G}
.

(d)
\sigma_{r}(\beta(ghg^{-1}))=\rho(ghg^{-1})=\rho(g)\circ\rho(h)\circ\rho(g)^{-1}=\rho(g)\circ\sigma_{r}(\beta(h))\circ\rho(g)^{-1}
by Proposition
5.2.10
. This last equals
\sigma_{r}(g\cdot\beta(h))
by Lemma
5.2.6
. Once again the result follows because
\sigma_{r}
is injective. ∎

It follows from Theorem
5.2.11
and Lemma
5.1.4
that whenever
X
is a
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
, we may form the crossed product

\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\hskip 2.84544pt\overset{\hskip 2.84544pt\beta}{\underset{\mathcal{G}_{r}\cap\mathcal{G}_{X}}{\rtimes}}\mathcal{G}_{X}.

Corollary 5.2.12
.

Let
X
be a
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
. Then the action of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\rtimes\mathcal{G}_{X}
on
\mathcal{O}(X)
descends to an action of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\hskip 2.84544pt\rtimes_{\mathcal{G}_{r}\cap\mathcal{G}_{X}}^{\beta}\mathcal{G}_{X}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Proposition
5.2.10
. ∎

To finish
\lx@sectionsign\ref{IwahoriAction}
, we record a formula which computes the effect of applying a twisting automorphism
\theta_{u,d}
from Theorem
2.4.9
to the special infinite-order differential operators of the form
\beta(g)
. Recall the functions
h_{u,d}^{[m]}
from Definition
2.4.5
.

Proposition 5.2.13
.

Let
X
be a
\partial_{x}/r
-admissible affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
, let
g\in\mathcal{G}_{r}\cap\mathcal{G}_{X}
, let
u\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
and suppose that
p\nmid d
.

(1)

The element
c_{u,d}(g):=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}(g\cdot x-x)^{m}h_{u,d}^{[m]}
lies in
1+\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ\circ}.

(2)

Inside
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
we have

\theta_{u,d}(\beta(g))=c_{u,d}(g)\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(g).

(3)

We have
c_{uv,d}(g)=c_{u,d}(g)\,c_{v,d}(g)
for any other
v\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
.

(4)

We have
c_{u,d}(g)^{d}=\frac{u}{g\cdot u}
in
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
.

Proof.

(a) Write
w:=g\cdot x-x
. Since
g\in\mathcal{G}_{r}
and
X\subseteq\mathbb{D}
, we know that

|w|_{X}\leqslant|w|_{\mathbb{D}}<\varpi/r

by Definition
5.2.8
(a). Also,
|h_{u,d}^{[m]}|_{X}\leqslant(r(X)/\varpi)^{m}
by Theorem
2.4.7
, hence

\left|w^{m}h_{u,d}^{[m]}\right|_{X}<\left(\frac{\varpi}{r}\right)^{m}\cdot\left(\frac{r(X)}{\varpi}\right)^{m}=\left(\frac{r(X)}{r}\right)^{m}

which tends to zero as
m\to\infty
because
r(X)<r
. We conclude that
c_{u,d}(g)
converges in
\mathcal{O}(X)
to an element of
1+\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ\circ}\subset\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
.

(b) By Definition
5.2.8
(b), we have
\beta(g)=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}w^{n}\partial^{[n]}
. Therefore

\begin{array}[]{lll}\theta_{u,d}(\beta(g))&=&\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}w^{n}\sum\limits_{\alpha=0}^{n}h_{u,d}^{[n-\alpha]}\partial^{[\alpha]}=\\
&=&\sum\limits_{\alpha=0}^{\infty}w^{\alpha}\left(\sum\limits_{n=\alpha}^{\infty}w^{n-\alpha}h_{u,d}^{[n-\alpha]}\right)\partial^{[\alpha]}=\\
&=&\sum\limits_{\alpha=0}^{\infty}w^{\alpha}\left(\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}w^{m}h_{u,d}^{[m]}\right)\partial^{[\alpha]}=\\
&=&c_{u,d}(g)\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(g)\end{array}

by Theorem
2.4.4
, as claimed.

(c) Lemma
2.4.3
implies that
\theta_{uv,d}=\theta_{u,d}\circ\theta_{v,d}
as automorphisms of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
. Applying part (b) to the units
uv
,
v
and
u
in turn shows that

c_{uv,d}(g)\beta(g)=\theta_{uv,d}(\beta(g))=\theta_{u,d}(\theta_{v,d}(\beta(g))=\theta_{u,d}(c_{v,d}(g)\beta(g))=c_{v,d}(g)c_{u,d}(g)\beta(g)

since
\theta_{u,d}
is
\mathcal{O}(X)
-linear. The claimed identity now follows because
\beta(g)
is a unit in
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
by Theorem
5.2.11
.

(d) By Lemma
2.4.12
together with Proposition
5.2.10
, inside
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))
we have

\sigma_{r}(\theta_{u^{d},d}(\beta(g))=\sigma_{r}(u\,\beta(g)\,u^{-1})=u\,\sigma_{r}(\beta(g))\,u^{-1}=u\,\rho(g)\,u^{-1}.

But
\theta_{u^{d},d}(\beta(g))=c_{u^{d},d}(g)\beta(g)=c_{u,d}(g)^{d}\beta(g)
by (b,c) above, so

u\,\rho(g)\,u^{-1}=\sigma_{r}(c_{u,d}(g)^{d}\beta(g))=c_{u,d}(g)^{d}\sigma_{r}(\beta(g))=c_{u,d}(g)^{d}\rho(g)

again by Proposition
5.2.10
. Apply these operators to
1\in\mathcal{O}(X)
to find

c_{u,d}(g)^{d}=c_{u,d}(g)^{d}\rho(g)(1)=(u\,\rho(g)\,u^{-1})(1)=u\,\rho(g)(u^{-1})=u/g\cdot u.\qed

5.3.
Equivariant line bundles with connection on local Drinfeld space

Recall the generalised Iwahori subgroup
\mathcal{G}
of
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(K)
from Definition
5.2.1
; it consists of matrices with coefficients in
K
. We will now focus on certain groups of matrices with entries in
F
.

Notation 5.3.1
.

(1)

Let
I=\mathcal{G}\cap\mathbb{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
be the
Iwahori subgroup
.

(2)

Let
J
be a closed subgroup of
I
.

(3)

For each
n\geqslant 0
, write
I_{n}:=\ker(I\to\mathbb{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F}/\pi_{F}^{n}\mathcal{O}_{F}))
and
J_{n}:=J\cap I_{n}
.

(4)

Let
\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
denote the
G
-topology on
\mathbb{D}
whose open sets are the
J
-stable members of
\mathbb{D}_{n}
and whose coverings are the finite coverings.

The
G
-topology
\mathbb{D}_{n}
on
\mathbb{D}
was introduced at Definition
4.3.9
.

Definition 5.3.2
.

(1)

[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)_{\tors}
is such that
\omega[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon(\Upsilon)^{I}[p^{\prime}]
,

(2)

d\geqslant 1
is the order of
\omega[\mathscr{L}]
in
\PicCon(\Upsilon)^{I}
,

(3)

e\in d\mathbb{Z}
is the order of
[\mathscr{L}]
in
\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)
.

Thus
\mathscr{L}
is an
I
-equivariant line bundle with flat connection on
\Upsilon=\mathbb{D}\cap\Omega
, and we can find an
I
-equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-linear isomorphism

\psi^{\prime}:\mathscr{L}^{\otimes e}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}.

Clearly, whenever
J
is as in Definition
5.3.1
,
J_{n}
is a normal subgroup of
J
; note also that
J_{n}
is a pro-
p
group whenever
n\geqslant 1
because the groups
I_{1}/I_{m+1}
are all finite
p
-groups. Recall now the normal subgroups
\mathcal{G}_{r}
of
\mathcal{G}
from Definition
5.2.8
.

Lemma 5.3.3
.

I_{n+1}\leqslant\mathcal{G}_{r}
whenever
r<\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n+1}
.

Proof.

If
g=\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&g_{12}\\
g_{21}&g_{22}\end{pmatrix}\in I_{n+1}
then
g_{11}-1,g_{12},g_{21},g_{22}-1
all lie in
\pi_{F}^{n+1}\mathcal{O}_{F}
, which implies that

\max\{|g_{12}|,|g_{21}|,|g_{11}-g_{22}|\}\leqslant|\pi_{F}|^{n+1}<\varpi/r,

by our assumption on
r
. Hence
g\in\mathcal{G}_{r}
by Lemma
5.2.9
.
∎

Recall
\beta\colon\mathcal{G}_{r}\cap\mathcal{G}_{X}\to\mathscr{D}_{r}(X)^{\times}
from Theorem
5.2.11
for
X\in\mathbb{D}_{w}
and
r>r(X)
.

Proposition 5.3.4
.

Let
n\geqslant 0
and let
H
be a closed normal subgroup of
J
. Then there is a sheaf
\mathscr{D}_{n}\rtimes_{H_{n+1}}J
on
\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
whose sections over
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
are given by
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\rtimes_{H_{n+1}}^{\beta}J
. This sheaf has vanishing higher Čech cohomology.

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
. Note that
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)=\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}}(X)=\bigcup\limits_{r>\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}}\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
by Definition
2.3.9
(d). Let
r>0
satisfy

\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}<r<\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n+1}.

Then because
r>\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}\geqslant r(X)
, the
\mathcal{G}_{X}
-equivariant trivialisation

\beta:\mathcal{G}_{r}\cap\mathcal{G}_{X}\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)^{\times}

is well-defined by Theorem
5.2.11
. On the other hand
H_{n+1}\leqslant\mathcal{G}_{r}
by Lemma
5.3.3
because
r<\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n+1}
. Since
X
is
J
-stable, the restriction of
\beta
to
H_{n+1}
is a
J
-equivariant trivialisation, which means that we can form the crossed product
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\rtimes_{H_{n+1}}^{\beta}J
which is an associative ring by Lemma
5.1.4
. Letting
r
approach
\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}
from above and taking the colimit, we obtain the ring
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\rtimes_{H_{n+1}}^{\beta}J
which is still a crossed product of
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)
with
J/H_{n+1}
.

This construction is functorial in
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
, and in this way we obtain the presheaf
\mathscr{D}_{n}\rtimes_{H_{n+1}}J
on this
G
-topology. Because this presheaf is a free
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-module on
J/H_{n+1}
, we conclude that it is a sheaf with vanishing higher Čech cohomology using Theorem
2.3.10
.
∎

Recall from Definition
4.3.2
the truncation
\mathscr{L}_{n}=j_{\ast}(\mathscr{L}_{\overline{\Upsilon_{n}}})
of
\mathscr{L}
to
\Upsilon_{n}
, which is naturally a
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-module on
\mathbb{D}_{n}
by Corollary
4.3.11
.

Lemma 5.3.5
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{M}
is another
\mathcal{D}_{\Upsilon}
-module satisfying the same hypotheses as
\mathscr{L}
, and let
\mathscr{M}_{n}=j_{\ast}(\mathscr{M}_{\overline{\Upsilon_{n}}})
.

(1)

\mathscr{L}_{n}\otimes_{(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{D}})_{\overline{\Upsilon_{n}}}}\mathscr{M}_{n}
is naturally a
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-module.

(2)

Let
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}
, and
r>\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}
. Then

\beta(g)\cdot(l\otimes v)=(\beta(g)\cdot l)\otimes(\beta(g)\cdot v)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad g\in\mathcal{G}_{r}\cap\mathcal{G}_{X},l\in\mathscr{L}_{n}(X),v\in\mathscr{M}_{n}(X).

Proof.

By Theorem
4.3.7
and Proposition
4.3.8
there is
\dot{w}\in\mathscr{M}_{n}(V_{n})
such that
\mathscr{M}_{n}=(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{D}}\dot{w})_{\overline{\Upsilon_{n}}}
. Thus by Lemma
4.1.4
,
\mathscr{L}_{n}\otimes_{(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{D}})_{\overline{\Upsilon_{n}}}}\mathscr{M}_{n}\cong(\mathscr{L}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\Omega\cap\mathbb{D}}}\mathscr{M})_{\overline{\Upsilon_{n}}}
so
\mathscr{D}_{n}
acts naturally on this by Corollary
4.3.11
. Now for each
m\geqslant 0
,

\partial_{x}^{[m]}(l\otimes v)=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{m}\partial_{x}^{[i]}(l)\otimes\partial_{x}^{[m-i]}(v)\quad\mbox{ for all }\quad l\in\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)\quad\mbox{and}\quad v\in\mathscr{M}_{n}(X),

so writing
w=g\cdot x-x
as in Proposition
5.2.10
we see that

\beta(g)(l\otimes v)=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}w^{m}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{m}\partial_{x}^{[i]}(l)\otimes\partial_{x}^{[m-i]}(v)=\beta(g)(l)\otimes\beta(g)(v).\qed

We may view
\mathscr{L}_{n}
as a
J
-equivariant

\mathscr{D}_{n}
-module on
\mathbb{D}_{n}
, so its restriction to
\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
may be viewed as a
\mathscr{D}_{n}\rtimes J
-module.

Theorem 5.3.6
.

Let
H
be a closed normal subgroup of
J
and recall that
e
is the order of
[\mathscr{L}]
in
\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)_{\tors}
. For each
n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
, the
\mathscr{D}_{n}\rtimes J
-action on
\mathscr{L}_{n}
factors through
\mathscr{D}_{n}\rtimes_{H_{n+1}}J
.

Proof.

Since
H_{n+1}\leqslant J_{n+1}
it suffices to consider the case
H=J
. We fix
n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
. Write
e=p^{k}f
with
f
coprime to
p
. Then there are tensor powers
\mathscr{L}_{p}
and
\mathscr{L}_{p^{\prime}}
of
\mathscr{L}
such that
[\mathscr{L}_{p}]
has order
p^{k}
,
[\mathscr{L}_{p^{\prime}}]
has order
f
and

[\mathscr{L}]=[\mathscr{L}_{p}][\mathscr{L}_{p^{\prime}}]\in\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon).

Because
\omega[\mathscr{L}]
has order
d
coprime to
p
by Definition
5.3.2
, the order of
\omega[\mathscr{L}_{p}]=\omega[\mathscr{L}]\cdot\omega[\mathscr{L}_{p^{\prime}}]^{-1}
is also coprime to
p
. Hence
\omega[\mathscr{L}_{p}]=[\mathcal{O}_{\Upsilon}]
. Because
\Upsilon
is geometrically connected and quasi-Stein, using
[
8
, Proposition 3.2.14]
we can find a character
\chi\in\Hom(I,\mu_{p^{k}}(K))
such that
[\mathscr{L}_{p}]=[\mathcal{O}_{\chi}]
in
\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)
. The assumption
n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
implies that
I_{n}\leqslant I^{p^{k}}
, and therefore
\chi(I_{n})=1
. It follows that the image of
[\mathscr{L}]
in
\PicCon^{I_{n}}(\Upsilon)
under the restriction map has order
f
, and so we can choose an
I_{n}
-equivariant isomorphism of line bundles with connection

\psi\colon\mathscr{L}^{\otimes f}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}.

Let
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
. Since
\Upsilon_{n}
lies in
\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
as well by Corollary
2.3.17
, we see that
X\cap\Upsilon_{n}\in\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
also. Let
g\in J_{n+1}
, so that
g\in\mathcal{G}_{r}
for any
r
satisfying
\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}<r<\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n+1}
by Lemma
5.3.3
. Corollary
5.2.12
implies that
\beta(g)-g\in\mathscr{D}_{n}(X\cap\Upsilon_{n})\rtimes J
acts trivially on
\mathcal{O}(X\cap\Upsilon_{n})
; hence
\tilde{\beta}(g):=\beta(g)^{-1}g\in(\mathscr{D}_{n}(X\cap\Upsilon_{n})\rtimes J)^{\times}
fixes
\mathcal{O}(X\cap\Upsilon_{n})
pointwise.

Let
A:=\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X\cap\Upsilon_{n}}}(X)
and use Theorem
4.3.7
to find an algebraic generator
\dot{z}\in\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)
with associated rational function
u=\psi(\dot{z}^{\otimes f})
; then
\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)=A\cdot\dot{z}
by Proposition
4.3.8
. On the other hand,
\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)
is a
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)
-module by Corollary
4.3.11
and it is even a
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\rtimes J
-module by the above remarks. Write
\tilde{\beta}(g)\cdot\dot{z}=c(g)\dot{z}
for some
c(g)\in A
. Since
\psi
is
J_{n}
-equivariant and since
\tilde{\beta}(g)
fixes
A
pointwise, we see via Lemma
5.3.5
that for all
g\in J_{n+1}
,

c(g)^{f}u=\psi((c(g)\dot{z})^{\otimes{f}})=\psi((\tilde{\beta}(g)\cdot\dot{z})^{\otimes{f}})=\tilde{\beta}(g)\cdot u=u

which implies that
c(g)^{f}=1
for all
g\in J_{n+1}.
Because
\beta:J_{n+1}\to\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)^{\times}
is a trivialisation, it follows from
[
9
, Lemma 2.2.2]
that
\tilde{\beta}(gh)=\tilde{\beta}(g)\tilde{\beta}(h)
for all
g,h\in J_{n+1}
. Now,
\tilde{\beta}(g)
fixes
c(h)\in A
and therefore commutes with it in
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X\cap\Upsilon_{n})\rtimes J
. Therefore

\begin{array}[]{lllllll}c(gh)\dot{z}&=&\tilde{\beta}(gh)\dot{z}&=&\tilde{\beta}(g)\tilde{\beta}(h)\dot{z}&=&\tilde{\beta}(g)c(h)\dot{z}\\
&=&c(h)\tilde{\beta}(g)\dot{z}&=&c(h)c(g)\dot{z}&=&c(g)c(h)\dot{z}.\end{array}

Hence
c:J_{n+1}\to A^{\times}
is a group homomorphism such that
c(g)^{f}=1
for all
g\in J_{n+1}
. Since
J_{n+1}
is a pro-
p
group and since
p\nmid f
, for each
g\in J_{n+1}
there exists
h\in J_{n+1}
such that
g=h^{f}
. Hence
c(g)=c(h^{f})=c(h)^{f}=1
for all
g\in J_{n+1}
and we conclude that
\tilde{\beta}(g)\cdot\dot{z}=\dot{z}
. Hence
\tilde{\beta}(J_{n+1})
fixes
\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)=A\cdot\dot{z}
pointwise and the result follows.
∎

Since
\Upsilon_{n+1}
contains
\Upsilon_{n}
, there is a canonical
\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})
-linear restriction map

\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D}).

Let
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}
. Post-composing
\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
with the restriction map
\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)
, in view of Corollary
4.3.11
we obtain a morphism

(41)

\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n}

of sheaves of
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-modules on
\mathbb{D}_{n}
. For the moment, suppose further that
X
is
J
-stable. Then we find ourselves in the setting of Hypothesis
5.1.5
with
G:=J
,
f:S^{\prime}\to S
the restriction map
S^{\prime}:=\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\longrightarrow S:=\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)
,
H^{\prime}:=J_{n+2}
,
H:=J_{n+1}
, and the trivialisations
\beta^{\prime}
and
\beta
used in Proposition
5.3.4
.

Then
M^{\prime}:=\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})
is an
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}G
-module by Theorem
5.3.6
, so by Lemma
5.1.6
there is a secret
H=J_{n+1}
-action on

S\underset{S^{\prime}}{\otimes}M^{\prime}=\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})

by
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)
-linear endomorphisms, given by

h\star(s\otimes m^{\prime})=s\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(h)^{-1}\otimes h\cdot m^{\prime}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad h\in H,s\in S,m^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}.

Inspecting this formula, we see that in fact it makes sense for any
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}
, not necessarily
J
-stable, because we can view
\beta(h)
as an element of
\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})^{\times}
and then consider its image under the
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)^{\times}
. We will now study the module of coinvariants
(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M)_{H}
with respect to this secret
H
-action.

Proposition 5.3.7
.

Whenever
n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
,
X\mapsto\left(\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\right)_{J_{n+1}}
is a sheaf on
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-modules on
\mathbb{D}_{n}
, with vanishing higher Čech cohomology.

Proof.

Whenever
Y\subseteq X
are members of
\mathbb{D}_{n}
, the restriction maps

\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}_{n}(Y)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})

are equivariant with respect to the secret action, so we do have a presheaf of left
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-modules. By Theorem
4.3.7
we may choose an algebraic generator
\dot{z}
for
\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})
with associated rational function
u
. We write
r:=R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)
for brevity. By Corollary
4.3.12
, we have an exact sequence of
\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})
-modules

0\to\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot r}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\to 0.

Applying the functor
\mathscr{D}_{n}\otimes_{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}-
to this sequence, we obtain the following sequence of presheaves of
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-modules on
\mathbb{D}_{n}
:

0\to\mathscr{D}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot r}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\to 0

which is exact because
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)
is a flat right
\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})
-module by Lemma
4.3.10
(a,b) for any
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}
. Let
\mathcal{X}
be a
\mathbb{D}_{n}
-covering. Then

0\to C^{\aug}(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{D}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot r}}{{\longrightarrow}}C^{\aug}(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{D}_{n})\to C^{\aug}(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D}))\to 0

is an exact sequence of augmented Čech complexes. Since the first two of these complexes is exact by Theorem
2.3.10
, so is the third. Now,
\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})
carries a secret
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-linear
J_{n+1}
-action, and the operation of taking
J_{n+1}
-coinvariants is exact since
K
is a field of characteristic zero, and the action of
J_{n+1}
factors through the finite group
J_{n+1}/J_{n+2}
by Lemma
5.1.6
. Therefore the complex
C^{\aug}(\mathcal{X},(\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D}))_{J_{n+1}})
is exact, and the result follows.
∎

We denote this sheaf by
\left(\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\right)_{J_{n+1}}
.

Lemma 5.3.8
.

The comparison map
(\ref{CompMap})
induces a morphism

\Xi:\left(\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\right)_{J_{n+1}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n}

of sheaves of
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-modules on
\mathbb{D}_{n}
, whenever
n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
.

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}
. The comparison map from
(\ref{CompMap})
factors through the
J_{n+1}
-coinvariants of
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\otimes_{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})
, because
\beta(h)^{-1}h
fixes
\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)
for any
h\in J_{n+1}
by Theorem
5.3.6
, applied with
J_{n+1}
instead of
J
.
∎

Our goal is now to show that under a certain mild additional condition on the group
J
, the connecting map
\Xi
is in fact an isomorphism. We will verify that this is the case
locally
on the
G
-topology
\mathbb{D}_{n}
. We will use a particular covering of
\mathbb{D}
, similar to the covering of
\mathbb{A}
appearing in Lemma
4.2.20
above.

Definition 5.3.9
.

For each
a\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
define

B_{a,n}:=C_{K}(a;\pi^{n})\quad\mbox{and}\quad W_{a,n}:=C_{K}(a,b_{1},\ldots,b_{q-1};\pi_{F}^{n},\ldots,\pi_{F}^{n})

where
b_{1},\ldots,b_{q-1}\in a+\pi_{F}^{n}\mathcal{O}_{F}
are chosen so that

\pi^{n}\mathcal{O}_{F}/\pi^{n+1}\mathcal{O}_{F}=\{\pi_{F}^{n+1}\mathcal{O}_{F},(b_{1}-a)+\pi^{n+1}_{F}\mathcal{O}_{F},(b_{q-1}-a)+\pi^{n+1}_{F}\mathcal{O}_{F}\}.

We also define
\mathcal{U}_{n}=\{\Upsilon_{n},W_{a,n}\mid a\in\mathcal{O}_{F}\}
.

Recall here that
\Upsilon_{n}=\mathbb{D}\backslash\bigcup\limits_{a\in\mathcal{O}_{F}}\{|z-a|<|\pi_{F}|^{n}\}
— see Definition
4.3.2
(a,b).

We note that
W_{a,n}
does not depend on the choices of
b_{1},\ldots,b_{q-1}
and both
W_{a,n}
and
B_{a,n}
only depend on the image of
a
in
\mathcal{O}_{F}/\pi^{n+1}\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Thus
|\mathcal{U}_{n}|=h_{n}+1
. We also note that
W_{a,n}\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}=a+\pi^{n+1}\mathcal{O}_{F}
by construction.

Lemma 5.3.10
.

\mathcal{U}_{n}
is an
\mathbb{D}_{n}/I_{n+1}
-admissible covering of
\mathbb{D}
.

Proof.

Let
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{h_{n}}\}
be a set of coset representatives for
\mathcal{O}_{F}/\pi^{n+1}\mathcal{O}_{F}
so that
\mathcal{U}_{n}=\{W_{a_{1}},\ldots,W_{a_{h_{n}}},\Upsilon_{n}\}
. Because
\min\limits_{i\neq j}|a_{i}-a_{j}|=|\pi_{F}|^{n}
, it follows from Lemma
4.2.20
that
\mathcal{U}_{n}
is indeed a
\mathbb{D}_{n}
-admissible covering of
\mathbb{D}
, so it remains to verify that each of its members is
I_{n+1}
-stable.

Now
\Upsilon_{n}
itself is
I_{n+1}
-stable: it is even stable under the Iwahori subgroup
\mathbb{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F})\cap\mathcal{G}
because it permutes connected components of
\mathbb{D}-V_{n}
.

Fix
g\in I_{n+1}
, and
a,b_{1},\ldots,b_{q-1}
in
\mathcal{O}_{F}
so that

W_{a,n}=C_{K}(a,b_{1},\ldots,b_{q-1};\pi_{F}^{n},\ldots,\pi_{F}^{n}).

Moreover let
z\in W_{a,n}(\overline{K})
. Then
|z-a|\leqslant|\pi_{F}|^{n}
and
|z-b_{j}|\geqslant|\pi_{F}|^{n}
for each
j=1,\ldots,q-1
. Because each entry of
g-1
lies in
\pi_{F}^{n+1}\mathcal{O}_{F}
, we see that

|g\cdot z-z|\leqslant|\pi_{F}^{n+1}|.

Hence
|g\cdot z-a|\leqslant|\pi_{F}|^{n}
and
|g\cdot z-b_{j}|=|z-b_{j}|\geqslant|\pi_{F}|^{n}
for each
j=1,\ldots q-1
. We conclude that
g\cdot z\in W_{a,n}(\overline{K})
as well. So
W_{a,n}
is
I_{n+1}
-stable.∎

Definition 5.3.11
.

We introduce the following standard subgroup of
\GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
:

N:=\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathcal{O}_{F}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\quad\leqslant\quad\GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F}).

We can now state the main technical result of this part of the paper.

We assume until the end of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CoadDrin}
that the field
K
is discretely valued.

Theorem 5.3.12
.

Fix
n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
,
a\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
and write
W:=W_{a,n}
. Assume that
d\mid(q+1)
and
N_{n+1}\leqslant J_{n+1}
. Then the following comparison map is an isomorphism:

\Xi(W):\left(\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)\right)_{J_{n+1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{L}_{n}(W).

The proof of Theorem
5.3.12
will occupy the entirety of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{PfOfCompOnW}
and can be found at the end of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{PfOfCOW}
.

In the remainder of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CompSect}
we will use Theorem
5.3.12
to derive some important consequences. To do this, we need two more technical Lemmas.

Lemma 5.3.13
.

Let
Y\subseteq X
both lie in
\mathbb{D}_{n}
. Then the action map

\mathscr{D}_{n}(Y)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{M}(X)\longrightarrow\mathscr{M}(Y)

is an isomorphism when
\mathscr{M}
is one of the following
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-modules:

(1)

\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{L}_{n}
, or

(2)

\mathscr{M}=\left(\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\right)_{J_{n+1}}
and
n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
.

Proof.

(a) Using Theorem
4.3.7
, choose an algebraic generator
\dot{z}
for
\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
with associated rational function
u
, write
r:=R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)
and consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathscr{D}_{n}(Y)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)}{\otimes}\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot r}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}_{n}(Y)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)}{\otimes}\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}_{n}(Y)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)}{\otimes}\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}_{n}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot r}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}_{n}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{L}_{n}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

The rows are exact by Corollary
4.3.12
. The first two vertical maps are isomorphisms, so the third vertical map is also an isomorphism by the Five Lemma. This deals with the case
\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{L}_{n}
.

(b) Now, using Theorem
4.3.7
again, choose an algebraic generator
\dot{z}
for
\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})
with associated rational function
u
. Write
D:=\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)
,
D^{\prime}:=\mathscr{D}_{n}(Y)
,
r:=R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)
and
H:=J_{n+1}
for brevity, and let
\epsilon\in K[J_{n+1}/J_{n+2}]
be the principal idempotent — the average of all the group elements of the finite group
J_{n+1}/J_{n+2}
. Then the sequence
D/Dr\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1-\epsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}D/Dr\longrightarrow(D/Dr)_{H}\to 0
is exact, so the first row in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{D^{\prime}\underset{D}{\otimes}{}\frac{D}{Dr}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\hskip 2.84544pt\otimes\hskip 2.84544pt(1-\epsilon)\star}
\textstyle{D^{\prime}\underset{D}{\otimes}{}\frac{D}{Dr}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D^{\prime}\underset{D}{\otimes}{}(\frac{D}{Dr})_{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\frac{D^{\prime}}{D^{\prime}r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1-\epsilon)\star}
\textstyle{\frac{D^{\prime}}{D^{\prime}r}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\frac{D^{\prime}}{D^{\prime}r})_{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

is exact. The second row is exact for the same reason, and the first two vertical maps are isomorphisms. Hence the third map is an isomorphism by the Five Lemma.

By Corollary
4.3.12
, we have isomorphisms
\mathscr{M}(X)\cong(D/Dr)_{H}
and
\mathscr{M}(Y)\cong(D^{\prime}/D^{\prime}r)_{H}
. Hence
\mathscr{D}_{n}(Y)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{M}(X)\longrightarrow\mathscr{M}(Y)
is also an isomorphism. ∎

Lemma 5.3.14
.

For each
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}
, the restriction map
\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(X)
induces a natural
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)
-linear isomorphism

\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(X)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(X).

Proof.

By Lemma
5.3.13
(a) applied with
n
replaced by
n+1
, the action map

\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(X)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(X)

is an isomorphism. Now apply the functor
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(X)}{\otimes}{}-
to this isomorphism, and use the associativity of tensor product.
∎

Next, we deal with the ‘easy’ member of the covering
\mathcal{U}_{n}
, namely
\Upsilon_{n}
.

Proposition 5.3.15
.

The comparison map

\Xi(\Upsilon_{n}):\left(\mathscr{D}_{n}(\Upsilon_{n})\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\Upsilon_{n})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\Upsilon_{n})\right)_{J_{n+1}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n}(\Upsilon_{n})

is an isomorphism for each
n\geqslant 0
.

Proof.

The map
\Xi(\Upsilon_{n})
appears in the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathscr{D}_{n}(\Upsilon_{n})\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\Upsilon_{n})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\Upsilon_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{L}_{n}(\Upsilon_{n})}
\textstyle{\left(\mathscr{D}_{n}(\Upsilon_{n})\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\Upsilon_{n})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\Upsilon_{n})\right)_{J_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(\Upsilon_{n})}

Now,
\varphi
is non-zero and
\mathscr{L}_{n}(\Upsilon_{n})
is simple as a
\mathscr{D}_{n}(\Upsilon_{n})
-module by Corollary
4.2.13
, so
\varphi
must be surjective. On the other hand,
\mathscr{L}_{n}(\Upsilon_{n})
is a free
\mathcal{O}(\Upsilon_{n})
-module of rank
1
by Corollary
4.2.13
, and examining the proof of Corollary
4.2.13
we see that
\mathscr{D}_{n}(\Upsilon_{n})\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\Upsilon_{n})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\Upsilon_{n})
is a free
\mathcal{O}(\Upsilon_{n})
-module of rank one as well. Because a surjective
\mathcal{O}(\Upsilon_{n})
-linear map between two such modules must be injective, we conclude that
\varphi
is an isomorphism.

It follows that the vertical map is injective. Since it is clearly surjective, it is an isomorphism. Therefore
\Xi(\Upsilon_{n})
is also an isomorphism.
∎

The next two statements are the main results of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CompSect}
.

Theorem 5.3.16
.

Assume that
n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
,
N_{n+1}\leqslant J_{n+1}
and
d\mid(q+1)
. Then the comparison map from Lemma
5.3.8

\Xi:\left(\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\right)_{J_{n+1}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n}

is an isomorphism of sheaves of
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-modules on
\mathbb{D}_{n}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{U}_{n}
is a covering of
\mathbb{D}
by Lemma
5.3.10
and since
\Xi
is a map of sheaves, it is enough to show
\Xi_{|Y}
is an isomorphism for each
Y\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
. After Lemma
5.3.13
, it is enough to check that
\Xi(Y)
is an isomorphism for each
Y\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
. However in view of Lemma
5.3.14
, this follows from Proposition
5.3.15
and Theorem
5.3.12
.
∎

Corollary 5.3.17
.

Assume that
n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
,
N_{n+1}\leqslant J_{n+1}
and
d\mid(q+1)
. Then for each
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
, the action map

\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\underset{J_{n+1}}{\rtimes}{}J\quad\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\underset{J_{n+2}}{\rtimes}{}J}{\otimes}{}\quad\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\quad\longrightarrow\quad\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)

is an
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\underset{J_{n+1}}{\rtimes}{}J
-linear isomorphism.

Proof.

The action map in question is an instance of the map
\widetilde{\tau}
from (
39
); hence it is
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\rtimes_{J_{n+1}}J
-linear. It appears as the diagonal map in the following commutative triangle, where the vertical arrow is the isomorphism from Theorem
5.1.8
:

\textstyle{\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\underset{J_{n+1}}{\rtimes}{}J\quad\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\underset{J_{n+2}}{\rtimes}{}J}{\otimes}{}\quad\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\left(\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\right)_{J_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Xi(X)}
\textstyle{\mathscr{L}_{n}(X).}

Since
\Xi(X)
is an isomorphism by Theorem
5.3.16
, this diagonal map must also be an isomorphism. ∎

6.
Microlocal analysis

6.1.
Some microlocal rings

We fix an affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{A}
in this section. Recall from Definition
2.3.1
that
r(X)
denotes the spectral seminorm of
\partial_{x}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))
and recall the notation
A\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
from Definition
2.2.1
.

Definition 6.1.1
.

Define
\mathcal{E}_{[s,r]}(X):=\mathcal{O}(X)\langle\partial/r,s/\partial\rangle
for every
r\geqslant s\geqslant r(X)
.

By Theorem
2.2.6
,
\mathcal{E}_{[s,r]}(X)
is an associative
K
-Banach algebra with the norm

(42)

\left|\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{n}\partial^{n}\right|=\sup\limits_{s\leqslant t\leqslant r}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|a_{n}|t^{n}

which contains an inverse
\partial^{-1}
for
\partial
. By Lemma
2.2.9
, there is a natural isometric embedding of
K
-Banach algebras
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{[s,r]}(X)
given by regarding a power series in
\partial
as a Laurent series with zero negative terms.

Now let
u\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
be a unit as in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{DivTwDer}
, let
d
be a non-zero integer coprime to
p
and let
z
satisfy the equation
z^{d}=u
. We will show that each
\mathcal{E}_{[s,r]}(X)
contains an inverse that we will denote by
\xi_{u,d}
to the twisted derivation
\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x})
from
\lx@sectionsign\ref{DivTwDer}
. Recall the coefficients
h_{u,d}^{[n]}=\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x}^{[n]})(1)=z\partial_{x}^{[n]}(z^{-1})
from Definition
2.4.5
.

Lemma 6.1.2
.

For every
\ell\geqslant 0
we have

(1)

(\ell+1)h_{u,d}^{[\ell+1]}=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\ell}h_{u,d}^{[n]}\partial_{x}^{[\ell-n]}(h_{u,d}^{[1]})
, and

(2)

(\ell+1)h_{u,d}^{[\ell+1]}=\partial_{x}(h_{u,d}^{[\ell]})+h_{u,d}^{[1]}h_{u,d}^{[\ell]}
.

Proof.

(a) By the Leibniz rule,
\partial_{x}^{[\ell]}(ab)=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\ell}\partial_{x}^{[n]}(a)\partial_{x}^{[\ell-n]}(b)
. Applying this with
a=z^{-1}
and
b=z\partial_{x}(z^{-1})=h_{u,d}^{[1]}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{lll}(\ell+1)h_{u,d}^{[\ell+1]}&=&z\partial_{x}^{[\ell]}(\partial_{x}(z^{-1}))\\
&=&z\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\ell}\partial_{x}^{[n]}(z^{-1})\hskip 2.84544pt\partial_{x}^{[\ell-n]}(h_{u,d}^{[1]})\\
&=&\hskip 2.84544pt\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\ell}h_{u,d}^{[n]}\partial_{x}^{[\ell-n]}(h_{u,d}^{[1]}).\end{array}

(b) We expand the right hand side:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\partial_{x}(h_{u,d}^{[\ell]})+h_{u,d}^{[1]}h_{u,d}^{[\ell]}&=&\partial_{x}(z\partial_{x}^{[\ell]}(z^{-1}))+z\partial_{x}(z^{-1})\cdot z\partial_{x}^{[\ell]}(z^{-1})\\
&=&\partial_{x}(z)\partial_{x}^{[\ell]}(z^{-1})+z\partial_{x}(\partial_{x}^{[\ell]}(z^{-1}))-z^{-1}\partial_{x}(z)\cdot z\partial_{x}^{[\ell]}(z^{-1})\\
&=&(\ell+1)h_{u,d}^{[\ell+1]}.\end{array}

We have used here that
z\partial_{x}(z^{-1})+z^{-1}\partial_{x}(z)=\partial_{x}(z^{-1}\cdot z)=\partial_{x}(1)=0
. ∎

Definition 6.1.3
.

Define
\xi_{u,d}:=\sum\limits_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}(\xi_{u,d})_{n}\partial^{n}\in\prod\limits_{n=-\infty}^{-1}\mathcal{O}(X)\partial^{n}
, where

(\xi_{u,d})_{-n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!h_{u,d}^{[n-1]}&\mbox{if }n\geqslant 1,\\
0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Lemma 6.1.4
.

The element
\xi_{u,d}
lies in
\mathcal{E}_{[s,r]}(X)
whenever
r\geqslant s>r(X)
.

Proof.

Since
(\xi_{u,d})_{n}=0
for any
n\geqslant 0
, by Definition
2.2.1
it is enough to prove that for if
s\leqslant t\leqslant r
, then
|(\xi_{u,d})_{-n}|t^{-n}\to 0
as
n\to+\infty
. Now for
n\geqslant 1
we have

\frac{|(\xi_{u,d})_{-n}|}{t^{n}}=\frac{|(n-1)!h_{u,d}^{[n-1]}|}{t^{n}}\leqslant\frac{|(n-1)!|}{t^{n}}\left(\frac{r(X)}{\varpi}\right)^{n-1}\leqslant\frac{p(n-1)}{t}\left(\frac{r(X)}{t}\right)^{n-1}

by Lemma
2.3.16
and Proposition
2.4.7
. This converges to zero as
t\geqslant s>r(X)
.
∎

Proposition 6.1.5
.

Suppose that
r\geqslant s>r(X)
. Then
\xi_{u,d}
is a two-sided inverse to
\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x})
in
\mathcal{E}_{[s,r]}(X)
.

Proof.

First note that
\xi_{u,d}
does lie in
\mathcal{E}_{[s,r]}(X)
by Lemma
6.1.4
. Let
a=\xi_{u,d}
and
b=\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x})
so that

a_{-n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!h_{u,d}^{[n-1]}&\quad\mbox{if}\quad n\geqslant 1\\
0&\quad\mbox{otherwise,}\end{array}\right.\quad b_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}h_{u,d}^{[1]}&\quad\mbox{if}\quad j=0,\\
1&\quad\mbox{if}\quad j=1,\\
0&\quad\mbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Note that
\binom{-n}{m}=(-1)^{m}\binom{n+m-1}{m}
for any
n,m\geqslant 0
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
; then using
(\ref{MicroMult})
we have

\begin{array}[]{lll}a\ast_{-1-\ell}b&=&\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{-n}\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\binom{-n}{m}\partial_{x}^{m}(b_{m+n-1-\ell})\\
&=&\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!h_{u,d}^{[n-1]}\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{m}\binom{n+m-1}{m}\partial_{x}^{m}(b_{m+n-1-\ell})\\
&=&\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}h_{u,d}^{[n]}\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n+m}(n+m)!\partial_{x}^{[m]}(b_{m+n-\ell}).\end{array}

Now since
b_{j}
vanishes unless
j\in\{0,1\}
and since
m+n\geqslant 0
in this sum, we see that this expression vanishes whenever
\ell\leqslant-2
, i.e.
-1-\ell\geqslant 1
. When
\ell=-1
(so that
-\ell-1=0
),
m+n-\ell\in\{0,1\}
forces
m=n=0
and the expression collapses to give
1
. So we will assume that
\ell\geqslant 0
. Now we still have to have
m+n-\ell\in\{0,1\}
otherwise
b_{n+m-\ell}
vanishes. If
m+n-\ell=1
then
\partial_{x}^{[m]}(b_{m+n-\ell})=\partial_{x}^{[m]}(1)=0
unless
m=0
; so this happens only when
m=0
and
n=\ell+1
; in this case we get a contribution of

h_{u,d}^{[\ell+1]}(-1)^{\ell+1}(\ell+1)!

to the big sum. On the other hand, if
m+n-\ell=0
then
m=\ell-n\geqslant 0
forces
n\leqslant\ell
, and we obtain a contribution of

\sum_{n=0}^{\ell}h_{u,d}^{[n]}(-1)^{\ell}\ell!\partial_{x}^{[\ell-n]}(h_{u,d}^{[1]})

to the big sum. This is
(-1)^{\ell}\ell!(\ell+1)h_{u,d}^{[\ell+1]}=(-1)^{\ell}(\ell+1)!h_{u,d}^{[\ell+1]}
by Lemma
6.1.2
(a) and it therefore cancels with the first term. This proves that

a\ast_{k}b=\delta_{k,0}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad k\in\mathbb{Z}

and therefore
\xi_{u,d}\cdot\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x})=1
. Showing that
\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x})\cdot\xi_{u,d}=1
using
(\ref{MicroMult})
is similar but much easier, and reduces to Lemma
6.1.2
(b). We leave the details to the reader.
∎

Definition 6.1.6
.

For each affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{A}
, we define the
Robba ring

\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X):=\bigcup\limits_{r>r(X)}\bigcap\limits_{r(X)<s\leqslant r}\mathcal{E}_{[s,r]}(X).

Corollary 6.1.7
.

\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x})
is a unit in
\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X)
with inverse
\xi_{u,d}
.

Proof.

We know that
u=z^{d}
is a unit in
\mathcal{O}(X)
. Now apply Proposition
6.1.5
to see that whenever
r(X)<s\leqslant r
,
\xi_{u,d}\in\mathcal{E}_{[s,r]}(X)
is a two-sided inverse to
\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x})
in
\mathcal{E}_{[s,r]}
. It follows that

\xi_{u,d}\in\bigcap\limits_{r(X)<s\leqslant r}\mathcal{E}_{[s,r]}(X)\subset\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X).\qed

6.2.
The characteristic cycle

We now specialise to the case where
X\subseteq\mathbb{D}
is a cheese with
\rho(X)=1
, and the ground field
K
is discretely valued, with uniformiser
\pi_{K}
and residue field
k
. In this case,
\mathscr{X}:=\Spf\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
is a smooth affine formal scheme, whose special fibre
\mathscr{X}_{0}
is the complement of finitely many points in the affine line over
k
. In
[
2
]
, Tomoyuki Abe defined several sheaves of microlocal rings

\pi^{-1}\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m)}\subset\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m,{\dagger})}\subset\cdots\subset\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m,m+2)}\subset\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m,m+1)}\subset\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m,m)}=\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m)}

of level
m
on the cotangent bundle
T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}
of
\mathscr{X}_{0}
; here
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m)}
denotes Berthelot’s sheaf of level-
m
arithmetic differential operators on
\mathscr{X}_{0}
and
\pi:T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}\to\mathscr{X}_{0}
denotes the structure morphism of this cotangent bundle. The definition of these sheaves uses the microlocalization of
\pi^{-1}\gr\mathscr{D}^{(m)}_{\mathscr{X}}
on the level-
m
cotangent bundle
T^{(m)\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}
of
\mathscr{X}
,
[
2
, §2.2, 2.4]
, together an identification of
T^{(m)\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}
with
T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}
which is carried out in
[
2
, §3.3]
.

Recall the numbers
\varpi_{m}=(p^{m})!^{\frac{1}{p^{m}}}\in\overline{K}
from Notation
3.1.8
.

Definition 6.2.1
.

(1)

For each
m\geqslant 0
, write
r_{m}:=|\varpi_{m}|=|(p^{m})!|^{\frac{1}{p^{m}}}
.

(2)

Let
\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{X}_{0}}=T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}\backslash s(\mathscr{X}_{0})
denote the complement of the zero-section
s(\mathscr{X}_{0})
in the cotangent bundle
T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}
of
\mathscr{X}_{0}
.

Our next result explains the relationship between the microlocal rings
\mathcal{E}_{[s,r]}(X)
that appeared in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{MicRings}
and Abe’s rings.

Proposition 6.2.2
.

Let
m^{\prime}\geqslant m\geqslant 0
and let
X\in\mathbb{D}(\partial_{x}/\varpi)^{\dagger}
. Then the embedding
\mathcal{O}(X)\hookrightarrow\Gamma(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{X}_{0}},\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m,m^{\prime})})
extends to an injective bounded
K
-algebra map

\mathcal{E}_{[r_{m^{\prime}},r_{m}]}(X)\hookrightarrow\Gamma(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{X}_{0}},\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m,m^{\prime})}).

This extension is functorial in
X\in\mathbb{D}(\partial_{x}/\varpi)^{\dagger}
.

To prove this, we will need to recall the explicit description of Abe’s ring appearing on the right hand side as a set of Laurent series in
\partial
satisfying a particular convergence condition. This, unfortunately, requires some detailed notation involving Berthelot’s modified divided powers
\partial^{\langle n\rangle_{(m)}}
, as well as their
inverses
.

Notation 6.2.3
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

Write
q_{n}:=\left\lfloor n/p^{m}\right\rfloor
and
\partial^{\langle n\rangle_{(m)}}:=\frac{q_{n}!}{n!}\partial^{n}
.

(2)

Let
i_{n}:=\left\lceil n/p^{m}\right\rceil
and
\ell_{n}=i_{n}p^{m}-n
and define

\partial^{\langle-n\rangle_{(m)}}:=\partial^{\langle\ell_{n}\rangle_{(m)}}\left(\partial^{\langle i_{n}p^{m}\rangle_{(m)}}\right)^{-1}=\frac{i_{n}!}{\ell_{n}!(i_{n}p^{m})!}\partial^{-n}

with the product understood to be carried out in
\Gamma(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{X}_{0}},\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m)})
.

This notation comes from
[
3
, p. 282]
.

Lemma 6.2.4
.

Let
0\leqslant m\leqslant m^{\prime}
. We have the following explicit descriptions:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\Gamma(\mathscr{X},\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m)})&=&\left\{\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}a_{n}\partial^{\langle n\rangle_{(m)}}:a_{n}\in\mathcal{O}(X),\lim\limits_{n\to+\infty}a_{n}=0\right\},\\
\Gamma(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{X}_{0}},\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m)})&=&\left\{\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{n}\partial^{\langle n\rangle_{(m)}}:a_{n}\in\mathcal{O}(X),\hskip 2.84544pt\sup\limits_{n<0}|a_{n}|<\infty,\hskip 2.84544pt\lim\limits_{n\to+\infty}a_{n}=0\right\},\\
\Gamma(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{X}_{0}},\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m,m^{\prime})})&=&\left\{\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{n}\partial^{n}:\sum\limits_{n<0}a_{n}\partial^{n}\in\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m^{\prime})},\sum\limits_{n\geqslant 0}a_{n}\partial^{n}\in\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m)}\right\}.\end{array}

Proof.

This follows from
[
13
, (2.4.1.2)]
and
[
3
, §1.1.1, §1.1.2]
.
∎

Lemma 6.2.5
.

For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, there exists
\epsilon^{(m)}_{n}\in\overline{K}
such that

(43)

\left(\frac{\partial}{\varpi_{m}}\right)^{n}=\epsilon^{(m)}_{n}\partial^{\langle n\rangle_{(m)}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad 1\leqslant|\epsilon^{(m)}_{n}|\leqslant|p|^{-m}.

Proof.

Fix
m\geqslant 0
. For every
n\geqslant 0
we define

\epsilon^{(m)}_{-n}:=\frac{\varpi_{m}^{n}\hskip 2.84544pt\ell_{n}!\hskip 2.84544pti_{n}!}{(i_{n}\hskip 2.84544ptp^{m})!}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\epsilon^{(m)}_{n}=\frac{\hskip 2.84544ptn!}{\varpi_{m}^{n}q_{n}!}.

Note that
\partial^{\langle\ell_{n}\rangle_{(m)}}
is simply
\frac{\partial^{\ell_{n}}}{\ell_{n}!}
because
0\leqslant\ell_{n}<p^{m}
. Now we can compute

\begin{array}[]{lll}\epsilon^{(m)}_{-n}\partial^{\langle-n\rangle_{(m)}}&=&\epsilon^{(m)}_{-n}\cdot\partial^{\langle\ell_{n}\rangle_{(m)}}\cdot\left(\partial^{\langle i_{n}p^{m}\rangle_{(m)}}\right)^{-1}=\\
&=&\frac{\varpi_{m}^{n}\hskip 2.84544pt\ell_{n}!\hskip 2.84544pti_{n}!}{(i_{n}\hskip 2.84544ptp^{m})!}\cdot\frac{\partial^{\ell_{n}}}{\ell_{n}!}\cdot\left(\frac{i_{n}!\partial^{i_{n}p^{m}}}{(i_{n}p^{m})!}\right)^{-1}=\\
&=&\varpi_{m}^{n}\partial^{\ell_{n}-i_{n}p^{m}}=\left(\frac{\partial}{\varpi_{m}}\right)^{-n},\quad\mbox{and}\\
\epsilon^{(m)}_{n}\partial^{\langle n\rangle_{(m)}}&=&\frac{n!}{\varpi_{m}^{n}q_{n}!}\cdot\frac{q_{n}!\partial^{n}}{n!}=\left(\frac{\partial}{\varpi_{m}}\right)^{n}.\end{array}

Therefore regardless of the sign of
n
, the first equality in
(\ref{Epsilon})
holds.

Next, using the fact that
(p-1)v_{p}(a!)=a-s_{p}(a)
for any
a\in\mathbb{N}
where
s_{p}(a)
denotes the sum the
p
-adic digits of
a
, we compute

\begin{array}[]{lll}(p-1)v_{p}(\epsilon^{(m)}_{-n})&=&\frac{n}{p^{m}}(p^{m}-1)+(\ell_{n}-s_{p}(\ell_{n}))+(i_{n}-s_{p}(i_{n}))-(i_{n}p^{m}-s_{p}(i_{n}p^{m}))=\\
&=&-\frac{n}{p^{m}}+n+(\ell_{n}-i_{n}p^{m})+i_{n}+s_{p}(i_{n}p^{m})-s_{p}(i_{n})-s_{p}(\ell_{n})=\\
&=&\frac{\ell_{n}}{p^{m}}-s_{p}(\ell_{n})\geqslant 0-(p-1)m\end{array}

because
\ell_{n}-i_{n}p^{m}=-n
,
0\leqslant\ell_{n}<p^{m}
and because
s_{p}(i_{n}p^{m})=s_{p}(i_{n})
.

Similarly, writing
n=\alpha_{n}+p^{m}q_{n}
we compute

\begin{array}[]{lll}(p-1)v_{p}(\epsilon^{(m)}_{n})&=&(n-s_{p}(n))-\frac{n}{p^{m}}(p^{m}-1)-(q_{n}-s_{p}(q_{n}))=\\
&=&\frac{n}{p^{m}}-(s_{p}(\alpha_{n})+s_{p}(q_{n}))-q_{n}+s_{p}(q_{n})=\\
&=&\frac{\alpha_{n}}{p^{m}}-s_{p}(\alpha_{n})\geqslant 0-(p-1)m\end{array}

because
0\leqslant\alpha_{n}<p^{m}
and
s_{p}(n)=s_{p}(\alpha_{n})+s_{p}(q_{n})
. The result follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.2.2
.

We will apply Proposition
2.2.7
. Set
A=\mathcal{O}(X)=\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{X})_{K}
,
\delta=\partial_{x}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))
,
s:=r_{m^{\prime}}\leqslant r_{m}=:r
,
B:=\Gamma(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{X}_{0}},\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m,m^{\prime})})
and
b:=\partial\in B
. Note that
B
is a
K
-Banach algebra with a norm
|\cdot|_{B}
extending the standard norm on
K
and unit ball given by
