\Gamma(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{X}_{0}},\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X}}^{(m,m^{\prime})})
: more precisely this norm on
B
is determined by the following relations:

(44)

|\partial^{\langle n\rangle_{(m)}}|_{B}=|\partial^{\langle-n\rangle_{(m^{\prime})}}|_{B}=1\quad\mbox{for all}\quad n\geqslant 0.

We must verify the conditions of Proposition
2.2.7
, of which (i) and (ii) are clear. Thus it remains to show that

\sup\limits_{n\geqslant 0}|\partial^{n}|_{B}/r_{m}^{n}<\infty\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sup\limits_{n\leqslant 0}|\partial^{n}|_{B}/r_{m^{\prime}}^{n}<\infty.

Since
|\varpi_{m}|=r_{m}
, both of these facts follow from Lemma
6.2.5
together with equation (
44
). Thus Proposition
2.2.7
gives the required bounded
K
-algebra homomorphism
\mathcal{E}_{[r_{m^{\prime}},r_{m}]}(X)=\mathcal{O}(X)\langle\partial/r_{m},r_{m^{\prime}}/\partial\rangle\to B
extending the inclusion
\mathcal{O}(X)\hookrightarrow B
. It is easy to see that this homomorphism is injective, as well as functorial for those affinoid subdomains
X
of
\mathbb{D}
with
\rho(X)=1
.∎

Now recall the notation
\lx@sectionsign\ref{KummerSect}
:

u=\lambda\prod_{i=1}^{h}(x-a_{i})^{k_{i}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad z^{d}=u

for some
k_{1},\ldots,k_{h}\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
, some
\lambda\in K^{\times}
and some pairwise distinct
a_{1},\ldots,a_{h}\in X(K)
. Our next goal is to study the following coherent
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(0)}
-module on
\mathscr{X}

\mathscr{M}^{(0)}:=\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(0)}/\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(0)}R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d).

We will now calculate its
characteristic variety

\Char^{(0)}(\mathscr{M}^{(0)})
, in the sense of
[
14
, §5.2.5]
,
[
6
, §5.14]
and
[
2
, §2.14]
.

Lemma 6.2.6
.

If
p\nmid d
, then
\Char^{(0)}(\mathscr{M}^{(0)})=\mathscr{X}_{0}\cup T^{\ast}_{\overline{a_{0}}}\mathscr{X}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup T^{\ast}_{\overline{a_{h}}}\mathscr{X}_{0}
.

Proof.

By Definition
4.2.10
(b), we know that

R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{h}(x-a_{i})\partial_{x}\hskip 2.84544pt-\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{1}{d}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{h}k_{i}\prod\limits_{j\neq i}(x-a_{j}).

Because
p\nmid d
, this is an element of
D:=\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{(0)}(\mathscr{X})
, and we see that the principal symbol of the image of
R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)
in
D/\pi_{K}D
is

(45)

\Gr R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)=\prod\limits_{i=1}^{h}(x-\overline{a_{i}})\cdot y

where we identify
\Gr D
with the polynomial ring
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{X}_{0})[y]
. Hence

\Char^{(0)}(\mathscr{M}^{(0)})=\Supp\left(\frac{\Gr D}{\Gr D\cdot\prod\limits_{i=1}^{h}(x-\overline{a_{i}})\cdot y}\right)=\mathscr{X}_{0}\cup T^{\ast}_{\overline{a_{1}}}\mathscr{X}_{0}\cup\cdots\cup T^{\ast}_{\overline{a_{h}}}\mathscr{X}_{0}.\qed

Write
\mathscr{Y}_{i}:=T^{\ast}_{\overline{a_{i}}}\mathscr{X}_{0}
for each
i=1,\ldots,h
. We assume without loss of generality that
\mathscr{Y}_{1},\ldots,\mathscr{Y}_{g}
are pairwise distinct and that every other
\mathscr{Y}_{i}
equals
\mathscr{Y}_{j}
for some
j\leqslant g
. Thus
|a_{i}-a_{j}|=1
whenever
1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant g
.

Proposition 6.2.7
.

Let
0\leqslant m\leqslant m^{\prime}
and write
\mathcal{E}:=\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m,m^{\prime})}
. Then

(1)

R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)
is a unit in
\mathcal{E}(T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}-\left(\mathscr{X}_{0}\cup\mathscr{Y}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{Y}_{h})\right)
,

(2)

R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)
is
not
a unit in
\mathcal{E}\left(T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}-(\mathscr{X}_{0}\cup\mathscr{Y}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\widehat{\mathscr{Y}_{i}}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{Y}_{g})\right)
for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant g
, and

(3)

R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)
is
not
a unit in
\mathcal{E}(T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}-\left(\mathscr{Y}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{Y}_{g}\right))
.

Proof.

(a) Let
\mathscr{U}=\mathscr{X}-\{\overline{a_{1}},\cdots,\overline{a_{h}}\}
so that

T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}-\left(\mathscr{X}_{0}\cup\mathscr{Y}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{Y}_{h})\right)=\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{U}_{0}}.

Now
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(u)}=\prod_{i=1}^{h}(x-a_{i})
is a polynomial in
x
which does not vanish on
\mathscr{U}_{\rig}
. Hence

\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(u)}\in\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{U}_{\rig})^{\times}\subset\left(\Gamma(\mathscr{U},\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m)})\right)^{\times}\subset\left(\Gamma(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{U}_{0}},\mathcal{E})\right)^{\times}.

On the other hand,
\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x})
is a unit in
\mathcal{E}_{[r_{m^{\prime}},r_{m}]}(\mathscr{U}_{\rig})
by Proposition
6.1.5
. Using Proposition
6.2.2
, we deduce that
\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x})
is also a unit in
\mathcal{E}(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{U}_{0}})
. Hence
R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)\in\mathcal{E}(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{U}_{0}})^{\times}
as claimed.

(b) Fix
i=1,\ldots,g
and let
\mathscr{V}:=\mathscr{U}\cup\{\overline{a_{i}}\}
, so that

T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}-\left(\mathscr{X}_{0}\cup\mathscr{Y}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\widehat{\mathscr{Y}_{i}}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{Y}_{g}\right)=\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{V}_{0}}

because the
\mathscr{Y}_{1},\ldots,\mathscr{Y}_{g}
are pairwise distinct. Now, by part (a),
R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)\in\mathcal{E}(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{U}_{0}})^{\times}
; we must show that
R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)^{-1}
does not lie in the subring
\mathcal{E}(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{V}_{0}})
of
\mathcal{E}(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{U}_{0}})
. Using Lemma
4.2.17
(b) and Proposition
6.1.5
we see that

R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)^{-1}=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(u)}^{-1}\theta_{u\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(u)}^{d},d}(\partial_{x})^{-1}=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(u)}^{-1}\xi_{u\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(u)}^{d},d}.

By Definition
6.1.3
, the coefficient of
\partial^{-1}
in this Laurent series is
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(u)}^{-1}
, which must lie in
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{V}_{\rig})
if
R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)^{-1}\in\mathcal{E}(\overset{\circ}{T}{}^{\ast}{\mathscr{V}_{0}})
. But
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(u)}
has a zero at
x=a_{i}\in\mathscr{V}_{\rig}
and therefore is not a unit in
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{V}_{\rig})
— a contradiction.

(c) This time we have
T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}-\left(\mathscr{Y}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{Y}_{g}\right)=T^{\ast}\mathscr{V}_{0}
, and

\mathcal{E}(T^{\ast}\mathscr{V}_{0})=\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m)}(\mathscr{U}).

If
R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)
is a unit in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m)}(\mathscr{U})
then since
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(u)}\in\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{U}_{\rig})^{\times}
, we see that
\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x})
has to be a unit in
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m)}(\mathscr{U})
as well. Choose
r\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\varpi<r<r_{m}
. Then
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m)}(\mathscr{U})
embeds naturally into
\mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathscr{U}_{\rig})
, so
\theta_{u,d}(\partial_{x})\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathscr{U}_{\rig})
. But the automorphism
\theta_{u,d}
of
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{U}_{\rig})
extends to a bounded
K
-algebra automorphism of
\mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathscr{U}_{\rig})
by Theorem
2.4.9
, and it follows that
\partial_{x}
is a unit in
\mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathscr{U}_{\rig})
which is not the case.
∎

Corollary 6.2.8
.

For any integers
m^{\prime}\geqslant m\geqslant 0
, we have

\Supp\left(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m,m^{\prime})}/\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m,m^{\prime})}R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)\right)=\mathscr{X}_{0}\cup\mathscr{Y}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathscr{Y}_{h}=\Char^{(0)}(\mathscr{M}^{(0)}).

Proof.

This follows immediately from Lemma
6.2.6
and Proposition
6.2.7
.
∎

We can now calculate the
characteristic cycle
of the module that we are really interested in, namely

\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}/\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d).

In the papers by Abe
[
1
]
and Abe-Marmora
[
3
]
, one finds at least two variants of the characteristic cycle, applicable for various kinds of modules. We are mainly interested in the cycle defined at
[
3
, §1.5.2]
for holonomic
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}
-modules
\mathscr{M}
, denoted
\Cycl(\mathscr{M})
. To calculate it, we will need the following notation.

Definition 6.2.9
.

Let
u=\lambda\prod_{i=1}^{h}(x-a_{i})^{k_{i}}
be given, with
\lambda\in K^{\times}
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{h}\in X(K)
pairwise distinct and
k_{1},\ldots,k_{h}
all non-zero. Arrange the
a_{i}
’s so that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{g}
satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

|a_{i}-a_{j}|=1
for all
1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant g
, and

(2)

for every
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant h
, there is a unique
i_{\ell}\in\{1,\ldots,g\}
such that
|a_{\ell}-a_{i_{\ell}}|<1
.

Then define for each
i=1,\ldots,g
the
i
-th multiplicity
as follows:

m_{\overline{a_{i}}}(u):=|\{1\leqslant\ell\leqslant h:i_{\ell}=i\}|.

Recall also that
\mathscr{Y}_{i}
denotes
T^{\ast}_{\overline{a_{i}}}\mathscr{X}_{0}
for each
i=1,\ldots,g
.

Theorem 6.2.10
.

Suppose that
p\nmid d
and that
u=\lambda\prod_{i=1}^{h}(x-a_{i})^{k_{i}}
is given. Then

\Cycl\left(\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}/\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)\right)=[\mathscr{X}_{0}]+\sum_{i=1}^{g}m_{\overline{a_{i}}}(u)[\mathscr{Y}_{i}].

Proof.

Let
\mathscr{M}^{(0)}:=\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(0)}/\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(0)}R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)
as above. It follows from Corollary
6.2.8
that

\Supp\left(\widehat{\mathscr{E}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(m,m^{\prime})}\underset{\pi^{-1}\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(0)}}{\otimes}{}\pi^{-1}\mathscr{M}^{(0)}\right)=\Char^{(0)}(\mathscr{M}^{(0)})

for any integers
m^{\prime}\geqslant m\geqslant 0
. This means that the holonomic
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(0)}
-module
\mathscr{M}^{(0)}
is
stable
in the sense of
[
3
, §1.3.8]
. Let
\mathscr{M}:=\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(0)}}\mathscr{M}^{(0)}
. Because we are assuming that each
a_{i}
is a
K
-rational point of
\mathscr{X}
, we can now apply
[
3
, Definition 1.5.2 and Proposition 1.4.3]
to see that

\Cycl(\mathscr{M})=\Cycl^{(0)}(\mathscr{M}^{(0)})

where
\Cycl^{(0)}(\mathscr{M}^{(0)})
is the characteristic cycle of the holonomic
\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}^{(0)}
-module
\mathscr{M}^{(0)}
defined at
[
1
, Definition 2.1.17]
. Using
[
1
, §2.1.12]
, we see that
\Cycl^{(0)}(\mathscr{M}^{(0)})
is the usual class of the coherent
\mathcal{O}(T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0})
-module
\mathcal{O}(T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0})/\Gr R_{\mathcal{S}(u)}(u,d)\cdot\mathcal{O}(T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0})
in the Grothendieck group of coherent
\mathcal{O}(T^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0})
-modules of dimension at most
1
. The result now follows in view of equation
(\ref{GrRz})
and Definition
6.2.9
.
∎

Remark 6.2.11
.

Recall from
\lx@sectionsign\ref{ConvNotn}
that
q
denotes the size of the residue field of
k
. Since
K
contains the finite field extension
F
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
whose residue field has size
q
— see
\lx@sectionsign\ref{ConvNotn}
— it follows that
K
contains a primitive
(q-1)
-th root of unity
\zeta
.

Example 6.2.12
.

Suppose that
0\in X(K)
, and let

w:=\frac{1}{(x^{q}-\pi_{F}^{q-1}x)^{k}}

for some non-zero integer
k
, so that

\mathcal{S}(w)=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{q}\}=\{0,\pi_{F},\pi_{F}\zeta,\pi_{F}\zeta^{2},\cdots,\pi_{F}\zeta^{q-2}\}\subset K.

Because
|\pi_{F}|<1
, all of these points map to the same point
\overline{0}
in
\mathscr{X}_{0}
under the reduction map
X(K)\to\mathscr{X}_{0}
. Therefore
g=1
and
m_{\overline{0}}(w)=q
. So, in this case Theorem
6.2.10
tells us that

\Cycl\left(\frac{\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathscr{X},\mathbb{Q}}R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)}\right)=[\mathscr{X}_{0}]+q[T^{\ast}_{0}\mathscr{X}_{0}].

6.3.
The differential equation
\nabla(\zeta)=1-c

Recall the admissible open subspaces
V_{n}
of
\mathbb{A}
from Definition
4.3.2
. We are interested here in the following three affinoid subdomains of
\mathbb{D}=\Sp K\langle x\rangle
, one containing the next:

•

X=\Sp K\langle x,\frac{1}{x^{q-1}-1}\rangle
,

•

X\cap V_{1}:=X-\bigcup\limits_{a\in\pi_{F}\mathcal{O}_{F}}\{|z-a|<|\pi_{F}|\}
, and

•

X\cap V_{0}=\Sp K\langle x,\frac{1}{x^{q}-x}\rangle=X-\{|z|<1\}
.

Viewed as subsets of
\mathbb{P}^{1,\an}
,
X
has
q
big holes (each of radius
1
),
X\cap V_{0}
has
q+1
big holes and
X\cap V_{1}
has
2q
holes, of which
q
are big and
q
are small (radius
|\pi_{F}|
). Note that we have the following relations:

X\cap V_{0}\subset X\cap V_{1}\subset X\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})\supset\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{1})\supset\mathcal{O}(X).

Here is a drawing of these three affinoid domains when
q=5
:
X\cap V_{0}
X\cap V_{1}
X

For future use, we record the basic invariants
\rho(Y)
and
r(Y)
from Definition
2.3.14
and Definition
2.3.1
for these cheeses in the following table, using the basic relationship
r(Y)=\varpi/\rho(Y)
given by Corollary
2.3.17
:

\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}Y&X\cap V_{0}&X\cap V_{1}&X\\
\hline\cr\rho(Y)&1&|\pi_{F}|&1\\
r(Y)&\varpi&\varpi/|\pi_{F}|&\varpi\end{array}

Fix an integer
d\geqslant 1
which divides
q+1
and an integer
k
such that
1\leqslant k\leqslant d
. We choose the following unit on
X\cap V_{1}
whose mod-
d
-divisor is appropriately invariant:

w:=\frac{1}{(x^{q}-\pi_{F}^{q-1}x)^{k}}.

Definition 6.3.1
.

Define
h=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\pi_{F}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{G}
.

Since
h\cdot x=x+\pi_{F}
by Lemma
5.2.2
(a), it follows from Definition
5.2.8
that
h\in\mathcal{G}_{r}
whenever
\varpi<r<\varpi/|\pi_{F}|
and that

\beta(h)=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\pi_{F}^{n}\partial_{x}^{[n]}\in\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}(X).

We begin with the first step of the proof of our main result of interest, namely Theorem
6.3.21
, as a way of motivating the calculations that follow.

Let
Z
be an admissible open subspace of a rigid analytic space
Y
and let
\mathcal{F}
be a sheaf on
Y
. We say that the local section
f\in\mathcal{F}(Z)

extends to
Y
if it lies in the image of the restriction map
\mathcal{F}(Y)\to\mathcal{F}(Z)
.

Proposition 6.3.2
.

Suppose that
\beta(h)\in\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|\pi_{F}|}(X)+\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)
. Then
(\xi_{w,d}\beta(h))_{0}\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})
extends to
X\cap V_{1}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|\pi_{F}|}(X)
is such that
\beta(h)-Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)
. Note that
\theta_{w,d}(\partial_{x})
is a unit in the Robba ring
\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X\cap V_{0})
with inverse
\xi_{w,d}
by Corollary
6.1.7
, because
w\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})^{\times}
. Now, in
\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X\cap V_{0})
we have the equation

\beta(h)\xi_{w,d}=(\beta(h)-Q)\xi_{w,d}+Q\xi_{w,d}

Comparing the constant term coefficients in this equation, we have

(46)

(\beta(h)\xi_{w,d})_{0}=((\beta(h)-Q)\xi_{w,d})_{0}+(Q\xi_{w,d})_{0}.

By Lemma
2.4.1
applied to
w\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})^{\times}
, we have

R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(w)}\theta_{w,d}(\partial_{x})

which implies that
R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)\hskip 2.84544pt\xi_{w,d}=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(w)}
inside
\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X\cap V_{0})
. Since
\beta(h)-Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)
by assumption, we conclude that

(47)

((\beta(h)-Q)\xi_{w,d})_{0}\in\mathcal{O}(X).

Since
\xi_{w,d}
also lies in
\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X\cap V_{1})
— again by Corollary
6.1.7
— and since

Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|\pi_{F}|}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|\pi_{F}|}(X\cap V_{1}),

by comparing the multiplication in
\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X\cap V_{0})
and
\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X\cap V_{1})
we see that

(48)

(Q\xi_{w,d})_{0}\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{1}).

Since
X\supset X\cap V_{1}
, (
46
), (
47
) and (
48
) imply that
(\beta(h)\xi_{w,d})_{0}
extends to
X\cap V_{1}
.

Next,
\beta(h)\in\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}(X\cap V_{0})^{\times}\subset\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X\cap V_{0})^{\times}
, so conjugation by
\beta(h)
defines an automorphism
S
of
\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X\cap V_{0})
given by
S(u):=\beta(h)^{-1}\hskip 2.84544ptu\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(h)
for any
u\in\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X\cap V_{0})
. Note that
S(\beta(h)\xi_{w,d})=\xi_{w,d}\beta(h)
. Since
\beta(h)=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\pi_{F}^{n}\partial_{x}^{[n]}
commutes with
\partial_{x}
, it follows from statement (b) in the proof of Theorem
5.2.11
that

S(u)=\beta(h)^{-1}\hskip 2.84544ptu\hskip 2.84544pt\beta(h)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(h^{-1}\cdot u_{i})\partial_{x}^{i}\quad\mbox{for any}\quad u=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}u_{i}\partial_{x}^{i}\in\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{E}}(X\cap V_{0}).

Because both
X\cap V_{1}
and
X\cap V_{0}
are
h
-stable, we see that

(\xi_{w,d}\beta(h))_{0}=S(\beta(h)\xi_{w,d})_{0}=h^{-1}\cdot(\beta(h)\xi_{w,d})_{0}

also extends to
X\cap V_{1}
, as desired. ∎

The key to our argument is that the rigid analytic function
(\xi_{w,d}\beta(h))_{0}
on
X\cap V_{0}
can be explicitly computed, and shown that in fact it
cannot
extend to
X\cap V_{1}
. To do this, we study the solutions of a certain differential equation satisfied by this function. Recall the units
c_{u,d}(g)
from Proposition
5.2.13
.

Lemma 6.3.3
.

Let
\nabla:=\theta_{w,d}(\partial_{x})\in\mathcal{D}(X\cap V_{0})
. Then

\nabla\left(\xi_{w,d}\beta(h))_{0}\right)=1-c_{w,d}(h^{-1}).

Proof.

The constant term of the product
\xi_{w,d}\beta(h)
is given by formula
(\ref{MicroMult})
:

(\xi_{w,d}\beta(h))_{0}=\xi_{w,d}\ast_{0}\beta(h)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}(\xi_{w,d})_{i}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\binom{i}{m}\partial_{x}^{m}(\beta(h)_{m-i}).

Since all coefficients of
\beta(h)
are scalars, we see that
\partial_{x}^{m}(\beta(h)_{m-i})
is non-zero only when
m=0
. Changing the summation to
n=-i
and using Definition
6.1.3
, this sum is equal to

\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(\xi_{w,d})_{-n}\beta(h)_{n}=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!h_{w,d}^{[n-1]}\cdot\frac{\pi_{F}^{n}}{n!}=-\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}(-\pi_{F})^{n}h_{w,d}^{[n-1]}.

Note that by Lemma
2.4.7
,
|h_{w,d}^{[n-1]}|_{X\cap V_{0}}\leqslant(r(X\cap V_{0})/\varpi)^{n}=1
for all
n\geqslant 1
, so this sum converges in
\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})
.

On the other hand, since
h_{w,d}^{[n-1]}=\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\nabla^{n-1}(1)
by Definition
2.4.5
, we have

\nabla(h_{w,d}^{[n-1]})=\nabla\left(\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\nabla^{n-1}(1)\right)=n\,h_{w,d}^{[n]}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad n\geqslant 1.

The twisted derivation
\nabla:\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})\to\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})
is continuous, so

\nabla((\xi_{w,d}\beta(h))_{0})=-\nabla\left(\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}(-\pi_{F})^{n}h_{w,d}^{[n-1]}\right)=-\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}(-\pi_{F})^{n}h_{w,d}^{[n]}.

Using Proposition
5.2.13
(a), we see that this is equal to
1-c_{w,d}(h^{-1})
because
h^{-1}\cdot x=x-\pi_{F}
by Lemma
5.2.2
(a). ∎

Definition 6.3.4
.

Let
Y
be the subset
\mathbb{P}^{1,\an}
obtained by removing all the open balls of radius
|\pi_{F}|
around all points in
\pi_{F}\mathcal{O}_{F}
:

Y:=\mathbb{P}^{1,\an}\,\backslash\,\bigcup\limits_{a\in\pi_{F}\mathcal{O}_{F}}\{|z-a|<|\pi_{F}|\}.

Note that this
Y
contains
X\cap V_{1}
and therefore also
X\cap V_{0}
. It turns out that the differential equation from Lemma
6.3.3
satisfied by
(\xi_{w,d}\beta(h))_{0}
makes sense on this larger subset of
\mathbb{P}^{1,an}
, namely
Y\supset X\cap V_{0}
.

To understand
Y
better, we consider the involution
s:\mathbb{P}^{1,\an}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1,\an}
given by the Möbius action of
\begin{pmatrix}0&\pi_{F}\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{GL}_{2}(F)
on
\mathbb{P}^{1,\an}
:

s(a)=\pi_{F}/a\quad\mbox{for all}\quad a\in\mathbb{P}^{1,\an}.

Lemma 6.3.5
.

s
swaps
X
and
Y
.

Proof.

To show that
s
sends
X
onto
Y
, it is enough to do this on
{\bf C}
-points, and for this, it suffices to show that
s
exchanges the complements of
X({\bf C})
and
Y({\bf C})
in
\mathbb{P}^{1,\an}({\bf C})
. These complements are disjoint unions of
q
open balls. Now,
s
exchanges the polar hole
\{z\in\mathbb{P}^{1}({\bf C}):|z|>1\}
of
X
with the open ball of radius
|\pi_{F}|
around zero. Next, if
c\in\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}
and
z\in{\bf C}
, then using the ultrametric inequality, we see that

|s(z)-\pi_{F}c|<|\pi_{F}|\Leftrightarrow|1-cz|<|z|\Leftrightarrow|z-c^{-1}|<|z|\Leftrightarrow|z-c^{-1}|<|c^{-1}|=1.

This shows that
s
exchanges the remaining
q-1
open balls contained in the complements of
X
and
Y
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.6
.

Y
is an affinoid subvariety of
\mathbb{P}^{1,\an}
and there is an isomorphism of
K
-affinoid algebras

K\left\langle x,\frac{1}{x^{q-1}-1}\right\rangle=\mathcal{O}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}(Y)

which sends
x\in\mathcal{O}(X)
to
y:=\pi_{F}/x\in\mathcal{O}(Y)
.

Lemma 6.3.7
.

(1)

The function
\left(\frac{y^{q}}{\pi_{F}^{q}(1-y^{q-1})}\right)^{k}
is an extension of
w\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})^{\times}
to
Y\backslash\{\infty\}
.

(2)

The differential operator
-\frac{1}{\pi_{F}}\theta_{(1-y^{q-1})^{-k},d}\left(y^{2}\partial_{y}-\frac{qk}{d}y\right)\in\mathcal{D}(Y)
is an extension of
\nabla\in\mathcal{D}(X\cap V_{0})
from Lemma
6.3.3
to
Y
.

(3)

There is an extension of
c_{w,d}(h^{-1})\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})^{\times}
to
Y
of the form

c_{w,d}(h^{-1})=\left(1+py\frac{f(y)}{1-y^{q-1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\in\mathcal{O}(Y)^{\times\times}\quad\mbox{for some}\quad f(y)\in\mathbb{Z}[y].

Proof.

(a) Recall that
w=1/(x^{q}-\pi_{F}^{q-1}x)^{k}
is a meromorphic function on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with no zeroes or poles on
Y\backslash\{\infty\}
. Now substitute
x=\pi_{F}/y
into
w
.

(b) First we note that
-\frac{1}{\pi_{F}}y^{2}\partial_{y}
is an extension of the derivation
\partial_{x}\in\mathcal{T}(X\cap V_{0})
to
Y
since
-\frac{1}{\pi_{F}}y^{2}\partial_{y}(x)=1
. Since
w
is a unit on
Y\backslash\{\infty\}
by (a),
\theta_{w,d}(\partial_{x})\in\mathcal{D}(X\cap V_{0})
extends to
Y\backslash\{\infty\}
. Next, using Lemma
2.4.1
on
Y\backslash\{\infty\}
, we compute

\theta_{y^{qk},d}(y^{2}\partial_{y})=y^{2}\left(\partial_{y}-\frac{qk}{d}\frac{1}{y}\right)=y^{2}\partial_{y}-\frac{qk}{d}y.

So, this differential operator in fact extends to
Y
. Since
w=\pi_{F}^{-qk}(1-y^{q-1})^{-k}y^{qk}
and
(1-y^{q-1})^{-k}\in\mathcal{O}(Y)^{\times}
, we can now apply Lemma
2.4.3
on
Y\backslash\{\infty\}
to find

\theta_{w,d}(y^{2}\partial_{y})=\theta_{(1-y^{q-1})^{-k},d}\left(\theta_{y^{qk},d}(y^{2}\partial_{y})\right)\in\mathcal{D}(Y).

Putting everything together, we see that

-\frac{1}{\pi_{F}}\theta_{(1-y^{q-1})^{-k},d}\left(y^{2}\partial_{y}-\frac{qk}{d}y\right)\in\mathcal{D}(Y)

extends
\nabla\in\mathcal{D}(X\cap V_{0})
to
Y
as claimed.

(c) We will show that there exists a unit
c\in\mathcal{O}(Y)^{\times\times}
such that

w/h^{-1}\cdot w=c^{d}.

We can then use Proposition
5.2.13
(d) to see that
c|_{X\cap V_{0}}
and
c_{w,d}(h^{-1})
are both
d
th roots of
w/h^{-1}\cdot w\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})
. Now Proposition
5.2.13
(a) tells us that
c_{w,d}(h^{-1})\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})^{\times\times}
, whereas
c|_{X\cap V_{0}}\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})^{\times\times}
because
c\in\mathcal{O}(Y)^{\times\times}
. Since this group is
d
-divisible by
[
8
, Lemma 4.3.2(a)]
, we conclude that
c|_{X\cap V_{0}}=c_{w,d}(h^{-1})
. Hence
c\in\mathcal{O}(Y)^{\times\times}
extends
c_{w,d}(h^{-1})
to
Y
.

It remains to produce the unit
c\in\mathcal{O}(Y)^{\times\times}
such that
c^{d}=w/h^{-1}\cdot w
. It is enough to consider the case
k=1
. First, we compute

(49)

h^{-1}\cdot y=\frac{\pi_{F}}{h^{-1}\cdot x}=\frac{\pi_{F}}{x-\pi_{F}}=\frac{y}{1-y}

which implies that
h
stabilises
Y
. Using this together with part (a), we then have

w=\pi_{F}^{-q}\frac{y^{q+1}}{y-y^{q}}\quad\quad\mbox{and}\quad\quad h^{-1}\cdot w=\pi_{F}^{-q}\frac{\left(\frac{y}{1-y}\right)^{q+1}}{\frac{y}{1-y}-\left(\frac{y}{1-y}\right)^{q}}

Dividing through,
y^{q+1}\pi_{F}^{-q}
cancels and we obtain

\frac{w}{h^{-1}\cdot w}=\frac{y(1-y)^{q}-y^{q}(1-y)}{y-y^{q}}.

Since
y(1-y)^{q}-y^{q}(1-y)\equiv y-y^{q}\mod py^{2}\mathbb{Z}[y]
, we see that

\frac{w}{h^{-1}\cdot w}=1+py\frac{f(y)}{1-y^{q-1}}\quad\mbox{for some}\quad f(y)\in\mathbb{Z}[y].

Thus
w/h^{-1}\cdot w\in\mathcal{O}(Y)^{\times\times}
and we can use
[
8
, Lemma 4.3.2(a)]
to define

(50)

c:=\left(1+py\frac{f(y)}{1-y^{q-1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\in\mathcal{O}(Y)^{\times\times}.

Then
c^{d}=w/h^{-1}\cdot w
as desired.
∎

Because of Lemma
6.3.7
, we can consider the differential equation on
Y
:

\nabla(\zeta)=c-1,\quad\zeta\in\mathcal{O}_{Y},

and its sheaf of solutions. More precisely, with
\nabla\in\mathcal{D}(Y)
the extension defined by Lemma
6.3.7
(b) and
c\in\mathcal{O}(Y)^{\times}
as defined by Lemma
6.3.7
(c), for each admissible open subspace
U
of
Y
, we can consider the following subset
\mathcal{F}(U)
of
\mathcal{O}(U)
:

\mathcal{F}(U):=\{\zeta\in\mathcal{O}(U):\nabla_{|U}(\zeta)=c_{|U}.\}

It is clear that
\mathcal{F}
is a
subsheaf of sets
of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. We begin the study of
\mathcal{F}
by looking at formal meromorphic solutions near the point
x=\infty
or
y=0
. We identify the completed local ring
\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{Y,\infty}}
of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
at
y=0
with the ring of formal power series
K[[y]]
and look for formal solutions in its field of fractions
K((y))
. Actually, it will be more convenient to work in a larger field, namely

K((t)):=K((y))[t]/\langle t^{d}-y\rangle.

For every admissible open subset
U
of
Y
containing
\infty
, we have natural
K
-algebra and
\mathcal{D}(U)
-module homomorphisms

\mathcal{O}(U)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Y,\infty}\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{Y,\infty}}=K[[y]]\hookrightarrow K((y))\hookrightarrow K((t)).

Lemma 6.3.8
.

Let
U
be a connected affinoid subdomain of
Y
containing
\infty
, and let
\rho^{U}_{\infty}:\mathcal{O}(U)\to\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{Y,\infty}}=K[[y]]
be the natural map. Then
\rho^{U}_{\infty}
is injective and
\mathcal{D}(U)
-linear.

Proof.

Only the injectivity requires proof. For this, note that the closed disc
\Sp K\langle y/\pi_{F}^{n}\rangle
is contained in
U
for sufficiently large
n
since
\infty\in U
. Because
U
is connected, the restriction map
\mathcal{O}(U)\to K\langle y/\pi_{F}^{n}\rangle
is injective by
[
10
, Proposition 4.2]
. The result follows, because
K\langle y/\pi_{F}^{n}\rangle
embeds into
K[[y]]
.
∎

For every connected affinoid subdomain
U
of
Y
containing
\infty
, we will also write

\rho^{U}_{\infty}:\mathcal{O}(U)[1/y]\longrightarrow K((y))

for the localisation at powers of
y
of the map
\rho^{U}_{\infty}
from Lemma
6.3.8
. This map is also injective and
\mathcal{D}(U)
-linear.

Lemma 6.3.9
.

(1)

The derivation
y^{2}\partial_{y}:\mathcal{O}(Y)\to\mathcal{O}(Y)
extends to a continuous
K
-linear derivation of
K((t))
.

(2)

The action of
h^{-1}
on
\mathcal{O}(Y)
extends to a continuous
K
-algebra automorphism
\eta
of
K((t))
via
\eta(t)=t(1-t^{d})^{-\frac{1}{d}}
.

(3)

The resulting
K
-linear operators on
K((t))
commute.

Proof.

(a) Consider the
K
-linear derivation
\frac{1}{d}t^{d+1}\partial_{t}:K[[t]]\to K[[t]]
. It respects the
t
-adic filtration on
K[[t]]
and restricts to
y^{2}\partial_{y}
on
K[[y]]\subset K[[t]]
because

\frac{1}{d}t^{d+1}\partial_{t}(y)=t^{d+1}t^{d-1}=y^{2}=y^{2}\partial_{y}(y).

Now extend this derivation to the field of fractions
K((t))
of
K[[t]]
.

(b) Define
\eta:K[[t]]\to K[[t]]
by setting

(51)

\eta(f(t))=f(t(1-t^{d})^{-\frac{1}{d}})\quad\mbox{for all}\quad f(t)\in K[[t]].

This is a
K
-algebra automorphism which respects the
t
-adic filtration on
K[[t]]
. Its unique extension
\eta
to the field of fractions
K((t))
of
K[[t]]
is then given by the same formula. Because
y=t^{d}
, its restriction to
K[[y]]\subset K[[t]]
satisfies

\eta(y)=(t(1-t^{d})^{-\frac{1}{d}})^{d}=y/(1-y).

On the other hand, the automorphism
h^{-1}:\mathcal{O}(Y)\to\mathcal{O}(Y)
sends
y
to
\frac{y}{1-y}
by (
49
), so it preserves the ideal
y\mathcal{O}(Y)
and therefore extends uniquely to a
y
-adically continuous
K
-algebra automorphism of
\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{Y,\infty}}=K[[y]]
which sends
y
to
\frac{y}{1-y}
. This extension is then the restriction of
\eta
to
K[[y]]
.

(c) It remains to see that the derivation
D=\frac{1}{d}t^{d+1}\partial_{t}
commutes with
\eta
. It is enough to show that the restriction of
D
to
K((y))
agrees with the restriction of
\eta D\eta^{-1}
to
K((y))
, because then the derivation
D-\eta D\eta^{-1}
of
K((t))
is
K((y))
-linear and therefore must vanish on the finite étale extension
K((t))
of
K((y))
. Now
D(x)=y^{2}\partial_{y}(x)=-\pi_{F}
and
\eta(x)=h^{-1}\cdot x=x-\pi_{F}
, so
D(\eta(x))=D(x-\pi_{F})=D(x)=\eta(D(x))
. Therefore
\eta D\eta^{-1}
and
D
agree on
K[y]
, but both are
y
-adically continuous and
K[y]
is
y
-adically dense in
K[[y]]
, so they must be equal.
∎

Now we define
\epsilon:=(1-y^{q-1})^{\frac{k}{d}}\in K[[y]]
and
z:=\epsilon^{-1}t^{qk}\in K((t))
. Using Lemma
6.3.7
(a), we have

z^{d}=(1-y^{q-1})^{-k}y^{qk}=\pi_{F}^{k}\rho^{Y}_{\infty}(w).

We assume from now on until the end of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{TheIntegral}
that
k\neq d
.
Under this assumption, we see that
qk\equiv-k\neq 0\mod d
, so that
a-\frac{qk}{d}\notin\mathbb{Z}
for any integer
a
. This allows us to make the following

Definition 6.3.10
.

For each
m\geqslant 0
, define
\alpha_{m}:=\frac{(-1)^{m}\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m}}{(q-1)m-\frac{qk}{d}-1}
, and set

J:=t^{-qk}y^{-1}\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{m}y^{(q-1)m}\in t^{-qk}K((y)).

Lemma 6.3.11
.

We have
(y^{2}\partial_{y})(J)=t^{-qk}\epsilon=z^{-1}
.

Proof.

Let
f(t)\in K((t))
. Since
y=t^{d}
, using the Leibniz rule we have

\begin{array}[]{lll}(y^{2}\partial_{y})(t^{-qk}y^{-1}f(t))&=&f(t)\left(\frac{1}{d}t^{d+1}\partial_{t}\right)(t^{-qk-d})+t^{-qk}y^{-1}\,(y^{2}\partial_{y})(f(t))\\
&=&t^{-qk}\left(y\partial_{y}-\frac{qk}{d}-1\right)(f(t)).\end{array}

Since
y^{2}\partial_{y}
is
t
-adically continuous on
K((t))
, it remains to show that

(y\partial_{y}-\frac{qk}{d}-1)\left(\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{m}y^{(q-1)m}\right)=\epsilon.

Using the binomial expansion inside
K[[y]]
, we have

\epsilon=(1-y^{q-1})^{\frac{k}{d}}=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{m}\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m}y^{(q-1)m}.

The result follows, because

(y\partial_{y}-\frac{qk}{d}-1)(y^{(q-1)m})=\left((q-1)m-\frac{qk}{d}-1\right)y^{(q-1)m}

and
((q-1)m-\frac{qk}{d}-1)\alpha_{m}=(-1)^{m}\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m}
for all
m\geqslant 0
.
∎

In other words, we think of
J
informally as the integral

\boxed{J=\int^{y}\left(\frac{1-y^{q-1}}{y^{q}}\right)^{\frac{k}{d}}\frac{dy}{y^{2}}}

Definition 6.3.12
.

Define
\zeta_{w,d}:=-\pi_{F}z\,(\eta(J)-J)\in K((t))
.

The operator
\nabla
also extends to
K((t))
, by Lemma
6.3.9
and Lemma
6.3.7
(b). Because
z^{d}=\pi_{F}^{k}\rho^{W}_{\infty}(w)
holds in
K((t))
, Lemma
6.3.7
(b) implies that

\nabla=-\frac{1}{\pi_{F}}z(y^{2}\partial_{y})z^{-1}.

Proposition 6.3.13
.

We have
\nabla(\zeta_{w,d})=\rho^{Y}_{\infty}(c)-1
.

Proof.

Since
\eta
and
y^{2}\partial_{y}
commute by Lemma
6.3.9
(c), Lemma
6.3.11
tells us that

\begin{array}[]{lll}z^{-1}\nabla(\zeta_{w,d})&=&z^{-1}\nabla(-\pi_{F}z(\eta(J)-J))\\
&=&(y^{2}\partial_{y})(\eta(J)-J)\\
&=&\eta((y^{2}\partial_{y})(J))-(y^{2}\partial_{y})(J)\\
&=&\eta(z^{-1})-z^{-1}.\end{array}

Next, recall from the proof of Lemma
6.3.7
(c) that
c^{d}=\frac{w}{h^{-1}\cdot w}
in
\mathcal{O}(Y)
. Hence

\rho^{Y}_{\infty}(c)^{d}=\frac{\rho^{W}_{\infty}(w)}{\eta(\rho^{W}_{\infty}(w))}=\left(\frac{z}{\eta(z)}\right)^{d}.

The definition of
\eta
given at (
51
) shows that
\eta
respects the
t
-adic filtration on
K((t))
and induces the trivial automorphism on the associated graded. Hence
\frac{z}{\eta(z)}\equiv 1\mod tK[[t]]
. Looking at
(\ref{DefnOfC})
, we see that
\rho^{Y}_{\infty}(c)\in K((t))
also satisfies
\rho^{Y}_{\infty}(c)\equiv 1\mod tK[[t]]
. Since
1+tK[[t]]
is
d
-divisible, we conclude that

\rho^{Y}_{\infty}(c)=\frac{z}{\eta(z)}.

Therefore
\nabla(\zeta_{w,d})=z(\eta(z^{-1})-z^{-1})=\rho^{Y}_{\infty}(c)-1
as desired.∎

Proposition 6.3.14
.

The element
t^{qk}(\eta(J)-J)
lies in
K[[y]]
.

Proof.

Let
r
be an integer; then since
t/\eta(t)=(1-y)^{\frac{1}{d}}\in K[[y]]
and
y/\eta(y)=1-y\in K[[y]]
, using the binomial expansion for
(1-y)^{\alpha}
we see that

t^{qk}(\eta(t^{-qk}y^{r-1})-t^{-qk}y^{r-1})=y^{r-1}\left(\frac{t^{qk}y^{1-r}}{\eta(t^{qk}y^{1-r})}-1\right)=y^{r}\left(\frac{(1-y)^{1+\frac{qk}{d}-r}-1}{y}\right)

lies in
K[[y]]
, provided that
r\geqslant 0
. Using Definition
6.3.10
, and the above with
r=m(q-1)
where
m\geqslant 0
, we conclude that

(52)

t^{qk}(\eta(J)-J)=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{m}y^{m(q-1)}\left(\frac{(1-y)^{1+\frac{qk}{d}-m(q-1)}-1}{y}\right)

lies in
K[[y]]
as desired. ∎

Corollary 6.3.15
.

The element
\zeta_{w,d}
lies in
K[[y]]
. In fact, we have

\zeta_{w,d}=\pi_{F}(1-y^{q-1})^{-\frac{k}{d}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{m}\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m}y^{(q-1)m}\left[\frac{(1-y)^{1+\frac{qk}{d}-(q-1)m}-1}{\left(1+\frac{qk}{d}-(q-1)m\right)y}\right].

Proof.

Recall that
z=\epsilon^{-1}t^{qk}
where
\epsilon=(1-y^{q-1})^{\frac{k}{d}}\in K[[y]]^{\times}
. Then

\zeta_{w,d}=-\pi_{F}z(\eta(J)-J)=-\pi_{F}(1-y^{q-1})^{-\frac{k}{d}}t^{qk}(\eta(J)-J).

Substituting
\alpha_{m}=\frac{(-1)^{m}\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m}}{(q-1)m-\frac{qk}{d}-1}
into (
52
), gives the formula for
\zeta_{w,d}
.∎

For future use, we record the following

Proposition 6.3.16
.

\zeta_{w,d}
is the
unique
solution to the differential equation

\nabla(\zeta)=\rho^{Y}_{\infty}(c)-1
with
\zeta\in K((y))
.

Proof.

Proposition
6.3.13
and Corollary
6.3.15
tell us that
\zeta_{w,d}
is
a
solution of
\nabla(\zeta)=\rho^{Y}_{\infty}(c)-1
, so we need to show it is unique. It is enough to show that the map
\nabla:K((y))\to K((y))
is injective. Now,
\nabla=-\frac{1}{\pi_{F}}\epsilon^{-1}(y^{2}\partial_{y}-\frac{qk}{d}y)\epsilon
by Lemma
6.3.7
(b), so it is enough to show that
y\partial_{y}-\frac{qk}{d}:K((y)\to K((y))
is injective. Since

(y\partial_{y}-\frac{qk}{d})\left(\sum_{n}a_{n}y^{n}\right)=\sum_{n}\left(n-\frac{qk}{d}\right)a_{n}y^{n}

this is an immediate consequence of the fact that
\frac{qk}{d}\notin\mathbb{Z}
. ∎

Next, we introduce the following affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{P}^{1,\an}
:

W:=\Sp K\langle y/\pi_{F}\rangle.

We identify
Y
with
\Sp K\langle y,\frac{1}{y^{q-1}-1}\rangle
using Corollary
6.3.6
, and note that
W
is contained in
Y
because
1-y^{q-1}=1+\pi_{F}^{q-1}(y/\pi_{F})^{q-1}\in\mathcal{O}(W)^{\times\times}
.

Lemma 6.3.17
.

\mathcal{F}(W)\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Recall
[
30
, Definition 13.1.1]
that the
type
of a number
\lambda\in K
is the radius of convergence of the formal power series
\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{n}}{\lambda-n}
. If
\lambda
happens to lie in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\cap\mathbb{Q}
, then
\lambda
has a
recurrent

p
-adic expansion, and now
[
30
, Proposition 13.1.4]
implies that
\lambda
has type
1
. In other words, for any real number
0<r<1
, we have

(53)

\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{r^{n}}{|\lambda-n|}=0\quad\mbox{for any}\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\cap\mathbb{Q}.

Now, the spectral norm
|\cdot|_{W}
on
\mathcal{O}(W)=K\langle y/\pi_{F}\rangle
satisfies
|y|_{W}=|\pi_{F}|<1
. Then for any
m\geqslant 0
, we have

\left|(-1)^{m}\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m}y^{(q-1)m}\left[\frac{(1-y)^{1+\frac{qk}{d}-(q-1)m}-1}{\left(1+\frac{qk}{d}-(q-1)m\right)y}\right]\right|_{W}\leqslant\frac{|\pi_{F}|^{(q-1)m-1}}{|\frac{qk}{d}-((q-1)m-1)|}

because
|(-1)^{m}\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m}|\leqslant 1
and
|(1-y)^{1-\frac{k}{d}-(q-1)m}-1|_{W}\leqslant 1
. Because
\frac{qk}{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\cap\mathbb{Q}
, (
53
) implies that the partial sums

\zeta_{n}:=\pi_{F}(1-y^{q-1})^{-\frac{k}{d}}\sum_{m=0}^{n}(-1)^{m}\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{m}y^{(q-1)m}\left[\frac{(1-y)^{1+\frac{qk}{d}-(q-1)m}-1}{\left(1+\frac{qk}{d}-(q-1)m\right)y}\right]

of the power series
\zeta_{w,d}
converge with respect to the spectral norm
|\cdot|_{W}
to an element
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}(W)
. Because
\zeta_{n}-\zeta_{n-1}
converges to zero
y
-adically in
\mathcal{O}(W)
, it follows that
\zeta_{n}
also converges
y
-adically to
\zeta^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{O}(W)
.

Recall the
\mathcal{D}(W)
-linear and injective map
\rho^{W}_{\infty}:\mathcal{O}(W)\to\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{Y,\infty}}=K[[y]]
from Lemma
6.3.8
. Since
\rho^{W}_{\infty}
is continuous with respect to the
y
-adic topologies on
\mathcal{O}(W)
and
K[[y]]
, we deduce that

\rho^{W}_{\infty}(\zeta^{\prime})=\rho^{W}_{\infty}(\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\zeta_{n})=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\rho^{W}(\zeta_{n})=\zeta_{w,d}.

Since
\rho^{W}_{\infty}
is
\mathcal{D}(W)
-linear,
\rho^{W}_{\infty}(\nabla(\zeta^{\prime}))=\nabla(\zeta_{w,d})=\rho^{W}_{\infty}(c_{|W}-1)
by Proposition
6.3.16
, so
\nabla(\zeta^{\prime})=c_{|W}-1
because
\rho^{W}_{\infty}
is injective. Thus
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}(W)
as desired. ∎

Our next result relies on a long computation of the
p
-adic valuations of certain binomial coefficients, which we perform in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{BinEsts}
.

Theorem 6.3.18
.

The series
\zeta_{w,d}\in K[[y]]
does
not
have bounded coefficients.

Proof.

Using the binomial theorem, we compute that for any
0\neq\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
,

\frac{(1-y)^{\mu}-1}{\mu y}=\frac{1}{\mu y}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\binom{\mu}{n}(-y)^{n}=-\frac{1}{\mu}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\binom{\mu}{n+1}(-y)^{n}.

Let
s:=y^{q-1}
, and let
\Phi:K[[y]]\twoheadrightarrow K[[s]]
be the
K[[s]]
-linear projection operator, which sends
y^{i}
to
0
if
q-1\nmid i
, and which is the identity on
K[[s]]\subset K[[y]]
. Using the elementary identity
\frac{1}{\mu}\binom{\mu}{n+1}=\frac{(\mu-1)\cdots(\mu-n)}{(n+1)!}=\frac{1}{n+1}\binom{\mu-1}{n}
we compute that

(54)

\Phi\left(\frac{(1-y)^{\mu}-1}{\mu y}\right)=-\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\binom{\mu-1}{\ell(q-1)}\frac{(-s)^{\ell}}{\ell(q-1)+1}.

Set
\mu_{r}:=1+\frac{qk}{d}-(q-1)r
and note that
\mu_{r}\neq 0
for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
because
\frac{k}{d}\notin\mathbb{Z}
by our choice of
k
and
d
. Take the series appearing in Corollary
6.3.15
, change the dummy variable from
m
to
r
, apply
\Phi
and substitute in
(\ref{PhiMuY})
to obtain

\begin{array}[]{lll}\frac{1}{\pi_{F}}(1-s)^{\frac{k}{d}}\Phi(\zeta_{w,d})&=&\sum\limits_{r=0}^{\infty}\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{r}(-s)^{r}\cdot\left(-\sum\limits_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\binom{\mu_{r}-1}{\ell(q-1)}\frac{(-s)^{\ell}}{\ell(q-1)+1}\right)\\
&=&-\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum\limits_{r=0}^{n}\frac{\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{r}\binom{\frac{qk}{d}-(q-1)r}{(n-r)(q-1)}}{(n-r)(q-1)+1}\right)s^{n}.\\
\end{array}

We observe that the formula on the right hand side only involves
\frac{k}{d}
. Since
d\mid(q+1)
and
1\leqslant k<d
, after multiplying both
k
and
d
by
\frac{q+1}{d}
if necessary, we may assume that
d=q+1
and
1\leqslant k\leqslant q
.

By Corollary
6.4.17
below, the
p
-adic valuation of the coefficient of
s^{n}
appearing in the above expansion is not bounded below as
n
varies: in other words, this series does not have bounded coefficients. Since the operator
\Phi
sends
K^{\circ}[[y]]_{K}
to
K^{\circ}[[s]]_{K}
we conclude that
\zeta_{w,d}
also does not have bounded coefficients.
∎

Corollary 6.3.19
.

We have
\mathcal{F}(Y)=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose for a contradiction that
\zeta\in\mathcal{O}(Y)
satisfies
\nabla(\zeta)=c-1
. Now
\mathcal{O}(Y)=K\langle y,\frac{1}{y^{q-1}-1}\rangle
by Corollary
6.3.6
, so

\rho^{Y}_{\infty}\left(\mathcal{O}(Y)\right)\subseteq K^{\circ}[[y]]_{K}.

Since
\rho^{Y}_{\infty}:\mathcal{O}(Y)\to K[[y]]
is
\mathcal{D}(Y)
-linear by Lemma
6.3.8
, we have
\nabla(\rho^{Y}_{\infty}(\zeta))=\rho^{Y}_{\infty}(c)-1
, so
\rho^{Y}_{\infty}(\zeta)=\zeta_{w,d}
by Proposition
6.3.16
. But then
\zeta_{w,d}\in K^{\circ}[[y]]_{K}
which contradicts Theorem
6.3.18
.∎

Lemma 6.3.20
.

We have
|\mathcal{F}(X\cap V_{0})|\leqslant 1
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
\nabla=\theta_{w,d}(\partial_{x})
is injective on
\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})
. Now,

w=\frac{1}{(x^{q}-\pi_{F}^{q-1}x)^{k}}=\frac{x^{-qk}}{(1-(\pi_{F}/x)^{q-1})^{k}}\equiv x^{-qk}\,\mod\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})^{\times\times}.

Since
\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})^{\times\times}
is
d
-divisible by
[
8
, Lemma 4.3.2(a)]
, we can now use Lemma
2.4.3
and Lemma
2.4.2
to see that

\nabla=u\hskip 2.84544pt\theta_{x^{-qk},d}(\partial_{x})\hskip 2.84544ptu^{-1}\quad\mbox{for some}\quad u\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})^{\times}.

Thus it remains to show that
\theta_{x^{-qk},d}(\partial_{x})=\partial_{x}+\frac{qk}{d}\frac{1}{x}
is injective on
\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})
. Suppose that
\partial_{x}(v)=-\frac{qk}{d}\frac{v}{x}
for some non-zero
v\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})
. Then
\partial_{x}(v^{d}x^{qk})=0
so
v^{d}=\lambda x^{-qk}
for some
\lambda\in K
. Then
\lambda\neq 0
since
v\neq 0
, so
v\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})^{\times}
. Let
D:=\{|x|<1\}
and apply the map
\mu_{X\cap V_{0}}
from
[
8
, Proposition 4.3.1]
to see that
d\cdot\mu_{X\cap V_{0}}(v)(D)=-qk\cdot\mu_{X\cap V_{0}}(x)(D)=-qk
. This contradicts
-\frac{qk}{d}\notin\mathbb{Z}
.∎

We can finally prove the main result of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{TheIntegral}
.

Theorem 6.3.21
.

Let
w=\frac{1}{(x^{q}-\pi_{F}^{q-1}x)^{k}}
, where
d\mid(q+1)
and
1\leqslant k\leqslant d
. Then

\beta(h)\notin\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|\pi_{F}|}(X)+\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d).

Proof.

First, we treat the case when
k\neq d
. Suppose for a contradiction that
\beta(h)\in\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|\pi_{F}|}(X)+\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)
. Then we can find
\zeta\in\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{1})
such that
\zeta_{|X\cap V_{0}}=-(\xi_{w,d}\beta(h))_{0}
by Proposition
6.3.2
. Now,
-(\xi_{w,d}\beta(h))_{0}\in\mathcal{F}(X\cap V_{0})
by Lemma
6.3.3
. Because the restriction map
\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{1})\to\mathcal{O}(X\cap V_{0})
is injective, we deduce that
\zeta\in\mathcal{F}(X\cap V_{1})
.

Now, since
W({\bf C})=\{a\in{\bf C}:|a|\geqslant 1\}\cup\{\infty\}
in the
x
-coordinate, we see that
X\cap W=X\cap V_{0}
. Since also
V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}
, we have

(X\cap V_{1})\cap W=X\cap V_{0}.

On the other hand, if
a\in{\bf C}
and
|a-\zeta^{i}|<1
for some
i=0,\cdots,q-2
, then
|a|=1
and
a\in W({\bf C})
. Therefore
W
contains all of the
q
‘large’ holes of
X\cap V_{1}
. Hence

(X\cap V_{1})\cup W=Y

and
\{W,X\cap V_{1}\}
is an affinoid covering of
Y
.

By Lemma
6.3.17
, we can find some
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}(W)
. Then both
\zeta|_{X\cap V_{0}}
and
\zeta^{\prime}|_{X\cap V_{0}}
lie in
\mathcal{F}(X\cap V_{0})
, so they are equal by Lemma
6.3.20
. Finally, Tate’s Acyclicity Theorem implies that the local solutions
\zeta\in\mathcal{F}(X\cap V_{1})
and
\zeta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}(W)
glue to give an element in
\mathcal{F}(Y)
, which contradicts Corollary
6.3.19
.

The case
k=d
requires a special, but much easier, argument. First note that when
k=d
,
w=(x^{q}-\pi_{F}^{q-1}x)^{-d}
is a
d
-th power, and in fact we that we have

w=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(w)}^{-d}.

Hence we can apply Lemma
4.2.17
(b) to see that

R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)=\theta_{w\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(w)}^{d},d}(\partial_{x})\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(w)}=\partial_{x}\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(w)},

because
\theta_{1,d}
is the identity map in view of equation (
9
). Therefore
R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)
lies in the left ideal
\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}(X)x
, and it will be enough to show that

\beta(h)\notin\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|\pi_{F}|}(X)+\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}(X)x.

Suppose for a contradiction that
\beta(h)=P+Qx
for some
P\in\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|\pi_{F}|}(X)
and
Q\in\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}(X)
. Hence
P\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
for some
r>\varpi/|\pi_{F}|
and
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{s}(X)
for some
s>\varpi
, by Definition
2.3.9
(d). Since
r>\varpi/|\pi_{F}|>\varpi
, we may shrink
s
if necessary to ensure that
r\geqslant s>\varpi
. Now, applying the involutive transpose automorphism
(-)^{T}:\mathcal{D}_{s}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{s}(X)
from Lemma
2.2.11
shows that

(55)

\beta(h)^{T}=P^{T}+xQ^{T}.

By Definition
2.2.8
we can find a sequence of elements
(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
in
\mathcal{O}(X)
, such that

\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}|a_{n}|_{X}r^{n}=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad P^{T}=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}\partial^{n}\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X).

Next, we use the fact that
(-)^{T}
is continuous and
\partial^{T}=-\partial
to compute

\beta(h)^{T}=\left(\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\pi_{F}^{n}}{n!}\partial^{n}\right)^{T}=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\pi_{F}^{n}}{n!}(-\partial)^{n}=\beta(-h).

Setting
x=0
in equation (
55
) and equating coefficients shows that

\frac{(-\pi_{F})^{n}}{n!}=a_{n}(0)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad n\geqslant 0.

Finally, Lemma
2.3.16
tells us that
\frac{1}{|n!|}\geqslant\frac{pn}{\varpi^{n}}
. Hence

pn\cdot\left(\frac{|\pi_{F}|r}{\varpi}\right)^{n}\leqslant\frac{|\pi_{F}|^{n}r^{n}}{|n!|}|a_{n}(0)|\leqslant|a_{n}|_{X}r^{n}\to 0\quad\mbox{as}\quad n\to\infty

which is impossible because
r>\varpi/|\pi_{F}|
implies that
\frac{|\pi_{F}|r}{\varpi}>1
.
∎

6.4.
Estimates of
p
-adic valuations of binomial coefficients

The goal of this section is to estimate the
p
-adic growth rate of the coefficient

(56)

\sum_{r=0}^{n}\frac{\binom{\frac{k}{d}}{r}\binom{\frac{qk}{d}-(q-1)r}{(n-r)(q-1)}}{(n-r)(q-1)+1}

that arises in the proof of Theorem
6.3.18
for
1\leqslant k\leqslant q
. In particular we will see that for certain special values of
n
, the term corresponding to the value of
r
that maximises the
p
-adic valuation of the denominator
(n-r)(q-1)+1
will dominate this sum and moreover the
p
-adic norm of this term will be unbounded as
n
increases through these special values. To achieve this we will appeal to a Theorem of Kummer to estimate the
p
-adic valuation of the binomial coefficients that appear in the sum.

For the remainder of the section we
fix
1\leqslant k\leqslant q
and define
f=v_{p}(q)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
be given, and consider their
p
-adic expansions

\lambda=\lambda_{0}+p\lambda_{1}+p^{2}\lambda_{2}+\cdots,\quad n=n_{0}+pn_{1}+pn_{2}+\cdots

where the
p
-adic digits
\lambda_{i}
and
n_{i}
all lie in
\{0,1,...,p-1\}
. Since
n
is assumed to be a natural number, we know that
n_{i}=0
for all sufficiently large
i
.

Definition 6.4.1
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
be as above.

(1)

Let
i\geqslant 0
. The
i
-th carry function

\gamma_{i}(\lambda,n)
is defined as follows:

\gamma_{0}(\lambda,n)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}1&\quad\mbox{if}&\lambda_{0}+n_{0}>p-1\\
0&\quad\mbox{if}&\lambda_{0}+n_{0}\leqslant p-1\end{array}\right.

and for
i\geqslant 1
, it is defined recursively by

\gamma_{i}(\lambda,n)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}1&\quad\mbox{if}&\lambda_{i}+n_{i}+\gamma_{i-1}(\lambda,n)>p-1\\
0&\quad\mbox{if}&\lambda_{i}+n_{i}+\gamma_{i-1}(\lambda,n)\leqslant p-1.\end{array}\right.

(2)

We define

L(\lambda,n):=\inf\{i\geqslant 0:\gamma_{j}(\lambda,n)=0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad j\geqslant i\}.

(3)

For each
m\geqslant 0
, the
m
-th non-carry function
is defined to be

\mathcal{N}_{m}(\lambda,n):=|\{0\leqslant i<m:\gamma_{i}(\lambda,n)=0\}|.

(4)

We define
\langle\lambda|n\rangle
to be the binomial coefficient
\binom{\lambda+n}{n}
.

Thus
L(\lambda,n)
records the position after which there are no further carries when performing the addition of
\lambda
and
n
in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
; it is possible that
L(\lambda,n)=\infty
but this happens only when
\lambda_{i}=p-1
for all sufficiently large
i
. More precisely we have the following Lemma.

Lemma 6.4.2
.

L(\lambda,n)=\infty
only when
\lambda
is a negative integer and
n\geqslant-\lambda
.

Proof.

Since
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is
m\geqslant 1
such that
n_{j}=0
for all
j\geqslant m
.

Suppose that
L(\lambda,n)=\infty
. Then by definition
\gamma_{j}(\lambda,n)=1
for infinitely many values of
j
. But, by
6.4.1
(a), if
\gamma_{j}(\lambda,n)=1
for
j\geqslant m
then
\gamma_{j-1}(\lambda,n)=1
and
\lambda_{j}=p-1
. It follows that
\gamma_{j}(\lambda,n)=1
for all
j\geqslant m-1
and
\lambda_{j}=p-1
for all
j\geqslant m
and
\lambda
is a negative integer. Moreover
(\lambda+n)_{j}=0
for all
j\geqslant m
; that is
\lambda+n\in bN
and so
n\geqslant-\lambda
as claimed.
∎

In our applications,
L(\lambda,n)
will always be finite. The function
\mathcal{N}_{m}(\lambda,n)
records the number of non-carries that occurred in the first
m
digits when performing the addition of
\lambda
and
n
in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

We have now developed enough language to precisely state Kummer’s theorem on the
p
-adic valuation of binomial coefficients.

Theorem 6.4.3

(Kummer, 1852)
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
be given, and suppose that
L(\lambda,n)<\infty
. Then

v_{p}\langle\lambda|n\rangle=m-\mathcal{N}_{m}(\lambda,n)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad m\geqslant L(\lambda,n).

Under the assumption that
L(\lambda,n)<\infty
, the carrying will stop in finite time in the sense that eventually all
\gamma_{i}(\lambda,n)
are zero. Then for sufficiently large
m
, the quantity on the right hand side is independent of
m
and returns the total number of carries that occurred during the addition of
\lambda
and
n
in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
— this is perhaps a more usual way of formulating Kummer’s theorem. We also note that if
L(\lambda,n)=\infty
then
\langle\lambda|n\rangle=0
so
v_{p}(\langle\lambda|n\rangle)=\infty
in that case.

Proof of Theorem
6.4.3
.

A proof in the case that
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
appeared in
[
31
]
. A modern account of the proof in that case can be found in
[
26
, Theorem 3.7]
.

Now let
\mu\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
0\leqslant\mu<p^{L(\lambda,n)}
and
v_{p}(\lambda-\mu)\geqslant L(\lambda,n)
; that is the
p
-adic expansion of
\mu
is the truncation of the
p
-adic expansion of
\lambda
at the
L(\lambda,n)
th digit. Then it is easy to verify that
L(\lambda,n)=L(\mu,n)
and
\mathcal{N}_{m}(\lambda,n)=\mathcal{N}_{m}(\mu,n)
for all
m
. Moreover
v_{p}\langle\lambda|n\rangle=v_{p}\langle\mu|n\rangle
since
v_{p}(\lambda+i)=v_{p}(\mu+i)\leqslant L(\lambda,n)
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.
∎

In most cases of interest to us, the possibility of carrying process
not
stopping in finite time is ruled out by the following

Notation 6.4.4
.

Given
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{j-1}\in\{0,1,\cdots,q-1\}
, write

[\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{j-1}]_{j}:=\alpha_{0}q^{0}+\alpha_{1}q^{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{j-1}q^{j-1}.

We call
\alpha_{i}
the
i
th
q
-adic digit
of this
q
-expansion
.

Lemma 6.4.5
.

Suppose that
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
,
n,j\in\mathbb{N}
with

\lambda\equiv[\alpha_{0},\ldots,\alpha_{j-1}]_{j}\mod q^{j}\mbox{ and }n=[\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{j-1}]_{j}.

(1)

If
\beta_{i}+\alpha_{i}<q-1
for some
0<i<j
then
\gamma_{(i+1)f-1}(\lambda,n)=0
.

(2)

If
\beta_{j-1}+\alpha_{j-1}<q-1
then
L(\lambda,n)<(j+1)f
.

Proof.

(a) Under the hypothesis
\beta_{i}+\alpha_{i}<q-1

\gamma_{(i+1)f-1}(\lambda,n)=\begin{cases}\gamma_{f-1}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})&\mbox{if }\gamma_{if-1}(\lambda,n)=0\\
\gamma_{f-1}(\alpha_{i}+1,\beta_{i})&\mbox{if }\gamma_{if-1}(\lambda,n)=1\end{cases}

and since
\alpha_{i}+1+\beta_{i}<q
this is zero in either case.

(b) Since
n<q^{j}
, we have
n_{i}=0
whenever
i\geqslant jf
. The result now follows from part (a) and Definition
6.4.1
(a).
∎

Definition 6.4.6
.

For
n\geqslant 1
, let
M=M_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
be largest possible such that
1+q+\cdots+q^{M}\leqslant n
. We also define

s:=s_{n}:=n-(1+q+\cdots+q^{M})\geqslant 0.

It is easy to see that
s_{n}<q^{M_{n}+1}
since otherwise we could make
M
larger.

Lemma 6.4.7
.

Let
0\leqslant r\leqslant n
be an integer. Then

v_{p}(r-s_{n})<(M_{n}+1)f

whenever
r\neq s_{n}
.

Proof.

Since
s<q^{M+1}
and
n-s=1+q+\cdots+q^{M}<q^{M+1}
,

s-q^{M+1}<0\leqslant s\leqslant n<s+q^{M+1},

which implies that

\{0,1,\cdots,n\}\cap(s+q^{M+1}\mathbb{Z})=\{s\}.

Suppose that
v_{p}(r-s)\geqslant(M+1)f
. Then
q^{M+1}
divides
r-s
, so
r\in s+q^{M+1}\mathbb{Z}
. Since
0\leqslant r\leqslant n
by assumption, we conclude that
r=s
.∎

Corollary 6.4.8
.

Let
0\leqslant r\leqslant n
be an integer. Then

v_{p}((n-r)(q-1)+1)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}(M_{n}+1)f&\quad\mbox{if}&r=s_{n},\\
v_{p}(r-s)&\quad\mbox{if}&r\neq s_{n}.\end{array}\right.

Proof.

By definition of
s
,
v_{p}((n-s)(q-1)+1)=v_{p}(q^{M+1})=(M+1)f
. Now

(n-r)(q-1)+1=(q-1)(s-r)\quad+\quad(n-s)(q-1)+1

and
m:=v_{p}(r-s)
is strictly less than
v_{p}(q^{M+1})=(M+1)f
whenever
r\neq s
by Lemma
6.4.7
. Since
v_{p}(q-1)=0
, the result now follows from the non-Archimedean triangle inequality. ∎

Thus, combining the last two results, we see that
s_{n}
has been chosen to maximise
p
-adic value of the denominator of (
56
) for given
n
.

We are also interested in the following complicated-looking binomial coefficient.

Definition 6.4.9
.

For each
n,r\in\mathbb{N}
with
0\leqslant r\leqslant n
, define

S_{n,r}:=\langle\frac{qk}{q+1}-n(q-1)\hskip 2.84544pt|\hskip 2.84544pt(n-r)(q-1)\rangle=\binom{\frac{qk}{q+1}-r(q-1)}{(n-r)(q-1)}.

In order to control this, until Corollary
6.4.17
below, we
fix
a possibly very large positive integer
N\geqslant 0
, and define
n_{N}
to be the smallest positive integer such that

n_{N}\equiv\frac{qk}{q^{2}-1}\mod q^{N}

This expression obviously depends on
N
, but because of its frequent appearance we will abbreviate it to
n:=n_{N}
until Corollary
6.4.17
. The reason for this choice of the form of
n
is the following result which will enable us to ignore the
S_{n,r}
in the numerator of (
56
) when estimating its
p
-adic value.

Proposition 6.4.10
.

v_{p}(S_{n,r})\geqslant 0
for all
0\leqslant r\leqslant n
and
v_{p}(S_{n,s_{n}})=0
.

Proof.

The first part is well-known since
qk/(q+1)
is a
p
-adic integer.

We define
\alpha:=\frac{qk}{q+1}-n(q-1)\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
so that
\alpha\equiv 0\mod q^{N}
.
That is, the
p
-adic digits
\alpha_{j}
of
\alpha
are zero whenever
j<Nf
. Similarly, since
M<N
,

\beta:=(n-s)(q-1)\leqslant q^{M+1}-1<q^{N}

so
\beta_{j}=0
for all
j\geqslant Nf
. It follows that
\gamma_{j}(\alpha,\beta)=0
for all
j\geqslant 0
, and therefore
v_{p}(S_{n,s})=v_{p}\langle\alpha|\beta\rangle=0
by Theorem
6.4.3
.
∎

For reasons that will become apparent later
we now assume

\begin{cases}N\mbox{ is even }&\mbox{if }1\leqslant k\leqslant q-1\mbox{ or }k=2=q\\
N\mbox{ is odd }&\mbox{if }k=q>2.\end{cases}

It is also convenient to suppose that
N\geqslant 6
.

We can now compute the precise value of
M_{n}
in terms of
N
depending on the values of
k
and
q
.

Lemma 6.4.11
.

M_{n}=\begin{cases}N-1&\mbox{if }1\leqslant k\leqslant q-2\mbox{ or }k=q>2,\\
N-2&\mbox{if }\left(k=q-1\mbox{ and }q>2\right)\mbox{ or }k=q=2,\\
N-3&\mbox{if }k=1,q=2.\end{cases}

Proof.

Since
n\leqslant q^{N}
it is immediate that
M\leqslant N-1
in all cases.

We see that

n\equiv 1+\frac{(q-k-1)q+(q-1)}{1-q^{2}}=2+\frac{(2q-k-1)q+(q-2)}{1-q^{2}}\mod q^{N}

so, for
1\leqslant k\leqslant q-1
, since
N
is even, the
q
-adic expansion of
n
is

n=0+(q-k)q+(q-1)q^{2}+(q-1-k)q^{3}+\cdots+(q-1)q^{N-2}+(q-1-k)q^{N-1},

For
k=q>2
, since
N
is odd, the
q
-adic expansion of
n
is

n=0+0\cdot q+(q-1)q^{2}+(q-1)q^{3}+(q-2)q^{4}+\cdots+(q-1)q^{N-2}+(q-2)q^{N-1},

and for
k=q=2
, since
N
is even,

n=0+0\cdot q+(q-1)q^{2}+(q-1)q^{3}+(q-2)q^{4}+\cdots+(q-2)q^{N-2}+(q-1)q^{N-1},

where in all cases, the
q
-digits after the
3
rd digit repeat with period
2
until the last
q
-digit.

If
1\leqslant k\leqslant q-2
then
q\geqslant 3
, and it is now easy to see that

1+q+q^{2}+\cdots+q^{N-1}<2q^{N-2}+q^{N-1}<n

so
M=N-1
.

Similarly if
k=q-1
and if
q\geqslant 3
, then

1+q+q^{2}+\cdots+q^{N-2}<2q^{N-2}<n<q^{N-1}

and so
M=N-2
, and if
k=q
and
q\geqslant 3
, then

1+q+q^{2}+\cdots+q^{N-1}<n<q^{N}

so
M=N-2
.

The two cases where
q=2
can be treated in a similar manner noting that when
q=2
,
1+q+q^{2}+\cdots+q^{M}=q^{M+1}-1
.
∎

Our next job will be to compute the
q
-adic expansions of
\frac{k}{q+1}-s_{n}
and
s_{n}
up to the
f(M+1)
st
p
-adic digit in all cases.

Lemma 6.4.12
.

Write
\lambda:=\frac{k}{q+1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\backslash\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

Suppose that
1\leqslant k\leqslant q-2
. Then
M
is odd and

\begin{array}[]{rclcccccr}s&=&[q-1,&q-k-2,&q-2,&q-k-2,&\cdots,&q-2,&q-k-2]_{M+1}\\
\lambda-s&\equiv&[k+1,&1,&k+1,&1,&\cdots,&k+1,&1]_{M+1}\end{array}

(2)

Suppose that
k=q-1
and
q>2
. Then
M
is even and

\begin{array}[]{rclccccr}s&=&[q-1,&q-1,&q-3,&\cdots,&q-1,&q-3]_{M+1}\\
\lambda-s&\equiv&[0,&2,&0,&\cdots,&2,&0]_{M+1}\end{array}

(3)

Suppose that
k=1
and
q=2
. Then
M
is odd and

\begin{array}[]{rclcccccr}s&=&[1,&1,&1,&0,&\cdots,&1,&0]_{M+1}\\
\lambda-s&\equiv&[0,&0,&1,&0,&\cdots,&1,&0]_{M+1}\end{array}

(4)

Suppose that
k=2
and
q=2
. Then
M
is even and

\begin{array}[]{rclccccccr}s&=&[1,&0,&1,&1,&0,&\cdots,&1,&0]_{M+1}\\
\lambda-s&\equiv&[1,&0,&0,&1,&0&\cdots,&1,&0]_{M+1}\end{array}

(5)

Suppose that
k=q
and
q>2
. Then
M
is even and

\begin{array}[]{rclccccr}s&=&[q-1,&q-2,&q-3,&\cdots,&q-2,&q-3]_{M+1}\\
\lambda-s&\equiv&[1,&2,&1,&\cdots,&2,&1]_{M+1}\end{array}

where
\equiv
denotes congruence modulo
q^{M+1}
.

Note that all of the displayed expansions are recurrent with period
2f
but not purely periodic: the two
q
-digits just preceeding the
\cdots
sign are the repeating ones.

Proof.

The parity of
M
can be deduced in each case from Lemma
6.4.11
and the parity of
N
.

Since
q^{M+1}
divides
q^{N}
in all cases,

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{-qk}{1-q^{2}}\mod q^{M+1}\mbox{ so}

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{-qk}{1-q^{2}}-\frac{1}{1-q}\mod q^{M+1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{-qk-q-1}{1-q^{2}}\mod q^{M+1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1+\frac{(q-2)+(q-k-2)q}{1-q^{2}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 2+\frac{(q-3)+(2q-k-2)q}{1-q^{2}},\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle\lambda-s

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{k}{q+1}+\frac{qk+q+1}{1-q^{2}}=\frac{k+q+1}{1-q^{2}}\mod q^{M+1}.

All the calculations can now be done in a straightforward manner. For the case where
k=q
, it is helpful to note that
s\leqslant n<q^{2N+1}
by definition of
n
, so that the last two
q
-digits in the
q
-expansion of
s
are
q-3
and
q-2
. For the cases where
q=2
it is helpful to remember that
s+2^{M+1}=n+1
and to use the
q
-expansions of
n
found in the proof of Lemma
6.4.11
.
∎

Corollary 6.4.13
.

In all five cases appearing in Lemma
6.4.12
, the carrying in the sum
s+(\lambda-s)
stops before the
(M+1)f
position. More precisely, we have

L(\lambda-s,s)<(M+1)f.

We note that the parity of
N
in each case was chosen to make this true.

Proof.

In each case we can use the expressions for
s
and
\lambda-s
in Lemma
6.4.12
and Lemma
6.4.5
(b).
∎

The remaining substantive result will be the following Proposition, whose proof highlights the importance of
counting the
non
-carry positions
in the sum
(\lambda-r)+r=\lambda
.

Proposition 6.4.14
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\backslash\mathbb{N}
,
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
\ell\geqslant 1
be given, such that

L(\lambda-s,s)<\ell.

Then for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
with
v_{p}(r-s)<\ell
, we have

v_{p}\langle\lambda-r|r\rangle-v_{p}(r-s)\quad>\quad v_{p}\langle\lambda-s|s\rangle-\ell.

Proof.

Theorem
6.4.3
implies that

(57)

v_{p}\langle\lambda-s|s\rangle=\ell-\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(\lambda-s,s).

Define the
m
-th truncation function

t_{m}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to\mathbb{N}
for
m\geqslant 0
by

t_{m}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\mu_{i}p^{i}\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\mu_{i}p^{i}

where
\mu_{i}\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}
. Note that the sum on the left is computed in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
but the sum on the right is computed in
\mathbb{N}
. Because
L(\lambda-s,s)<\ell
by assumption, Definition
6.4.1
(b) implies that
\gamma_{\ell-1}(\lambda-s,s)=0
. Therefore

(58)

\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(\lambda-s,s)>\mathcal{N}_{\ell-1}(\lambda-s,s)\geqslant\mathcal{N}_{m}(\lambda-s,s)=\mathcal{N}_{m}(t_{m}(\lambda-s),t_{m}(s))

whenever
0\leqslant m<\ell
. This inequality applies in particular when
m:=v_{p}(r-s)
which is strictly less than
\ell
by hypothesis. Note also that because
r\equiv s
mod
p^{m}
, we know that
t_{m}(r)=t_{m}(s)
and
t_{m}(\lambda-r)=t_{m}(\lambda-s)
.

Next, we have the crude estimate

(59)

\mathcal{N}_{j}(\lambda-r,r)\leqslant\mathcal{N}_{m}(t_{m}(\lambda-r),t_{m}(r))+(j-m)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad j\geqslant m.

Finally, let
j=\max\{m,L(\lambda-r,r)\}
which is finite by Lemma
6.4.2
because
\lambda\notin\mathbb{N}
. Then Theorem
6.4.3
implies that

(60)

v_{p}\langle\lambda-r|r\rangle=j-\mathcal{N}_{j}(\lambda-r,r).

Applying
(\ref{vpBin2})
,
(\ref{Crude})
,
(\ref{LastNoCarry})
and
(\ref{vpBin})
, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{lll}v_{p}\langle\lambda-r|r\rangle-m&=&j-\mathcal{N}_{j}(\lambda-r,r)-m\\
&\geqslant&-\mathcal{N}_{m}(t_{m}(\lambda-r),t_{m}(r))\\
&=&-\mathcal{N}_{m}(t_{m}(\lambda-s),t_{m}(s))\\
&>&-\mathcal{N}_{\ell}(\lambda-s,s)\\
&=&v_{p}\langle\lambda-s|s\rangle-\ell\end{array}

as required. ∎

We now put everything together to obtain our main estimate.

Theorem 6.4.15
.

Let
0\leqslant r\leqslant n
be given with
r\neq s
. Then

v_{p}\left(\frac{\langle\lambda-r|r\rangle}{(n-r)(q-1)+1}\right)>v_{p}\left(\frac{\langle\lambda-s|s\rangle}{(n-s)(q-1)+1}\right).

Proof.

By Corollary
6.4.8
, it is enough to show that

v_{p}(\langle\lambda-r|r\rangle)-v_{p}(r-s)>v_{p}(\langle\lambda-s|s\rangle)-(M+1)f.

We set
\ell:=(M+1)f
. Then Corollary
6.4.13
tells us
L(\lambda-s,s)<\ell
, and Lemma
6.4.7
tells us that
v_{p}(r-s)<\ell
. Now the required inequality follows from Proposition
6.4.14
.
∎

It remains to estimate the carries in the sum
s+(\lambda-s)
.

Proposition 6.4.16
.

For each
N\geqslant 6
of suitable parity depending on
k
and
q

v_{p}\left(\frac{\langle\lambda-s|s\rangle}{s(q-1)+1}\right)\leqslant\frac{3-N}{2}.

Proof.

We note that by Theorem
6.4.3
and Corollary
6.4.13
,

v_{p}(\langle\lambda-s|s\rangle)=(M+1)f-1-\mathcal{N}_{(M+1)f-1}(\lambda-s,s).

Considering Lemma
6.4.5
(a) and Lemma
6.4.12
we see that

\mathcal{N}_{(M+1)f-1}(\lambda-s,s)\geqslant\left\lfloor\frac{M-2}{2}\right\rfloor.

Moreover by Corollary
6.4.8

v_{p}((n-s)(q-1)+1)=(M+1)f
.

However,
M\geqslant N-3
by Lemma
6.4.11
. Hence

v_{p}\left(\frac{\langle\lambda-s|s\rangle}{s(q-1)+1}\right)\leqslant-\left\lfloor\frac{M}{2}\right\rfloor\leqslant-\frac{N-3}{2}.\qed

Here is the main result of
\lx@sectionsign
6.4
.

Corollary 6.4.17
.

The
p
-adic valuation of the rational number

\sum_{r=0}^{n}\frac{\binom{\frac{k}{q+1}}{r}\binom{\frac{qk}{q+1}-(q-1)r}{(n-r)(q-1)}}{(n-r)(q-1)+1}

is not bounded below as
n
varies.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
6.4.10
, Theorem
6.4.15
and Corollary
6.4.16
.
∎

6.5.
Proof of Theorem
5.3.12

We assume in this section that the hypotheses of Theorem
5.3.12
hold, namely:

•

[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)_{\tors}
and
\omega[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon(\Upsilon)[p^{\prime}]
,

•

the order
d
of
\omega[\mathscr{L}]
in
\PicCon(\Upsilon)
divides
q+1
,

•

e\in d\mathbb{Z}
is the order of
[\mathscr{L}]
in
\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)
,

•

n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
and
a\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
,

•

N_{n+1}\leqslant J_{n+1}
.

The map
M_{n,d}
from
\lx@sectionsign\ref{UpperHP}
sends
\PicCon(\Upsilon)[d]^{I}
into
M_{0}(h(\Upsilon_{n}),\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z})^{I}
. Since
\omega[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon(\Upsilon)[d]^{I}
, we can now use Lemma
4.3.5
(a) to make the following

Definition 6.5.1
.

We let
k\in\{1,2,\cdots,d\}
be the unique integer such that

M_{n,d}(\omega[\mathscr{L}])=k\nu_{n}.

Recall from Definition
5.3.9
and
[
8
, Definition 4.1.1]
the affinoid
W:=W_{a,n}
:

W=\Sp K\left\langle\tau,\frac{1}{\tau^{q-1}-1}\right\rangle\quad\mbox{where}\quad\tau:=\frac{x-a}{\pi_{F}^{n}}.

We fix a primitive
(q-1)^{th}
root
\zeta
of
1
in
K^{\times}
using Remark
6.2.11
and note the following basic properties of our affinoid domain
W
.

Lemma 6.5.2
.

(1)

W\cap V_{n}
is obtained from
W
by removing the open disc
\{|\tau|<1\}
.

(2)

W\cap V_{n+1}
is obtained from
W
by removing the
q
open discs

\{|\tau-\pi_{F}|<|\pi_{F}|\},\quad\{|\tau-\pi_{F}\zeta|<|\pi_{F}|\},\quad\cdots\quad\{|\tau-\pi_{F}\zeta^{q-2}|<|\pi_{F}|\},\quad\{|\tau|<|\pi_{F}|\}.

Proof.

(a)
V_{n}
is obtained from
\mathbb{D}
by removing all open discs of radius
|\pi_{F}|^{n}
around all points in
\mathcal{O}_{F}
with respect to the
x
-coordinate. Now
W
is contained in a closed disc
\Sp K\langle\tau\rangle
of radius
|\pi_{F}|^{n}
which contains exactly
q
such open discs. We have removed
q-1
of these when forming
W
. Hence
W\cap V_{n}
is obtained from
W
by removing the last of these open discs, namely
\{|\tau|<1\}
.

(b) This time
\Sp K\langle\tau\rangle
contains
q^{2}
open discs of radius
|\pi_{F}|^{n+1}
with respect to the
x
-coordinate. Of these,
q(q-1)
are contained in the
q-1
open discs of radius
|\pi_{F}|^{n}
which are removed when we form
W
. This shows that
W
contains exactly
q
more such open discs that have to be removed when forming
W\cap V_{n+1}
.
∎

When
n=0,q=5
, a picture of
W\supset W\cap V_{n+1}\supset W\cap V_{n}
can be found in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{TheIntegral}
.

Theorem 6.5.3
.

There exists
c\in K(\tau)\cap\mathcal{O}(W)^{\times}
with
\mathcal{S}(c)\subseteq F
, and an algebraic generator
\dot{z}\in\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)
with associated rational function

\psi(\dot{z}^{\otimes d})=u\hskip 2.84544ptc\quad\mbox{where}\quad u:=\frac{1}{(\tau^{q}-\pi_{F}^{q-1}\tau)^{k}}.

Proof.

We define
\mathcal{S}_{1}:=\{0\}\cup\{1,\zeta,\zeta^{2},\cdots,\zeta^{q-2}\}
and note that
\mathcal{S}_{1}
forms a set of coset representatives for
\pi_{F}\mathcal{O}_{F}
in
\mathcal{O}_{F}
containing
0
.
Let
\mathcal{S}
be any set of coset representatives for
\pi_{F}^{n+2}\mathcal{O}_{F}
in
\mathcal{O}_{F}
containing
a+\pi_{F}^{n}\mathcal{S}_{1}=W(\overline{K})\cap\mathcal{O}_{F}
.

Using Definition
6.5.1
together with Lemma
4.3.5
(b) we see that

r_{n+1}(M_{n+1,d}(\omega[\mathscr{L}]))=M_{n,d}(\omega[\mathscr{L}])=k\nu_{n}=r_{n+1}(-k\nu_{n+1}).

Lemma
4.3.5
(c) then implies that
M_{n+1,d}(\omega[\mathscr{L}])=-k\nu_{n+1}
, and hence

M_{n+1,d}(\omega[\mathscr{L}])(D_{b,n+1})=-k+d\mathbb{Z}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad b\in\mathcal{O}_{F}.

Note that
-\frac{k}{d}\notin\mathbb{N}
because
k\geqslant 1
. We can now apply Theorem
4.3.7
(a) with
n+1
in place of
n
with
k_{b}:=-k
for all
b\in\mathcal{S}
, to choose an algebraic generator
\dot{y}\in\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)
with associated rational function
v
such that

v_{b}(v)=k\quad\mbox{for all}\quad b\in\mathcal{S}.

Now, since
u=\frac{1}{(\tau^{q}-\pi_{F}^{q-1}\tau)^{k}},\tau=\frac{x-a}{\pi^{n}_{F}}
and
k\neq 0
, we see that
\mathcal{S}(u)=a+\pi^{n}_{F}\mathcal{S}_{1}
, and that
v_{b}(u)=v_{b}(v)
for all
b\in\mathcal{S}(u)
. Therefore the rational function
c:=v/u
satisfies
\mathcal{S}(c)=\mathcal{S}(v)\backslash\mathcal{S}(u)=\mathcal{S}\backslash(a+\pi^{n}_{F}\mathcal{S}_{1})\subseteq F
. This finite set does not meet
W(\overline{K})
because
\mathcal{S}\cap W(\overline{K})=a+\pi^{n}_{F}\mathcal{S}_{1}
. Hence
c\in\mathcal{O}(W)^{\times}
as claimed. ∎

Proposition 6.5.4
.

Let
[\dot{z}]
denote the image of
\dot{z}\in\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)
in
\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
under the natural restriction map
\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)\to\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
.
Then the element

\dot{y}:=(1-(\pi_{F}/\tau)^{q-1})^{k/d}[\dot{z}]\in\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)

is an algebraic generator for
\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
with associated rational function
c/\tau^{qk}
.

Proof.

Since
\dot{z}
generates
\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)
as an
(\mathcal{O}_{W})_{\overline{W\cap V_{n+1}}}(W)
-module, we see that
[\dot{z}]
generates
\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
as an
(\mathcal{O}_{W})_{\overline{W\cap V_{n}}}(W)
-module. Note that
1-(\pi_{F}/\tau)^{q-1}\in 1+\mathcal{O}(W)\langle\pi_{F}/\tau^{2}\rangle^{\circ\circ}
, so by
[
8
, Lemma 4.3.2(a)]
it is a
d
th power in
1+\mathcal{O}(W)\langle\pi_{F}/\tau^{2}\rangle^{\circ\circ}
. Since
\mathcal{O}(W)\langle\pi_{F}/\tau^{2}\rangle\subseteq\mathcal{O}(W)_{\overline{W\cap V_{n}}}
in view of Lemma
6.5.2
(a), we conclude that
\dot{y}
also generates
\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
as an
\mathcal{O}(W)_{\overline{W\cap V_{n}}}
-module as required by Definition
4.3.6
(a). Using Theorem
6.5.3
, we see that the associated rational function to
\dot{y}
is

\psi(\dot{y}^{\otimes d})=\left(1-(\pi_{F}/\tau)^{q-1}\right)^{k}\frac{c}{(\tau^{q}-\pi_{F}^{q-1}\tau)^{k}}=\frac{c}{\tau^{qk}}.

Next,
\mathcal{S}(c/\tau^{qk})\subseteq\{a\}\cup\mathcal{S}(c)\subseteq K
by Theorem
6.5.3
, so Definition
4.3.6
(c) is satisfied.
Since
c\in\mathcal{O}(W)^{\times}
and
k\neq 0
, we see that
\mathcal{S}(c/\tau^{qk})\cap W=\{a\}
. Definition
4.3.6
(d) is now satisfied vacuously by
\dot{y}
, whereas Definition
4.3.6
(e) holds because
v_{a}(c/\tau^{qk})/d=-qk/d\notin\mathbb{N}
as
k\geqslant 1
.
∎

After Theorem
6.5.3
and Proposition
6.5.4
and Corollary
4.3.12
, we have explicit presentations for the
\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)
-module
\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
and
\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)
-module
\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)
. Next, we will make the connection with the theory developed earlier on in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{PfOfCompOnW}
by making a change of coordinates: recall from
\lx@sectionsign\ref{TheIntegral}
the affinoid domain

X:=\Sp K\left\langle x,\frac{1}{x^{q-1}-1}\right\rangle.

Lemma 6.5.5
.

Let
g_{a,n}:=\begin{pmatrix}1&-a\\
0&\pi_{F}^{n}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{B}(K)
. Then
g_{a,n}(W)=X
.

Proof.

We have
g_{a,n}\cdot z=\frac{z-a}{\pi_{F}^{n}}
for all
z\in\overline{K}
. Using the definitions of
W
and
X
, we see that
z\in W(\overline{K})
if and only if
g_{a,n}\cdot z\in X(\overline{K})
. Hence
g_{a,n}(W)=X
.
∎

Corollary 6.5.6
.

There is a
K
-algebra isomorphism

\chi:\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)

such that
\chi(\tau)=x
and
\chi(\pi_{F}^{n}\partial_{x})=\partial_{x}
, and
\chi(\mathscr{D}_{n+m}(W))=\mathscr{D}_{m}(X)
for all
m\geqslant 0
.

Proof.

Note that
\varrho(g_{a,n})=\pi_{F}^{-n}
by Definition
2.5.1
and that
g_{a,n}(W)=X
by Lemma
6.5.5
. The affinoid
W
lies in
\mathbb{A}(|\pi_{F}|^{n}\partial_{x}/\varpi)^{\dagger}
by Lemma
5.3.10
, so we can apply Corollary
2.5.6
with
r=\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}
to get the required
K
-algebra isomorphism

\chi:=(g_{a,n})^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}}(W):\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)=\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}}(W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}(X)=\mathscr{D}_{0}(X).

Then
\chi(\tau)=g_{a,n}\cdot\tau=(g_{a,n}\cdot x-a)/\pi_{F}^{n}=((a+\pi_{F}^{n}x)-a)/\pi_{F}^{n}=x
and
\chi(\partial_{x})=g_{a,n}\cdot\partial_{x}=\varrho(g_{a,n})\partial_{x}=\pi_{F}^{-n}\partial_{x}
by Lemma
2.5.2
(e).

Finally, we can apply Corollary
2.5.6
again with
r=\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n+m}
to see that
\chi
maps
\mathscr{D}_{n+m}(W)
onto
\mathscr{D}_{m}(X)
for all
m\geqslant 0
.
∎

Next, we record the presentations for
\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
and
\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)
after applying this change of coordinates as well as untwisting via the untwisting automorphism
\theta_{\chi(c),d}^{-1}
of
\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)=\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi}(X)
from Corollary
2.4.11
. More precisely:

Definition 6.5.7
.

(1)

Define
b:=\chi(c)\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
and
w:=(x^{q}-\pi_{F}^{q-1}x)^{-k}=\chi(u)
.

(2)

Let
M:=\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
where
\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)
is viewed as a
\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)
-module via the isomorphism
\theta_{b,d}^{-1}\circ\chi:\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)
.

(3)

Let
M^{\prime}:=\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)
where
\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)
is viewed as a
\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)
-module via the isomorphism
\theta_{b,d}^{-1}\circ\chi:\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)
.

Theorem 6.5.8
.

We have

M^{\prime}\cong\frac{\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)}{\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)}\quad\mbox{and}\quad M\cong\frac{\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)}{\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(x^{-qk})}(x^{-qk},d)}.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.5.3
and Corollary
4.3.12
, we have

\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)=\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)\cdot{z}\cong\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)/\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)R_{\mathcal{S}(uc)}(uc,d).

Now, applying Lemma
4.2.22
(b) with
r=\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}
shows that

\chi(R_{\mathcal{S}(uc)}(uc,d))\equiv R_{\mathcal{S}(\chi(uc))}(\chi(uc),d)\quad\mod K^{\times}.

Next,
\chi(uc)=wb
, so Lemma
4.2.17
(c) gives

R_{\mathcal{S}(\chi(uc))}(\chi(uc),d)=R_{\mathcal{S}(wb)}(wb,d)=\theta_{b,d}(R_{\mathcal{S}(wb)}(w,d)).

Since
\mathcal{S}(w)\cap\mathcal{S}(b)=\emptyset
,
\mathcal{S}(wb)
is the disjoint union of
\mathcal{S}(w)
and
\mathcal{S}(b)
, so
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(wb)}=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(w)}\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(b)}
and
R_{\mathcal{S}(wb)}(w,d)=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(b)}R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)
. Therefore

\chi(R_{\mathcal{S}(uc)}(uc,d))\equiv\theta_{b,d}\left(\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(b)}R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)\right)\quad\mod K^{\times}.

However,
b
and hence
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}(b)}
are units in
\mathcal{O}(X)
, so we have

(61)

\theta_{b,d}^{-1}\chi\left(\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)R_{\mathcal{S}(uc)}(uc,d)\right)=\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d).

This implies that
M^{\prime}\cong\frac{\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)}{\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)}
as
\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)
-modules. The second isomorphism is deduced similarly using Proposition
6.5.4
instead of Theorem
6.5.3
. ∎

We assume until the end of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{PfOfCOW}
that the field
K
is discretely valued.

Corollary 6.5.9
.

The
\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)
-module
\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
is simple if
k\neq d
.

Proof.

After Theorem
6.5.8
, it is enough to check that
\frac{\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)}{\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(x^{-qk})}(x^{-qk},d)}
is a simple
\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)
-module. Now
R_{\mathcal{S}(x^{-qk})}(x^{-qk},d)=x\partial_{x}+\frac{qk}{d}
by Definition
4.2.10
(b), and
-\frac{qk}{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\backslash\mathbb{Z}
because
q\equiv-1\mod d
and
k\neq 0\mod d
by our assumption on
k
. We can now apply Theorem
4.4.1
to conclude.
∎

Proposition 6.5.10
.

The natural action map

\varphi:\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)

given by
\varphi(Q\otimes v):=Q\cdot\tau(v)
for all
Q\in\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)
and
v\in\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)
, is surjective.

Proof.

The restriction map
\tau:\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)\to\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
is non-zero, so
\varphi
is a non-zero
\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)
-linear map. If
k\neq d
then
\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
is a simple
\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)
-module by Corollary
6.5.9
, so
\varphi
is surjective.

Suppose now that
k=d
. Inspecting the definition of the algebraic generator
\dot{y}\in\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
from Proposition
6.5.4
, we see that

\tau^{q-1}\dot{y}=(\tau^{q-1}-\pi_{F}^{q-1})[\dot{z}]\in\im\varphi.

Since
\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)=\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)\cdot\dot{y}
, it remains to show that
\dot{y}\in\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)\cdot\tau^{q-1}\dot{y}
. Now,

M=\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)\cong\frac{\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)}{\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(x^{-qd})}(x^{-qd},d)}

as
\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)
-modules by Theorem
6.5.8
. However,
\mathcal{S}(x^{-qd})=\{0\}
, and

R_{\{0\}}(x^{-qd},d)=x\partial_{x}+q=R_{\{0\}}(x^{-q},1),

so applying Theorem
4.2.16
with
u=x^{-q}
,
t=1
and
d=1
, we see that

(62)

M\cong(j_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}-\{0\}})_{\overline{U_{0}}}(X)

as
\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)
-modules. Inside
(j_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}-\{0\}})_{\overline{U_{0}}}(X)
, we compute

x^{-q}=(-1)^{q-1}\partial_{x}^{[q-1]}\cdot x^{-1}\in\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)\cdot x^{q-1}x^{-q},

and hence
\dot{y}\in\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)\cdot\tau^{q-1}\dot{y}
because the isomorphism (
62
) sends
1\otimes\dot{y}
to
x^{-q}
. ∎

Next, we need to obtain the following upper bound.

Lemma 6.5.11
.

We have
\ell(\ker\varphi)\leqslant q-1
.

Proof.

After applying the change of coordinates made precise by Theorem
6.5.8
, the map
\varphi
induces a non-zero
\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)
-linear map

\psi:\frac{\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)}{\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)}\cong\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)}{\otimes}{}M^{\prime}\longrightarrow M\cong\frac{\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)}{\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(x^{-qk})}(x^{-qk},d)}.

Now, by applying Theorem
6.2.10
— see also Example
6.2.12
— we know that

\begin{array}[]{lll}\Cycl\left(\frac{\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X})}{\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X})R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)}\right)&=&[\mathscr{X}_{0}]+q[T^{\ast}_{0}\mathscr{X}_{0}],\quad\mbox{whereas}\\
\Cycl\left(\frac{\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X})}{\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X})R(x^{-qk},d)}\right)&=&[\mathscr{X}_{0}]+[T^{\ast}_{0}\mathscr{X}_{0}]\end{array}.

Since
\varphi
is surjective by Proposition
6.5.10
, so is
\psi
. Therefore

\Cycl(\ker\psi)=(q-1)[T_{0}^{\ast}\mathscr{X}_{0}].

Since
|\Cycl|
is additive on short exact sequences and
|\Cycl(V)|\geqslant 1
for non-zero holonomic
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X})
-modules
V
, we see that
\ell(V)\leqslant|\Cycl(V)|
for every holonomic
\mathscr{D}^{\dagger}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathscr{X})
-module
V
. Hence
\ell(\ker\varphi)=\ell(\ker\psi)\leqslant|\Cycl(\ker\psi)|=q-1
. ∎

Definition 6.5.12
.

Define
g=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\pi_{F}^{n+1}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in N_{n+1}
and
h:=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\pi_{F}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in N_{1}
.

It follows from Lemma
5.3.3
that
\beta(g)\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(\mathbb{D})^{\times}
whenever
r<\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n+1}
. Since
\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n}<\varpi/|\pi_{F}|^{n+1}
,
\beta(g)
lies in
\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})^{\times}
and hence can also be viewed as an element of
\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)^{\times}
.

Proposition 6.5.13
.

\beta(g)\notin\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)+\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)R_{\mathcal{S}(uc)}(uc,d).

Proof.

Suppose for a contradiction that
\beta(g)\in\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)+\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)R_{\mathcal{S}(uc)}(uc,d)
. Then we can use equation
(\ref{ChiRwd})
to deduce that

\theta_{b,d}^{-1}\chi(\beta(g))\in\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)+\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)\cdot\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d).

Since
\chi(\pi_{F}^{n}\partial_{x})=\partial_{x}
and
g\cdot x-x=\pi_{F}^{n+1}
by Lemma
5.2.2
(a), we see that

\chi(\beta(g))=\chi\left(\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\pi_{F}^{(n+1)m}\partial_{x}^{[m]}\right)=\sum\limits_{m=0}^{\infty}\pi_{F}^{m}\partial_{x}^{[m]}=\beta(h).

By Proposition
5.2.13
, we can find an element
c_{b^{-1},d}(h)\in\mathcal{O}(X)^{\times}
such that

\theta_{b,d}^{-1}\chi(\beta(g))=\theta_{b^{-1},d}(\beta(h))=c_{b^{-1},d}(h)\beta(h).

Since
\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)\subseteq\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)
, we conclude that
c_{b^{-1},d}(h)\beta(h)\in\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)+\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)
. Since
c_{b^{-1},d}(h)
is a unit in
\mathcal{O}(X)
, this implies that
\beta(h)\in\mathscr{D}_{1}(X)+\mathscr{D}_{0}(X)R_{\mathcal{S}(w)}(w,d)
. This contradicts Theorem
6.3.21
.
∎

Note that since
g\in N_{n+1}\leqslant J_{n+1}
by our assumption on
J
, and
W
is
N_{n+1}
-stable by Lemma
5.3.10
, the
\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)
-module carries a compatible action of
g
.

Corollary 6.5.14
.

The secret
g
-action from Lemma
5.1.6
on

\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)}{\otimes}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)

is non-trivial.

Proof.

Write
S:=\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)
and
S^{\prime}:=\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)
. Choose an algebraic generator
\dot{z}\in\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)
as in Theorem
6.5.3
and write
r:=R_{\mathcal{S}(uc)}(uc,d)
. By Corollary
4.3.12
, we can choose
Q\in S^{\prime}
such that
Q\cdot\dot{z}=g\cdot\dot{z}
. If the secret action was trivial, then

1\otimes\dot{z}=g\star(1\otimes\dot{z})=\beta(g)^{-1}\otimes g\cdot\dot{z}=\beta(g)^{-1}\otimes Q\cdot\dot{z}=\beta(g)^{-1}Q\otimes\dot{z}.

Now
\ann_{S^{\prime}}(\dot{z})=S^{\prime}r
by Corollary
4.3.12
, so
\ann_{S}(1\otimes\dot{z})=Sr
because
S
is a flat
S^{\prime}
-module by Lemma
4.3.10
(a), Therefore
\beta(g)^{-1}Q-1\in Sr
. Hence
Q-\beta(g)\in Sr
so
\beta(g)\in S^{\prime}+Sr
, which contradicts Proposition
6.5.13
.
∎

Next, we need some elementary group theory. Let
k_{F}
denote the residue field of our
p
-adic field
F
and let
k_{F}^{\times}=k_{F}-\{0\}
. We view
k_{F}
as a finite elementary abelian
p
-group of order
q
, and
k_{F}^{\times}
as a cyclic group of order
q-1
. As in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CPandSA}
, let
\Irr_{\overline{K}}(k_{F})
be the set of isomorphism classes of simple
\overline{K}[k_{F}]
-modules, which is naturally in bijection with the set
\widehat{k_{F}}
of
\overline{K}^{\times}
