-valued characters of
k_{F}
. The group
k_{F}^{\times}
acts on
k_{F}
by multiplication; since this action is by abelian group homomorphisms, we obtain an induced action of
k_{F}^{\times}
on
\widehat{k_{F}}
given by

(t\cdot\chi)(a)=\chi(t^{-1}a)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\chi\in\widehat{k_{F}},a\in k_{F},t\in k_{F}^{\times}.

Lemma 6.5.15
.

k_{F}^{\times}
acts transitively and freely on
\widehat{k_{F}}\backslash\{0\}
.

Proof.

Suppose
t\in k_{F}^{\times}
fixes
0\neq\chi\in\widehat{k_{F}}
under the above action. Then
(t^{-1}-1)a\in\ker\chi
for all
a\in k_{F}
. Since
k_{F}
is a
field
,
(t^{-1}-1)k_{F}=k_{F}
unless
t=1
. But
\chi\neq 0
forces
\ker\chi<k_{F}
, so indeed
t=1
. The result follows since
|k_{F}^{\times}|=|\widehat{k_{F}}\backslash\{0\}|
.
∎

Corollary 6.5.16
.

The action of
k_{F}^{\times}
on
\Irr_{K}(k_{F})
has exactly two orbits.

Proof.

When
K=\overline{K}
, this follows directly from Lemma
6.5.15
because the bijection
\Irr_{\overline{K}}(k_{F})\cong\widehat{k_{F}}
respects the
k_{F}^{\times}
-action on both sides. In general, because our ground field
K
has characteristic zero, we may identify
\Irr_{K}(k_{F})
with the set of primitive idempotents of the group ring
K[k_{F}]
, and every such idempotent is the sum of a
\mathcal{G}_{K}
-orbit of primitive idempotents of
\overline{K}[k_{F}]
, where
\mathcal{G}_{K}=\Gal(\overline{K}/K)
. Since the
\mathcal{G}_{K}
-action on
\overline{K}[k_{F}]
commutes with the
k_{F}^{\times}
-action, we deduce from the case
K=\overline{K}
that any two non-principal idempotents in
K[k_{F}]
lie in the same
k_{F}^{\times}
-orbit.
∎

We now spell out the application of these ideas that we will need.

Corollary 6.5.17
.

Let
B:=\begin{pmatrix}1&a\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&-a\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
. Then

(1)

B
stabilises
W=\Sp K\langle\tau,\frac{1}{\tau^{q-1}-1}\rangle
where
\tau=\frac{x-a}{\pi_{F}^{n}}
.

(2)

B
normalises
N_{n+1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\pi_{F}^{n+1}\mathcal{O}_{F}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
and
N_{n+2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\pi_{F}^{n+2}\mathcal{O}_{F}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
.

(3)

the
B
-action on
\Irr_{K}(N_{n+1}/N_{n+2})
has exactly two orbits.

Proof.

(a) This is an easy calculation using Lemma
5.2.2
(a).

(b,c) We compute that
\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
conjugates
\begin{pmatrix}1&\pi_{F}^{n+1}a\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
to
\begin{pmatrix}1&\pi_{F}^{n+1}a\alpha^{-1}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
, so that the conjugation
B
-action on
N_{n+1}/N_{n+2}
is completely determined by the multiplication action of
k_{F}^{\times}
on
k_{F}
. Now apply Corollary
6.5.16
.
∎

We can finally put everything together and prove Theorem
5.3.12
.

Proof of Theorem
5.3.12
.

Consider the commutative triangle

\textstyle{\left(\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)\right)_{N_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)}
\textstyle{\left(\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)\right)_{J_{n+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where we must show that the diagonal map is an isomorphism. Because
J_{n+1}
contains
N_{n+1}
by assumption, the vertical map is surjective. Proposition
6.5.10
implies that the diagonal map is surjective. Hence, to show that it is an isomorphism it is enough to check that the horizontal map is injective. By enlarging
K
if necessary, we will assume that
K
contains a primitive
p^{th}
-root of unity
\zeta_{p}
.

We will apply Theorem
5.1.11
with the following parameters:
S=\mathscr{D}_{n}(W)
,
S^{\prime}=\mathscr{D}_{n+1}(W)
,
M=\mathscr{L}_{n}(W)
,
M^{\prime}=\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(W)
,
G
is the subgroup
B
from Lemma
6.5.17
,
H=N_{n+1}
and
H^{\prime}=N_{n+2}
. Note that
M
is an
S\rtimes B
-module and
M^{\prime}
is an
S^{\prime}\rtimes B
-module, and these actions descend to
S\rtimes_{H}B
and
S^{\prime}\rtimes_{H^{\prime}}B
respectively by Theorem
5.3.6
. Note also that the restriction map
M^{\prime}\to M
is
B
-equivariant because our line bundle
\mathscr{L}
is assumed to be
I
-equivariant in Definition
5.3.2
. All points of Hypotheses
5.1.5
clearly hold, except possibly for points (e,f) of Hypothesis
5.1.5
which, after decoding the notation, follow from Theorem
5.2.11
since
G=B\leqslant\mathcal{G}_{W}
.

Next, we verify the conditions (a-c) of Theorem
5.1.11
.

(a) This is Corollary
6.5.14
.

(b) This is Corollary
6.5.17
(c).

(c) Since
K
is assumed to contain
\zeta_{p}
and since
H/H^{\prime}=N_{n+1}/N_{n+2}\cong k_{F}
is an elementary abelian
p
-group of order
q
, we see that
\left|\Irr_{K}(H/H^{\prime})\right|=q
. Now the required inequality follows from Lemma
6.5.11
.

Theorem
5.1.11
now tells us that the comparison map
(S\otimes_{S^{\prime}}M^{\prime})_{H}\to M
is injective as desired. ∎

7.
Coadmissibility and applications

7.1.
Quasi-coherent modules over a tower of rings

We begin with some algebraic preliminaries. Recall that a
tower of rings
is a diagram

S_{\bullet}=S_{0}\leftarrow S_{1}\leftarrow S_{2}\leftarrow\cdots

of associative, unital rings, and unital ring homomorphisms. A
morphism
of towers of rings
f_{\bullet}:T_{\bullet}\to S_{\bullet}
is a commutative diagram of the form

\textstyle{T_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{T_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{T_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{0}}
\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

An
S_{\bullet}
-module
is a diagram
M_{\bullet}:=M_{0}\leftarrow M_{1}\leftarrow M_{2}\leftarrow\cdots
where each
M_{n}
is an
S_{n}
-module, and every connecting map
\mu^{M}_{n+1,n}:M_{n+1}\to M_{n}
is an
S_{n+1}
-linear map for
n\geqslant 1
, where
M_{n}
is regarded as an
S_{n+1}
-module via restriction of scalars along the ring homomorphism
\mu^{S}_{n+1,n}:S_{n+1}\to S_{n}
. A
morphism of
S_{\bullet}
-modules
is defined in the obvious way. The space of
global sections
of
M_{\bullet}
is

\Gamma(M_{\bullet}):=\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}M_{n},

this is naturally a module over
\Gamma(S_{\bullet})=\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}S_{n}
. We say that the
S_{\bullet}
-module
M_{\bullet}
is
quasi-coherent
if the following map is an isomorphism for all
n\geqslant 0
:

1\otimes\mu^{M}_{n+1,n}:S_{n}\underset{S_{n+1}}{\otimes}{}M_{n+1}\longrightarrow M_{n}.

Given a morphism
f_{\bullet}:T_{\bullet}\to S_{\bullet}
and an
S_{\bullet}
-module
M_{\bullet}
, we can form the
pullback

T_{\bullet}
-module
T_{\bullet}\otimes_{S_{\bullet}}M_{\bullet}
defined by

(T_{\bullet}\otimes_{S_{\bullet}}M_{\bullet})_{n}=T_{n}\otimes_{S_{n}}M_{n}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad n\geqslant 0,

whose connecting maps for
n\geqslant m
are given by

\mu^{T\otimes_{S}M}_{n,m}=\mu^{T}_{n,m}\otimes_{\mu^{S}_{n,m}}\mu^{M}_{n,m}:T_{n}\otimes_{S_{n}}M_{n}\longrightarrow T_{m}\otimes_{S_{m}}M_{m}.

Lemma 7.1.1
.

If
M_{\bullet}
quasi-coherent, then so is
T_{\bullet}\otimes_{S_{\bullet}}M_{\bullet}
.

Proof.

Consider the following commutative square:

\textstyle{T_{n}\underset{T_{n+1}}{\otimes}{}\left(T_{n+1}\underset{S_{n+1}}{\otimes}{}M_{n+1}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\otimes\mu^{T\otimes_{S}M}_{n+1,n}}
\textstyle{T_{n}\underset{S_{n}}{\otimes}{}M_{n}}
\textstyle{T_{n}\underset{S_{n+1}}{\otimes}{}M_{n+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{T_{n}\underset{S_{n}}{\otimes}{}(S_{n}\underset{S_{n+1}}{\otimes}{}M_{n+1}).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\otimes\mu^{M}_{n+1,n}}

The vertical arrow on the left and the bottom horizontal arrow are isomorphisms by the associativity of tensor product, whereas the vertical arrow on the right is an isomorphism because
M_{\bullet}
is quasi-coherent. So the top horizontal arrow is also an isomorphism.
∎

For each integer
c\geqslant 0
, we can form the
shifted tower

S_{\bullet}[c]
:

S_{\bullet}[c]=S_{c}\leftarrow S_{c+1}\leftarrow S_{c+2}\leftarrow\cdots

so that
S_{\bullet}[c]_{n}=S_{n+c}
for all
n\geqslant 0
. There is a
shift functor

M_{\bullet}\mapsto M_{\bullet}[c]
from
S_{\bullet}
-modules to
S_{\bullet}[c]
-modules, given by
M_{\bullet}[c]_{n}=M_{n+c}
for all
n\geqslant 0
. The connecting maps
\mu^{S}_{n+c,c}:S_{n+c}\to S_{n}
in
S_{\bullet}
induce a morphism of towers of rings

\iota_{S}^{c}:S_{\bullet}[c]\longrightarrow S_{\bullet}

and there is a similar morphism of towers of abelian groups
\iota_{M}^{c}:M_{\bullet}[c]\to M_{\bullet}
.

Lemma 7.1.2
.

Let
c\geqslant 0
and let
M_{\bullet}
be an
S_{\bullet}
-module.

(1)

The map
\Gamma(\iota_{S}^{c}):\Gamma(S_{\bullet}[c])\to\Gamma(S_{\bullet})
is a ring isomorphism.

(2)

The map
\Gamma(\iota_{M}^{c}):\Gamma(M_{\bullet}[c])\to\Gamma(M_{\bullet})
is an isomorphism of abelian groups.

Proof.

(a) Because
c\geqslant 0
, we have the projection map
\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}S_{n}\to\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}S_{n+c}
, and it is a two-sided inverse to
\Gamma(\iota^{c}_{S})=\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}\mu^{S}_{n+c,n}:\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}S_{n+c}\to\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}S_{n}
.

(b) This is entirely similar.∎

In fact,
\Gamma(\iota_{M}^{c})
is a
\Gamma(S_{\bullet}[c])
-linear isomorphism, when its codomain is regarded as a
\Gamma(S_{\bullet}[c])
-module via the map
\Gamma(\iota^{c}_{S})
.

Proposition 7.1.3
.

Let
c\geqslant 0
and let
f_{\bullet}:T_{\bullet}\to S_{\bullet}
and
g_{\bullet}:S_{\bullet}[c]\to T_{\bullet}
be morphisms of towers of rings such that the diagram

\textstyle{T_{\bullet}[c]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\bullet}[c]}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{c}_{T}}
\textstyle{T_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{S_{\bullet}[c]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{c}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{g_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{S_{\bullet}}

is commutative. Then:

(1)

\Gamma(f_{\bullet}):\Gamma(T_{\bullet})\to\Gamma(S_{\bullet})
is a ring isomorphism.

(2)

Let
M_{\bullet}
be a quasi-coherent
S_{\bullet}
-module and let
L_{\bullet}:=T_{\bullet}\otimes_{S_{\bullet}[c]}M_{\bullet}[c]
. Then there is a
\Gamma(T_{\bullet})
-linear isomorphism
\Gamma(L_{\bullet})\to\Gamma(M_{\bullet})
, where the
\Gamma(S_{\bullet})
-module
\Gamma(M_{\bullet})
is regarded as a
\Gamma(T_{\bullet})
-module via
\Gamma(f_{\bullet})
.

Proof.

(a) Applying
\Gamma
gives the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Gamma(T_{\bullet}[c])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma(f_{\bullet}[c])}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma(\iota^{c}_{T})}
\textstyle{\Gamma(T_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma(f_{\bullet})}
\textstyle{\Gamma(S_{\bullet}[c])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma(\iota^{c}_{S})}
\scriptstyle{\Gamma(g_{\bullet})}
\textstyle{\Gamma(S_{\bullet}).}

Since the horizontal arrows are isomorphisms by Lemma
7.1.2
(a), we conclude that the diagonal arrow has both a right inverse and a left inverse. Hence it is an isomorphism, and consequently so is
\Gamma(f_{\bullet})
.

(b) Noting that
L_{m}=T_{m}\otimes_{S_{m+c}}M_{m+c}
for each
m\geqslant 0
, we define additive maps

M_{m+c}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{m}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad L_{m}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta_{m}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad M_{m}

as follows: for
x,y\in M_{m+c}
and
t\in T_{m}
we set

(63)

\alpha_{m}(x)=1\otimes x\quad\mbox{and}\quad\beta_{m}(t\otimes y)=f_{m}(t)\cdot\mu^{M}_{m+c,m}(y).

Now let
n\geqslant m\geqslant 0
and consider the following diagrams:

\textstyle{M_{n+c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{M}_{n+c,m+c}}
\textstyle{L_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{L}_{n,m}}
\textstyle{M_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{M}_{n,m}}
\textstyle{M_{m+c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{m}}
\textstyle{L_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{m}}
\textstyle{M_{m}}

\textstyle{L_{m+c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{m+c}}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{L}_{m+c,c}}
\textstyle{L_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{m}}
\textstyle{M_{m+c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{M}_{m+c,m}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{m}}
\textstyle{M_{m}.}

The verification that these diagrams commute is straightforward; for example, to show that
\alpha_{m}\circ\beta_{m+c}=\mu^{L}_{m+c,c}
, let
t\in T_{m+c}
and
y\in M_{m+2c}
and use the fact that
g_{m}\circ f_{m+c}=\mu^{T}_{m+c,m}
to calculate as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\alpha_{m}(\beta_{m+c}(t\otimes y))&=&\alpha_{m}(f_{m+c}(t)\cdot\mu^{M}_{m+2c,m+c}(y))\\
&=&1\otimes f_{m+c}(t)\cdot\mu^{M}_{m+2c,m+c}(y)\\
&=&g_{m}(f_{m+c}(t))\otimes\mu^{M}_{m+2c,m+c}(y)\\
&=&\mu^{T}_{m+c,m}(t)\otimes\mu^{M}_{m+2c,m+c}(y)\\
&=&\mu^{L}_{m+c,m}(t\otimes y).\end{array}

Thus we have defined morphisms of towers of abelian groups
\alpha_{\bullet}:M_{\bullet}[c]\to L_{\bullet}
and
\beta_{\bullet}:L_{\bullet}\to M_{\bullet}
, and they fit into the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{L_{\bullet}[c]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{\bullet}[c]}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{c}_{L}}
\textstyle{L_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{M_{\bullet}[c]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{c}_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{M_{\bullet}.}

Considering the
T_{\bullet}
-module
L_{\bullet}
as an
S_{\bullet}[c]
-module via the morphism
g_{\bullet}:S_{\bullet}[c]\to T_{\bullet}
, and the
S_{\bullet}
-module
M_{\bullet}
as a
T_{\bullet}
-module via the morphism
f_{\bullet}:T_{\bullet}\to S_{\bullet}
, we can verify that
\alpha_{\bullet}
becomes a morphism of
S_{\bullet}[c]
-modules, whereas
\beta_{\bullet}
becomes a morphism of
T_{\bullet}
-modules. Apply the global sections functor
\Gamma
to this diagram. Using Lemma
7.1.2
(b) we deduce as in part (a) that
\Gamma(\beta_{\bullet}):\Gamma(L_{\bullet})\to\Gamma(M_{\bullet})
is an isomorphism of
\Gamma(T_{\bullet})
-modules.
∎

7.2.
An alternative presentation for the algebra
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,J)

We now return to the setting of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CoadDrin}
so that
\mathbb{D}\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the closed unit disc, with local coordinate
x
. Recall the
G
-topology
\mathbb{D}_{m}
on
\mathbb{D}
from Definition
4.3.9
.

Definition 7.2.1
.

For each affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{D}
, let
m_{X}
be the least integer
m
such that
X\in\mathbb{D}_{m}
.

Lemma 7.2.2
.

Let
X
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
and let
m\geqslant 0
. The derivation
\pi_{F}^{m}\partial_{x}
of
\mathcal{O}(X)
preserves
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
if and only if
m\geqslant m_{X}
.

Proof.

By Definitions
4.3.9
(a) and
2.3.9
(a),
m\geqslant m_{X}
if and only if
r(X)\leqslant\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{m}
. By Corollary
2.3.19
, this is equivalent to
||\partial_{x}||_{\mathcal{O}(X)}\leqslant 1/|{\pi_{F}}|^{m}
. This, in turn, is equivalent to
\pi_{F}^{m}\partial_{x}
preserving
\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
inside
\mathcal{O}(X)
.
∎

Notation 7.2.3
.

We will write
\mathcal{A}:=\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{A}\partial_{x}
until the end of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CompareSandT}
.

We refer the reader to
[
7
, Definition 6.1]
for the definition of Lie lattices.

Corollary 7.2.4
.

Let
X
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
. For every
m\geqslant m_{X}
,

(1)

\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}
is an
\mathcal{A}
-Lie lattice in
\mathcal{T}(X)
, and

(2)

there is a natural isometric isomorphism of
K
-Banach algebras

j:\widehat{U(\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L})_{K}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X).

Proof.

(a) Because
m\geqslant m_{X}
, Lemma
7.2.2
implies that
\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}
is a sub
(K^{\circ},\mathcal{A})
-Lie algebra of
\mathcal{T}(X)
. Now apply
[
7
, Definition 6.1]
.

(b) There is a unique
\mathcal{A}
-linear map
j_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}:\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)=:D
that sends
\pi_{F}^{m}\partial_{x}
to
\pi_{F}^{m}\partial
. Let
j_{\mathcal{A}}
denote the inclusion of
\mathcal{A}
into
D
. It is easy to verify that
j_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}
respects Lie brackets and satisfies
[j_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}(v),j_{\mathcal{A}}(a)]=j_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}(v(a))
for all
v\in\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
. The universal property
[
7
, §2.1]
of the enveloping algebra
U(\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L})
implies that
j_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}
extends to an
K^{\circ}
-algebra homomorphism
j:U(\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L})\to D
. Because the image of this map by construction lands in the unit ball of the
K
-Banach algebra
D
, it extends further to a
K
-Banach algebra homomorphism
j:\widehat{U(\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L})_{K}}\to D
. Finally, Rinehart’s Theorem
[
39
, Theorem 3.1]
implies that
\{\pi_{F}^{mn}\partial_{x}^{n}:n\geqslant 0\}
is an orthonormal basis for the
\mathcal{O}(X)
-Banach module
\widehat{U(\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L})_{K}}
in the sense of
[
22
, §1.2, p.7]
, whereas it follows from the construction of
D
that
\{\pi_{F}^{mn}\partial^{n}:n\geqslant 0\}
is an orthonormal basis for
D
. Since
j
sends
\partial_{x}^{n}
to
\partial^{n}
for each
n\geqslant 0
, we conclude that it is an isometric isomorphism.
∎

Recall from Notation
5.3.1
the
Iwahori subgroup

I
of
GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
, given by

(64)

I:=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F}):c\equiv 0\mod\pi_{F}\mathcal{O}_{F}\right\}.

We fix a closed subgroup
J
of
I
and let
X
be a
J
-stable affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
. We now recall the construction of the algebra
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,J)
from
[
9
, Definition 3.3.1]
.

Recall from
[
9
, Definition 3.2.13]
that
(\mathcal{J},H)
is an
\mathcal{A}
-trivialising pair
if
\mathcal{J}
is a
J
-stable
\mathcal{A}
-Lie lattice in
\Der_{K}(\mathcal{O}(X))
and
H
is an open normal subgroup of
J
such that
\rho_{X}(H)\leqslant\exp(p^{\epsilon}\mathcal{J})
inside
\Aut_{K}(\mathcal{O}(X))
, where
\rho_{X}:J\to\Aut_{K}(\mathcal{O}(X))
is the action of
J
on
\mathcal{O}(X)
. For each
\mathcal{A}
-trivialising pair
(\mathcal{J},H)
, there is a crossed product
\widehat{U(\mathcal{J})_{K}}\rtimes_{H}J
, and
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,J)
is the inverse limit of these crossed products taken over all possible
\mathcal{A}
-trivialising pairs
(\mathcal{J},H)
.

Lemma 7.2.5
.

Let
X
be a
J
-stable affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
. Then
(\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L},J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)})
is an
\mathcal{A}
-trivialising pair whenever
m\geqslant m_{X}
.

Proof.

The affine formal model
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{O}(X)^{\circ}
in
\mathcal{O}(X)
is
J
-stable since
X
is
J
-stable, and it is
\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}
-stable by Lemma
7.2.2
. Hence by
[
9
, Corollary 4.3.7]
, it will be enough to verify that
(\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})^{\circ}\partial_{x},J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)})
is an
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})^{\circ}
-trivialising pair. Therefore we may assume that
X=\mathbb{D}
.

Recall congruence subgroup
J_{n}
of
J
from Definition
5.3.1
and note that
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}(J_{n})
acts trivially on
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})^{\circ}
modulo
\pi_{F}^{n}
. If
\mathcal{E}
denotes the ring of
K^{\circ}
-linear endomorphisms of
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})^{\circ}
, then
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}(J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)})\leqslant 1+p^{\epsilon}\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{E}
. Since every element of
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}(J)
is an
K^{\circ}
-algebra automorphism of
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})^{\circ}
, it follows from the proof of
[
9
, Lemma 3.2.5(a)]
that
\log\rho_{\mathbb{D}}(J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)})\leqslant p^{\epsilon}\pi_{F}^{m}\Der_{K^{\circ}}\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})^{\circ}=p^{\epsilon}\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})^{\circ}\partial_{x}
. ∎

After Lemma
7.2.5
,
[
9
, Theorem 3.2.12]
and Corollary
7.2.4
(b), we have at our disposal the crossed product

\hskip 2.84544pt\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)\underset{J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}}{\rtimes}{}J

for every
J
-stable affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{D}
and every
m\geqslant m_{X}
. Note that the trivialisation of the
J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}
-action on
\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)
is
j\circ\beta_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}
, where
\beta_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}
is the trivialisation of the
J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}
-action on
\widehat{U(\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L})_{K}}
obtained from
[
9
, Theorem 3.2.12]
and Lemma
7.2.5
, and
j
comes from Corollary
7.2.4
(b). On the other hand, when
m\geqslant m_{X}
, by Proposition
5.3.4
we also have the crossed product

\mathscr{D}_{m}(X)\underset{J_{m+1}}{\rtimes}{}J\hskip 2.84544pt=\hskip 2.84544pt\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{m}}(X)\underset{J_{m+1}}{\rtimes}{}J.

Definition 7.2.6
.

Let
X
be a
J
-stable affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
and let
m\geqslant m_{X}
. Define

(1)

\mathcal{T}_{m}(X)\hskip 2.84544pt:=\hskip 2.84544pt\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)\underset{J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}}{\rtimes}{}J
, and

(2)

\mathcal{S}_{m}(X):=\mathscr{D}_{m}(X)\underset{J_{m+1}}{\rtimes}{}J\hskip 2.84544pt=\hskip 2.84544pt\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{m}}(X)\underset{J_{m+1}}{\rtimes}{}J
.

Note that the algebra
\mathcal{T}_{m}(X)
is functorial in
X
and therefore defines a presheaf
\mathcal{T}_{m}
on the
G
-topology
\mathbb{D}_{m}/J
— see Definition
5.3.1
(d).

Proposition 7.2.7
.

For every
J
-stable affinoid subdomain
X
of
\mathbb{D}
, the tower

\mathcal{T}_{m_{X}}(X)\leftarrow\mathcal{T}_{m_{X}+1}(X)\leftarrow\mathcal{T}_{m_{X}+2}(X)\leftarrow\cdots

gives a Fréchet-Stein presentation for the algebra
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,J)
.

Proof.

Use Lemma
7.2.5
,
[
9
, Lemma 3.3.4]
and the proof of
[
9
, Theorem 3.4.8]
.
∎

We will now make a precise comparison of the two constructions.

Theorem 7.2.8
.

For every
m\geqslant 0
, there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{m}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}\rtimes J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{m}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{m}.}

of presheaves of
K
-algebras on
\mathbb{D}_{m}/J
.

Proof.

Fix
X\in\mathbb{D}_{m}/J
. We will first construct the
K
-algebra map

f_{m}(X):\mathcal{T}_{m}(X)\to\mathcal{S}_{m}(X).

Let
r
be a real number satisfying
\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{m}<r<|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}\min\{\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|,1\}
. Then in particular
r<|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}
, so by Lemma
2.2.10
there is a
K
-Banach algebra homomorphism
f_{m,r}:\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
which fixes
\mathcal{O}(X)
and sends
\partial\in\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)
to
\partial\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
. This map is
J
-equivariant for the
J
-actions on
\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)
and
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
that were constructed in Proposition
5.2.4
. Because also
\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{m}<r<\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{m+1}
, we can apply Lemma
5.3.3
together with Theorem
5.2.11
to construct the crossed product
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\underset{J_{m+1}}{\rtimes}J
as in the proof of Proposition
5.3.4
. Then by
[
9
, Lemma 2.2.7]
,
f_{m,r}
extends to a
K
-algebra homomorphism

f_{m,r}\rtimes 1_{J}:\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)\underset{J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}}{\rtimes}{}J\quad\longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\underset{J_{m+1}}{\rtimes}J

provided we can show that it respects the two trivialisations

j\circ\beta_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}:J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)^{\times}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\beta:J_{m+1}\to\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)^{\times}.

Recall from
[
9
, Lemma 3.2.10(a)]
the action map
\psi_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}:\widehat{U(\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L})_{K}}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))
so that
\psi_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}\circ\beta_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}
is the restriction of
\rho_{X}
to
J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}
by
[
9
, Definition 3.2.11]
. Recall the action map
\sigma_{r}:\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))
from Lemma
2.3.12
, and note that
\psi_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}
is just the restriction of
\sigma_{r}
to
\widehat{U(\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L})_{K}}
; more precisely, we have

(65)

\sigma_{r}\circ f_{m,r}\circ j=\psi_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}

where
j
is the isomorphism from Corollary
7.2.4
(b). Then

\sigma_{r}\circ f\circ j\circ\beta_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}=\psi_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}\circ\beta_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}=\rho_{X|J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}}=\sigma_{r}\circ\beta_{|J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}}

by Proposition
5.2.10
, and therefore
\beta_{|J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}}=f\circ j\circ\beta_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}
because
\sigma_{r}
is injective by Lemma
2.3.12
(b). This is precisely what is required to apply
[
9
, Lemma 2.2.7]
, and completes the construction of
f_{m,r}\rtimes 1_{J}
. It is clear that if
\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{m}<r^{\prime}\leqslant r
then
f_{m,r^{\prime}}\rtimes 1
is compatible with
f_{m,r}\rtimes 1
in the sense that
f_{m,r^{\prime}}\rtimes 1_{J}
is the composition of
f_{m,r}\rtimes 1_{J}
with
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\rtimes_{J_{m+1}}J\to\mathcal{D}_{r^{\prime}}(X)\rtimes_{J_{m+1}}J
. We can now define

f_{m}(X):\mathcal{T}_{m}(X)\to\mathcal{S}_{m}(X)

to be the colimit of the maps
f_{m,r}\rtimes 1_{J}
as
r
approaches
\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{m}
from above.

We will next similarly construct the
K
-algebra homomorphism

g_{m}(X):\mathcal{S}_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}(X)\to\mathcal{T}_{m}(X).

Let
r
be a real number such that
\varpi<r|{\pi_{F}}|^{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}<\varpi/{\pi_{F}}
, so that the crossed product
\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\rtimes_{J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)+1}}J
is defined as in the proof of Proposition
5.3.4
. Note that then
r|{\pi_{F}}|^{m}>\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}=|p|^{\frac{1}{p-1}-\epsilon}\geqslant 1
, so
r\geqslant|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}
. Hence by Lemma
2.2.10
, there is a natural
K
-Banach algebra homomorphism
g_{m,r}:\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\to\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)
which fixes
\mathcal{O}(X)
and sends
\partial\in\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)
to
\partial\in\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)
. Now, the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{m,r}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{r}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{O}(X))}
\textstyle{\widehat{U(\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L})_{K}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}}

is commutative, so that
\sigma_{r}=\psi_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}\circ j^{-1}\circ g_{m,r}
. Therefore

\psi_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}\circ j^{-1}\circ g_{m,r}\circ\beta=\sigma_{r}\circ\beta=\rho_{X}=\psi_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}\circ\beta_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}|J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)+1}}

again by Proposition
5.2.10
and
[
9
, Definition 3.2.11]
. The factorisation (
65
) together with Lemma
2.3.12
(b) shows that
\psi_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}}
is injective, and we deduce that

g_{m,r}\circ\beta=j\circ\beta_{\pi_{F}^{m}\mathcal{L}|J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)+1}}.

Because the map
g_{m,r}
is
J
-equivariant, this equation allows us to apply
[
9
, Lemma 2.2.7]
and deduce that
g_{m,r}
extends to a
K
-algebra homomorphism

g_{m,r}\rtimes 1_{J}:\mathcal{D}_{r}(X)\underset{J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)+1}}{\rtimes}{}J\quad\longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-m}}(X)\underset{J_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}}{\rtimes}{}J.

Passing to the limit as
r
approaches
\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}
from above, we obtain the required
K
-algebra homomorphism

g_{m}(X):\mathcal{S}_{m+\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)}(X)\to\mathcal{T}_{m}(X).

Finally, we leave to the reader the straightforward verification that the diagram in the statement of the Theorem is commutative, and that the morphisms
f_{m}(X)
and
g_{m}(X)
are functorial in
X
.
∎

Corollary 7.2.9
.

For each
n\geqslant 0
there is an isomorphism

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(-,J)\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad\lim\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{m\geqslant n}}}\mathscr{D}_{m}\underset{J_{m+1}}{\rtimes}{}J

of presheaves of
K
-algebras on
\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
.

Proof.

Use Proposition
7.2.7
, Theorem
7.2.8
and Proposition
7.1.3
(a).
∎

For future use, we establish here the following flatness result.

Theorem 7.2.10
.

\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)
is a flat right
\mathcal{T}_{n}(X)
-module whenever
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
.

Proof.

Write
c:=\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)
. Form the crossed product
C:=\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{n}}(X)\underset{J_{n+c}}{\rtimes}{}J
, and consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{n}(X)=\mathcal{D}_{|{\pi_{F}}|^{-n}}(X)\underset{J_{n+c}}{\rtimes}{}J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)=\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{n}}(X)\underset{J_{n+1}}{\rtimes}{}J}
\textstyle{C=\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{n}}(X)\underset{J_{n+c}}{\rtimes}{J}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D:=\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{n}}(X).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Note that
C
is a flat right
D
-module being a crossed product of
D
with the group
J/J_{n+c}
, which allows us to deduce from the right-module version of
[
40
, Lemma 2.2]
that
\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)
is a flat right
C
-module. On the other hand, we deduce from Theorem
3.4.1
and Lemma
3.2.2
that
C
is a flat right
\mathcal{T}_{n}(X)
-module. Hence
\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)
is a flat right
\mathcal{T}_{n}(X)
-module as claimed. ∎

7.3.
Coadmissibility of
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}
on the local Drinfeld space

We begin with the following elementary result.

Lemma 7.3.1
.

Let
D\hookrightarrow S
be an extension of rings such that
S
is finitely generated and projective as a left
D
-module. Then every
S
-module
M
which is finitely presented as a
D
-module is also finitely presented as an
S
-module.

Proof.

We can find a finite generating set
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
for
M
as a
D
-module such that if
\theta:D^{n}\twoheadrightarrow M
denotes the corresponding surjection then
\ker\theta
is also finitely generated. Clearly
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\}
also generates
M
as an
S
-module; let
\psi:S^{n}\twoheadrightarrow M
be the corresponding surjection. Since
S
is a projective
D
-module, Schanuel’s Lemma implies that
S^{n}\oplus\ker\theta\cong D^{n}\oplus\ker\psi
as
D
-modules. Since
S
is finitely generated as a
D
-module,
\ker\psi
is finitely generated as a
D
-module being a homomorphic image of
S^{n}\oplus\ker\theta
. Hence
\ker\psi
is also finitely generated as an
S
-module.
∎

We now return to the setting of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{CompSect}
. We assume
[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)_{\tors}
satisfies Definition
5.3.2
, and fix a closed subgroup
J
of the Iwahori group
I
.

Corollary 7.3.2
.

Let
n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
and let
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
. Then
\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)
is a finitely presented
\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)
-module.

Proof.

We know that
\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)
is a finitely presented
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)
-module, by Theorem
4.3.7
and Corollary
4.3.12
. The
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)
-action on
\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)
extends to an
\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)
-action by Theorem
5.3.6
. By construction,
\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)
is a crossed product of
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)
with the finite group
J/J_{n+1}
and is therefore finitely generated and free as a left
\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)
-module. Now apply Lemma
7.3.1
.
∎

The following consequence will be useful later on.

Proposition 7.3.3
.

Let
n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
and let
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
. Then the map

\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathcal{S}_{n}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)

induced by the
\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)
-action on
\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We abbreviate
D:=\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D}),D^{\prime}:=\mathscr{D}_{n}(X),S:=\mathcal{S}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
and
S^{\prime}:=\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)
; these rings form a commutative square

\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}.}

There is a natural transformation
\eta:D^{\prime}\otimes_{D}-\to S^{\prime}\otimes_{S}-
between two functors from
S
-modules to
D^{\prime}
-modules; since
S
(respectively,
S^{\prime}
) is a crossed product of
D
(respectively,
D^{\prime}
) with the same finite group
J/J_{n+1}
, we see that the map
\eta_{S}
is an isomorphism. Since both functors are right exact, we conclude using the Five Lemma that
\eta_{M}
is an isomorphism for every finitely presented
S
-module
M
.

Now let
M:=\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
and
M^{\prime}:=\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)
and consider the commutative triangle

\textstyle{D^{\prime}\underset{D}{\otimes}{}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{M}}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\underset{S}{\otimes}{}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{\prime}.}

Since
M
is a finitely presented
S
-module by Corollary
7.3.2
, the horizontal arrow
\eta_{M}
is an isomorphism by the above. Because the diagonal arrow on the left is an isomorphism by Lemma
5.3.13
(a), we conclude that the diagonal arrow on the right is also an isomorphism. ∎

Recall from Definition
5.3.11
that
N=\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathcal{O}_{F}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
.

Proposition 7.3.4
.

Let
X
be a
J
-stable affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
. Suppose that
d\mid(q+1)
and
N_{m_{0}+1}\leqslant J
for some
m_{0}\geqslant\max\{m_{X},v_{\pi_{F}}(e)\}
. Then the canonical action map

\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathcal{S}_{n+1}(X)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(X)\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)

is an isomorphism for all
n\geqslant m_{0}
.

Proof.

This map appears as the top horizontal arrow in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathcal{S}_{n+1}(X)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathcal{S}_{n+1}(X)}{\otimes}{}\left(\mathcal{S}_{n+1}(X)\underset{\mathcal{S}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathcal{S}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}\mathscr{L}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D}).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Now
X\in\mathbb{D}_{n+1}/J
because
n+1>n\geqslant m_{0}\geqslant m_{X}
, so the vertical arrow on the left is an isomorphism by Proposition
7.3.3
. Also, because
n\geqslant m_{0}
and because
N_{m_{0}+1}\leqslant J
by assumption, we have

N_{n+1}=N_{m_{0}+1}\cap I_{n+1}\leqslant J\cap I_{n+1}=J_{n+1}.

Therefore the vertical arrow on the right is an isomorphism by Corollary
5.3.17
. The bottom horizontal arrow is an isomorphism by the associativity of tensor products and the result follows.
∎

We can now invoke the formalism of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{QcohTower}
and prove our first main result.

Theorem 7.3.5
.

Let
X
be a
J
-stable affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
. Suppose that
d\mid(q+1)
and
N_{m_{0}+1}\leqslant J
for some
m_{0}\geqslant\max\{m_{X},v_{\pi_{F}}(e)\}
. Then

(1)

(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(X)
is a coadmissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,J)
-module, and

(2)

for each
m\geqslant m_{0}
, the canonical map

\mathcal{S}_{m}(X)\underset{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,J)}{\otimes}{}(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(X)\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{m}(X)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

(a) For each
m\geqslant 0
, let
S_{m}:=\mathcal{S}_{m_{0}+m}(X)
,
T_{m}:=\mathcal{T}_{m_{0}+m}(X)
and
M_{m}:=\mathscr{L}_{m_{0}+m}(X)
. Then
S_{\bullet}
is a tower of rings, and
M_{\bullet}
is a quasi-coherent
S_{\bullet}
-module by Proposition
7.3.4
. Theorem
7.2.8
gives us a diagram of towers of rings

\textstyle{T_{\bullet}[c]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\bullet}[c]}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{c}_{T}}
\textstyle{T_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{S_{\bullet}[c]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{c}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{g_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{S_{\bullet}}

where
c:=\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)
. Note that
\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}T_{m}\cong\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,J)
by Proposition
7.2.7
. Now
L_{\bullet}:=T_{\bullet}\otimes_{S_{\bullet}[c]}M_{\bullet}[c]
is a quasi-coherent
T_{\bullet}
-module by Lemma
7.1.1
, and each

L_{m}=T_{m}\otimes_{S_{m+c}}M_{m+c}

is a finitely presented
T_{m}
-module because
M_{m+c}
is a finitely presented
S_{m+c}
-module by Corollary
7.3.2
. In other words,
\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}L_{m}
is a coadmissible
\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}T_{m}\cong\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,J)
-module. Now, Proposition
7.1.3
(b) implies that there is a natural isomorphism

(66)

\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}M_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}L_{m}.

Since the restriction map
(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(X)\to\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}M_{m}
is an isomorphism by Proposition
4.3.3
, we conclude that
(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(X)
is a coadmissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,J)
-module as claimed.

(b) Write
T_{\infty}:=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,J)
and
M_{\infty}:=\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}M_{m}
. By
[
44
, Corollary 3.1]
, the canonical map
\alpha_{m}:T_{m}\otimes_{T_{\infty}}M_{\infty}\to L_{m}
is an isomorphism for each
m\geqslant 0
. This map, together with the map
\psi_{m}:S_{m}\otimes_{T_{\infty}}M_{\infty}\to M_{m}
in question, appears in the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{S_{m}\underset{T_{\infty}}{\otimes}{}M_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{M_{m}}
\textstyle{S_{m}\underset{T_{m}}{\otimes}{}(T_{m}\underset{T_{\infty}}{\otimes}{}M_{\infty})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\otimes\alpha_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{S_{m}\underset{T_{m}}{\otimes}{}(T_{m}\underset{S_{m+c}}{\otimes}{}M_{m+c})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{S_{m}\underset{S_{m+c}}{\otimes}{}M_{m+c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the arrow on the right is induced by the action of
S_{m}
on
M_{m}
. We see that this arrow is an isomorphism by a repeated application of Proposition
7.3.4
, and conclude that
\psi_{m}
is also an isomorphism as required. ∎

Lemma 7.3.6
.

j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}
is a locally Fréchet
I
-equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-module on
\mathbb{D}
.

Proof.

The local Drinfeld space
\Upsilon=\mathbb{D}\cap\Omega
is an admissible
I
-stable open subspace of
\mathbb{D}
. Since
\mathscr{L}
is an
I
-equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-module on
\Upsilon
, its pushforward
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}
is an
I
-equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-module on
\mathbb{D}
. According
[
9
, Definition 3.6.1(a)]
we must show that
(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(U)
carries a Fréchet topology for each affinoid subdomain
U
of
\mathbb{D}
, and that the action maps
g^{j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}}(U):(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(U)\to(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(gU)
are continuous for each
g\in I
. We observed in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{UpperHP}
that
\Upsilon
admits a quasi-Stein covering
(\Upsilon_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
. Therefore
U\cap\Omega
admits a quasi-Stein covering
(U\cap V_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
and there is a natural isomorphism

\mathscr{L}(U\cap\Omega)=\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}\mathscr{L}(U\cap V_{n}).

Since
\mathscr{L}
is a coherent
\mathcal{O}
-module on
\Upsilon
and since
U\cap V_{n}
is affinoid, each
\mathscr{L}(U\cap V_{n})
is naturally a
K
-Banach space. In this way, we see that
(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(U)=\mathscr{L}(U\cap\Omega)
carries a natural
K
-Fréchet space topology.

The continuity of the maps
g^{j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}}(U):(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(U)\to(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(gU)
now follows from the continuity of the maps
g^{\mathscr{L}}(U\cap V_{n}):\mathscr{L}(U\cap V_{n})\to\mathscr{L}(g(U\cap V_{n}))
, which holds by viewing
\mathscr{L}(g(U\cap V_{n}))
as an
\mathcal{O}(U\cap V_{n})
-modulle via
g^{\mathcal{O}}(U\cap V_{n})
, automatically by
[
22
, Corollary 1.2.4]
.
∎

We can finally prove our first main local result.

Theorem 7.3.7
.

Let
[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)_{\tors}
. Suppose that the image
\omega[\mathscr{L}]
of
[\mathscr{L}]
in
\PicCon(\Upsilon)
is killed by
q+1
. Let
j\colon\Upsilon\hookrightarrow\mathbb{D}
be the open embedding, Then

j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{D}/J}

for any closed subgroup
J
of
I
which contains an open subgroup of
N=\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathcal{O}_{F}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that the isomorphism in Lemma
4.3.3
is
J
-equivariant, and so by that result and Theorem
7.3.5
(a) we know that

(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(\mathbb{D})=\mathscr{L}(\Upsilon)\cong\lim\limits_{\longleftarrow}\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})

is a coadmissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{D},J)
-module. Because
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}
is a locally Fréchet
I
-equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-module on
\mathbb{D}
by Lemma
7.3.6
, and because
J\leqslant I
, by
[
9
, Definition 3.6.7]
it remains to exhibit a continuous
\mathcal{D}
-linear
J
-equivariant isomorphism

\Loc^{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{D},J)}_{\mathbb{D}}\left((j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(\mathbb{D})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}.

Recall that by
[
9
, Definition 3.5.12]
,
\Loc^{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{D},J)}_{\mathbb{D}}((j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(\mathbb{D}))
is the unique extension of a sheaf
\mathcal{P}^{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{D},J)}_{\mathbb{D}}((j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(\mathbb{D}))
on the basis
\mathbb{D}_{w}
of
\mathbb{D}
, defined at
[
9
, Definition 3.5.3]
. Because
\Loc^{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{D},J)}_{\mathbb{D}}((j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(\mathbb{D}))
and
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}
are sheaves, by
[
7
, Theorem 9.1]
it will suffice to exhibit an isomorphism of
J
-equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-modules on
\mathbb{D}_{w}

\varphi:\mathcal{P}^{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{D},J)}_{\mathbb{D}}((j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(\mathbb{D}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})_{|\mathbb{D}_{w}}.

Let
X
be an affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
and let
H
be an
X
-small open subgroup
2
2

2

Since
I
preserves the free
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})^{\circ}
-Lie lattice
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{D})^{\circ}\partial_{x}
in
\mathcal{T}(\mathbb{D})
, we see that in fact, in this situation
every
open subgroup
H
of
J_{X}
is
X
-small.
of
J_{X}=\Stab_{J}(X)
, in the sense of
[
9
, Definition 3.4.4]
. Because
J
contains an open subgroup of
N
by assumption, so does its open subgroup
H
. Hence we can find an integer
m_{0}\geqslant\max\{m_{X},v_{\pi_{F}}(e)\}
such that
N_{m_{0}+1}\leqslant H
. For each
n\geqslant m_{0}
, let
\mathcal{S}_{n}(X):=\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\rtimes_{H_{n+1}}H
and note that the natural action map

\mathcal{S}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathcal{S}_{n}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{n}(X)

is an isomorphism by Proposition
7.3.3
applied with
J
replaced by
H
. Note that

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,H)\cong\lim\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{n\geqslant m_{0}}}}\mathcal{T}_{n}(X)\quad\mbox{and}\quad(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(\mathbb{D})\cong\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}\mathcal{N}_{n}(\mathbb{D})

by Corollary
7.2.9
and equation (
66
), where we replace
J
with
H
so that

\mathcal{T}_{n}(X):=\mathscr{D}_{n}(X)\underset{H_{n+1}}{\rtimes}{}H\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{N}_{n}(\mathbb{D}):=\mathcal{T}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\underset{\mathcal{S}_{n+c}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+c}(\mathbb{D})

and
c:=\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)
. Now, for each
n\geqslant m_{0}
there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathcal{T}_{n}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{N}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathcal{S}_{n+c}(X)}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+c}(X)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{n}(X)\underset{S_{n+c}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+c}(\mathbb{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathcal{T}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathcal{S}_{n+c}(X)}{\otimes}{}\left(\mathcal{S}_{n+c}(X)\underset{S_{n+c}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n+c}(\mathbb{D})\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where the maps on the left and on the bottom are isomorphisms arising from the associativity of tensor products. The vertical map on the right is an isomorphism by Proposition
7.3.3
, so the top horizontal map is an isomorphism. Taking the inverse limit of these maps and using equation (
66
) together with the definition of
\wideparen{\otimes}
from
[
7
, §7.3]
and
[
9
, Lemma 3.4.13]
, we obtain a
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,H)
-linear isomorphism

\varphi_{H}(X):\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,H)\underset{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{D},H)}{\wideparen{\otimes}}{}(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(\mathbb{D})=\lim\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{n\geqslant m_{0}}}}\mathcal{T}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathcal{T}_{n}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{N}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(X).

Write
M(X,H):=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,H)\underset{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{D},H)}{\wideparen{\otimes}}{}(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(\mathbb{D})
, let
X^{\prime}
be an
H
-stable affinoid subdomain of
X
, let
H^{\prime}
be another open subgroup of
J
containing
H
and let
g\in J
. We leave to the reader the verification that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{M(X^{\prime},H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{H}(X^{\prime})}
\textstyle{(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(X^{\prime})}
\textstyle{M(X,H^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{H^{\prime}}(X)}
\textstyle{M(X,H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{H}(X)}
\scriptstyle{g^{M}_{X,H}}
\textstyle{(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M(gX,{}^{g}H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{{}^{g}H}(gX)}
\textstyle{(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(gX)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}}(X)}

It follows that the isomorphisms
\varphi_{H}(X)
are compatible as
H
shrinks to
1
, and commute with the restriction maps on both sides. Passing to the limit over all
X
-small
H
we obtain the required
J
-equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-linear isomorphism

\varphi:\mathcal{P}^{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{D},J)}_{\mathbb{D}}((j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})(\mathbb{D}))=\lim\limits_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longleftarrow}}{{H}}}M(-,H)\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}.\qed

7.4.
Coadmissibility and irreducibility

Let
j:\Omega\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
j_{0}:\Upsilon\hookrightarrow\mathbb{D}
be the open inclusions and let
G_{0}:=\mathbb{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
.

Theorem 7.4.1
.

Let
[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon^{G_{0}}(\Omega)_{\tors}
. Then

j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}/H}

for every closed subgroup
H
of
G_{0}
which contains an open subgroup of
\mathbb{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
.

Proof.

Using the argument given in the proof of Lemma
7.3.6
, we see that
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}
is a
G_{0}
-equivariant locally Fréchet
\mathcal{D}
-module on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Let
w:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in G_{0}
so that
\{\mathbb{D},w\mathbb{D}\}
forms an admissible affinoid covering of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
by two copies of the closed unit disc
\mathbb{D}
. Note that the Iwahori subgroup
I
from
(\ref{IwahoriEq})
stabilises
\mathbb{D}
, so the opposite Iwahori subgroup
{}^{w}I:=wIw^{-1}
stabilises
w\mathbb{D}
; we will show that
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}
is
\{\mathbb{D},w\mathbb{D}\}
-coadmissible in the sense of
[
9
, Definition 3.6.7(a)]
.

Next, consider the restriction
\mathscr{L}_{|\Upsilon}
so that
[\mathscr{L}_{|\Upsilon}]\in\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)_{\tors}
. Because
\omega[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon(\Omega)^{G_{0}}
is killed by
q+1
by
[
8
, Corollary 4.3.9]
, we see that
\omega[\mathscr{L}_{0}]\in\PicCon(\Upsilon)^{I}
is also killed by
q+1
. Since
H
contains an open subgroup of
\mathbb{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
,
J:=H\cap I
contains an open subgroup of
N
. Therefore
(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})_{|\mathbb{D}}=j_{0,\ast}(\mathscr{L}_{|\Upsilon})\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{D}/J}
by Theorem
7.3.7
. Entirely similarly we deduce that
(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})_{|w\mathbb{D}}\in\mathcal{C}_{w\mathbb{D}/(H\cap{}^{w}I)}
. Hence
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}/H}
by
[
9
, Definition 3.6.7]
.
∎

We will now work towards the irreducibility of
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}
viewed as an object in the abelian category
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}/G_{0}}
. We start in the following abstract setting. Let
X
be a set equipped with a
G
-topology, let
\mathcal{S}
be a sheaf of rings on
X
, let
\mathcal{U}
be a covering of
X
and let
\mathcal{M}
be a sheaf of
\mathcal{S}
-modules on
X
. We say
\mathcal{M}
is
\mathcal{U}
-quasi-coherent
if the canonical map

\mathcal{S}(V)\underset{\mathcal{S}(U)}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{M}(U)\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}(V)

is an isomorphism whenever
U\in\mathcal{U}
and
V
is an admissible open subset of
U
. For
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
, write
U\sim_{\mathcal{M}}V
if
\mathcal{M}(U\cap V)\neq 0
and let
\cong_{\mathcal{M}}
be the equivalence relation on
\mathcal{U}
generated by
\sim_{\mathcal{M}}
. We say that
\mathcal{U}
is
\mathcal{M}
-connected
if there is only one
\cong_{\mathcal{M}}
equivalence class in
\mathcal{U}
.

Proposition 7.4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an
\mathcal{S}
-module on
X
and let
\mathcal{U}
be an admissible covering of
X
. Suppose that

(1)

\mathcal{M}
is
\mathcal{U}
-quasi-coherent,

(2)

\mathcal{U}
is
\mathcal{M}
-connected, and

(3)

\mathcal{M}(U)
is a simple
\mathcal{S}(U)
-module for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
.

Then
\mathcal{M}
contains no non-zero proper
\mathcal{U}
-quasi-coherent submodules.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a
\mathcal{U}
-quasi-coherent
\mathcal{S}
-submodule of
\mathcal{M}
. We first show that for any
U,V\in\mathcal{U}
with
U\sim_{\mathcal{M}}V
,
\mathcal{N}(U)=\mathcal{M}(U)
if and only
\mathcal{N}(V)=\mathcal{M}(V)
. If not, then because
\mathcal{M}(U)
and
\mathcal{M}(V)
are simple, we may assume that without loss of generality that
\mathcal{N}(U)=0
and
\mathcal{N}(V)=\mathcal{M}(V)
. But then because both
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{M}
are
\mathcal{U}
-quasi-coherent,

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\mathcal{M}(U\cap V)&=&\mathcal{S}(U\cap V)\underset{\mathcal{S}(V)}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{M}(V)&=&\mathcal{S}(U\cap V)\underset{\mathcal{S}(V)}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{N}(V)\\
&=&\mathcal{N}(U\cap V)&=&\mathcal{S}(U\cap V)\underset{\mathcal{S}(U)}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{N}(U)=0\end{array}

which contradicts the hypothesis that
U\sim_{\mathcal{M}}V
.

Suppose now that the
\mathcal{S}
-submodule
\mathcal{N}
is non-zero. Then we can find at least one
U\in\mathcal{U}
such that
\mathcal{N}(U)\neq 0
, and hence
\mathcal{N}(U)=\mathcal{M}(U)
because
\mathcal{M}(U)
is simple. Since
\mathcal{U}
is
\mathcal{M}
-connected, we conclude that in fact
\mathcal{N}(U)=\mathcal{M}(U)
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Since
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{M}
are both
\mathcal{U}
-quasi-coherent, we see that

\mathcal{N}(W)=\mathcal{S}(W)\underset{\mathcal{S}(U)}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{N}(U)=\mathcal{S}(W)\underset{\mathcal{S}(U)}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{M}(U)=\mathcal{M}(W)

for any
U\in\mathcal{U}
and any admissible open
W\subseteq U
; thus
\mathcal{N}_{|U}=\mathcal{M}_{|U}
for all
U\in\mathcal{U}
. Since
\mathcal{U}
is an admissible covering of
X
and since
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{M}
are sheaves, we conclude that
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{M}
.∎

Here is how we apply this general result. Recall the sheaf of rings
\mathscr{D}_{n}=\mathcal{D}^{\dagger}_{\varpi/|{\pi_{F}}|^{n}}
from Definition
4.3.9
on the
G
-topology
\mathbb{D}_{n}
on
\mathbb{D}
, and recall that the truncated line bundle
\mathscr{L}_{n}:=j_{0,\ast}(\mathscr{L}_{\overline{\Upsilon_{n}}})
is a
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-module by Corollary
4.3.11
.

Theorem 7.4.3
.

Let
[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)_{\tors}
. Suppose that the image
\omega[\mathscr{L}]
of
[\mathscr{L}]
in
\PicCon(\Upsilon)_{\tors}
is non-zero and of order
d
dividing
q+1
. Let
j_{0}:\Upsilon\hookrightarrow\mathbb{D}
be the inclusion, let
n\geqslant 0
and let
\mathscr{L}_{n}:=j_{0,\ast}(\mathscr{L}_{\overline{\Upsilon_{n}}})
. Then
\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
is a simple
\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
-module.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{n}
be the covering
\{W_{1,n},\ldots,W_{h_{n},n},\Upsilon_{n}\}
from Definition
5.3.9
. We can use Lemma
5.3.13
(a) to see that
\mathscr{L}_{n}
is a
\mathcal{U}_{n}
-quasi-coherent
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-module on
\mathbb{D}_{n}
. Then by construction,
\mathscr{L}_{n}(W_{i,n}\cap\Upsilon_{n})
is non-zero for any
i=1,\ldots,h_{n}
, so the covering
\mathcal{U}_{n}
is
\mathscr{L}_{n}
-connected.

Using Lemma
4.3.5
(d), we see that
\omega[\mathscr{L}|_{\Upsilon_{n}}]\in\Con(\Upsilon_{n})^{I}[d]
is non-zero. Hence
k\nu_{n}=M_{n,d}(\omega[\mathscr{L}])=\mu_{\Upsilon_{n},d}(\theta_{d}(\omega[\mathscr{L}|_{\Upsilon_{n}}]))
is non-zero as well by
[
8
, Corollary 4.3.4]
. Since
k\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
by Definition
6.5.1
, Lemma
4.3.5
(a) now implies that
k\neq d
. Hence
\mathscr{L}_{n}(W_{i,n})
is a simple
\mathscr{D}_{n}(W_{i,n})
-module for each
i
by Corollary
6.5.9
.

On the other hand,
\mathscr{L}_{n}(\Upsilon_{n})
is a simple
\mathscr{D}_{n}(\Upsilon_{n})
-module by Corollary
4.2.13
, because
\Upsilon_{n}
is a connected affinoid subdomain of
\mathbb{D}
. Hence
\mathscr{L}_{n}
contains no non-zero proper
\mathcal{U}_{n}
-quasi-coherent
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-submodules by Proposition
7.4.2
.

Let
L
be a
\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
-submodule of
\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
, and consider the presheaf of
\mathscr{D}_{n}
-modules
\mathcal{L}:=\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}L
. For any
\mathbb{D}_{n}
-admissible covering
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathbb{D}
, the augmented Čech complex
C^{\bullet}_{\aug}(\mathcal{V},\mathscr{D}_{n})
is exact by Theorem
2.3.10
. Each of its terms is flat as a right
\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
-module by Lemma
4.3.10
(b). Because the covering
\mathcal{V}
is finite by Definition
2.3.9
(b), the complex is bounded above, and therefore, by induction on its length, each of its syzygies is flat as a right
\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
-module. Tensoring this complex on the right by
L
over
\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
and splicing, we conclude that the augmented Čech complex
C^{\bullet}_{\aug}(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{L})
is also exact. In particular, this means that
\mathcal{L}
is a sheaf on
\mathbb{D}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{D})=L
. Applying Lemma
4.3.10
(b) again, we see that
\mathcal{L}
is a subsheaf of
\mathscr{D}_{n}\underset{\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})}{\otimes}{}\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\cong\mathscr{L}_{n}
. Since
\mathcal{L}
is
\mathcal{U}_{n}
-quasi-coherent by construction, we conclude from the first paragraph that either
\mathcal{L}=0
or
\mathcal{L}=\mathscr{L}_{n}
. Because
L=\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{D})
, we conclude that either
L=0
or
L=\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
as required. ∎

Recall the sheaves
\mathcal{T}_{n}
and
\mathcal{S}_{n}
on
\mathbb{D}_{n}/J
from Definition
7.2.6
.

Corollary 7.4.4
.

With the notation and assumptions of Theorem
7.4.3
,
\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
is a simple
\mathcal{S}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
-module, whenever
n\geqslant v_{\pi_{F}}(e)
.

Proof.

We know that the
\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
-action on
\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
extends to the crossed product
\mathcal{S}_{n}(\mathbb{D})=\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\rtimes_{J_{n+1}}J
by Theorem
5.3.6
, and that
\mathscr{L}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
is a simple
\mathscr{D}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
-module by Theorem
7.4.3
. So it is per force simple as an
\mathcal{S}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
-module.
∎

Theorem 7.4.5
.

Let
[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)_{\tors}
. Suppose that the image
\omega[\mathscr{L}]
of
[\mathscr{L}]
in
\PicCon(\Upsilon)
is non-zero and its order divides
q+1
. Then
\mathscr{L}(\Upsilon)
is a topologically irreducible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{D},J)
-module for any closed subgroup
J
of
I
which contains an open subgroup of
N
.

Proof.

We know that
M_{\infty}:=\mathscr{L}(\Upsilon)
is a coadmissible module over the Fréchet-Stein algebra
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{D},J)
by Lemma
4.3.3
and Theorem
7.3.5
. Let
S_{n}:=\mathcal{S}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
and
T_{n}:=\mathcal{T}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
for each
n\geqslant 0
; it follows from Lemma
7.2.2
that the integer
m_{\mathbb{D}}
from Definition
7.2.1
is zero, so the tower of rings
T_{\bullet}
gives a Fréchet-Stein presentation of
T_{\infty}:=\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{D},J)
by Proposition
7.2.7
. Hence each
T_{n}
is a flat right
T_{\infty}
-module by
[
44
, Remark 3.2]
. On the other hand,
S_{n}
is a flat right
T_{n}
-module by Theorem
7.2.10
and hence it is also a flat right
T_{\infty}
-module.

Now suppose that
L
is a non-zero, closed, proper
T_{\infty}
-submodule of
M_{\infty}
. Then by
[
44
, Corollary 3.3]
,
T_{n}\otimes_{T_{\infty}}L
and
T_{n}\otimes_{T_{\infty}}(M_{\infty}/L)
are both non-zero for all sufficiently large integers
n
. Let
c:=\epsilon v_{\pi_{F}}(p)
; because the map
T_{\infty}\to T_{n}
factors through
S_{n+c}
by Theorem
7.2.8
, it follows that
S_{n+c}\otimes_{T_{\infty}}L
and
S_{n+c}\otimes_{T_{\infty}}(M_{\infty}/L)
are also both non-zero. Since
S_{n+c}
is a flat
T_{\infty}
-module by the first paragraph, we conclude that
S_{n+c}\otimes_{T_{\infty}}M_{\infty}
is not a simple
S_{n+c}
-module. However this module is isomorphic to
M_{n+c}
by Theorem
7.3.5
(b), which contradicts Corollary
7.4.4
. ∎

Corollary 7.4.6
.

Let
[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)_{\tors}
. Suppose that the image
\omega[\mathscr{L}]
of
[\mathscr{L}]
in
\PicCon(\Upsilon)
is non-zero and its order divides
q+1
. Let
J
be a closed subgroup of
I
which contains an open subgroup of
N
. Then
j_{0,\ast}\mathscr{L}
is a simple object in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{D}/J}
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
7.4.5
and
[
9
, Theorem 3.6.11]
.
∎

Theorem 7.4.7
.

Let
[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon^{G_{0}}(\Omega)_{\tors}
whose image
\omega[\mathscr{L}]
in
\PicCon(\Omega)_{\tors}
is non-zero. Then
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}
is a simple object in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}/H}
for
every
closed subgroup
H
of
G_{0}
which contains an open subgroup of
\mathbb{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
.

Proof.

Note first that
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}/H}
by Theorem
7.4.1
. Let
w:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in G_{0}
and recall the covering
\{\mathbb{D},w\mathbb{D}\}
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
from the proof of Theorem
7.4.1
.

We will apply Corollary
7.4.6
to
[\mathscr{L}|_{\Upsilon}]\in\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)_{\tors}
, but first we have to check the required conditions on
\omega[\mathscr{L}|_{\Upsilon}]
hold. Since
(q+1)\cdot\omega[\mathscr{L}]=0
by
[
8
, Corollary 4.3.9]
, the order of
\omega[\mathscr{L}|_{\Upsilon}]
in
\PicCon(\Upsilon)
divides
q+1
. Consider the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\PicCon(\Omega)^{G_{0}}[q+1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\PicCon(\Upsilon)^{I}[q+1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Con(\Upsilon_{0})^{G_{0}}[q+1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Con(\Upsilon_{0})^{I}[q+1].}

Note that
\Upsilon_{0}=\Omega_{0}
in view of Definition
4.3.2
(b) and
[
8
, Definition 4.2.12(b)]
. Then it follows from
[
8
, Proposition 3.1.9(b) and Proposition 4.3.8(a)]
that the vertical restriction map on the left is an isomorphism. Since the bottom horizontal map is an inclusion, it follows from Lemma
4.3.5
(d) that the top horizontal map is injective. Since
\omega[\mathscr{L}]\neq 0
in
\PicCon(\Omega)^{G_{0}}[q+1]
by assumption, it follows that
\omega[\mathscr{L}|_{\Upsilon}]\neq 0
in
\PicCon(\Upsilon)^{I}[q+1]
as required.

The group
J:=H\cap I
contains an open subgroup of
N
because
H
contains an open subgroup of
\mathbb{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
by assumption. We can now apply Corollary
7.4.6
to see that
(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})_{|\mathbb{D}}=j_{0,\ast}(\mathscr{L}_{|\Upsilon})
is a simple object in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{D}/H\cap I}
.

Similarly,
{}^{w}H\cap I
contains an open subgroup of
N
because
H
contains an open subgroup of
\mathbb{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
. A similar argument to the above shows that we can apply Corollary
7.4.6
to
w^{\ast}(\mathscr{L}_{|w\Upsilon})\in\PicCon^{I}(\Upsilon)[e]
and
J:={}^{w}H\cap I
to deduce that
j_{0,\ast}w^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{|w\Upsilon}
is a simple object in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{D}/{}^{w}H\cap I}
. Hence
(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})_{|w\mathbb{D}}=w_{\ast}(j_{0,\ast}w^{\ast}\mathscr{L}_{|w\Upsilon})
is a simple object in
\mathcal{C}_{w\mathbb{D}/H\cap{}^{w}I}
.

Now, consider a short exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{M}\to j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}\to\mathcal{N}\to 0
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}/H}
with
\mathcal{M}\neq 0
. By
[
9
, Proposition 3.6.10(b)]
it induces exact sequences

0\to\mathcal{M}_{|\mathbb{D}}\to(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})_{|\mathbb{D}}\to\mathcal{N}_{|\mathbb{D}}\to 0
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{D}/H\cap I}
and
0\to\mathcal{M}_{|w\mathbb{D}}\to(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})_{|w\mathbb{D}}\to\mathcal{N}_{|w\mathbb{D}}\to 0
in
\mathcal{C}_{w\mathbb{D}/H\cap{}^{w}I}
.
Since
\mathcal{M}\neq 0
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{M}_{|\mathbb{D}}\neq 0
. Then the map
\mathcal{M}_{|\mathbb{D}}\to(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})_{|\mathbb{D}}
is an isomorphism, and
\mathcal{N}_{|\mathbb{D}}=0
. Next,
\mathcal{M}_{|w\mathbb{D}}
cannot be zero, as otherwise
(j_{\ast}\mathscr{L})_{|\mathbb{D}\cap w\mathbb{D}}\cong(\mathcal{M}_{|\mathbb{D}})_{|\mathbb{D}\cap w\mathbb{D}}=(\mathcal{M}_{|w\mathbb{D}})_{|\mathbb{D}\cap w\mathbb{D}}=0
which is not the case. So in fact
\mathcal{M}_{|w\mathbb{D}}\neq 0
, which forces
\mathcal{N}_{|w\mathbb{D}}=0
. Hence
\mathcal{N}=0
and
\mathcal{M}\to j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Let
D(H,K)
denote the locally
F
-analytic distribution algebra of a locally
F
-analytic group
H
with coefficients in
K
. Let
\mathfrak{g}=\Lie(H)\otimes_{F}K
denote the
K
-Lie algebra of
H
and let
\mathfrak{{m}}_{0}=D(H,K)\cdot(\mathfrak{g}U(\mathfrak{g})\cap Z(U(\mathfrak{g})))
be the closed ideal of
D(H,K)
generated by the kernel of the trivial infinitesimal character
Z(U(\mathfrak{g}))\to F
that sends
\mathfrak{g}
to zero. We have the following general ‘rigid-analytic equivariant Beilinson-Bernstein localisation’ result.

Theorem 7.4.8
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a connected, split semisimple algebraic group over
F
, let
\mathbb{X}
be its flag variety and let
\mathbf{X}
be the
K
-rigid analytification of
\mathbb{X}\times_{F}K
. For every open subroup
H
of
\mathbb{G}(F)
, the functor of global sections

\Gamma(\mathbf{X},-):\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{X}/H}\longrightarrow\{M\in\mathcal{C}_{D(H,K)}:\mathfrak{{m}}_{0}\cdot M=0\}.

is a well-defined equivalence of categories.

Proof.

First, note that by
[
9
, Theorem 6.5.1]
,
D(H,K)
is isomorphic as a Fréchet-Stein algebra to a certain completed skew-group algebra denoted
\wideparen{U}(\mathfrak{g},H)
that was introduced at
[
9
, Definition 6.2.7]
. We wish to apply
[
9
, Theorem 6.4.9]
to the
K
-algebraic group
\mathbb{G}\times_{F}K
and the continuous inclusion
H\hookrightarrow\mathbb{G}(K)
. In
[
9
]
it was assumed that the algebraic group
\mathbb{G}
is simply connected; however this assumption is only used in the proof of
[
9
, Theorem 5.3.5]
, where it can be easily avoided altogether (by choosing an
K^{\circ}
-module basis for
\mathfrak{h}
) or instead by working (as we are currently doing) with a discretely valued ground field
K
. With this in mind, Theorem
7.4.8
then follows from
[
9
, Theorem 6.4.9]
, once we observe that the functor of global sections
\Gamma(\mathbf{X},-)
is quasi-inverse to the localisation functor
\Loc_{\mathbf{X}}^{\wideparen{U}(\mathfrak{g},G)}
, in view of
[
9
, Theorem 6.4.8]
. ∎

Recall that
G^{0}=\{g\in\mathbb{GL}_{2}(F):v_{\pi_{F}}(\det g)=0\}
.

Corollary 7.4.9
.

Suppose
[\mathscr{L}]\in\PicCon^{G^{0}}(\Omega)_{\tors}
whose image
\omega[\mathscr{L}]
in
\PicCon(\Omega)
is non-zero. Then
\mathscr{L}(\Omega)
is a coadmissible and topologically irreducible
D(H,K)
-module for
any
closed subgroup
H
of
G^{0}
containing an open subgroup of
\mathbb{SL}_{2}(F)
.

Proof.

Let
Z
denote the centre of
\GL_{2}(F)
; note that
Z
is contained in
G^{0}
and that it acts trivially on the
K
-analytic projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
via Möbius transformations. Since
[\mathscr{L}]
is a torsion element of
\PicCon^{G^{0}}(\Omega)
,
\mathscr{L}^{\otimes e}
is
G^{0}
-equivariantly isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
for some
e\geqslant 1
. Since
Z
acts trivially on
\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
, some finite-index open subgroup
Z^{\prime}
of
Z
acts trivially on
\mathscr{L}
. We can then choose a finite-index open subgroup
H^{\prime}
of
H
such that
H^{\prime}\cap Z\leqslant Z^{\prime}
. Then the
H^{\prime}
-action on
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}
factors through
\overline{H^{\prime}}:=H^{\prime}Z/Z
and Theorem
7.4.7
tells us that
j_{\ast}\mathscr{L}\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}/\overline{H^{\prime}}}
is a simple object.

Our condition on
H
guarantees that its image
\overline{H}:=HZ/Z
is open in
\mathbb{PGL}_{2}(F)=\GL_{2}(F)/Z
. Hence
\overline{H^{\prime}}
is open in
\mathbb{PGL}_{2}(F)
as well. We now apply Theorem
7.4.8
to
\mathbb{G}:=\mathbb{PGL}_{2,F}
and
\overline{H^{\prime}}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{PGL}_{2}(F)
to see that
\mathscr{L}(\Omega)
is a coadmissible and topologically irreducible
D(\overline{H^{\prime}},K)
-module, killed by
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
. Since
H\cap Z^{\prime}
acts trivially on
\mathscr{L}(\Omega)
, it follows by inflation that the same statements hold for
\mathscr{L}(\Omega)
viewed as a
D(H^{\prime},K)
-module. Hence they also hold
a fortiori
for
\mathscr{L}(\Omega)
viewed as a
D(H,K)
-module.∎

7.5.
j_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
and proof of Corollary B

We begin by recording a very general and very important example of a coadmissible
G
-equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-module.

Proposition 7.5.1
.

Let
G
be a
p
-adic Lie group acting continuously on a smooth rigid
K
-analytic space
X
. Then

(1)

\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a coadmissible
G
-equivariant
\mathcal{D}
-module on
X
:
\mathcal{O}_{X}\in\mathcal{C}_{X/G}
.

(2)

\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a simple object in
\mathcal{C}_{X/G}
whenever
X
is connected.

Proof.

(1) Certainly
\mathcal{O}_{X}
is a
G
-equivariant locally Fréchet
\mathcal{D}
-module on
X
. Then in view of
[
9
, Definition 3.6.7]
, it is enough to consider the case where
(X,G)
is small in the sense of
[
9
, Definition 3.4.4]
. We will show that in this case

(1)

\mathcal{O}(X)
is a coadmissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,G)
-module, and

(2)

there is a natural continuous
G-\mathcal{D}
-linear isomorphism

\Loc^{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,G)}_{X}(\mathcal{O}(X))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{X}.

(a) Because
(X,G)
is small, we can choose a
G
-stable affine formal model
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{O}(X)
and a
G
-stable free
\mathcal{A}
-Lie lattice
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{T}(X)
. By replacing
\mathcal{L}
by
\pi\mathcal{L}
if necessary, we may assume that
[\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}]\subseteq\pi\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}\cdot\mathcal{A}\subseteq\pi\mathcal{A}
. Since
\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{T}(X)
is an
\mathcal{A}
-Lie lattice,
\mathcal{A}
is naturally a
U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})
-module. Since
\mathcal{A}
is
\pi
-adically complete, the
U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})
-action extends to
\mathcal{U}_{n}:=\widehat{U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})}
and clearly
1\in\mathcal{A}
generates
\mathcal{A}
as a
\mathcal{U}_{n}
-module. Since
\mathcal{T}(X)
is a free
\mathcal{A}
-Lie lattice, we can choose an
\mathcal{A}
-module basis
\{\partial_{1},\cdots,\partial_{d}\}
for
\mathcal{L}
. Then the left ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{U}_{n}
generated by this finite set annihilates
1\in\mathcal{A}
:
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\ann_{\mathcal{U}_{n}}(1)
.

For the reverse inclusion, let
Q\in\ann_{\mathcal{U}_{n}}(1)
and write
Q=\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}f_{\alpha}(\pi^{n}\partial)^{\alpha}
for some family
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\subset\mathcal{A}
converging to zero as
|\alpha|\to\infty
. Then
Q\cdot 1=0
implies that
f_{0}=0
, and since
f_{\alpha}\to 0
as
|\alpha|\to\infty
we see that for every
m\geqslant 0
,
Q
lies in
\mathcal{I}+\pi^{m}\mathcal{U}_{n}
. Since
[\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}]\subseteq\pi\mathcal{L}
,
[
9
, Proposition 4.1.6(b)]
tells us that the cyclic
\mathcal{U}_{n}
-module
\mathcal{U}_{n}/\mathcal{I}
is
\pi
-adically separated. Hence
Q\in\mathcal{I}
and
\ann_{\mathcal{U}_{n}}(1)=\mathcal{I}
. This implies that
\mathcal{A}
is a finitely presented
\mathcal{U}_{n}
-module, and hence that
\mathcal{O}(X)=\mathcal{A}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}K
is a finitely presented
\mathcal{U}_{n}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}K
-module for every
n\geqslant 0
.

Using
[
9
, Corollary 3.3.7]
, choose a good chain
G_{\bullet}
for
\mathcal{L}
. In view of
[
9
, Definitions 3.3.3 and 3.2.13]
this means that
G_{0}\geqslant G_{1}\geqslant\cdots
is a chain of open normal subgroups of
G
with trivial intersection, such that
G_{n}\leqslant G_{\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}}
for all
n\geqslant 0
. Define

\mathscr{T}_{n}(X):=\widehat{U(\pi^{n}\mathcal{L})_{K}}=\mathcal{U}_{n}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}K\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathscr{S}_{n}(X):=\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)\rtimes_{G_{n}}G

for each
n\geqslant 0
; then by
[
9
, Lemma 3.3.4]
we have the Fréchet-Stein presentation

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,G)\cong\mathop{\underset{\scriptstyle}{\underset{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.75346pt][-1.50694pt]{$\longleftarrow$}}{\operatorname{lim}}}}\mathscr{S}_{n}(X).

Since
G_{n}\leqslant G_{\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}}
, it follows from
[
9
, Definition 3.2.11]
that for every
g\in G_{n}
, the action of
\beta_{\pi^{n}\mathcal{L}}(g)\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
on
\mathcal{A}
agrees with the action of
g\in G_{n}
. Therefore the natural
\mathcal{U}_{n}\rtimes G
-action on
\mathcal{A}
factors through the crossed product
\mathcal{U}_{n}\rtimes_{G_{n}}G
, and hence the natural
\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)
-action on
\mathcal{O}(X)
extends to
\mathscr{S}_{n}(X)=\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)\rtimes_{G_{n}}G
. Since
\mathcal{O}(X)
is a finitely presented
\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)
-module, Lemma
7.3.1
implies that it is also a finitely presented
\mathscr{S}_{n}(X)
-module. To see that
\mathcal{O}(X)
is a coadmissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,G)
-module, it remains to see that for every
n\geqslant 0
, the following natural maps are isomorphisms:

(67)

\mathscr{S}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(X)}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{O}(X)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(X).

Fix
n\geqslant 0
. We saw above that
\ann_{\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)}(1)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)\partial_{i}
. Therefore

(68)

\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)^{d}\longrightarrow\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(X)\to 0

is a presentation of
\mathcal{O}(X)
as a
\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)
-module, where the first map sends
(Q_{i})_{i=1}^{d}\in\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)^{d}
to
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}Q_{i}\partial_{i}
and the second map sends
Q\in\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)
to
Q\cdot 1
. This presentation implies that the canonical
\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)
-linear action map

\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathscr{T}_{n+1}(X)}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{O}(X)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(X)

is an isomorphism. Since this map factors through
\mathscr{S}_{n}(X)\underset{\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(X)}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{O}(X)
by Theorem
5.1.8
, we conclude that the map (
67
) is an isomorphism as well.

(b) After possibly replacing
G
by an open subgroup and inspecting
[
9
, 3.5.12, 3.5.1 and 3.5.6]
, it suffices to prove that the natural action map

\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(Y,G)\underset{\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,G)}{\wideparen{\otimes}}{}\mathcal{O}(X)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(Y)

is an isomorphism, whenever
Y
is a
G
-stable affinoid subdomain of
X
. For this, in view of
[
9
, Lemma 3.3.4]
and
[
7
, Lemma 7.3]
, it is enough to prove that

\mathscr{S}_{n}(Y)\underset{\mathscr{S}_{n}(X)}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{O}(X)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}(Y)

is an isomorphism for all sufficiently large
n
. We can use Theorem
5.1.8
again to reduce to showing that
\mathscr{T}_{n}(Y)\underset{\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)}{\otimes}{}\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(Y)
is an isomorphism for all sufficiently large
n
. Since
\{\partial_{1},\cdots,\partial_{d}\}
is still a basis for
\mathcal{T}(Y)
as an
\mathcal{O}(Y)
-module, this follows immediately from the explicit presentations
\mathcal{O}(X)\cong\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)/\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\mathscr{T}_{n}(X)\partial_{i}
and
\mathcal{O}(Y)\cong\mathscr{T}_{n}(Y)/\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\mathscr{T}_{n}(Y)\partial_{i}
coming from (
68
).

(2) Suppose first that
X
is a small affinoid, in the sense that
\mathcal{T}(X)
is a free
\mathcal{O}(X)
-module. Then because
X
is connected,
\mathcal{O}(X)
is a simple
\mathcal{D}(X)
-module and therefore a simple coadmissible
\wideparen{\mathcal{D}}(X,H)
-module for any open subgroup
H
of
G
such that
(X,H)
is small. Hence
\mathcal{O}_{X}\in\mathcal{C}_{X/H}
is a simple object by
[
9
, Theorem 3.6.11]
in this case, and it is therefore
a fortiori
simple in
\mathcal{C}_{X/G}
.

Returning to the general case, we can choose an admissible open covering
\mathcal{U}
of
X
consisting of small, connected, affinoids. Suppose that
\mathcal{M}
is a subobject of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
and consider
\mathcal{V}_{1}:=\{U\in\mathcal{U}:\mathcal{M}|_{U}=\mathcal{O}_{U}\}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}=\{U\in\mathcal{U}:\mathcal{M}|_{U}=0\}
. Then
\mathcal{U}
is the disjoint union of
\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}
by the first paragraph. Now if
U\in\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
V\in\mathcal{V}_{2}
with
U\cap V\neq\emptyset
, then

(\mathcal{M}|_{V})|_{U\cap V}=\mathcal{M}|_{U\cap V}=(\mathcal{M}|_{U})|_{U\cap V}=\mathcal{O}_{U\cap V}

since
U\in\mathcal{V}_{1}
. Thus
\mathcal{M}|_{V}\neq 0
a contradiction. Since
X
is connected if follows that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{V}_{1}
or
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{V}_{2}
i.e.
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
or
\mathcal{M}=0
as required.
∎

Recall that
j:\Omega\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the open embedding.
We write
G:=\mathbb{GL}_{2}(F)
and observe that there is a natural injective morphism of
G
-equivariant locally Fréchet
\mathcal{D}
-modules on
\mathbb{P}^{1}

\alpha:\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\to j_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}.

Proposition 7.5.2
.

\coker\alpha
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}/G}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.5.1
(1),
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}/G}
. The trivial line bundle with flat connection
\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
is lies in
\Con^{G}(\Omega)
by
[
8
, Lemma 3.2.4]
. Applying Theorem
7.4.1
to
[\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}]
with
H=G_{0}
, we see that
j_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}/G_{0}}
as well. Since it is naturally a
G
-equivariant locally Fréchet
\mathcal{D}
-module on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
[
9
, Definition 3.6.7]
implies that
j_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}/G}
. Finally, the morphism
\alpha
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}/G}
, and we can conclude by applying
[
9
, Lemma 3.7.5]
.
∎

Lemma 7.5.3
.

\coker\alpha
is supported on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(F)
in the sense of
[
5
, Definition 1.2.1(a)]
.

Proof.

The restriction of
j_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
back to
\Omega
is equal to
\mathcal{O}_{\Omega}
. Therefore
(\coker\alpha)_{|\Omega}=0
. Since
\mathbb{P}^{1}=\Omega\cup\mathbb{P}^{1}(F)
, this means that
\coker\alpha
is supported on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(F)
.
∎

Recall from
\lx@sectionsign\ref{AffTrans}
that
\mathbb{B}=\{g\in\mathbb{GL}_{2}:g_{21}=0\}
denotes the subgroup scheme of upper-triangular matrices in
\mathbb{GL}_{2}
, and let
B:=\mathbb{B}(F)
. Let
\iota:\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the closed embedding. Recall the algebra of smooth distributions
D^{\infty}(B,K)
from
[
42
]
.

In what follows, we will silently identify the category
